1

Module 1·Months 1–2 · about 1 weekMust

SQL basics

19 topics · about 2.5 h of reading

Almost every Java application you will build keeps its data in a relational database: users, orders, payments, bookings. Spring Boot, JPA and Hibernate sit on top of it, but underneath they all send SQL to the database. If you understand SQL well, you can write correct queries, debug slow or wrong results, read what Hibernate generates and answer the SQL round that almost every Java interview has.

This module covers the core: how relational databases organise data, how to install PostgreSQL (or MySQL) and a client tool, how to read data with SELECT, filters, sorting and paging, how to change data with INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE, and how to define tables with the right data types and constraints. Every example runs on one sample database (a small company plus a small online shop) that you create in The sample database; Modules 2 and 3 use the same tables. PostgreSQL syntax is the default and MySQL differences are noted where they matter. Database design is in Module 4, and indexes and transactions inside the engine are in Module 5. The same tables are used from Java in Spring Boot · Module 3.

  • Explain tables, rows, columns, primary keys and foreign keys, and what a DBMS does for you.
  • Install PostgreSQL or MySQL, connect with psql / mysql and a GUI client, and create a database and a user.
  • Write SELECT queries with WHERE, ORDER BY, LIMIT and DISTINCT, and handle NULL correctly.
  • Insert, update, delete and upsert rows safely, without destroying data by accident.
  • Create and change tables with suitable data types and constraints, and recognise the error each constraint raises.

Relational databases: tables, rows, columns and keys

A relational database stores data in tables. Each row is one record, each column is one attribute with a fixed data type, and a primary key gives every row a unique identity that other tables can point to with foreign keys.

Database, DBMS and RDBMS

A database is an organised collection of data that programs can store and find again later. A DBMS (database management system) is the software that manages it. It writes the data to disk, answers queries, lets hundreds of users work at the same time, and keeps the data safe when the machine crashes. An RDBMS (relational DBMS) is a DBMS that organises data as tables and is queried with SQL. PostgreSQL, MySQL, MariaDB, Oracle Database, Microsoft SQL Server and SQLite are all RDBMSs. Databases that do not use tables (MongoDB, Redis, Cassandra) are called NoSQL databases; they are covered in Module 6.

Why not just write the data to files from your Java code? Because the DBMS solves problems you would otherwise have to solve yourself, badly:

NeedPlain files (CSV, JSON)RDBMS
Find one record among millionsRead and parse the whole fileIndex lookup in milliseconds
Two users write at the same timeLost updates or a corrupted fileTransactions and locks
Power cut in the middle of a writeHalf-written fileAtomic commit and crash recovery
Keep the data validYour code must check everything, everywhereConstraints enforced by the engine for every client
Answer a new questionWrite new parsing codeWrite a new query
Control who can see whatOperating-system file permissionsUsers, roles and GRANT

Tables, rows and columns

A table holds data about one kind of thing: departments, employees, orders. A column is one attribute of that thing (name, location) and has a fixed data type, so every value in the column is of that type. A row is one record: one department, one employee. The set of columns and their types is the table's schema (its structure); the rows are its data.

The theory behind this comes from Edgar F. Codd's relational model (1970). In that theory a table is called a relation, which is why these are "relational" databases. You will meet the formal words in interviews and in DBMS courses:

Relation
A table.
Tuple
A row.
Attribute
A column.
Domain
The set of values a column may hold (its data type plus its constraints).
Degree
The number of columns in a table.
Cardinality
The number of rows in a table. (The same word also describes relationships: one-to-many, many-to-many.)

A relation has a few properties that matter every day:

  • Every row has the same columns. You cannot add a column to just one row.
  • Each cell holds one value (atomic). A list such as 'java,sql,react' in one column breaks this rule and makes queries painful (see first normal form in Module 4).
  • Rows have no order. A table is a set, not a list. When you query without ORDER BY, the database may return rows in any order, and the order can change after an update or a new index.
  • Rows should be distinguishable. That is the job of the primary key.

Primary keys

A primary key (PK) is a column, or a set of columns, whose value uniquely identifies one row. The database enforces two things for it: the value must be unique and it must be NOT NULL. A table has at most one primary key, and the database builds an index on it automatically, so looking up a row by its key is fast.

  • A natural key is a real-world value that is already unique: an email address, a PAN number, an ISBN.
  • A surrogate key is a value the database generates only to identify the row: an auto-incrementing number or a UUID. It has no business meaning, so it never needs to change.
  • A composite key uses two or more columns together. In the sample database, employee_projects uses (emp_id, project_id): one employee can be on many projects and one project has many employees, but the same pair appears only once.

Any column set that could serve as the primary key is a candidate key; the one you choose is the primary key and the others usually get a UNIQUE constraint.

Foreign keys and relationships

A foreign key (FK) is a column whose value must match a key value in another table (or in the same table). employees.dept_id points to departments.dept_id: the database refuses an employee row with dept_id = 99 if department 99 does not exist. This guarantee is called referential integrity. Foreign keys create the relationships between tables:

  • One-to-many: one department has many employees. The FK lives on the "many" side (employees.dept_id).
  • Many-to-many: employees and projects. You need a third table (a junction table, here employee_projects) with an FK to each side.
  • One-to-one: an FK that is also unique, for example a user_profiles row per user.

Where tables live: server, database, schema

In PostgreSQL one server (called a cluster) holds many databases. Each database holds one or more schemas, which are namespaces for tables; the default schema is public, so the full name of a table is public.employees. A connection always belongs to one database, and a query cannot simply join tables from two databases. In MySQL the words database and schema mean the same thing, and one query can read shop.orders and hr.employees together.

Rule: every table gets a primary key

Without one, duplicate rows can appear, you cannot reliably update or delete one specific row, and tools depend on it: JPA entities need an @Id, and replication and many admin tools expect a key.

Convention: surrogate primary key plus unique natural key

Most Java projects use a generated BIGINT or UUID as the primary key and add a UNIQUE constraint on natural identifiers such as email. Emails and phone numbers change; a surrogate key never has to.

Edge cases

  • SQL itself allows a table without a primary key. The table then behaves like a bag of rows and can hold exact duplicates. MySQL's InnoDB engine quietly uses the first UNIQUE NOT NULL index as the clustered key, or creates a hidden row id if there is none.
  • A foreign key column may be NULL unless you declare it NOT NULL. A NULL FK means "no related row", for example an employee not yet assigned to a department.
  • A foreign key may point to the same table: employees.manager_id refers to employees.emp_id. This is a self-reference.

Mistake: trusting the order of rows

"It always comes back sorted by id" is luck, not a guarantee. After updates, deletes, a new index or a parallel plan, the order changes. If the order matters, write ORDER BY.

Interview angle

  • Primary key vs unique key: both reject duplicates; a primary key also rejects NULL and there is only one per table, while a table can have many unique keys and a unique column can usually hold NULLs.
  • Can a table have two primary keys? No. It can have one primary key made of two columns (a composite key).
  • Can a foreign key be NULL? Yes, unless the column is NOT NULL.
  • Candidate key vs primary key vs super key: a super key is any column set that is unique; a candidate key is a minimal super key; the primary key is the candidate key you pick.

SQL: a declarative language and its sublanguages

In SQL you describe the result you want, not the steps to get it; the database's planner chooses how. SQL statements fall into five groups: DDL, DML, DQL, DCL and TCL.

What "declarative" means

SQL (Structured Query Language, said "ess-queue-ell" or "sequel") is the standard language for relational databases. It is declarative: you state what you want, and the database decides how to get it. Java is imperative: you write every step. Compare finding Sales employees who earn more than ₹1,00,000, highest paid first:

// You decide HOW: loop, test, collect, sort
List<Employee> result = new ArrayList<>();
for (Employee e : allEmployees) {
    if (e.getDeptId() == 2 && e.getSalary().compareTo(new BigDecimal("100000")) > 0) {
        result.add(e);
    }
}
result.sort(Comparator.comparing(Employee::getSalary).reversed());
-- You say WHAT you want; the database picks the method
SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 2
  AND salary > 100000
ORDER BY salary DESC;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Karan      | 160000.00
 Ananya     | 150000.00
(2 rows)

The SQL version does not say whether to scan the whole table or use an index, or which sort algorithm to use. The database's query planner (also called the optimizer) looks at table sizes, indexes and statistics and picks the cheapest plan. The same query can run differently on a table of 10 rows and a table of 10 crore rows, and you do not change a word. This is also why you can ask the database to explain its plan with EXPLAIN (covered in Module 5).

Standard SQL and dialects

SQL is an ISO/ANSI standard (first published in 1986; the current edition is SQL:2023). Every database implements most of the core and adds its own extensions, so each has a dialect. The basics (SELECT, WHERE, JOIN, GROUP BY, INSERT) are the same everywhere. Differences show up in details: paging (LIMIT vs FETCH FIRST vs SQL Server's TOP), auto-generated ids, string functions, date functions and upserts. PostgreSQL follows the standard closely; MySQL is more lenient and has more non-standard behaviour. Learn one dialect well and the others take days, not months.

The five sublanguages

SQL statements are grouped by what they do. Interviewers like this classification.

GroupFull namePurposeStatements
DDLData Definition LanguageDefine and change structure CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE, COMMENT
DMLData Manipulation LanguageChange the rows INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, MERGE
DQLData Query LanguageRead rowsSELECT
DCLData Control LanguagePermissions GRANT, REVOKE
TCLTransaction Control LanguageGroup changes into transactions BEGIN / START TRANSACTION, COMMIT, ROLLBACK, SAVEPOINT
 
CREATE TABLE skills (skill_id INT PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(50));   -- DDL
INSERT INTO skills (skill_id, name) VALUES (1, 'Java');             -- DML
SELECT skill_id, name FROM skills;                                  -- DQL
GRANT SELECT ON skills TO report_user;                              -- DCL
BEGIN;                                                              -- TCL
UPDATE skills SET name = 'Java 21' WHERE skill_id = 1;              -- DML
COMMIT;                                                             -- TCL

Note: textbooks disagree on the boxes

Many books put SELECT inside DML and have only four groups. TRUNCATE removes rows but is classed as DDL, because it works on the whole table rather than row by row. If an interviewer uses four groups, follow their lead and mention that some sources split out DQL.

Basic syntax rules

  • A statement ends with a semicolon ;. Tools such as psql wait for it before running anything.
  • Keywords are case-insensitive: select, SELECT and Select are the same. Convention is uppercase (see SQL style).
  • String and date literals use single quotes: 'Mumbai', '2026-04-05'. To put a quote inside a string, double it: 'D''Souza'.
  • Double quotes are for identifiers in PostgreSQL ("Order"), not for strings.
  • Whitespace and line breaks do not matter, so format for readability.

PostgreSQL vs MySQL at a glance

TopicPostgreSQLMySQL (InnoDB)
Owner and licenceCommunity project, permissive PostgreSQL LicenceOracle; Community Edition under GPL, plus commercial editions
Default port54323306
StandardsStrict and close to the SQL standardMore lenient; behaviour depends on sql_mode
String comparisonCase-sensitive by defaultCase-insensitive with the default collation utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci
Auto idsGENERATED … AS IDENTITY (or SERIAL) AUTO_INCREMENT
Return changed rowsRETURNINGNot available; use LAST_INSERT_ID()
DDL inside a transactionYes: CREATE/ALTER can be rolled backNo: DDL commits implicitly
Full outer joinYesNo (emulate with UNION)
JSONJSON and indexed binary JSONBJSON (binary format)
ExtrasMaterialized views, arrays, rich types, extensions (PostGIS, pgvector) Simple to run; very common in PHP and older Java stacks

Both are excellent, free and fully supported by Spring Boot. Learn PostgreSQL as your default (it is the more common choice for new Java projects and its strictness teaches good habits) and learn the MySQL differences noted throughout these notes, because many service-company projects run MySQL.

Tip: think in sets, not loops

SQL works on whole sets of rows at once. If you catch yourself fetching rows into Java just to filter, count or join them in a loop, the database can almost always do it faster in one query, close to the data.

Mistake: double quotes around strings

Wrong: PostgreSQL reads "Mumbai" as a column name

SELECT name FROM departments WHERE location = "Mumbai";
-- ERROR:  column "Mumbai" does not exist

Right: single quotes for values

SELECT name FROM departments WHERE location = 'Mumbai';

MySQL accepts double-quoted strings by default (unless the ANSI_QUOTES mode is on), which hides this bug until you move to PostgreSQL.

Interview angle

  • Why is SQL declarative? You describe the result; the optimizer chooses the access paths, join order and algorithms.
  • DDL vs DML: DDL changes structure (tables, columns), DML changes rows. In MySQL DDL commits the current transaction automatically; in PostgreSQL most DDL can be rolled back.
  • Is TRUNCATE DDL or DML? DDL. It removes all rows without scanning them one by one.

Install PostgreSQL or MySQL and a client tool

Install a database server (natively or in Docker), connect with the command-line client (psql or mysql) and a GUI such as pgAdmin, DBeaver or MySQL Workbench, then create a database and a non-superuser account for your work.

The pieces

A database has two parts. The server is a background process that owns the data files and listens on a network port (5432 for PostgreSQL, 3306 for MySQL). A client connects to that port, sends SQL and shows the results. Your Spring Boot application is just another client, using a JDBC driver. You can install the server directly on Windows or run it in Docker; either is fine for learning.

PostgreSQL on Windows

  1. Open postgresql.org/download/windows and download the installer (packaged by EDB). Pick the newest version offered.
  2. Run it and keep the default components: PostgreSQL Server, pgAdmin 4 (GUI) and Command Line Tools (psql, pg_dump). Stack Builder is optional.
  3. Set a password for the postgres superuser and write it down. Keep port 5432.
  4. Finish. The server is installed as a Windows service and starts with Windows.
  5. Open a new terminal and run psql --version. If it is not found, add C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\<version>\bin to your PATH, or use the SQL Shell (psql) shortcut from the Start menu.

PostgreSQL in Docker

If Docker Desktop is installed (see DevOps · Module 3), one command gives you a server, and deleting the container leaves your machine clean:

# Start PostgreSQL 18 in the background, data kept in a named volume
docker run --name pg-learn \
  -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=change_me \
  -p 5432:5432 \
  -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql \
  -d postgres:18

# Open psql inside the container as the postgres superuser
docker exec -it pg-learn psql -U postgres

Version note: Docker volume path

The official image changed its data directory layout in version 18, so mount the volume at /var/lib/postgresql. For images up to 17 the usual mount point is /var/lib/postgresql/data. Check the image's page on Docker Hub when you change versions.

MySQL on Windows or in Docker

From dev.mysql.com/downloads, install MySQL Community Server (the 8.4 LTS release is the safe choice) with its MSI installer, and MySQL Workbench as the GUI. The installer asks for a root password and offers to run MySQL as a Windows service on port 3306. With Docker:

docker run --name mysql-learn \
  -e MYSQL_ROOT_PASSWORD=change_me \
  -p 3306:3306 \
  -v mysqldata:/var/lib/mysql \
  -d mysql:8.4

docker exec -it mysql-learn mysql -u root -p

GUI clients

ToolWorks withGood for
pgAdmin 4PostgreSQLComes with the Windows installer; query tool, table browser, server admin
DBeaver CommunityPostgreSQL, MySQL, Oracle, SQL Server and many more Free; one tool for every database you meet at work; ER diagrams; data export
MySQL WorkbenchMySQLOfficial MySQL GUI; visual modelling
IntelliJ IDEA Ultimate / DataGripMost databasesSQL inside your IDE with completion that knows your schema (paid)

Learn the command-line client too. On servers, in Docker containers and in CI there is no GUI, and interviewers sometimes ask you to work in a terminal.

psql basics

# host, port, user and database are explicit; psql asks for the password
psql -h localhost -p 5432 -U postgres -d postgres

# the same as a connection URI
psql postgresql://postgres@localhost:5432/postgres

Inside psql, lines that start with a backslash are meta-commands: psql handles them itself and they need no semicolon. Everything else is SQL and runs only when you type ;.

CommandWhat it does
\lList databases
\c learn_sqlConnect to another database
\dtList tables in the current schema (\dt+ adds sizes)
\d employeesDescribe a table: columns, types, defaults, indexes, constraints
\dn, \du, \dv, \di, \df List schemas, roles (users), views, indexes, functions
\x autoShow wide rows vertically when they do not fit
\pset null '(null)'Print NULL visibly instead of an empty cell
\timingToggle showing how long each statement takes
\i file.sqlRun the SQL in a file
\conninfoShow which database, user, host and port you are on
\? and \h SELECTHelp on meta-commands; syntax help for a SQL statement
\qQuit

The prompt tells you where you are: learn_sql=# means you are a superuser, learn_sql=> a normal user, and learn_sql-# means psql is still waiting for the rest of a statement (usually a missing semicolon).

mysql client basics

Taskmysql clientpsql equivalent
Connectmysql -h localhost -P 3306 -u root -ppsql -h localhost -p 5432 -U postgres
List databasesSHOW DATABASES;\l
Switch databaseUSE learn_sql;\c learn_sql
List tablesSHOW TABLES;\dt
Describe a tableDESCRIBE employees; or SHOW CREATE TABLE employees\G\d employees
Run a fileSOURCE file.sql;\i file.sql
Quitexit\q

Note the capital -P for the port in mysql; lowercase -p means "ask for the password". Ending a statement with \G instead of ; prints each row vertically, like psql's \x.

Create a database and a user

Do not run your work, or your application, as the superuser. A superuser can drop every database by mistake. Create a normal user that owns one database:

CREATE USER app_user WITH PASSWORD 'change_me';
CREATE DATABASE learn_sql OWNER app_user;

-- Show timestamps in Indian time for every new session on this database
ALTER DATABASE learn_sql SET timezone TO 'Asia/Kolkata';
psql -h localhost -U app_user -d learn_sql
CREATE DATABASE learn_sql;
CREATE USER 'app_user'@'localhost' IDENTIFIED BY 'change_me';
GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON learn_sql.* TO 'app_user'@'localhost';

In PostgreSQL, CREATE USER is the same as CREATE ROLE … LOGIN: users and groups are both roles. The timezone setting only changes how timestamps are displayed in each session; you will see why that matters in Dates, times, UUID and JSON types. In MySQL a user is the pair of a name and a host: 'app_user'@'localhost' can connect only from the same machine, while 'app_user'@'%' can connect from anywhere (needed when MySQL runs in Docker and you connect from the host).

Version note: PostgreSQL 15 changed the public schema

Before PostgreSQL 15, every user could create tables in the public schema of any database they could connect to. From 15 on, only the database owner (and roles it grants) can. That is why the example makes app_user the OWNER of learn_sql. If you see ERROR: permission denied for schema public, either make the user the owner or run GRANT CREATE ON SCHEMA public TO app_user; as the owner.

Connecting from Spring Boot

Your application connects with the same details, written as a JDBC URL:

# PostgreSQL (driver dependency: org.postgresql:postgresql)
spring.datasource.url=jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/learn_sql
spring.datasource.username=app_user
spring.datasource.password=${DB_PASSWORD}

# MySQL (driver dependency: com.mysql:mysql-connector-j)
# spring.datasource.url=jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/learn_sql

Keep the password out of the file and read it from an environment variable, as shown. Connection pools, JPA and migrations are in Spring Boot · Module 3.

Common setup problems

  • Nothing happens after Enter in psql: you forgot the ;. The prompt shows -#. Type ; and press Enter.
  • Tables "disappear": you are connected to the default postgres database, not learn_sql. Check with \conninfo.
  • password authentication failed for user: wrong password, or wrong user name (on Windows, psql defaults to your Windows user name if you leave out -U).
  • Port already in use: another PostgreSQL (a second install or a Docker container) holds 5432. Stop one, or map Docker to another port: -p 5433:5432.

In real projects

Teams usually run the database for local development in Docker (often through a docker-compose.yml or Testcontainers in tests), so everyone uses the same version as production. The application connects with a limited user, and only administrators hold the superuser password.

Interview angle

Setup questions are rare, but you may be asked: the default ports (5432 and 3306), how to describe a table from the command line (\d or DESCRIBE), why an application should not use the superuser (least privilege: a bug or SQL injection then cannot drop the whole database), and what a JDBC URL looks like.

The sample database: a company and a shop

Every example in Modules 1–3 runs on one small database: a company (departments, employees, projects) and an online shop (customers, products, orders). Run the script on this page once in learn_sql and you can paste every query in these notes and get the same results.

Learning SQL on made-up tables that change in every chapter wastes time. Here you create the tables once and keep them for the whole course. The data is tiny on purpose (a dozen employees, ten orders), so you can check every result in your head. It also contains a few deliberate "traps" (an employee with no manager, a department with no staff, a customer who never ordered) because real data is never tidy, and the classic interview questions are all about these edge cases.

What you get

TableRowsOne row is…Worth knowing
departments4a department and its cityMarketing has no employees
employees12a staff membermanager_id points to another employee; the CEO has no manager and no department
projects5a project owned by a departmentBrand Campaign has nobody assigned
employee_projects11one employee on one projectJunction table, composite key (emp_id, project_id)
customers7a shop customerTwo never ordered; some phones are NULL
products7an item for saleOne has stock 0, one was never sold
orders10one order by one customerOne order is CANCELLED
order_items19one product line inside an orderComposite key (order_id, product_id); copies the price at purchase time

Step 1: the company tables

Do not worry if some syntax is new. Each part (data types, PRIMARY KEY, FOREIGN KEY, CHECK, DEFAULT) is explained later in this module; for now just run it. Connect to learn_sql as app_user (see Install PostgreSQL or MySQL), save all five blocks below in order as sample_schema.sql, and run the file.

-- Start clean (children first) so the script can be re-run at any time.
-- WARNING: this drops tables with these names. Use a practice database only.
DROP TABLE IF EXISTS order_items, orders, products, customers,
                     employee_projects, projects, employees, departments CASCADE;

CREATE TABLE departments (
    dept_id   INT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name      VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    location  VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE employees (
    emp_id      INT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    first_name  VARCHAR(50)    NOT NULL,
    last_name   VARCHAR(50)    NOT NULL,
    email       VARCHAR(100)   NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    dept_id     INT,
    manager_id  INT,
    salary      NUMERIC(10, 2) NOT NULL CHECK (salary > 0),
    hire_date   DATE           NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE,
    CONSTRAINT fk_employees_dept
        FOREIGN KEY (dept_id) REFERENCES departments (dept_id)
        ON DELETE SET NULL,
    CONSTRAINT fk_employees_manager
        FOREIGN KEY (manager_id) REFERENCES employees (emp_id)
        ON DELETE SET NULL
);

CREATE TABLE projects (
    project_id  INT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name        VARCHAR(100)   NOT NULL,
    dept_id     INT            NOT NULL,
    start_date  DATE           NOT NULL,
    end_date    DATE,
    budget      NUMERIC(12, 2) NOT NULL CHECK (budget >= 0),
    CONSTRAINT fk_projects_dept
        FOREIGN KEY (dept_id) REFERENCES departments (dept_id),
    CONSTRAINT ck_projects_dates
        CHECK (end_date IS NULL OR end_date >= start_date)
);

CREATE TABLE employee_projects (
    emp_id          INT         NOT NULL,
    project_id      INT         NOT NULL,
    role            VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,
    hours_per_week  SMALLINT    NOT NULL CHECK (hours_per_week BETWEEN 1 AND 60),
    PRIMARY KEY (emp_id, project_id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_ep_employee
        FOREIGN KEY (emp_id) REFERENCES employees (emp_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    CONSTRAINT fk_ep_project
        FOREIGN KEY (project_id) REFERENCES projects (project_id) ON DELETE CASCADE
);

Step 2: the shop tables

CREATE TABLE customers (
    customer_id  INT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name         VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    email        VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    city         VARCHAR(50)  NOT NULL,
    phone        VARCHAR(15),
    joined_on    DATE         NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE
);

CREATE TABLE products (
    product_id  INT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name        VARCHAR(100)   NOT NULL,
    category    VARCHAR(30)    NOT NULL,
    price       NUMERIC(10, 2) NOT NULL CHECK (price >= 0),
    stock       INT            NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (stock >= 0)
);

CREATE TABLE orders (
    order_id     INT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id  INT         NOT NULL,
    order_date   DATE        NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE,
    status       VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PLACED'
                 CHECK (status IN ('PLACED', 'SHIPPED', 'DELIVERED', 'CANCELLED')),
    CONSTRAINT fk_orders_customer
        FOREIGN KEY (customer_id) REFERENCES customers (customer_id)
);

CREATE TABLE order_items (
    order_id    INT            NOT NULL,
    product_id  INT            NOT NULL,
    quantity    INT            NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
    unit_price  NUMERIC(10, 2) NOT NULL CHECK (unit_price >= 0),
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_items_order
        FOREIGN KEY (order_id) REFERENCES orders (order_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    CONSTRAINT fk_items_product
        FOREIGN KEY (product_id) REFERENCES products (product_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT
);

The rows are inserted with explicit ids so the relationships are easy to read: employee 5 has manager_id = 2, so Priya reports to Vikram. Parents come before children, because MySQL checks foreign keys row by row.

Step 4: move the id counters

-- We inserted ids by hand, so move each counter to the largest id in its table
SELECT setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('departments', 'dept_id'), 4),
       setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('employees', 'emp_id'), 12),
       setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('projects', 'project_id'), 5),
       setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('customers', 'customer_id'), 7),
       setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('products', 'product_id'), 7),
       setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('orders', 'order_id'), 10);

Why? An identity column hands out ids from its own counter, which starts at 1. When you insert ids yourself the counter does not move, so the first insert without an id would try to use 1 again and fail with ERROR: duplicate key value violates unique constraint "departments_pkey". Loading seed data with explicit ids and then fixing the counter is exactly what pg_dump writes into backups. MySQL's AUTO_INCREMENT moves its counter by itself.

Run it and check

# from a normal terminal (use forward slashes in the path on Windows)
psql -h localhost -U app_user -d learn_sql -f sample_schema.sql

# or from inside psql
\i C:/Users/you/sample_schema.sql

# pgAdmin or DBeaver: paste the whole file into a query tool and run it as a script

Then check a few counts, for example SELECT COUNT(*) FROM employees; should print 12. The expected row counts for every table are in the table at the top of this page.

How results are shown in these notes

Results are printed the way psql prints them: a header, a dashed line, the rows, then the row count. Numbers are right-aligned and text is left-aligned. Run this once per psql session so that NULL is visible instead of an empty cell:

\pset null '(null)'

Almost every example has an ORDER BY, so your rows come back in the same order as shown. Without it the order is not guaranteed (see ORDER BY, LIMIT and pagination). A GUI such as DBeaver shows the same data in a grid.

The deliberate quirks

These facts come back again and again in later examples and interview questions:

  • Meera Iyer (the CEO) has dept_id = NULL and manager_id = NULL. She is the only employee without a department, and the root of the management tree.
  • Marketing has no employees, and the Brand Campaign project has nobody assigned.
  • Ananya, Priya and Rahul all earn 150000. Ties matter for ranking and "Nth highest" questions.
  • Karan (160000) earns more than his manager Ananya (150000); Rahul earns exactly the same as his manager Priya.
  • Customers Sara and Tanvi never ordered, the Desk Lamp was never sold, order 4 is cancelled, and three customers have no phone number.

Using MySQL instead

The script is written for PostgreSQL. Three things change for MySQL 8:

  • Replace INT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY with INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY, and delete the setval block.
  • Write date defaults with parentheses: DEFAULT (CURRENT_DATE).
  • Use MySQL 8.0.16 or newer, the first version that enforces CHECK constraints.

Foreign keys are written as separate CONSTRAINT … FOREIGN KEY clauses on purpose. MySQL silently ignores a foreign key written inline, as in dept_id INT REFERENCES departments (dept_id), while the table-level form works in both databases. (MySQL also accepts the word CASCADE in DROP TABLE and ignores it; the order of the table names already puts children first.)

Tip: reset whenever you break something

Practise UPDATE and DELETE freely. When the data no longer matches the outputs in these notes, run sample_schema.sql again: it drops and rebuilds all eight tables in a second.

Mistake: running the script in the wrong database

The script drops tables called employees, orders and so on. Run it only in your practice database. Check with \conninfo first, and never run it against a database that holds real data.

Note: why so little data?

Tiny tables hide performance problems. Slow queries, indexes and EXPLAIN only become interesting with thousands or millions of rows. When you need those, generate them with generate_series (see Recursive CTEs and generating series).

SQL style: formatting, naming, quoting and comments

SQL ignores layout and keyword case, so style is a team agreement: uppercase keywords, lowercase snake_case names, one clause per line, one naming rule for tables, and no quoted identifiers. Consistent style makes queries easy to read in code review and easy to compare in a diff.

Why bother?

The database does not care how a query looks. People do. A 40-line query pasted as one line is unreadable, and a team where every developer names tables differently wastes time guessing. The rules below are the common defaults in Java teams. If your company has its own guide, follow that one instead; the point is to be consistent.

Layout

  • Keywords in UPPERCASE (SELECT, FROM, WHERE, ORDER BY) and names in lowercase. Keywords then stand out from table and column names.
  • One clause per line, starting at the left edge. A long column list gets one column per line.
  • Put AND and OR at the start of the continuation line, indented, so you can comment out one condition without touching the others.
  • Write joins explicitly with JOIN … ON …, one join per line.
  • Use short table aliases (e for employees) and qualify every column with the alias as soon as a query has two tables.
  • End every statement with a semicolon.

Wrong: legal, but hard to read and review

select e.first_name,d.name,e.salary from employees e join departments d
on e.dept_id=d.dept_id where e.salary>100000 and d.location='Bengaluru' order by e.salary desc;

Right: same query, laid out for people

SELECT e.first_name,
       d.name AS department,
       e.salary
FROM employees AS e
JOIN departments AS d ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
WHERE e.salary > 100000
  AND d.location = 'Bengaluru'
ORDER BY e.salary DESC;

Naming

ThingConventionExample
Tablessnake_case; either plural or singular, but pick one and never mix employees (these notes), or employee
Columnssnake_case, no table prefixfirst_name, hire_date
Primary keyid, or <singular>_id id, or emp_id (these notes)
Foreign keySame name as the key it points to employees.dept_id → departments.dept_id
Booleansis_ or has_ prefixis_active
Timestamps and dates_at for moments, _date or _on for dayscreated_at, hire_date
Constraints and indexesType prefix, table, column fk_employees_dept, uq_employees_email, idx_orders_customer_id

Avoid reserved words as names (order, user, group, table, desc, end, check), spaces, CamelCase, and abbreviations nobody can decode. This is why the shop table is called orders and not order. Spring Boot and Hibernate map a Java field firstName to the column first_name by default, so snake_case in the database costs you nothing in Java.

Quoting identifiers

PostgreSQL converts unquoted names to lowercase, so FirstName, FIRSTNAME and firstname are the same column. Double quotes switch that off: "FirstName" is a different, case-sensitive name, and from then on you must type the quotes every time. Quotes are also the only way to use a reserved word or a name with a space. MySQL uses backticks for the same job: `order`. The simple rule: do not create anything that needs quotes.

Wrong: quoted CamelCase name

CREATE TABLE "Employee" (emp_id INT PRIMARY KEY);
SELECT * FROM Employee;
-- ERROR:  relation "employee" does not exist

Right: plain lowercase name

CREATE TABLE employee (emp_id INT PRIMARY KEY);
SELECT * FROM employee;
SELECT * FROM Employee;   -- also works: folded to lowercase

The same problem hits reserved words: SELECT * FROM order; fails with ERROR: syntax error at or near "order", because ORDER starts an ORDER BY clause. It works only as "order".

Comments

-- A line comment: everything to the end of the line is ignored
SELECT first_name,        -- trailing comment
       salary /* inline block comment */
FROM employees
/* A block comment can
   span several lines. */
WHERE dept_id = 3;

MySQL also accepts # for a line comment, and requires a space after --. PostgreSQL allows block comments to nest; MySQL does not. To store documentation inside the database use COMMENT ON TABLE employees IS 'Staff of the company'; in PostgreSQL (psql's \d+ shows it) or the COMMENT '…' clause of CREATE TABLE in MySQL.

Tools that enforce style

Do not argue about spaces in code review; let a tool decide. pgAdmin, DBeaver and IntelliJ can all reformat a selected query. SQLFluff is a linter and formatter you can run in a CI pipeline, and pgFormatter is a formatter for PostgreSQL.

Convention: write SQL like code

SQL files that live in your repository (migrations, reports, seed data) go through the same review as Java. Teams that use Flyway or Liquibase keep every schema change as a versioned SQL file, so naming and layout rules are checked in pull requests.

Mistake: positional and clever shortcuts

ORDER BY 3 and GROUP BY 1, 2 are legal, but nobody can tell which column is meant, and the query silently changes meaning when someone edits the select list. Write the column name or the alias.

Tip: comment the why, not the what

-- keep cancelled orders: finance reports include them helps the next reader. -- select the name above SELECT name does not.

Interview angle

Style is rarely asked directly, but it shows in every whiteboard query. Interviewers notice uppercase keywords, aliases, one clause per line, and explicit joins. If asked about naming, say you pick one convention per project, avoid reserved words and quoted identifiers, and name constraints explicitly so error messages are readable.

SELECT, FROM and WHERE: reading data

SELECT chooses the columns, FROM names the table, and WHERE keeps only the rows that match a condition. A query only reads: it never changes the table.

The shape of a query

The smallest useful query has two parts, and a third part that is optional:

SELECT first_name, last_name, salary   -- which columns to show
FROM employees                          -- which table to read
WHERE dept_id = 3;                      -- which rows to keep (optional)
 first_name | last_name |  salary
------------+-----------+-----------
 Sunita     | Patel     | 120000.00
 Farhan     | Shaikh    |  65000.00
 Isha       | Banerjee  |  55000.00
(3 rows)

Conceptually the database works in this order: take the table in FROM, keep the rows that pass WHERE, then pick the columns in SELECT. You write SELECT first, but it is processed after the filter. That is why a column alias created in SELECT cannot be used in WHERE (see Logical order of a query). Without WHERE you get every row. The columns come out in the order you list them, not the order in the table.

Choosing columns, and why to avoid SELECT *

SELECT * means "all columns". It is handy in a terminal when you explore a table:

SELECT * FROM departments ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id |    name     | location
---------+-------------+-----------
       1 | Engineering | Bengaluru
       2 | Sales       | Mumbai
       3 | HR          | Pune
       4 | Marketing   | Delhi
(4 rows)

In application code and in views, list the columns you need. The star causes real problems:

  • It moves data you do not use. A table with a large TEXT or JSON column sends that data over the network for every row, even if you only show a name.
  • It breaks when the table changes. Someone adds, drops or reorders a column, and code that reads results by position (rs.getString(3) in JDBC) quietly gets the wrong value.
  • It blocks index-only scans. If an index already contains the columns you need, the database can skip the table. With * it cannot (see Module 5).
  • It collides in joins. Joining two tables with SELECT * returns dept_id and name twice, and a program reading "name" gets one of them.

SELECT * is fine in COUNT(*), in EXISTS (SELECT * FROM …), and for quick exploration. JPA and Hibernate always generate an explicit column list.

Comparison operators

OperatorMeaningExample
=equal (one equals sign, not two)dept_id = 2
<> or !=not equal (<> is the standard form) status <> 'CANCELLED'
<, <=less than, less than or equalsalary < 100000
>, >=greater than, greater than or equal hire_date >= '2023-01-01'
 
SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
WHERE salary >= 150000
ORDER BY salary DESC, first_name;

SELECT first_name, hire_date
FROM employees
WHERE hire_date >= '2023-01-01'
ORDER BY hire_date;

SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE category = 'Electronics' ORDER BY price;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Meera      | 300000.00
 Vikram     | 220000.00
 Karan      | 160000.00
 Ananya     | 150000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00
(6 rows)

 first_name | hire_date
------------+------------
 Neha       | 2023-09-04
 Farhan     | 2023-11-20
 Divya      | 2024-01-08
 Isha       | 2024-07-01
(4 rows)

        name         |  price
---------------------+---------
 USB-C Cable         |  299.00
 Wireless Mouse      |  799.00
 Mechanical Keyboard | 3499.00
(3 rows)

Three rules cover most of the confusion:

  • Text and dates go in single quotes, numbers do not. Write dates in the ISO form 'YYYY-MM-DD'; it is unambiguous and sorts correctly.
  • Text comparison is case-sensitive in PostgreSQL: 'engineering' does not equal 'Engineering'. MySQL's default collation ignores case, so the same query finds the row there. See LIKE, ILIKE and case sensitivity.
  • Nothing equals NULL, not even NULL. dept_id = NULL never matches a row. The fix is IS NULL (see NULL and three-valued logic).

Column aliases with AS

An alias gives a column or expression a new name in the result. The name is what JDBC's getString("full_name") and Spring Data projections read. Write AS even though it is optional (the next section shows why).

SELECT first_name || ' ' || last_name AS full_name,
       salary * 12                    AS annual_salary
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 3
ORDER BY emp_id;
   full_name   | annual_salary
---------------+---------------
 Sunita Patel  |    1440000.00
 Farhan Shaikh |     780000.00
 Isha Banerjee |     660000.00
(3 rows)

To get spaces or capital letters in an alias, quote it: AS "Annual Salary" in PostgreSQL (backticks in MySQL). Avoid that for anything a program reads. An alias can be used in ORDER BY, but not in WHERE, because WHERE runs before SELECT creates it:

SELECT salary * 12 AS annual_salary FROM employees WHERE annual_salary > 1000000;
-- ERROR:  column "annual_salary" does not exist

-- Repeat the expression instead
SELECT salary * 12 AS annual_salary FROM employees WHERE salary * 12 > 1000000;

Expressions and calculations

In SELECT and WHERE you can use arithmetic (+ - * / %), functions (ROUND, UPPER) and literal values. In PostgreSQL and MySQL a SELECT needs no FROM, which makes the database a calculator (Oracle needs FROM DUAL).

SELECT 2 + 3 AS total, 7 / 2 AS int_div, 7 / 2.0 AS dec_div, 7 % 3 AS remainder;
 total | int_div |      dec_div       | remainder
-------+---------+--------------------+-----------
     5 |       3 | 3.5000000000000000 |         1
(1 row)

Look at 7 / 2: when both sides are integers, PostgreSQL does integer division and truncates to 3. Make one side a decimal (7 / 2.0) to get 3.5. MySQL behaves differently: 7 / 2 returns 3.5000, and 7 DIV 2 is the integer division. A real example with prices, rounded to two decimals:

SELECT name, price, ROUND(price * 0.18, 2) AS gst
FROM products
WHERE product_id <= 3
ORDER BY product_id;
        name         |  price  |  gst
---------------------+---------+--------
 Wireless Mouse      |  799.00 | 143.82
 Mechanical Keyboard | 3499.00 | 629.82
 USB-C Cable         |  299.00 |  53.82
(3 rows)
TaskPostgreSQLMySQL
Join stringsa || b or CONCAT(a, b)CONCAT(a, b)
A NULL piece|| returns NULL; CONCAT skips the NULL CONCAT returns NULL; CONCAT_WS skips it
Meaning of ||String concatenationLogical OR (unless PIPES_AS_CONCAT mode is on)
7 / 23 (integer division)3.5000
^PowerBitwise XOR

Mistake: a missing comma silently renames a column

Wrong: last_name becomes an alias of first_name

SELECT first_name last_name
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 3;

Right: a comma between columns

SELECT first_name, last_name
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 3;

The wrong query runs without an error and returns one column, named last_name, that holds first names (Sunita, Farhan, Isha). This is why you write AS for every alias: then a missing comma is a syntax error instead of a silent bug.

Errors you will see

  • ERROR: column "salry" does not exist: a typo in a column name. PostgreSQL often adds a hint such as Perhaps you meant to reference the column "employees.salary".
  • ERROR: relation "employee" does not exist: wrong table name, or you are connected to the wrong database or schema.
  • ERROR: syntax error at or near "FROM": very often a trailing comma, as in SELECT first_name, FROM employees.

Edge cases that are fine: a SELECT without WHERE returns every row, and the same column may appear twice in the select list.

Convention

List columns explicitly, give every computed column an AS alias, and always qualify columns with a table alias once a query has more than one table. Always write ORDER BY when the order of rows matters.

Interview angle

  • Why avoid SELECT *? Extra data over the network, breaks position-based code when the schema changes, blocks index-only scans, duplicate column names in joins.
  • Can you use a select alias in WHERE? No: WHERE is evaluated before SELECT. You can use it in ORDER BY.
  • What does SELECT 7 / 2 return? 3 in PostgreSQL (integer division), 3.5000 in MySQL.

ORDER BY, LIMIT, OFFSET and pagination

ORDER BY is the only way to get rows in a guaranteed order. LIMIT and OFFSET (or the standard FETCH FIRST) cut the result down to one page, and a page is only stable when the sort includes a unique tie-breaker column.

Sorting with ORDER BY

A table is a set of rows with no order. If you do not write ORDER BY, the database returns rows in whatever order is cheapest at that moment, and that order can change after an update, a new index or a different query plan. ORDER BY goes at the end of the query (before LIMIT) and sorts ascending (ASC, the default) or descending (DESC). With several columns, the database sorts by the first one and uses the next only to break ties. Each column has its own direction:

SELECT dept_id, first_name, salary
FROM employees
ORDER BY dept_id, salary DESC;
 dept_id | first_name |  salary
---------+------------+-----------
       1 | Vikram     | 220000.00
       1 | Priya      | 150000.00
       1 | Rahul      | 150000.00
       1 | Arjun      | 110000.00
       1 | Divya      |  90000.00
       2 | Karan      | 160000.00
       2 | Ananya     | 150000.00
       2 | Neha       |  75000.00
       3 | Sunita     | 120000.00
       3 | Farhan     |  65000.00
       3 | Isha       |  55000.00
  (null) | Meera      | 300000.00
(12 rows)

You can sort by a column that is not in the select list, by an expression (ORDER BY salary * 12), or by an alias from the select list (ORDER BY annual_salary). Text sorts by the database's collation, which decides whether 'apple' comes before 'Banana'; to ignore case, sort by LOWER(name). To sort in a custom order (for example PLACED, SHIPPED, DELIVERED) use a CASE expression, see CASE expressions.

Where do NULLs go?

PostgreSQL treats NULL as larger than every value. So ASC puts NULLs last and DESC puts them first (the output above shows the CEO's NULL department at the end). You can say what you want with NULLS FIRST or NULLS LAST:

SELECT name, phone FROM customers ORDER BY phone;               -- NULLs last
SELECT name, phone FROM customers ORDER BY phone NULLS FIRST;
     name     |   phone
--------------+------------
 Aarav Shah   | 9876500001
 Kabir Singh  | 9876500003
 Ishita Joshi | 9876500004
 Sara Khan    | 9876500006
 Diya Kapoor  | (null)
 Rohan Das    | (null)
 Tanvi Desai  | (null)
(7 rows)

     name     |   phone
--------------+------------
 Diya Kapoor  | (null)
 Rohan Das    | (null)
 Tanvi Desai  | (null)
 Aarav Shah   | 9876500001
 Kabir Singh  | 9876500003
 Ishita Joshi | 9876500004
 Sara Khan    | 9876500006
(7 rows)

MySQL does the opposite: it treats NULL as the smallest value, so ASC puts NULLs first, and it has no NULLS FIRST/LAST syntax. The usual workaround sorts on phone IS NULL first (which is 0 for real values and 1 for NULL): ORDER BY phone IS NULL, phone puts NULLs last.

LIMIT, OFFSET and FETCH FIRST

LIMIT n returns at most n rows. Together with ORDER BY it answers "top N" questions. OFFSET m skips the first m rows.

SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
ORDER BY salary DESC
LIMIT 3;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Meera      | 300000.00
 Vikram     | 220000.00
 Karan      | 160000.00
(3 rows)

The SQL standard spells the same thing FETCH FIRST n ROWS ONLY. PostgreSQL supports both, MySQL only LIMIT. The standard form can also keep ties (PostgreSQL 13 and later). Three employees share the salary 150000, so asking for the top 5 with ties returns 6 rows:

SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
ORDER BY salary DESC
FETCH FIRST 5 ROWS WITH TIES;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Meera      | 300000.00
 Vikram     | 220000.00
 Karan      | 160000.00
 Ananya     | 150000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00
(6 rows)
NeedPostgreSQLMySQLOther databases
First 10 rowsLIMIT 10 or FETCH FIRST 10 ROWS ONLY LIMIT 10SQL Server TOP 10; Oracle 12c+ FETCH FIRST
Skip 20, take 10LIMIT 10 OFFSET 20 LIMIT 10 OFFSET 20 or LIMIT 20, 10 SQL Server OFFSET 20 ROWS FETCH NEXT 10 ROWS ONLY
Include tiesFETCH FIRST n ROWS WITH TIES (13+)not available Oracle and SQL Server have it

Careful with the MySQL short form: LIMIT 20, 10 means offset 20, count 10 (the offset comes first). Prefer LIMIT 10 OFFSET 20, which reads the same in both databases.

Pagination

A web page that shows 4 employees at a time asks for page 1, 2, 3 and so on. The formula is OFFSET = (page - 1) * page_size. Spring Data's PageRequest.of(page, size) counts pages from 0, so for it the offset is page * size. Page 2 of size 4:

SELECT emp_id, first_name
FROM employees
ORDER BY emp_id
LIMIT 4 OFFSET 4;
 emp_id | first_name
--------+------------
      5 | Priya
      6 | Rahul
      7 | Arjun
      8 | Divya
(4 rows)

The page controls usually also need the total number of rows, which takes a second query, SELECT COUNT(*) FROM employees. Spring Data's Page type runs both for you.

Paging only works when the sort is deterministic. If you sort by salary, three employees tie at 150000, and the database may return those three in a different order for page 1 than for page 2. A row can then appear on both pages, or on neither. The fix is a tie-breaker: add a unique column last, as in ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id.

Why large OFFSETs are slow, and keyset pagination

OFFSET 100000 does not jump to row 100,001. The database reads and throws away the first 100,000 rows, so later pages get slower and slower. Keyset pagination (also called the seek method) remembers the last key of the previous page and asks for the rows after it. An index on the key makes every page equally fast:

SELECT emp_id, first_name
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id > 8          -- last emp_id seen on the previous page
ORDER BY emp_id
LIMIT 4;
 emp_id | first_name
--------+------------
      9 | Karan
     10 | Neha
     11 | Farhan
     12 | Isha
(4 rows)

The trade-off: you can go to the next or previous page, but not jump to "page 57", and the sort key must be unique (or you must compare two columns together). Infinite-scroll feeds and APIs with large tables use it; admin screens with page numbers usually use OFFSET.

Rule: LIMIT and OFFSET need ORDER BY

Without ORDER BY, "the first 3 rows" means any 3 rows. Always sort first, and make the sort unique when you page through results.

Mistake: LIMIT without ORDER BY

Wrong: "top 3 salaries" returns any 3 rows

SELECT first_name, salary FROM employees LIMIT 3;

Right: sort, then cut

SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id
LIMIT 3;

Edge cases and errors

  • LIMIT 0 returns no rows but still returns the column names, which is handy for checking a query's shape. An OFFSET larger than the row count returns an empty result, not an error.
  • Negative values fail: ERROR: LIMIT must not be negative. In PostgreSQL, LIMIT ALL and LIMIT NULL mean "no limit"; MySQL does not accept them.
  • With SELECT DISTINCT, the ORDER BY columns must appear in the select list (see DISTINCT and DISTINCT ON).
  • Ordering by position (ORDER BY 2) is legal but fragile; use the name or alias.

In real projects

Every list endpoint in a REST API should be paginated; an unbounded SELECT on a growing table is a production incident waiting to happen. Spring Data JPA turns a Pageable into exactly the ORDER BY … LIMIT … OFFSET … you see here (see Spring Boot · Module 3).

Interview angle

  • Is the order of rows guaranteed without ORDER BY? No. Not even "by primary key".
  • How do you implement pagination? ORDER BY a unique key, LIMIT size OFFSET (page - 1) * size; mention that big offsets are slow and keyset pagination (WHERE id > last_id) fixes that.
  • Where do NULLs sort? PostgreSQL: as the largest value (last in ASC); MySQL: as the smallest. NULLS FIRST/LAST is PostgreSQL only.

DISTINCT and DISTINCT ON

DISTINCT removes duplicate rows from a result. It compares the whole row, not one column. DISTINCT ON (PostgreSQL only) keeps one row per group, chosen by ORDER BY.

Removing duplicates

Many rows share the same value in some columns: several customers live in Mumbai, many employees work in the same department. SELECT DISTINCT returns each different row once:

SELECT DISTINCT city
FROM customers
ORDER BY city;
   city
-----------
 Ahmedabad
 Bengaluru
 Delhi
 Kolkata
 Mumbai
 Pune
(6 rows)

Internally the database sorts or hashes the result to find duplicates, so DISTINCT costs time on big results. Use it when duplicates are expected and unwanted, not as a reflex.

DISTINCT works on the whole row

With several columns, two rows are duplicates only if all selected columns are equal. Customer 1 has one DELIVERED and one SHIPPED order, so both pairs stay:

SELECT DISTINCT customer_id, status
FROM orders
ORDER BY customer_id, status;
 customer_id |  status
-------------+-----------
           1 | DELIVERED
           1 | SHIPPED
           2 | DELIVERED
           2 | PLACED
           3 | CANCELLED
           3 | DELIVERED
           4 | DELIVERED
           5 | PLACED
           5 | SHIPPED
(9 rows)

DISTINCT is not a function. SELECT DISTINCT(city), name FROM customers does not mean "distinct city"; the parentheses are just grouping, and the whole row (city, name) is compared. To count distinct values use COUNT(DISTINCT city) (see Aggregate functions).

NULL in DISTINCT

Two NULLs are not equal in a comparison, but DISTINCT treats them as the same value, so all NULLs collapse into one row. The CEO has no department, so a list of departments that appear among employees contains a NULL:

SELECT DISTINCT dept_id FROM employees ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id
---------
       1
       2
       3
  (null)
(4 rows)

DISTINCT ON: one row per group (PostgreSQL)

A very common task: "for each department, the highest paid employee" or "for each customer, the latest order". PostgreSQL has a shortcut: DISTINCT ON (columns) keeps only the first row of each group, and "first" is decided by ORDER BY.

SELECT DISTINCT ON (customer_id)
       customer_id, order_id, order_date
FROM orders
ORDER BY customer_id, order_date DESC;
 customer_id | order_id | order_date
-------------+----------+------------
           1 |        7 | 2026-03-21
           2 |        9 | 2026-04-03
           3 |        6 | 2026-03-08
           4 |        5 | 2026-02-27
           5 |       10 | 2026-04-10
(5 rows)

Read it as: sort by customer, newest order first; then keep the first row for each customer. Another example, the best-paid person in each department (the NULL department forms its own group):

SELECT DISTINCT ON (dept_id)
       dept_id, first_name, salary
FROM employees
ORDER BY dept_id, salary DESC;
 dept_id | first_name |  salary
---------+------------+-----------
       1 | Vikram     | 220000.00
       2 | Karan      | 160000.00
       3 | Sunita     | 120000.00
  (null) | Meera      | 300000.00
(4 rows)

DISTINCT ON is not standard SQL and MySQL does not have it. The portable way is a window function, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY … ORDER BY …), covered in Ranking functions. It also handles "top 3 per group", which DISTINCT ON cannot.

DISTINCT versus GROUP BY

SELECT DISTINCT city FROM customers and SELECT city FROM customers GROUP BY city return the same rows. Use DISTINCT when you only want unique values, and GROUP BY when you also compute something per group, such as a count (see GROUP BY).

Rule: ORDER BY must start with the DISTINCT ON columns

The ORDER BY list has to begin with the same expressions as DISTINCT ON. Otherwise PostgreSQL stops with ERROR: SELECT DISTINCT ON expressions must match initial ORDER BY expressions. And without any ORDER BY the surviving row of each group is arbitrary.

Mistake: DISTINCT as a bandage for a join bug

A query returns the same employee three times, and someone adds DISTINCT to "fix" it. The real problem is usually a join that multiplies rows (for example joining employees to employee_projects and showing only employee columns). DISTINCT hides the symptom, costs time, and can still hide real duplicates you wanted to see. Find out why rows multiply (see join cardinality).

Edge cases

  • SELECT DISTINCT first_name FROM employees ORDER BY emp_id fails in PostgreSQL: ERROR: for SELECT DISTINCT, ORDER BY expressions must appear in select list. After removing duplicates there is no single emp_id per row to sort by.
  • DISTINCT applies to the whole select list, so adding one more column (such as emp_id) can make every row unique and "stop working".

Interview angle

  • Does DISTINCT apply to one column or all? All selected columns together.
  • How are NULLs treated? As equal to each other: they collapse into one row.
  • How do you get the latest row per group? DISTINCT ON in PostgreSQL, or ROW_NUMBER() in any database.

NULL and three-valued logic

NULL means "unknown or missing", not zero and not an empty string. Any comparison with NULL gives UNKNOWN, WHERE keeps only rows that are TRUE, so = NULL never matches: use IS NULL, COALESCE and IS DISTINCT FROM instead.

What NULL is

NULL marks a value that is missing or unknown. Meera, the CEO, has manager_id = NULL: she has no manager. Three customers have phone = NULL: we do not know their number. NULL is not 0 (a salary of zero is a known value), and it is not '' (an empty string is a known, empty text). Because "unknown" can be anything, SQL cannot say whether NULL equals something. This leads to three-valued logic: a condition is TRUE, FALSE or UNKNOWN.

Comparisons with NULL are UNKNOWN

ExpressionResultWhy
NULL = NULLNULLIs one unknown equal to another? Unknown.
5 > NULL, NULL <> 1NULLAny comparison with an unknown is unknown.
NULL IS NULLTRUEThis is the correct test.
TRUE AND NULLNULLIt depends on the unknown.
FALSE AND NULLFALSEFalse whatever the unknown is.
TRUE OR NULLTRUETrue whatever the unknown is.
FALSE OR NULLNULLIt depends on the unknown.
NOT NULLNULLThe opposite of unknown is unknown.
 
SELECT NULL = NULL AS eq, NULL IS NULL AS is_null, NULL <> 1 AS ne, 5 > NULL AS gt;
   eq   | is_null |   ne   |   gt
--------+---------+--------+--------
 (null) | t       | (null) | (null)
(1 row)

The key rule for queries: WHERE keeps a row only when the condition is TRUE. FALSE and UNKNOWN rows are both dropped. So a row whose condition is UNKNOWN silently disappears.

IS NULL and IS NOT NULL

SELECT first_name, dept_id FROM employees WHERE dept_id = NULL;

SELECT first_name, dept_id, manager_id FROM employees WHERE dept_id IS NULL;
 first_name | dept_id
------------+---------
(0 rows)

 first_name | dept_id | manager_id
------------+---------+------------
 Meera      | (null)  | (null)
(1 row)

The first query is valid SQL and returns nothing, with no error. That is what makes it dangerous.

The silent loss with <>

The same effect hides rows in "not equal" filters. Five employees work in Engineering (dept 1), so you expect seven others. dept_id <> 1 is UNKNOWN for the CEO, so she is dropped:

SELECT first_name FROM employees WHERE dept_id <> 1 ORDER BY emp_id;            -- 6 rows
SELECT first_name FROM employees WHERE dept_id IS DISTINCT FROM 1 ORDER BY emp_id; -- 7 rows
 first_name
------------
 Ananya
 Sunita
 Karan
 Neha
 Farhan
 Isha
(6 rows)

 first_name
------------
 Meera
 Ananya
 Sunita
 Karan
 Neha
 Farhan
 Isha
(7 rows)

a IS DISTINCT FROM b is a NULL-safe "not equal": it is TRUE when the values differ, treats two NULLs as equal, and never returns UNKNOWN. a IS NOT DISTINCT FROM b is the NULL-safe "equal". MySQL's NULL-safe equals operator is <=>.

COALESCE and NULLIF

COALESCE(a, b, c, …) returns the first argument that is not NULL. It replaces NULL with a default for display or calculation. It works in every database; MySQL also has IFNULL(a, b) and Oracle has NVL.

SELECT name, COALESCE(phone, 'no phone') AS phone
FROM customers
ORDER BY customer_id;
     name     |   phone
--------------+------------
 Aarav Shah   | 9876500001
 Diya Kapoor  | no phone
 Kabir Singh  | 9876500003
 Ishita Joshi | 9876500004
 Rohan Das    | no phone
 Sara Khan    | 9876500006
 Tanvi Desai  | no phone
(7 rows)

NULLIF(a, b) does the reverse: it returns NULL when a equals b, otherwise a. Its classic use is avoiding division by zero, because dividing by NULL gives NULL instead of an error:

SELECT 100 / 0;                -- ERROR:  division by zero
SELECT 100 / NULLIF(0, 0);     -- NULL (shown as an empty or (null) cell)

In a real query it looks like total / NULLIF(order_count, 0). Another use is turning empty strings from a form into NULL: NULLIF(TRIM(phone), '').

NULL in arithmetic, text and aggregates

  • Arithmetic and text: any operation with NULL gives NULL. salary + NULL is NULL, and so is 'Mr ' || NULL in PostgreSQL. Wrap nullable parts in COALESCE.
  • Aggregates skip NULLs (except COUNT(*)). The CEO has no department and no manager:
SELECT COUNT(*) AS total, COUNT(dept_id) AS with_dept, COUNT(manager_id) AS with_manager
FROM employees;
 total | with_dept | with_manager
-------+-----------+--------------
    12 |        11 |           11
(1 row)

AVG, SUM, MIN and MAX also ignore NULLs; more in Aggregate functions.

NULL in IN and NOT IN: the classic trap

x IN (1, 2, NULL) is TRUE when x is 1 or 2, and otherwise UNKNOWN (not FALSE), because x = NULL is unknown. The danger is NOT IN: it is a chain of <> tests joined by AND, and one UNKNOWN makes the whole chain never TRUE.

SELECT 2 IN (1, 2, NULL) AS a, 3 IN (1, 2, NULL) AS b, 3 NOT IN (1, 2, NULL) AS c;
 a |   b    |   c
---+--------+--------
 t | (null) | (null)
(1 row)

This bites in real queries. "Which departments have no employees?" The subquery below returns the dept_id of every employee, including the CEO's NULL:

SELECT name
FROM departments
WHERE dept_id NOT IN (SELECT dept_id FROM employees);
 name
------
(0 rows)

Marketing has no employees, but for it the test is 4 NOT IN (1, 2, 3, NULL), which is UNKNOWN. Two fixes: filter the NULLs out of the subquery, or (better) use NOT EXISTS, which does not have this problem (see EXISTS and anti-joins).

SELECT name
FROM departments
WHERE dept_id NOT IN (SELECT dept_id FROM employees WHERE dept_id IS NOT NULL);
   name
-----------
 Marketing
(1 row)

NULL in constraints, sorting and grouping

  • UNIQUE allows many NULLs in PostgreSQL and MySQL, because NULLs are not equal to each other. (PostgreSQL 15 adds UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT if you want only one.)
  • CHECK passes when the condition is TRUE or UNKNOWN. Only FALSE rejects the row. CHECK (price > 0) does not stop a NULL price; add NOT NULL.
  • Primary keys are never NULL. Foreign keys may be NULL, which means "no related row".
  • ORDER BY, DISTINCT, GROUP BY, UNION treat all NULLs as one group, even though NULL = NULL is unknown.

NULL in Java

JDBC can lose the difference between NULL and zero. rs.getInt("manager_id") returns 0 for a NULL, and employee 0 does not exist. Use rs.getObject("manager_id", Integer.class) (returns null) or call rs.wasNull() right after getInt. In JPA, map nullable columns to wrapper types (Integer, Long), not int or long.

Rule: test NULL only with IS NULL, IS NOT NULL or IS DISTINCT FROM

= NULL and <> NULL never return TRUE. Remember also that a comparison such as <> 1 drops NULL rows.

Mistake: = NULL and NOT IN with a nullable subquery

Wrong: always zero rows

SELECT * FROM customers WHERE phone = NULL;
SELECT * FROM departments
WHERE dept_id NOT IN (SELECT dept_id FROM employees);

Right

SELECT * FROM customers WHERE phone IS NULL;
SELECT * FROM departments d
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM employees e WHERE e.dept_id = d.dept_id);

Convention: fewer NULLs, on purpose

Make a column NOT NULL unless "unknown" is a real state of the data. Use a DEFAULT for the rest (stock INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0). Do not store an empty string to mean "none", and do not store 0 or 'N/A' to mean "unknown": they poison averages and comparisons. (Oracle treats '' as NULL; PostgreSQL and MySQL do not.)

Interview angle

  • Why does WHERE col = NULL return nothing? The comparison is UNKNOWN, and WHERE keeps only TRUE rows. Use IS NULL.
  • What is the difference between COUNT(*) and COUNT(col)? COUNT(*) counts rows; COUNT(col) counts rows where col is not NULL.
  • What does NOT IN return if the list contains a NULL? No rows. Explain the AND chain of <> and prefer NOT EXISTS.
  • NULL vs empty string vs 0? Three different things: unknown, known-empty, known-zero.

Filters: AND, OR, NOT, IN and BETWEEN

Combine conditions with AND, OR and NOT, remembering that AND binds tighter than OR, so use parentheses. IN replaces a chain of ORs, and BETWEEN is inclusive at both ends, which is a trap for timestamps.

AND, OR and NOT

WHERE accepts any condition that is TRUE, FALSE or UNKNOWN (see NULL and three-valued logic). You build bigger conditions from smaller ones:

  • a AND b: both must be true.
  • a OR b: at least one must be true.
  • NOT a: reverses a condition (NOT (dept_id = 1)).

Precedence: AND before OR

Like multiplication before addition in maths, SQL evaluates NOT first, then AND, then OR. Comparison operators (=, >, IN, BETWEEN, LIKE) bind tighter than all three. The famous bug: you want employees of Engineering or Sales who earn more than 100000.

Wrong: read as dept 1, OR (dept 2 AND salary > 100000)

SELECT first_name, dept_id, salary
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 1 OR dept_id = 2 AND salary > 100000
ORDER BY emp_id;

Right: parentheses say what you mean

SELECT first_name, dept_id, salary
FROM employees
WHERE (dept_id = 1 OR dept_id = 2)
  AND salary > 100000
ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name | dept_id |  salary
------------+---------+-----------
 Vikram     |       1 | 220000.00
 Ananya     |       2 | 150000.00
 Priya      |       1 | 150000.00
 Rahul      |       1 | 150000.00
 Arjun      |       1 | 110000.00
 Divya      |       1 |  90000.00
 Karan      |       2 | 160000.00
(7 rows)
 first_name | dept_id |  salary
------------+---------+-----------
 Vikram     |       1 | 220000.00
 Ananya     |       2 | 150000.00
 Priya      |       1 | 150000.00
 Rahul      |       1 | 150000.00
 Arjun      |       1 | 110000.00
 Karan      |       2 | 160000.00
(6 rows)

Whenever a WHERE mixes AND and OR, add parentheses even if you are sure about the precedence. The next reader will not be. To rewrite a negated condition use De Morgan's laws: NOT (a AND b) is NOT a OR NOT b, and NOT (a OR b) is NOT a AND NOT b.

IN: a list of allowed values

x IN (1, 3) is the same as x = 1 OR x = 3, shorter and harder to get wrong. NOT IN is the opposite.

SELECT first_name, dept_id FROM employees WHERE dept_id IN (1, 3) ORDER BY emp_id;

SELECT first_name, dept_id FROM employees WHERE dept_id NOT IN (1, 3) ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name | dept_id
------------+---------
 Vikram     |       1
 Sunita     |       3
 Priya      |       1
 Rahul      |       1
 Arjun      |       1
 Divya      |       1
 Farhan     |       3
 Isha       |       3
(8 rows)

 first_name | dept_id
------------+---------
 Ananya     |       2
 Karan      |       2
 Neha       |       2
(3 rows)

Count the second result: 12 employees, 8 in departments 1 and 3, so you may expect 4 others. Meera is missing, because NULL NOT IN (1, 3) is UNKNOWN. To include her write WHERE dept_id NOT IN (1, 3) OR dept_id IS NULL. IN also takes a subquery: dept_id IN (SELECT dept_id FROM departments WHERE location = 'Pune') (see Subqueries).

BETWEEN: an inclusive range

x BETWEEN a AND b means x >= a AND x <= b. Both ends are included. It works for numbers, dates and text.

SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
WHERE salary BETWEEN 90000 AND 150000
ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id;

SELECT order_id, order_date
FROM orders
WHERE order_date BETWEEN '2026-03-01' AND '2026-03-31'
ORDER BY order_id;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Ananya     | 150000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00
 Sunita     | 120000.00
 Arjun      | 110000.00
 Divya      |  90000.00
(6 rows)

 order_id | order_date
----------+------------
        6 | 2026-03-08
        7 | 2026-03-21
        8 | 2026-03-30
(3 rows)

Divya (exactly 90000) and the three employees at exactly 150000 are in the result, because the ends are included. Three more details:

  • Put the smaller value first. BETWEEN 150000 AND 90000 returns no rows, with no error. PostgreSQL has BETWEEN SYMMETRIC, which swaps the bounds for you.
  • NOT BETWEEN a AND b means x < a OR x > b.
  • NULL is never between anything, so NULL rows are dropped (as with every comparison).

The BETWEEN trap with timestamps

The order query above is safe because order_date is a DATE. If the column is a TIMESTAMP, the literal '2026-03-31' means 2026-03-31 00:00:00, so everything that happened later on March 31 is outside the range:

SELECT TIMESTAMP '2026-03-31 15:30:00'
       BETWEEN DATE '2026-03-01' AND DATE '2026-03-31' AS in_march;
 in_march
----------
 f
(1 row)

For dates with a time part, use a half-open range: >= '2026-03-01' AND < '2026-04-01'. It also keeps working when someone changes the column from DATE to TIMESTAMP. See Date ranges and time zones.

Putting it together

A realistic filter: staff of Engineering or Sales, paid between 100000 and 160000, who joined in 2020 or later.

SELECT first_name, dept_id, salary, hire_date
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id IN (1, 2)
  AND salary BETWEEN 100000 AND 160000
  AND hire_date >= '2020-01-01'
ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name | dept_id |  salary   | hire_date
------------+---------+-----------+------------
 Rahul      |       1 | 150000.00 | 2020-03-02
 Arjun      |       1 | 110000.00 | 2022-08-16
 Karan      |       2 | 160000.00 | 2021-05-24
(3 rows)

Priya is out (hired 2019), Vikram earns too much, Ananya was hired in 2017, and Divya and Neha earn too little. Each condition is on its own line, so you can comment one out to see its effect.

Rule: NOT, then AND, then OR

If a condition mixes AND and OR, use parentheses. The precedence is the same in PostgreSQL and MySQL, but nobody should have to remember it.

Mistake: OR with a bare value

Wrong: the second operand is a number, not a condition

SELECT * FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 1 OR 2;
-- PostgreSQL: ERROR:  argument of OR must be type boolean, not type integer
-- MySQL: runs, treats 2 as TRUE, and returns every row

Right: repeat the column, or use IN

SELECT * FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 1 OR dept_id = 2;
SELECT * FROM employees WHERE dept_id IN (1, 2);

Another beginner error is salary > 100000 AND < 200000: each condition needs its own column, salary > 100000 AND salary < 200000, or use BETWEEN when both ends are inclusive.

Edge cases

  • Combining NULL checks with other filters needs parentheses too: WHERE dept_id = 1 OR dept_id IS NULL returns the five Engineering employees and the CEO.
  • An empty list is a syntax error: IN () fails. When a Java program builds the list, handle the empty case before sending the query.
  • Very long IN lists are slow to plan and a statement can carry only a limited number of bind parameters. Put big lists in a temporary table or a subquery. In PostgreSQL you can also pass a single array parameter: WHERE id = ANY (?).

Tip: one condition per line

Start each continuation line with AND or OR. When a result looks wrong, comment out conditions one at a time and watch the row count change.

Interview angle

  • Which is evaluated first, AND or OR? AND. Show the a OR b AND c example and fix it with parentheses.
  • Is BETWEEN inclusive? Yes, both ends. Mention the timestamp trap and the half-open range fix.
  • IN versus several ORs? Same result, IN is shorter. The difference appears with NOT IN and NULL.

Pattern matching: LIKE, ILIKE and case sensitivity

LIKE matches text against a pattern where % means "any characters" and _ means "exactly one character". It is case-sensitive in PostgreSQL (use ILIKE for case-insensitive) and case-insensitive by default in MySQL.

The two wildcards

WildcardMatchesExampleMatches
%zero or more characters'A%'starts with A
%zero or more characters'%a'ends with a
%zero or more characters'%cable%'contains "cable"
_exactly one character'_r%'second letter is r
noneexact match, like ='Pune'exactly Pune
 
SELECT first_name FROM employees WHERE first_name LIKE 'A%' ORDER BY emp_id;

SELECT first_name FROM employees WHERE first_name LIKE '%a' ORDER BY emp_id;

SELECT first_name FROM employees WHERE first_name LIKE '_r%' ORDER BY emp_id;

SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE name LIKE '%Cable%';
 first_name
------------
 Ananya
 Arjun
(2 rows)

 first_name
------------
 Meera
 Ananya
 Sunita
 Priya
 Divya
 Neha
 Isha
(7 rows)

 first_name
------------
 Priya
 Arjun
(2 rows)

    name     | price
-------------+--------
 USB-C Cable | 299.00
(1 row)

NOT LIKE reverses the test. Remember that LIKE on a NULL value is UNKNOWN, so NOT LIKE also drops NULL rows.

Case sensitivity

This is where PostgreSQL and MySQL differ, and it surprises people who move between them.

QuestionPostgreSQLMySQL
'abc' = 'ABC'falsetrue (default collation utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci: case- and accent-insensitive)
first_name LIKE 'a%' finds Ananya?NoYes
Case-insensitive searchILIKE, or LOWER(col) LIKE LOWER(x) plain LIKE
Force case-sensitivedefault behaviour LIKE BINARY 'A%' or COLLATE utf8mb4_bin
 
SELECT first_name FROM employees WHERE first_name LIKE 'a%';    -- 0 rows
SELECT first_name FROM employees WHERE first_name ILIKE 'a%';   -- Ananya, Arjun

SELECT name FROM products WHERE name ILIKE '%mouse%';           -- Wireless Mouse

ILIKE is a PostgreSQL extension, not standard SQL. The portable way is LOWER(first_name) LIKE 'a%', which works everywhere. Another PostgreSQL option is the citext extension, a text type that compares case-insensitively; people use it for email columns.

Escaping % and _ with ESCAPE

What if you need to find a real underscore or percent sign? The underscore in 'SAVE_10' is a wildcard, so the pattern also matches SAVEX10 and SAVE-10. Put an escape character before the wildcard and tell SQL which character it is with ESCAPE:

SELECT code
FROM (VALUES ('SAVE_10'), ('SAVEX10'), ('SAVE-10')) AS t(code)
WHERE code LIKE 'SAVE_10';                    -- _ is a wildcard: all 3 match

SELECT code
FROM (VALUES ('SAVE_10'), ('SAVEX10'), ('SAVE-10')) AS t(code)
WHERE code LIKE 'SAVE!_10' ESCAPE '!';        -- !_ means a literal underscore
  code
---------
 SAVE_10
 SAVEX10
 SAVE-10
(3 rows)

  code
---------
 SAVE_10
(1 row)

The default escape character is a backslash in both databases, but backslashes are also special in some string literals (MySQL treats \ as an escape inside every string). Choosing your own character with ESCAPE avoids that confusion.

Regular expressions

When LIKE is too weak, PostgreSQL has POSIX regular expressions and MySQL has REGEXP.

TaskPostgreSQLMySQL
Matches regexfirst_name ~ '^[AE]'first_name REGEXP '^[AE]'
Case-insensitive regexfirst_name ~* '^a'default (collation decides)
Does not match!~ and !~*NOT REGEXP

Regular expressions are slower than LIKE and cannot use a normal index. Use them for validation-style checks and occasional searches, not for hot queries.

LIKE and indexes

A search for names that start with a prefix (LIKE 'Ana%') can use an index in principle. A search with a leading wildcard (LIKE '%nya' or '%cable%') cannot use a normal B-tree index, so the database reads the whole table. In PostgreSQL a plain index also helps prefix searches only if the database uses the C collation or you create the index with text_pattern_ops. For "contains" searches on large tables, use a trigram index (pg_trgm extension) or full-text search; indexes are covered in Module 5.

Searching from Java

Never glue the user's text into the SQL string. Pass the whole pattern as a parameter:

String sql = "SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE name ILIKE ?";
try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(sql)) {
    ps.setString(1, "%" + term + "%");      // the % signs are part of the parameter
    try (ResultSet rs = ps.executeQuery()) {
        while (rs.next()) {
            System.out.println(rs.getString("name"));
        }
    }
}

The parameter stops SQL injection (see Core Java · Module 8), but a % or _ typed by the user still acts as a wildcard; escape them if that matters. Spring Data JPA derives the query for you from method names such as findByNameContainingIgnoreCase.

Rule: the pattern must match the whole value

LIKE is not a "contains" search by itself. name LIKE 'Cable' matches only the exact text Cable. Add % where text may appear before or after.

Mistake: expecting MySQL behaviour in PostgreSQL

A search for WHERE email = 'Priya.Menon@Example.com' finds the row in MySQL and returns nothing in PostgreSQL, because the stored value is lowercase. Normalise on input (store emails in lowercase) or compare with LOWER(email) = LOWER(?). A unique index on LOWER(email) then stops duplicates that differ only by case.

Edge cases

  • LIKE '%' matches every non-NULL value; a NULL value never matches any pattern.
  • Wrapping the column in a function (LOWER(name) LIKE …) stops a normal index on name from being used. Create an index on the expression, or use ILIKE with a trigram index.
  • MySQL's default collation also ignores accents (a equals á); PostgreSQL does not unless you use the unaccent extension.

Interview angle

  • What do % and _ mean? Any number of characters, and exactly one character.
  • How do you search without caring about case in PostgreSQL? ILIKE, or compare LOWER() on both sides.
  • Why can LIKE '%abc' be slow? A leading wildcard cannot use a B-tree index, so the table is scanned.
