  • How do you match a literal %? Use ESCAPE and put the escape character before it.

INSERT: adding rows, RETURNING and upserts

INSERT INTO table (columns) VALUES (…) adds rows; list the columns every time, add many rows in one statement, copy rows with INSERT … SELECT, and use ON CONFLICT (MySQL: ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE) to insert-or-update in one atomic step.

Note: examples that change data

The statements in this article and the next two change rows. Each example is wrapped in BEGIN; … ROLLBACK;, so the sample database is exactly the same afterwards (the next article explains why this is also the safest habit in real work). The outputs below leave out the BEGIN and ROLLBACK lines that psql prints. In MySQL write START TRANSACTION; instead of BEGIN;.

One row, and RETURNING

BEGIN;
INSERT INTO departments (name, location)
VALUES ('Finance', 'Delhi')
RETURNING dept_id, name;
ROLLBACK;
 dept_id |  name
---------+---------
       5 | Finance
(1 row)

INSERT 0 1

The line INSERT 0 1 is the command tag: the statement inserted 1 row (the 0 is a historical object id and is always 0 today). We did not give a dept_id; the identity column generated it. RETURNING (PostgreSQL) sends back any columns of the new row, including generated ones, so you do not need a second SELECT. It also works with UPDATE and DELETE. MySQL has no RETURNING; you call SELECT LAST_INSERT_ID() instead.

From Java, ask JDBC for the generated key. The PostgreSQL driver does this with RETURNING behind the scenes:

String sql = "INSERT INTO departments (name, location) VALUES (?, ?)";
try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(sql, new String[] {"dept_id"})) {
    ps.setString(1, "Finance");
    ps.setString(2, "Delhi");
    ps.executeUpdate();
    try (ResultSet keys = ps.getGeneratedKeys()) {
        if (keys.next()) {
            System.out.println("New id: " + keys.getInt(1));
        }
    }
}

Always list the columns

The column list says which value goes where. Columns you leave out get their DEFAULT, or NULL if there is none. If a left-out column is NOT NULL and has no default, the insert fails.

BEGIN;
INSERT INTO customers (name, email, city)
VALUES ('Meghna Rao', 'meghna.rao@example.com', 'Chennai')
RETURNING *;
ROLLBACK;
 customer_id |    name    |         email          |  city   | phone  |  joined_on
-------------+------------+------------------------+---------+--------+------------
           8 | Meghna Rao | meghna.rao@example.com | Chennai | (null) | 2026-10-21
(1 row)

INSERT 0 1

The new customer got the next id, a NULL phone (no default) and today's date (the default expression CURRENT_DATE). You can also write the keyword DEFAULT in the value list (VALUES (DEFAULT, 'Meghna', …)), or insert a row made only of defaults with INSERT INTO t DEFAULT VALUES.

Wrong: no column list, values map to table order

INSERT INTO departments VALUES ('Finance', 'Delhi');
-- ERROR:  invalid input syntax for type integer: "Finance"

Right: name the columns

INSERT INTO departments (name, location) VALUES ('Finance', 'Delhi');

Without a column list the values must match all columns in table order. It breaks (or worse, silently stores data in the wrong columns) the day someone adds a column.

Many rows in one statement

List several rows after VALUES, separated by commas. The statement is atomic: either every row is inserted or none is. It is also much faster than separate inserts, because there is one round trip and one parse.

BEGIN;
INSERT INTO products (name, category, price, stock)
VALUES ('Phone Stand', 'Electronics', 349.00, 80),
       ('Pencil Box',  'Stationery',   60.00, 200)
RETURNING product_id, name;
ROLLBACK;
 product_id |    name
------------+-------------
          8 | Phone Stand
          9 | Pencil Box
(2 rows)

INSERT 0 2

From Java, use JDBC batching (addBatch() and executeBatch()) for thousands of rows. Note that Hibernate cannot batch inserts for entities whose id is generated by an IDENTITY column, because it needs the id right after every insert (see Spring Boot · Module 3).

INSERT … SELECT: copy rows from a query

Instead of VALUES, feed the insert with a SELECT. This copies, archives or transforms data entirely inside the database. The column types and the column count of the SELECT must match the insert's column list.

BEGIN;
CREATE TABLE clearance (
    product_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
    name       VARCHAR(100),
    sale_price NUMERIC(10, 2)
);
INSERT INTO clearance (product_id, name, sale_price)
SELECT product_id, name, ROUND(price * 0.8, 2)
FROM products
WHERE category = 'Stationery';
SELECT * FROM clearance ORDER BY product_id;
ROLLBACK;
INSERT 0 2

 product_id |     name     | sale_price
------------+--------------+------------
          4 | Notebook A5  |      96.00
          5 | Gel Pen Pack |      72.00
(2 rows)

To create the table and fill it in one step, use CREATE TABLE clearance AS SELECT …. That form copies the data and column types but no constraints, so add the primary key afterwards.

Upsert: insert, or update if it already exists

Sometimes you do not know whether a row exists: importing a customer list, handling a message that may arrive twice, counting page views. Checking with a SELECT first and then inserting is a race condition: two sessions can both see "not found" and both insert. An upsert does it in one atomic statement. The email column of customers is unique, so it can decide what "already exists" means:

BEGIN;
INSERT INTO customers (name, email, city)
VALUES ('Aarav Shah', 'aarav.shah@example.com', 'Navi Mumbai')
ON CONFLICT (email) DO UPDATE SET city = EXCLUDED.city
RETURNING customer_id, name, city;
ROLLBACK;
 customer_id |    name    |    city
-------------+------------+-------------
           1 | Aarav Shah | Navi Mumbai
(1 row)

INSERT 0 1

EXCLUDED is a pseudo-table that holds the row you tried to insert. ON CONFLICT (email) names the unique column (or constraint) that detects the conflict. Use DO NOTHING to skip a duplicate silently (the command tag becomes INSERT 0 0). MySQL spells it differently, and reacts to a conflict on any unique key:

INSERT INTO customers (name, email, city)
VALUES ('Aarav Shah', 'aarav.shah@example.com', 'Navi Mumbai') AS new
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE city = new.city;
PostgreSQLMySQLStandard SQL
Insert or updateON CONFLICT (col) DO UPDATE SET … ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE …MERGE (PostgreSQL 15+; not in MySQL)
Skip duplicatesON CONFLICT DO NOTHING INSERT IGNORE (also hides other errors; avoid)MERGE without a matched branch
What counts as a conflictThe unique index or constraint you name Any PRIMARY KEY or UNIQUE indexThe ON condition
Use the new valuesEXCLUDED.col Row alias new.col (older style: VALUES(col), deprecated)Columns of the source

MySQL also has REPLACE INTO, which deletes the old row and inserts a new one. That fires ON DELETE actions and can change ids, so prefer ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE.

What can go wrong

Error message (PostgreSQL)Cause
null value in column "email" of relation "customers" violates not-null constraint A NOT NULL column got no value and has no default
duplicate key value violates unique constraint "customers_email_key" The email already exists (the DETAIL line shows the value)
insert or update on table "orders" violates foreign key constraint "fk_orders_customer" The customer id does not exist in customers
new row for relation "products" violates check constraint "products_price_check" For example a negative price
invalid input syntax for type integer: "abc"Wrong type for a column
value too long for type character varying(15)Text longer than the column allows
INSERT has more expressions than target columnsColumn list and value list differ in length

Rule: one statement, one outcome

A single INSERT is all-or-nothing. If row 99 of 100 violates a constraint, no row is inserted. A multi-row insert inside a transaction is safe to retry.

Edge cases

  • Ids have gaps. A sequence never goes back, even when the transaction rolls back or an upsert turns into an update. After running the examples above, the next real department gets id 6 or higher, not 5. Never rely on ids being continuous.
  • With GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY you cannot supply an id: ERROR: cannot insert a non-DEFAULT value into column "id". (Our sample tables use BY DEFAULT, which allows it.)
  • In one upsert statement, two input rows must not hit the same existing row: ERROR: ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE command cannot affect row a second time.

In real projects

Upserts make jobs and message consumers idempotent: processing the same event twice leaves the same result. Counters use ON CONFLICT (page) DO UPDATE SET hits = page_hits.hits + 1. Data imports use INSERT … SELECT from a staging table, usually in a migration script.

Interview angle

  • How do you insert if absent, update if present? ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE (PostgreSQL), ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE (MySQL), or MERGE. Explain why "SELECT then INSERT" is a race condition.
  • How do you get the generated id? RETURNING, LAST_INSERT_ID(), or JDBC getGeneratedKeys().
  • Why list the column names? The statement keeps working when the table gets new columns.

UPDATE and DELETE

UPDATE … SET … WHERE changes existing rows and DELETE FROM … WHERE removes them. The WHERE clause decides which rows are touched; without it, every row is. Check the row count in the command tag after every change.

UPDATE

The syntax is UPDATE table SET column = expression [, column = expression …] WHERE condition. The expression can use the row's current values, so you can calculate the new value from the old one. Here is a 10 percent raise for the Sales department (inside a transaction that is rolled back, as in the previous article):

BEGIN;
UPDATE employees
SET salary = salary * 1.10
WHERE dept_id = 2
RETURNING emp_id, first_name, salary;
ROLLBACK;
 emp_id | first_name |  salary
--------+------------+-----------
      3 | Ananya     | 165000.00
      9 | Karan      | 176000.00
     10 | Neha       |  82500.00
(3 rows)

UPDATE 3

UPDATE 3 tells you three rows changed. If you expected three, good. If it says 12, you forgot the WHERE. An UPDATE that matches no row is not an error; it prints UPDATE 0.

  • Several columns: SET salary = 90000, dept_id = 2.
  • NULL and defaults: SET phone = NULL and SET stock = DEFAULT.
  • All right-hand sides use the old row. SET a = b, b = a swaps the two values; the second assignment does not see the first.
  • A subquery can supply a value: SET salary = (SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 2) as long as it returns one row and one column.

UPDATE using another table

Often the rows to change are chosen by looking at another table: "give a 5 percent raise to everyone who works on the Payments Platform project (id 1)". The portable way is a subquery:

UPDATE employees
SET salary = salary * 1.05
WHERE emp_id IN (SELECT emp_id FROM employee_projects WHERE project_id = 1);

This changes three rows: Priya becomes 157500.00, Rahul 157500.00 and Arjun 115500.00. PostgreSQL also has UPDATE … FROM, MySQL has UPDATE … JOIN:

UPDATE employees AS e
SET salary = e.salary * 1.05
FROM employee_projects AS ep
WHERE ep.emp_id = e.emp_id
  AND ep.project_id = 1;
UPDATE employees AS e
JOIN employee_projects AS ep ON ep.emp_id = e.emp_id
SET e.salary = e.salary * 1.05
WHERE ep.project_id = 1;

Edge case: one target row, many matching rows

If the join finds several source rows for one employee (for example an employee on projects 1 and 2 when you remove the project_id filter), PostgreSQL applies only one of them, chosen arbitrarily, and the employee is updated once, not twice. A subquery with IN never has this surprise.

DELETE

DELETE FROM table WHERE condition removes the matching rows. Rows that reference the deleted row through a foreign key are handled by the foreign key's ON DELETE rule (see Foreign key actions). In the sample database, items of an order are deleted together with the order:

BEGIN;
DELETE FROM orders WHERE status = 'CANCELLED' RETURNING order_id, customer_id;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS items_left FROM order_items;
ROLLBACK;
 order_id | customer_id
----------+-------------
        4 |           3
(1 row)

DELETE 1

 items_left
------------
         18
(1 row)

Order 4 had one item, so order_items went from 19 rows to 18 without you deleting anything there: fk_items_order is declared with ON DELETE CASCADE. The opposite case is a customer who has orders, where the default rule refuses the delete:

ERROR:  update or delete on table "customers" violates foreign key constraint
        "fk_orders_customer" on table "orders"
DETAIL:  Key (customer_id)=(1) is still referenced from table "orders".

Deleting rows by looking at another table works like UPDATE: use a subquery (WHERE order_id IN (SELECT …)), PostgreSQL's DELETE … USING, or MySQL's DELETE t FROM t JOIN ….

Check how many rows changed

Every UPDATE and DELETE reports a count (UPDATE 3, DELETE 1). Compare it with what you expected before you commit. In Java, executeUpdate() returns the same number, and code can use it: an UPDATE … WHERE id = ? AND version = ? that returns 0 means somebody else changed the row first (this is how optimistic locking works).

Soft delete: mark instead of remove

Many applications never run DELETE on business data. They add a column such as deleted_at TIMESTAMPTZ and set it, so the row can be restored and the history is kept:

ALTER TABLE customers ADD COLUMN deleted_at TIMESTAMPTZ;

UPDATE customers SET deleted_at = NOW() WHERE customer_id = 6;

SELECT * FROM customers WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;   -- every query must remember this

The costs: every query must filter deleted_at IS NULL (views or Hibernate's soft-delete support can hide that), unique constraints get awkward (a deleted email blocks a new signup unless you use a partial unique index such as CREATE UNIQUE INDEX … ON customers (email) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL), and personal data must still be erased when privacy rules require it.

Rule: every UPDATE and DELETE needs a WHERE you have checked

Write the WHERE first, run it as a SELECT, and only then turn it into an UPDATE or DELETE (see Changing data safely).

Mistake: a forgotten or wrong WHERE

Wrong: everyone gets the raise

UPDATE employees SET salary = salary * 1.10;      -- UPDATE 12

Right: only Sales

UPDATE employees SET salary = salary * 1.10 WHERE dept_id = 2;   -- UPDATE 3

The command tag is your alarm bell: 12 instead of 3. A similar bug is WHERE dept_id = 2 OR salary > 100000 written when you meant AND; it matches far more rows than you think.

Errors and limits

  • An update can violate a constraint: SET salary = -5 fails with new row for relation "employees" violates check constraint "employees_salary_check".
  • A subquery in SET that returns two rows fails: ERROR: more than one row returned by a subquery used as an expression.
  • MySQL allows UPDATE … LIMIT n and DELETE … LIMIT n; PostgreSQL does not. To limit a delete in PostgreSQL use WHERE ctid IN (SELECT ctid … LIMIT n) or a key subquery.

Interview angle

  • What happens if you forget WHERE in an UPDATE? Every row changes. Describe the safe workflow: SELECT first, transaction, check the count, then commit.
  • Hard delete vs soft delete? Hard delete removes the row for good; soft delete sets a flag or timestamp, keeps history, but every query must filter it.
  • How do you update one table using another? A subquery, UPDATE … FROM (PostgreSQL) or UPDATE … JOIN (MySQL).

Changing data safely: transactions, TRUNCATE and DROP

Databases do not ask "are you sure?". Look first with a SELECT, change inside a transaction, check the row count, then COMMIT or ROLLBACK. Know the difference between DELETE (some rows), TRUNCATE (all rows, fast) and DROP TABLE (the table itself).

The risk

Most tools run in autocommit mode: every statement is committed the moment it succeeds. An UPDATE employees SET dept_id = 1 without WHERE moves all twelve employees to Engineering, and there is no undo button. Your only way back is a backup. People lose real data this way, so experienced developers follow a routine.

The safe workflow

  1. Look first. Write the WHERE clause as a SELECT and check the rows and the count.
  2. Open a transaction with BEGIN;.
  3. Run the change with exactly the same WHERE. Read the command tag.
  4. Verify with another SELECT.
  5. COMMIT; if everything is as expected, ROLLBACK; if not.
learn_sql=> SELECT emp_id, first_name, salary FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 3 ORDER BY emp_id;
 emp_id | first_name |  salary
--------+------------+-----------
      4 | Sunita     | 120000.00
     11 | Farhan     |  65000.00
     12 | Isha       |  55000.00
(3 rows)

learn_sql=> BEGIN;
BEGIN
learn_sql=*> UPDATE employees SET salary = salary * 1.10 WHERE dept_id = 3;
UPDATE 3
learn_sql=*> SELECT emp_id, first_name, salary FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 3 ORDER BY emp_id;
 emp_id | first_name |  salary
--------+------------+-----------
      4 | Sunita     | 132000.00
     11 | Farhan     |  71500.00
     12 | Isha       |  60500.00
(3 rows)

learn_sql=*> ROLLBACK;
ROLLBACK

The * in the prompt (learn_sql=*>) means "a transaction is open". The count UPDATE 3 matched the three rows seen in step 1, so this is a plan you could have committed. After ROLLBACK the salaries are back to the old values. If a statement fails inside a transaction, PostgreSQL marks the whole transaction as failed (prompt learn_sql=!>) and answers every later command with ERROR: current transaction is aborted, commands ignored until end of transaction block; the only way out is ROLLBACK.

Transactions in short

CommandMeaning
BEGIN (or START TRANSACTION)Start a group of statements that succeed or fail together
COMMITMake all changes permanent and visible to others
ROLLBACKUndo every change since BEGIN
SAVEPOINT name / ROLLBACK TO nameUndo only part of a transaction

PostgreSQL can roll back CREATE TABLE, ALTER TABLE and DROP TABLE too. MySQL cannot: DDL statements commit the open transaction automatically, so there is no undo for them. Transactions, ACID and isolation levels are explained in Module 5; in Spring Boot you get them with @Transactional.

Where autocommit hides

  • psql: autocommit is on. \set AUTOCOMMIT off makes it open a transaction for you (then you must COMMIT).
  • DBeaver and MySQL Workbench: an auto-commit toggle sits in the toolbar. Switch to manual commit when you work on important data.
  • JDBC: connection.setAutoCommit(false) starts a transaction; the default is true.
  • MySQL safe mode: start the client with mysql --safe-updates (Workbench has a "Safe Updates" option). UPDATE and DELETE without a key condition in WHERE or a LIMIT are then rejected. PostgreSQL has no built-in equivalent.

DELETE vs TRUNCATE vs DROP

Three ways to get rid of data, and one of the most common interview questions:

DELETETRUNCATEDROP TABLE
RemovesThe rows you choose, or allAll rowsThe whole table: rows, columns, indexes, constraints
WHEREYesNoNo
GroupDMLDDLDDL
Speed on a big tableSlow (row by row)Very fastVery fast
Roll backYesPostgreSQL yes, MySQL noPostgreSQL yes, MySQL no
Row triggersFireDo not firen/a
Id counterUnchangedMySQL resets it; PostgreSQL only with RESTART IDENTITYGone with the table
Referenced by a foreign keyAllowed if no child rows remainRefused (PostgreSQL: unless CASCADE)Refused unless CASCADE (PostgreSQL)
 
DELETE FROM clearance;                                  -- all rows, row by row, can use WHERE
TRUNCATE TABLE clearance;                               -- all rows, fast, no WHERE
TRUNCATE TABLE orders, order_items RESTART IDENTITY;    -- PostgreSQL: several tables, reset ids
TRUNCATE TABLE orders CASCADE;                          -- PostgreSQL: also empties tables that reference it!
DROP TABLE clearance;                                   -- error if it does not exist
DROP TABLE IF EXISTS clearance;                         -- no error if it does not exist

Why is DELETE slow on a big table? It removes row by row, writes each change to the log, fires triggers, and in PostgreSQL leaves dead row versions behind for VACUUM to clean. TRUNCATE throws away the table's files in one step. The price is a strong lock (it waits for, and blocks, every other query on that table) and the missing WHERE. TRUNCATE on a table that other tables reference fails in both databases (ERROR: cannot truncate a table referenced in a foreign key constraint); PostgreSQL's CASCADE option "fixes" it by truncating the child tables too, which is rarely what you want.

Keep a way back

  • Before a risky change on real data, copy the table: CREATE TABLE employees_backup AS SELECT * FROM employees; (drop it later).
  • Real systems rely on full backups (pg_dump, mysqldump) and point-in-time recovery, and on least privilege: analysts get read-only accounts.
  • Schema and data changes in production go through reviewed migration scripts (Flyway or Liquibase), not through someone's SQL window.

Rule: read the row count before you commit

The count in UPDATE n or DELETE n must match what the preview SELECT showed. If it does not, ROLLBACK.

Mistake: DROP or TRUNCATE on the wrong database

The window title says production, you thought it said learn_sql. Check \conninfo before destructive commands, colour production connections red in DBeaver or pgAdmin, and never keep production credentials in a tool you use for practice.

Tip: type the WHERE before the verb

Write … WHERE emp_id = 7 first, then move the cursor to the start and type DELETE FROM employees. If you press Enter by accident halfway, there is no incomplete destructive statement.

Interview angle

  • DELETE vs TRUNCATE vs DROP: some or all rows with WHERE and logging, versus all rows quickly without WHERE, versus the table itself. Add the rollback difference between PostgreSQL and MySQL, and that TRUNCATE is DDL.
  • How do you protect against a bad update? Preview with SELECT, use a transaction, check the count, keep backups, use least-privilege accounts.
  • Can DDL be rolled back? In PostgreSQL yes; in MySQL no, it commits implicitly.

Data types: numbers, text and booleans

Every column has a type that decides what it can store, how much space it takes and how it behaves. Use integers for counts and ids, NUMERIC for money, VARCHAR or TEXT for text, and never use REAL or DOUBLE for rupees.

Why the type matters

A data type is a rule attached to a column. The database rejects values that do not fit ('abc' in an integer column), stores each value in the right number of bytes, and knows how to compare and calculate with it. A wrong type causes bugs that are hard to see later: a phone number stored as an integer loses its leading zero, a price stored as a float is off by a paisa, and an INT id column overflows when the table grows. Choose the type once, carefully, because changing it later on a big table is slow and risky.

Integers

TypeSizeRangeUse for
SMALLINT2 bytes−32,768 to 32,767small counters, hours per week
INT / INTEGER4 bytesabout ±2.1 billionmost ids and counts
BIGINT8 bytesabout ±9.2 × 1018ids of big tables, UPI transaction numbers, paise totals

MySQL has the same three plus TINYINT (1 byte) and MEDIUMINT, and allows UNSIGNED (so INT UNSIGNED reaches about 4.29 billion). PostgreSQL has no unsigned types; use a CHECK (x >= 0) instead. In Java: SMALLINT maps to short, INT to int, BIGINT to long. If a value does not fit you get ERROR: integer out of range (PostgreSQL) or ERROR 1264 ... Out of range value (MySQL in strict mode).

Exact decimals: NUMERIC and DECIMAL

NUMERIC(p, s) stores a decimal number exactly. p is the precision (total digits) and s is the scale (digits after the point). NUMERIC(10, 2) holds up to 8 digits before the point and 2 after: 99999999.99. DECIMAL is a synonym in both databases. Use it for money, tax rates and anything that must add up to the last paisa.

SELECT 123.456::NUMERIC(10, 2) AS rounded;        -- 123.46 (rounded, no error)
SELECT 123456789.5::NUMERIC(10, 2) AS too_big;
-- ERROR:  numeric field overflow
-- DETAIL:  A field with precision 10, scale 2 must round to an absolute value
--          less than 10^8.

Approximate decimals: REAL and DOUBLE PRECISION

REAL (4 bytes, about 6 digits) and DOUBLE PRECISION (8 bytes, about 15 digits; DOUBLE in MySQL) are binary floating-point types, the same as Java's float and double. They are fast, but they cannot store 0.1 exactly, just as 1/3 cannot be written exactly in decimal. Good for sensor readings and statistics. Wrong for money.

SELECT 0.1::DOUBLE PRECISION + 0.2::DOUBLE PRECISION = 0.3::DOUBLE PRECISION AS float_sum,
       0.1::NUMERIC + 0.2::NUMERIC = 0.3::NUMERIC                          AS numeric_sum;
 float_sum | numeric_sum
-----------+-------------
 f         | t
(1 row)

A float sum of many small amounts drifts; an invoice total that is one paisa off fails an audit. In Java the pair is BigDecimal for NUMERIC, never double. Another option is to store whole paise in a BIGINT (price_paise = 79900), which many payment systems do.

Text: CHAR, VARCHAR and TEXT

TypeMeaningNotes
CHAR(n)Exactly n characters, padded with spaces Only for truly fixed codes (country code 'IN'). Padding surprises people.
VARCHAR(n)Up to n characters Rejects longer values. Good when a real limit exists (email 100, PIN code 6).
TEXTAny length PostgreSQL: same speed as VARCHAR. MySQL: separate family (TEXT 64 KB, MEDIUMTEXT 16 MB, LONGTEXT 4 GB).

In PostgreSQL there is no performance gain from VARCHAR(50) over TEXT; the length is only a validation rule. Use VARCHAR(n) when the limit is a business rule, and TEXT for free text such as descriptions and comments. In MySQL VARCHAR is stored inside the row and can be indexed fully, while TEXT columns need a prefix length in an index, so prefer VARCHAR for searchable columns. Also in MySQL the limit counts characters, but the whole row cannot exceed 65,535 bytes.

INSERT INTO departments (name, location) VALUES ('Research', 'A very long city name ...
    repeated until it passes fifty characters in total length ok');
-- ERROR:  value too long for type character varying(50)

MySQL in strict mode (the default since 5.7) gives ERROR 1406 (22001): Data too long for column 'location' at row 1. With strict mode off, MySQL silently cuts the value, which hides bugs. Check SELECT @@sql_mode; contains STRICT_TRANS_TABLES.

Boolean

PostgreSQL has a real BOOLEAN type with values TRUE, FALSE and NULL. It also accepts 't', 'yes', '1' and similar text. MySQL's BOOLEAN is only another name for TINYINT(1), so TRUE is stored as 1 and the column accepts 2 or 7 as well. Write WHERE is_active or WHERE NOT is_active in PostgreSQL. Add NOT NULL DEFAULT FALSE to avoid a third, meaningless state.

All together: PostgreSQL and MySQL compared

You want to storePostgreSQLMySQL 8Java type
Small / normal / big whole numberSMALLINT / INT / BIGINT same, plus TINYINT, UNSIGNEDshort / int / long
Money, exactNUMERIC(12,2)DECIMAL(12,2)BigDecimal
Scientific, approximateREAL, DOUBLE PRECISION FLOAT, DOUBLEfloat, double
Short text with a limitVARCHAR(n)VARCHAR(n)String
Long textTEXTTEXT / MEDIUMTEXT / LONGTEXTString
Yes / noBOOLEANBOOLEAN (= TINYINT(1))boolean
Day, time, momentDATE, TIME, TIMESTAMPTZ DATE, TIME, DATETIME / TIMESTAMPsee next article

Rule: money is NUMERIC (or whole paise), never FLOAT

Use NUMERIC(p, s) with the scale your currency needs (2 for rupees) and map it to BigDecimal. Choose the precision for the largest value you can imagine, not the smallest.

Mistake: storing phone numbers, PIN codes and Aadhaar numbers as integers

You never do arithmetic on them, and integers drop leading zeros (0123 becomes 123) and break on +91. Store identifiers as VARCHAR, and add a CHECK for the format if you want. That is why customers.phone is VARCHAR(15).

Edge cases: silent changes and implicit casts

  • NUMERIC rounds extra decimals silently; only extra digits before the point raise an error.
  • Comparing text with a number (WHERE phone = 9876500001) makes PostgreSQL fail with operator does not exist: character varying = integer, while MySQL converts silently and may also stop using the index. Pass the right type from JDBC.
  • CHAR(5) pads 'ab' to 'ab '. PostgreSQL ignores the padding when comparing; avoid CHAR and the question never arises.

Interview angle

"Which type for money?" gets the answer NUMERIC/DECIMAL with a reason: floats cannot represent 0.1 exactly. Also expect "VARCHAR vs TEXT" (same speed in PostgreSQL, the length is a rule) and "INT vs BIGINT for ids" (an INT runs out at 2.1 billion rows, which a busy event table reaches in a few years).

Data types: dates, UUID, JSON, identity columns and ENUM

Store moments as TIMESTAMPTZ (PostgreSQL), days as DATE, structured extras as JSONB, and generate ids with GENERATED ... AS IDENTITY (MySQL: AUTO_INCREMENT). Prefer a CHECK or lookup table over ENUM.

Date and time types

TypeStoresExample value
DATEa calendar day2026-04-10
TIMEa time of day14:30:00
TIMESTAMPday + time, no time zone2026-04-10 14:30:00
TIMESTAMPTZan exact moment in time (PostgreSQL)2026-04-10 14:30:00+05:30
INTERVALa length of time (PostgreSQL)3 days 02:00:00

The difference between TIMESTAMP and TIMESTAMPTZ trips up many developers. TIMESTAMPTZ ("timestamp with time zone") does not store a zone. It converts every input to UTC for storage, and converts back to the session's TimeZone setting when it prints. So the same row shows 09:00 in London and 14:30 in Mumbai, and it is still the same instant. TIMESTAMP stores the digits you gave it and knows nothing about zones, so it is only right for things like "the shop opens at 10:00 local time".

CREATE TEMP TABLE t (at TIMESTAMPTZ);
INSERT INTO t VALUES ('2026-04-10 14:30:00+05:30');

SET TimeZone = 'Asia/Kolkata';
SELECT at FROM t;
SET TimeZone = 'UTC';
SELECT at FROM t;
           at
------------------------
 2026-04-10 14:30:00+05:30

           at
------------------------
 2026-04-10 09:00:00+00

MySQL has DATETIME (no conversion, years 1000–9999) and TIMESTAMP (converted to UTC for storage, range 1970 to 2038-01-19, so avoid it for far-future dates). Many Spring Boot teams run the database in UTC and map to Instant or OffsetDateTime; for DATE use LocalDate. Time-zone handling in queries is in Date ranges and time zones.

UUID

A UUID is a 128-bit identifier such as 3f2b8c1e-5a7d-4c9e-9b21-0d4e6f7a8b90. It can be created by any application server without asking the database, which is useful when ids are generated before the insert or across several services, and it does not reveal how many rows exist. PostgreSQL has a native UUID type (16 bytes) and the built-in gen_random_uuid() (PostgreSQL 13 and later). Random UUIDs make index inserts scatter across the whole B-tree, so for very write-heavy tables prefer time-ordered UUIDs (UUIDv7; PostgreSQL 18 added a uuidv7() function). MySQL has UUID() and stores it best as BINARY(16) via UUID_TO_BIN(). A BIGINT identity is smaller and faster; choose UUID for a reason, not by habit.

CREATE TABLE sessions (
    session_id  UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    customer_id INT  NOT NULL REFERENCES customers (customer_id)
);

JSON and JSONB

PostgreSQL can keep a JSON document in a column. JSON stores the text exactly as given. JSONB parses it into a binary form: slightly slower to write, faster to query, it removes duplicate keys and does not keep key order or spaces, and it supports indexes (GIN). Use JSONB unless you must keep the original text. MySQL's single JSON type is stored in binary too.

CREATE TEMP TABLE events (id INT, payload JSONB);
INSERT INTO events VALUES (1, '{"type": "login", "device": {"os": "Android"}}');

SELECT payload -> 'device'            AS device_json,   -- returns JSON
       payload ->> 'type'            AS type_text,     -- returns text
       payload -> 'device' ->> 'os'  AS os
FROM events
WHERE payload @> '{"type": "login"}';  -- containment, can use a GIN index
     device_json     | type_text |   os
---------------------+-----------+---------
 {"os": "Android"}   | login     | Android
(1 row)

MySQL uses the same -> and ->> operators on a path: payload->>'$.type'. JSON is good for data that really has no fixed shape (audit payloads, third-party webhooks, optional settings). If you filter or join on a field all the time, make it a normal column. More in Module 6.

Auto-generated ids: SERIAL, IDENTITY and AUTO_INCREMENT

OptionDatabaseHow it works
GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITYPostgreSQL 10+ SQL standard. The database always supplies the value; supplying your own is an error unless you add OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE.
GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITYPostgreSQL 10+ Same, but you may supply your own value (our sample script does, to load fixed ids).
SERIAL / BIGSERIALPostgreSQL Old shorthand: creates a sequence and a default. Works, but the sequence is a separate object with looser ownership. Seen in older code.
AUTO_INCREMENTMySQLCounter on the table; moves forward by itself, even after manual inserts.

For new PostgreSQL tables use GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY (or BY DEFAULT if you load data with fixed ids). In Spring Data JPA the matching setting is @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY). Ids are not gap-free: a rolled-back insert or a failed statement still uses a number, so never rely on "no gaps".

CREATE TEMP TABLE tickets (
    ticket_id INT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    title     VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO tickets (title) VALUES ('Login fails') RETURNING ticket_id;
INSERT INTO tickets (ticket_id, title) VALUES (99, 'Manual id');
 ticket_id
-----------
         1
(1 row)

ERROR:  cannot insert a non-DEFAULT value into column "ticket_id"
DETAIL:  Column "ticket_id" is an identity column defined as GENERATED ALWAYS.
HINT:  Use OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE to override.

ENUM, briefly

An ENUM is a column that accepts only a fixed list of words. PostgreSQL defines it as a separate type, MySQL writes it inline:

-- PostgreSQL
CREATE TYPE order_status AS ENUM ('PLACED', 'SHIPPED', 'DELIVERED', 'CANCELLED');
-- MySQL
CREATE TABLE orders_demo (status ENUM('PLACED', 'SHIPPED', 'DELIVERED', 'CANCELLED'));

Adding a value later is easy in PostgreSQL (ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE), but removing or renaming one is painful, and in MySQL changing the list rewrites the table. Our sample schema uses VARCHAR(20) with a CHECK (status IN (...)) instead: easy to change, easy to map to a Java enum with @Enumerated(EnumType.STRING). If the list has attributes or changes often, use a small lookup table with a foreign key.

Rule: store moments in UTC-aware columns

In PostgreSQL use TIMESTAMPTZ for "when did it happen" (created_at, paid_at). Use DATE for a day with no time (hire_date). Never store a moment as text.

Mistake: using TIMESTAMP for events and getting shifted times

A server in Mumbai writes 14:30 into a plain TIMESTAMP; a reporting job in UTC reads it as 14:30 UTC, five and a half hours off. With TIMESTAMPTZ both sides agree on the instant.

Edge cases

  • Invalid dates fail: '2026-02-30' raises ERROR: date/time field value out of range.
  • MySQL (non-strict) can store 0000-00-00; modern strict mode refuses it.
  • A JSON text that is not valid JSON fails with ERROR: invalid input syntax for type json.
  • Identity counters never go back after ROLLBACK or DELETE.

Interview angle

Common questions: TIMESTAMP vs TIMESTAMPTZ (the second normalises to UTC), JSON vs JSONB (binary, indexable), UUID vs auto-increment (UUID: generated anywhere, no guessable order, bigger, scattered index writes; identity: small and ordered), and SERIAL vs IDENTITY (identity is the standard, newer form).

CREATE TABLE, ALTER TABLE and DROP TABLE

CREATE TABLE defines a table with its columns, types and constraints, ALTER TABLE changes it afterwards, and DROP TABLE removes it with all its data. Real projects keep every such change in versioned migration files instead of typing it by hand.

CREATE TABLE

A table definition is a list of columns, each with a name, a type and optional rules, followed by optional table-level constraints. This is a small, complete example you can run next to the sample database:

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS leave_requests (
    request_id  INT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    emp_id      INT          NOT NULL REFERENCES employees (emp_id),
    start_date  DATE         NOT NULL,
    end_date    DATE         NOT NULL,
    reason      TEXT,
    status      VARCHAR(10)  NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PENDING',
    created_at  TIMESTAMPTZ  NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT ck_leave_dates  CHECK (end_date >= start_date),
    CONSTRAINT ck_leave_status CHECK (status IN ('PENDING', 'APPROVED', 'REJECTED'))
);

IF NOT EXISTS makes the statement do nothing (with a notice) when the table is already there, so a script can be re-run. Without it you get ERROR: relation "leave_requests" already exists. Two more ways to create a table:

-- same columns, no data (PostgreSQL: add INCLUDING ALL to copy constraints and indexes)
CREATE TABLE employees_backup (LIKE employees INCLUDING ALL);

-- table made from a query: columns and data, but no constraints
CREATE TABLE high_earners AS
SELECT emp_id, first_name, salary FROM employees WHERE salary >= 150000;

A temporary table (CREATE TEMP TABLE) lives only for your session and is handy for experiments, as in the examples above. Tables can also live in a schema, a named folder inside the database: hr.employees. The default schema in PostgreSQL is public (see Module 4).

ALTER TABLE: the common changes

TaskPostgreSQLMySQL 8
Add a columnALTER TABLE t ADD COLUMN c INT;
Drop a columnALTER TABLE t DROP COLUMN c;
Rename a columnALTER TABLE t RENAME COLUMN c TO d;
Rename a tableALTER TABLE t RENAME TO t2;
Change a typeALTER COLUMN c TYPE BIGINTMODIFY COLUMN c BIGINT
Set / drop defaultALTER COLUMN c SET DEFAULT 0 / DROP DEFAULT
Set / drop NOT NULLALTER COLUMN c SET NOT NULL / DROP NOT NULL repeat the whole column with MODIFY COLUMN c INT NOT NULL
Add a constraintALTER TABLE t ADD CONSTRAINT name ...;
Drop a constraintDROP CONSTRAINT name DROP FOREIGN KEY / DROP CHECK / DROP INDEX / DROP PRIMARY KEY
 
ALTER TABLE leave_requests ADD COLUMN approved_by INT;
ALTER TABLE leave_requests
    ADD CONSTRAINT fk_leave_approver FOREIGN KEY (approved_by) REFERENCES employees (emp_id);
ALTER TABLE leave_requests RENAME COLUMN reason TO reason_text;
ALTER TABLE leave_requests ALTER COLUMN reason_text SET DEFAULT 'Not given';
ALTER TABLE leave_requests ALTER COLUMN status TYPE VARCHAR(20);
ALTER TABLE leave_requests DROP CONSTRAINT ck_leave_status;
ALTER TABLE leave_requests DROP COLUMN approved_by;     -- also removes fk_leave_approver

Several actions can go in one statement separated by commas, and PostgreSQL runs them together. If the new type cannot be converted automatically, PostgreSQL asks you to say how: ALTER COLUMN age TYPE INT USING age::INT.

What goes wrong on a table that already has rows

ALTER TABLE employees ADD COLUMN grade VARCHAR(2) NOT NULL;
-- ERROR:  column "grade" of relation "employees" contains null values

ALTER TABLE employees ADD COLUMN grade VARCHAR(2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'L1';   -- works

Existing rows need a value for the new column. Give a default, or add the column as nullable, fill it with an UPDATE, then add SET NOT NULL. The same applies when you add a constraint: ADD CONSTRAINT ... CHECK (salary > 100000) fails with check constraint "..." of relation "employees" is violated by some row if any row breaks it. Fix the data first.

Cost and locks on production tables

Most ALTER TABLE forms take a strong lock, so other queries wait. Adding a column with a constant default is instant in PostgreSQL 11 and later, but changing a column type usually rewrites the whole table while holding the lock. Adding a foreign key or CHECK scans the table. For big tables, PostgreSQL lets you add a constraint as NOT VALID (new rows are checked, old rows are not) and later run ALTER TABLE ... VALIDATE CONSTRAINT name, which checks without blocking writes. Teams run schema changes through Flyway or Liquibase migrations, in a quiet period, after testing on a copy of production data (see Spring Boot · Module 3).

DROP TABLE

DROP TABLE IF EXISTS leave_requests;
DROP TABLE departments;
-- ERROR:  cannot drop table departments because other objects depend on it
-- DETAIL:  constraint fk_employees_dept on table employees depends on table departments
-- HINT:  Use DROP ... CASCADE to drop the dependent objects too.

DROP TABLE departments CASCADE;   -- removes the foreign keys that point at it (and views)

DROP TABLE deletes the structure and all data at once and cannot be undone except by a rollback or a backup. CASCADE does not delete the child table, only the constraints and views that depend on it; RESTRICT (the default) refuses. DROP TABLE a, b, c drops several. See Safe changes, TRUNCATE and DROP for the difference from DELETE and TRUNCATE.

Another difference: PostgreSQL has transactional DDL, so you can wrap ALTER and DROP in BEGIN ... ROLLBACK and test them safely. MySQL commits implicitly before and after every DDL statement, so there is nothing to roll back.

Rule: every table gets a primary key, and every constraint a name

A table without a primary key has no way to identify one row, and JPA refuses it. Name constraints yourself (fk_leave_approver), because only a named constraint can be dropped by a readable command, and its name appears in the error message.

Convention: add columns, do not edit history

Never edit an old migration after it ran anywhere. Add a new migration. Prefer additive, backwards compatible changes (new nullable column) first, deploy the code, and only then drop what is unused.

Mistake: DROP in the wrong database

Check \conninfo before a destructive script. The editor tab for staging and production look the same. Many teams give developers no DROP permission on production at all.

Exceptions and errors

  • ERROR: relation "x" already exists on repeated CREATE; use IF NOT EXISTS.
  • ERROR: column "x" of relation "t" already exists on a repeated ADD COLUMN (PostgreSQL also has ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS).
  • ERROR: cannot alter type of a column used by a view or rule: drop and recreate the view.
  • Dropping a column used by a foreign key in another table fails until you use CASCADE.

Interview angle

Expect to write a CREATE TABLE with a primary key, foreign key and a check on the board, and to answer "how do you add a NOT NULL column to a table with a million rows?" (nullable or default first, backfill in batches, then SET NOT NULL, in a migration tool).

Constraints in depth: PRIMARY KEY, UNIQUE, NOT NULL, CHECK and DEFAULT

A constraint is a rule the database enforces on every write, so bad data cannot get in whatever the application does. Each constraint has its own error message, and naming constraints makes those messages readable.

Why the database must enforce rules

Validation in Java (@NotNull, @Size) is good for friendly messages, but it can be bypassed: a second service, a data-fix script, a bug, or two requests racing each other. A constraint inside the database cannot be bypassed. It is also checked atomically, which is the only reliable way to guarantee "no two users with the same email" under concurrency. Use both layers.

The six constraints at a glance

ConstraintGuaranteesNULL allowed?Index created?
PRIMARY KEYunique and not null: identifies a rowNoYes (unique)
UNIQUEno two rows with the same valueYes; several NULLs allowedYes (unique)
NOT NULLa value must be presentNoNo
CHECK (condition)the condition is true or unknownYes, passes (see below)No
DEFAULT valuea value used when none is givenn/a (not a real constraint)No
FOREIGN KEYvalue exists in another tableYes, passesNo (add one yourself)

Foreign keys have their own article: Foreign keys and referential actions.

Column constraint vs table constraint

A column constraint is written on the column line and concerns that column. A table constraint is written after the columns and can involve several columns. Anything with two or more columns must be a table constraint.

CREATE TABLE enrollments_demo (
    student_id  INT          NOT NULL,                          -- column constraint
    course_id   INT          NOT NULL,
    grade       CHAR(1)      CHECK (grade IN ('A','B','C','D','F')),  -- column check
    credits     SMALLINT     NOT NULL DEFAULT 3,
    CONSTRAINT pk_enrollments_demo PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id),   -- table, composite
    CONSTRAINT uq_demo_pair        UNIQUE (course_id, student_id, grade)  -- table
);

If you do not name a constraint, PostgreSQL invents a name (employees_email_key, employees_pkey, employees_salary_check, orders_customer_id_fkey). The generated names are predictable, but your own (uq_employees_email) are clearer and let a Java exception handler recognise the constraint by name.

PRIMARY KEY

A primary key marks the column (or columns) that identify a row. It is UNIQUE plus NOT NULL, a table can have only one, and the database builds a unique index for it. A composite primary key uses two or more columns: employee_projects uses (emp_id, project_id), so one employee can be on many projects and each pair appears once. Choosing keys (natural vs surrogate) is in Module 4.

INSERT INTO departments (dept_id, name, location) VALUES (1, 'Legal', 'Chennai');
-- ERROR:  duplicate key value violates unique constraint "departments_pkey"
-- DETAIL:  Key (dept_id)=(1) already exists.

INSERT INTO employee_projects (emp_id, project_id, role, hours_per_week)
VALUES (5, 1, 'Lead', 30);
-- ERROR:  duplicate key value violates unique constraint "employee_projects_pkey"
-- DETAIL:  Key (emp_id, project_id)=(5, 1) already exists.

MySQL: ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry '1' for key 'departments.PRIMARY'.

UNIQUE

UNIQUE allows no repeats but, unlike a primary key, allows NULL, and since NULL is never equal to NULL, many NULLs are allowed in a unique column (PostgreSQL 15 added UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT to change this; MySQL also allows multiple NULLs). A table can have many unique constraints, and they can cover several columns together: UNIQUE (dept_id, name) means a name is unique inside a department.

INSERT INTO employees (first_name, last_name, email, salary)
VALUES ('Meera', 'Iyer 2', 'meera.iyer@example.com', 90000);
-- ERROR:  duplicate key value violates unique constraint "employees_email_key"
-- DETAIL:  Key (email)=(meera.iyer@example.com) already exists.

MySQL: ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'meera.iyer@example.com' for key 'employees.email'. Case matters: Meera.Iyer@example.com is a different value in PostgreSQL but a duplicate in MySQL (case-insensitive collation). To make emails case-insensitive in PostgreSQL use a unique index on lower(email) (indexes are in Module 5).

NOT NULL

The simplest and most valuable constraint. Columns are nullable by default, so you must say NOT NULL every time a value is required. Decide per column: is "unknown" a valid state? A hire date, yes it must exist; a project end_date, no, it can be open.

INSERT INTO employees (first_name, email, salary) VALUES ('Zoya', 'z@example.com', 50000);
-- ERROR:  null value in column "last_name" of relation "employees" violates not-null constraint
-- DETAIL:  Failing row contains (13, Zoya, null, z@example.com, null, null, 50000.00, ...).

MySQL: ERROR 1364 (HY000): Field 'last_name' doesn't have a default value when the column is omitted, or ERROR 1048 (23000): Column 'last_name' cannot be null when NULL is given.

CHECK

CHECK tests a condition on the row's own columns. The row is accepted when the condition is TRUE or unknown (NULL). This surprises people: CHECK (salary > 0) lets a NULL salary through, so pair it with NOT NULL when the value is mandatory. A check cannot contain a subquery or look at other rows; use a unique index, foreign key or trigger for that.

INSERT INTO employees (first_name, last_name, email, salary)
VALUES ('Zoya', 'Khan', 'zoya.khan@example.com', -5);
-- ERROR:  new row for relation "employees" violates check constraint "employees_salary_check"
-- DETAIL:  Failing row contains (13, Zoya, Khan, zoya.khan@example.com, null, null, -5.00, ...).

UPDATE projects SET end_date = '2023-01-01' WHERE project_id = 1;
-- ERROR:  new row for relation "projects" violates check constraint "ck_projects_dates"

MySQL 8.0.16+: ERROR 3819 (HY000): Check constraint 'employees_chk_1' is violated. Older MySQL accepted the syntax and ignored it, so confirm the version.

DEFAULT

DEFAULT is not a rule but a fallback: when an INSERT leaves the column out (or says the word DEFAULT), the database uses the default. It can be a constant or an expression such as now() or CURRENT_DATE. It is used only when the column is missing. Explicitly inserting NULL does not trigger the default; it stores NULL, or fails if the column is NOT NULL.

INSERT INTO orders (customer_id) VALUES (6);               -- status = 'PLACED', today's date
INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, status) VALUES (6, NULL);
-- ERROR:  null value in column "status" of relation "orders" violates not-null constraint

Adding and removing constraints later

ALTER TABLE employees ADD CONSTRAINT uq_employees_email UNIQUE (email);
ALTER TABLE employees ADD CONSTRAINT ck_employees_salary CHECK (salary > 0);
ALTER TABLE employees ALTER COLUMN last_name SET NOT NULL;
ALTER TABLE employees ALTER COLUMN hire_date SET DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE;
ALTER TABLE employees DROP CONSTRAINT ck_employees_salary;

Rule: constraints see rows, not statements' intent

They run on every INSERT and UPDATE. A constraint that fails aborts the whole statement (all rows of a multi-row insert) and, inside a transaction, marks the transaction as failed in PostgreSQL: you must ROLLBACK before running anything else.

Convention: be strict by default

Start every column as NOT NULL and relax it only when "no value" is a real state. Add CHECK for ranges, small value lists and date order. Put the real rules in the database and translate its errors (SQLState 23505 unique, 23503 foreign key, 23502 not null, 23514 check) into clean API messages in Spring.

Mistake: checking "does it exist" in Java, then inserting

Wrong: race condition

if (!repo.existsByEmail(email)) {   // two requests can both pass here
    repo.save(new Employee(email));
}

Right: UNIQUE plus handling the error

try {
    repo.save(new Employee(email));          // UNIQUE decides
} catch (DataIntegrityViolationException e) {
    throw new EmailAlreadyUsedException(email);
}

Edge cases

  • Several NULLs in a UNIQUE column are fine; several empty strings are not. Do not store '' where you mean NULL.
  • CHECK passes on NULL. CHECK (end_date >= start_date) passes when end_date is NULL, which is what ck_projects_dates relies on.
  • MySQL's CHECK before 8.0.16 was parsed and ignored.

Interview angle

Likely: "primary key vs unique key" (one per table, never NULL vs many, NULLs allowed), "can a unique column hold several NULLs?" (yes), "does CHECK reject NULL?" (no), and "how do you stop duplicate emails under concurrent requests?" (a unique constraint, not an application check).

Foreign keys and referential actions

A foreign key forces a column to hold only values that exist in another table's key, which keeps relationships valid. ON DELETE and ON UPDATE decide what happens to the child rows when the parent changes: refuse, delete, or set NULL.

What a foreign key guarantees

In employees, dept_id must be a real departments.dept_id or NULL. The table that holds the foreign key is the child; the table it points to is the parent. The database then blocks two things: inserting (or updating to) a child row whose parent does not exist, and deleting (or changing the key of) a parent that still has children, unless an action says otherwise. This is referential integrity. The foreign key must point to a column that is a primary key or has a unique constraint.

-- inline form (PostgreSQL; MySQL IGNORES this form)
dept_id INT REFERENCES departments (dept_id)

-- table form (works everywhere, can be named)
CONSTRAINT fk_employees_dept FOREIGN KEY (dept_id) REFERENCES departments (dept_id)
    ON DELETE SET NULL ON UPDATE CASCADE

Errors when the rule is broken

INSERT INTO employees (first_name, last_name, email, dept_id, salary)
VALUES ('Zoya', 'Khan', 'zoya.khan@example.com', 99, 50000);
-- ERROR:  insert or update on table "employees" violates foreign key constraint "fk_employees_dept"
-- DETAIL:  Key (dept_id)=(99) is not present in table "departments".

DELETE FROM departments WHERE dept_id = 1;
-- ERROR:  update or delete on table "departments" violates foreign key constraint
--         "fk_projects_dept" on table "projects"
-- DETAIL:  Key (dept_id)=(1) is still referenced from table "projects".

MySQL words: ERROR 1452 (23000): Cannot add or update a child row: a foreign key constraint fails (...) and ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails (...). A NULL foreign key is always allowed, as the CEO's dept_id shows; add NOT NULL when the relationship is mandatory (projects.dept_id).

ON DELETE and ON UPDATE actions

ActionWhen the parent row is deleted or its key changesUse when
NO ACTION (default)Error. Checked at the end of the statement; in PostgreSQL the check can be deferred to commit time.Safe default
RESTRICTError immediately, never deferrable. In MySQL (InnoDB) it behaves the same as NO ACTION.You want an unmistakable block (products that were sold)
CASCADEThe child rows are deleted (or their key is updated to the new value) Children have no meaning without the parent (order lines, project assignments)
SET NULLThe child's column becomes NULL (column must be nullable) The link is optional (employee's department)
SET DEFAULTThe child's column becomes its default value Rare. PostgreSQL only; InnoDB rejects it.

The sample schema uses all the common ones. Watch each in a transaction and roll back afterwards, so the data stays unchanged:

BEGIN;

-- SET NULL: HR (3) has employees 4, 11, 12, but also projects. Remove the project first.
DELETE FROM projects WHERE dept_id = 3;
DELETE FROM departments WHERE dept_id = 3;
SELECT emp_id, first_name, dept_id FROM employees WHERE emp_id IN (4, 11, 12) ORDER BY emp_id;

-- CASCADE: deleting order 1 deletes its two lines in order_items
DELETE FROM orders WHERE order_id = 1;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS lines_of_order_1 FROM order_items WHERE order_id = 1;

-- RESTRICT: product 1 was sold, so it cannot be deleted
DELETE FROM products WHERE product_id = 1;   -- ERROR (inside a transaction: now aborted)

ROLLBACK;
 emp_id | first_name | dept_id
--------+------------+---------
      4 | Sunita     |  (null)
     11 | Farhan     |  (null)
     12 | Isha       |  (null)
(3 rows)

 lines_of_order_1
------------------
                0
(1 row)

ERROR:  update or delete on table "products" violates foreign key constraint "fk_items_product"
        on table "order_items"
DETAIL:  Key (product_id)=(1) is still referenced from table "order_items".

ON UPDATE works the same way for changes to the parent key, but keys rarely change, so the default NO ACTION is almost always right. You do not need it with surrogate ids.

Choosing the action

Foreign keys need indexes on the child side

PostgreSQL indexes the parent key (it is a primary key) but does not create an index on the foreign-key column of the child. Deleting a department then has to scan all of employees to look for children, and joins on that column are slow. Add one yourself: CREATE INDEX idx_employees_dept_id ON employees (dept_id);. MySQL InnoDB creates it automatically if none exists. See Module 5.

Special cases

  • Self-reference: employees.manager_id points to employees.emp_id. The top of the tree has NULL. Inserting a row that refers to a row inserted later fails in MySQL (row-by-row check) unless you insert in the right order.
  • Composite foreign keys: a key with two columns references a composite key and is declared with both columns in the same constraint.
  • Deferrable constraints (PostgreSQL): DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED delays the check until COMMIT, useful for loading circular references.
  • Many-to-many uses a junction table with two foreign keys and a composite primary key, like employee_projects and order_items (designed in Module 4).

Rule: always declare foreign keys

"We check it in the application" is how orphan rows (orders for deleted customers) are born. Declare the foreign key, make the child column NOT NULL when the link is mandatory, and index it.

Mistake: CASCADE everywhere

ON DELETE CASCADE on orders.customer_id would delete a customer's whole order history, and the invoices, with one careless DELETE. Cascade only for true parts-of-a-whole. Many systems never delete at all and use a deleted_at or is_active column (soft delete).

Edge cases

  • SET NULL on a NOT NULL column fails at definition time in MySQL, and at delete time in PostgreSQL.
  • Cascades chain: deleting a customer can delete orders, which delete order items.
  • MySQL ignores inline REFERENCES, so always use the table-level form for portability.
  • A database dump may need constraints disabled to load tables in any order; in PostgreSQL pg_dump orders them correctly.

Interview angle

Typical questions: "what happens when you delete a parent row that has children?" (error by default; cascade or set null if declared), "RESTRICT vs NO ACTION" (when the check runs; same in MySQL), "does PostgreSQL index a foreign key column?" (no, create the index), and the JPA link: @OneToMany(cascade = REMOVE) is Hibernate deleting children, while ON DELETE CASCADE is the database doing it.

Interview questions — SQL basics

What is the difference between DELETE, TRUNCATE and DROP?

DELETE removes rows one by one, accepts a WHERE, fires row triggers and can be rolled back. TRUNCATE empties the whole table quickly, has no WHERE, resets identity counters if you ask (RESTART IDENTITY) and is transactional in PostgreSQL but commits implicitly in MySQL. DROP TABLE removes the table itself: structure, data, indexes and constraints. DELETE and TRUNCATE keep the table definition; DROP does not.

Why does WHERE salary = NULL return no rows, and what is the fix?

SQL uses three-valued logic: true, false and unknown. Any comparison with NULL, including NULL = NULL, gives unknown, and WHERE keeps only rows that are true. Use IS NULL / IS NOT NULL, or IS DISTINCT FROM when you want a NULL-safe comparison. COALESCE(x, 0) replaces NULL with a default.

What does NOT IN return if the list contains a NULL?

Nothing. x NOT IN (1, 2, NULL) means x <> 1 AND x <> 2 AND x <> NULL; the last part is unknown, so the whole expression is never true. A subquery that returns one NULL empties the result. Use NOT EXISTS, or filter the NULLs out of the subquery (NULL and three-valued logic).

Primary key vs unique key: what is the difference?

A table has at most one primary key; it is unique and cannot be NULL, and it identifies the row (JPA's @Id maps to it). A table may have many unique constraints; their columns may contain NULL, and several NULLs are allowed because NULL is never equal to NULL. Both create a unique index.

Which data type do you use for money, and why not FLOAT?

NUMERIC(p, s) / DECIMAL, mapped to BigDecimal in Java. Floats are binary approximations: 0.1 + 0.2 is not exactly 0.3 in a DOUBLE PRECISION, and errors add up across many rows. Some systems store whole paise in a BIGINT instead.

VARCHAR vs TEXT vs CHAR?

CHAR(n) is fixed length and space padded; avoid it except for fixed codes. VARCHAR(n) holds up to n characters and rejects more, a good way to express a business limit. TEXT has no limit. In PostgreSQL all three perform the same, so the length is only a rule; in MySQL TEXT is a separate family with index restrictions.

What is the difference between TIMESTAMP and TIMESTAMPTZ?

TIMESTAMP stores the date and time you typed, with no zone. TIMESTAMPTZ converts input to UTC, stores that, and shows it in the session time zone, so it always means one exact instant. Use it for event times (created_at). MySQL's equivalents are DATETIME (no conversion) and TIMESTAMP (UTC, limited to the year 2038).

What is the order of rows without ORDER BY, and why does paging need it?

It is unspecified. The database returns rows in whatever order is cheapest, which can change after an update, a vacuum or a different plan. With LIMIT 10 OFFSET 20 and no ORDER BY, a row can appear on two pages or none. Order by a unique column (add the id as a tie-breaker) for stable pages.

Does BETWEEN include its end points? Any trap with dates?

Yes, both ends are included. For a TIMESTAMP column, BETWEEN '2026-04-01' AND '2026-04-30' stops at midnight on the 30th and misses the rest of that day. Use a half-open range instead: >= '2026-04-01' AND < '2026-05-01'.

What happens if you run UPDATE or DELETE without WHERE? How do you work safely?

Every row is changed or removed. Safe workflow: write the SELECT with the same WHERE first and check the rows, run the change inside BEGIN, look at the row count and a verification query, then COMMIT or ROLLBACK. Add RETURNING * in PostgreSQL to see what changed, and take a backup before big changes. MySQL's --safe-updates mode refuses statements without a key in WHERE.

What error do you get when a constraint is violated? Name the four main ones.
  • NOT NULL: null value in column "x" violates not-null constraint (SQLState 23502)
  • UNIQUE / PRIMARY KEY: duplicate key value violates unique constraint "..." (23505)
  • CHECK: new row for relation "t" violates check constraint "..." (23514)
  • FOREIGN KEY: violates foreign key constraint "..." (23503)

In Spring these arrive as DataIntegrityViolationException.

Explain ON DELETE CASCADE, SET NULL and RESTRICT with an example.

They tell the database what to do with child rows when the parent is deleted. CASCADE: deleting an order deletes its order_items. SET NULL: deleting a department sets employees.dept_id to NULL (the column must be nullable). RESTRICT (and the default NO ACTION): deleting a product that was sold fails. Choose cascade only when the child is part of the parent.

How do you insert a row if it does not exist, otherwise update it?

An upsert. PostgreSQL: INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (email) DO UPDATE SET ... (or DO NOTHING). MySQL: INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE .... It relies on a unique constraint and is atomic, unlike "select then insert", which breaks when two requests race.

Why avoid SELECT * in application code?

It sends columns you do not use, breaks position-based readers when the table changes, blocks index-only scans, and returns duplicate column names in joins. List the columns you need. It is fine for COUNT(*), EXISTS and exploring in a terminal.

How do you add a NOT NULL column to a large live table?

Adding it as NOT NULL with no default fails if the table has rows. Safe path: add the column nullable (or with a constant default, which is instant in PostgreSQL 11+), backfill existing rows in batches, then ALTER COLUMN ... SET NOT NULL. Do it as a Flyway or Liquibase migration, and be aware of table locks.

Cheat sheet — SQL basics

  • Query order written SELECT … FROM … WHERE … ORDER BY … LIMIT; keywords uppercase, names snake_case.
  • NULL never equals anything; use IS NULL, COALESCE(x, d), NULLIF(a, b), IS DISTINCT FROM.
  • NOT IN + NULL returns no rows; use NOT EXISTS.
  • AND before OR; put parentheses around every OR group.
  • BETWEEN is inclusive on both ends; use >= … AND < … for timestamps.
  • LIKE % any text, _ one character; ILIKE is case-insensitive (PostgreSQL only).
  • Paging ORDER BY id LIMIT n OFFSET m or OFFSET m ROWS FETCH FIRST n ROWS ONLY; always order.
  • DISTINCT removes duplicate rows; DISTINCT ON (col) keeps the first row per group (PostgreSQL).
  • INSERT INSERT INTO t (a, b) VALUES (1, 2), (3, 4) RETURNING id;
  • Upsert ON CONFLICT (key) DO UPDATE SET c = EXCLUDED.c / MySQL ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE.
  • UPDATE / DELETE always with WHERE; test with SELECT first, inside BEGIN … ROLLBACK.
  • DELETE vs TRUNCATE vs DROP rows with filter, all rows fast, whole table.
  • Whole numbers SMALLINT 2 bytes, INT 4, BIGINT 8.
  • Money NUMERIC(12,2) → Java BigDecimal; never REAL / DOUBLE.
  • Text VARCHAR(n) for limits, TEXT for free text; ids like phone are text.
  • Time TIMESTAMPTZ for moments, DATE for days; store UTC.
  • Ids INT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY (MySQL AUTO_INCREMENT); gaps are normal.
  • DDL CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS, ALTER TABLE … ADD/DROP/RENAME COLUMN, DROP TABLE IF EXISTS … CASCADE.
  • Constraints PRIMARY KEY (unique, not null), UNIQUE (NULLs ok), NOT NULL, CHECK (NULL passes), DEFAULT (only when column omitted), FOREIGN KEY.
  • Error codes 23502 not null, 23505 unique, 23503 foreign key, 23514 check.
  • FK actions NO ACTION (default), RESTRICT, CASCADE, SET NULL, SET DEFAULT.
  • psql \l databases, \c db connect, \dt tables, \d table columns, \q quit.
  • MySQL differences backticks, || is OR, 7/2 = 3.5, no RETURNING, case-insensitive text, DDL auto-commits.

Practice — SQL basics

Run sample_schema.sql first, so you start from known data. Write every query yourself, then compare with the results in the earlier articles.

  1. Warm-up reads. List the first and last name of employees in Engineering (dept 1), highest salary first. Then list products costing between 100 and 1,000, sorted by price. Done when the first query returns five rows, Vikram (220000) first and Divya (90000) last.
  2. NULL hunting. Find employees with no department; find customers with no phone number; list customers with the phone shown as 'unknown' when missing (COALESCE). Done when you get 1 employee (Meera) and 3 customers (Diya, Rohan, Tanvi).
  3. Filters and patterns. Employees hired in 2023 or later whose last name starts with S or G, using parentheses correctly; customers whose email contains "an" (use ILIKE). Explain why the parentheses matter.
  4. Paging. Show employees ordered by salary descending, page size 4: write page 1, 2 and 3. Make the order stable with emp_id as a tie-breaker. Done when no employee appears twice.
  5. Safe change. In a transaction, give everyone in Sales a 10 percent raise. Check with SELECT first, use RETURNING, then ROLLBACK. Then do a failing one: set a salary to -1 and read the exact error and constraint name.
  6. Upsert. Write an upsert into departments keyed on name that updates the location when the name exists (Sales moves to Chennai) and inserts a new row otherwise (Legal in Chennai). Run it twice; the second run must not fail.
  7. Design a table. Create leave_requests as in the notes, then add a column approved_by with a foreign key to employees. Insert one valid row and one row for each constraint violation (bad employee, end before start, unknown status). Record the error text and SQLState of each. Done when you can predict each message before running it.
  8. Foreign key actions. Create reviews (review_id, product_id, customer_id, rating) with rating from 1 to 5, a unique pair (product_id, customer_id), product_id with ON DELETE CASCADE and customer_id with ON DELETE SET NULL. Insert three reviews, delete a product and a customer inside a transaction, and show what remains before rolling back.
  9. Type review. Take a column list from an app you know (user: id, name, phone, balance, created time, is_verified, preferences) and pick a PostgreSQL type, NOT NULL decision and default for each. Justify the money and time columns in one line each.
2

Module 2·Months 1–2 · about 1.5 weeksMust

Intermediate SQL

15 topics · about 1.5 h of reading

Module 1 showed you how to read and change rows one table at a time. Real questions are bigger: "How many orders did each customer place?", "Which department has the highest average salary?", "Which customers never bought anything?" To answer them you must summarise rows (aggregates and GROUP BY), combine tables (joins), nest queries (subqueries), stack results (UNION), branch inside a query (CASE) and work with text and dates. This module teaches all of it on the same company and shop tables you created in The sample database.

This is the part of SQL that every backend interview tests, and the part Spring Data JPA queries (@Query, JOIN FETCH, projections) are built on. The thread that ties it together is the logical order of a query: once you know in which order the database evaluates the clauses, most "why does this not work?" questions answer themselves. Window functions, CTEs and the classic interview puzzles come next in Module 3.

  • Summarise data with COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX, GROUP BY and HAVING, and say how HAVING differs from WHERE.
  • Write inner, left, right, full, self and cross joins, join three or more tables, and avoid the LEFT JOIN and row-multiplication traps.
  • Use scalar, IN, derived-table and correlated subqueries, and choose between IN, EXISTS and JOIN.
  • Combine results with UNION, UNION ALL, INTERSECT and EXCEPT, and branch with CASE.
  • Use string and date functions, filter date ranges correctly, handle time zones and cast types.
  • Explain the logical processing order and use it to debug a query.

Aggregate functions: COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX

An aggregate function reads many rows and returns one value. The five basic ones are COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN and MAX, and all of them except COUNT(*) silently ignore NULL.

What they do

FunctionReturnsWorks onOn an empty set
COUNT(*)number of rowsrows0
COUNT(col)number of rows where col is not NULLany type0
SUM(col)totalnumbersNULL
AVG(col)mean of the non-NULL valuesnumbersNULL
MIN(col), MAX(col)smallest / largestnumbers, text, datesNULL

Without GROUP BY, an aggregate collapses the whole table (or the rows that pass WHERE) into a single row.

SELECT COUNT(*)                 AS employees,
       SUM(salary)              AS payroll,
       ROUND(AVG(salary), 2)    AS avg_salary,
       MIN(salary)              AS lowest,
       MAX(salary)              AS highest,
       MIN(hire_date)           AS first_hire
FROM employees;
 employees |  payroll   | avg_salary | lowest  | highest   | first_hire
-----------+------------+------------+---------+-----------+------------
        12 | 1645000.00 |  137083.33 | 55000.00 | 300000.00 | 2015-04-01
(1 row)

You cannot mix an aggregate with a plain column in the same SELECT unless you group: SELECT first_name, MAX(salary) FROM employees fails with ERROR: column "employees.first_name" must appear in the GROUP BY clause or be used in an aggregate function. The database cannot know which name to show next to the maximum. (Finding the person with the maximum is a different query, see Nth highest salary.)

COUNT(*) vs COUNT(col) vs COUNT(DISTINCT col)

This is the most asked aggregate question. COUNT(*) counts rows. COUNT(col) counts rows where the column has a value. COUNT(DISTINCT col) counts different non-NULL values. They only differ when there are NULLs or repeats.

SELECT COUNT(*)                AS all_rows,
       COUNT(dept_id)          AS with_dept,
       COUNT(DISTINCT dept_id) AS distinct_depts
FROM employees;

SELECT COUNT(*) AS customers, COUNT(phone) AS with_phone FROM customers;
 all_rows | with_dept | distinct_depts
----------+-----------+----------------
       12 |        11 |              3
(1 row)

 customers | with_phone
-----------+------------
         7 |          4
(1 row)

Meera has no department, so COUNT(dept_id) is 11. Five employees share department 1, six share 2 and 3, but only three different ids exist. COUNT(1) and COUNT(*) are the same thing and equally fast; write COUNT(*).

NULLs are ignored

Aggregates skip NULL values. That is usually what you want, but it changes averages:

SELECT ROUND(AVG(x), 2)              AS avg_ignoring_null,
       ROUND(AVG(COALESCE(x, 0)), 2) AS avg_null_as_zero,
       SUM(x)              AS total,
       COUNT(x)            AS counted
FROM (VALUES (10), (20), (NULL)) AS t(x);
 avg_ignoring_null | avg_null_as_zero | total | counted
-------------------+------------------+-------+---------
             15.00 |            10.00 |    30 |       2
(1 row)

Decide what a missing value means. A missing rating should not count as zero stars; a missing sale probably does count as zero sales. Also remember: SUM of an empty set is NULL, not 0, while COUNT of an empty set is 0.

SELECT SUM(salary) AS total, COALESCE(SUM(salary), 0) AS safe_total, COUNT(*) AS n
FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 99;
  total   | safe_total | n
----------+------------+---
 (null)   |          0 | 0
(1 row)

AVG, integers and ROUND

AVG of an integer column returns NUMERIC in PostgreSQL (and a decimal in MySQL), so it does not truncate. The trap is doing the division yourself with two integers:

SELECT SUM(hours_per_week)                       AS total_hours,
       SUM(hours_per_week) / COUNT(*)            AS wrong_int_division,
       ROUND(AVG(hours_per_week), 2)             AS avg_hours,
       ROUND(SUM(hours_per_week)::NUMERIC / COUNT(*), 2) AS avg_by_hand
FROM employee_projects;
 total_hours | wrong_int_division | avg_hours | avg_by_hand
-------------+--------------------+-----------+-------------
         260 |                 23 |     23.64 |       23.64
(1 row)

ROUND(x, 2) rounds to two decimals; ROUND(x) to a whole number; a negative second argument rounds to tens or hundreds. In PostgreSQL ROUND(value, digits) needs a NUMERIC (cast a DOUBLE PRECISION first: ROUND(x::NUMERIC, 2)). Casting is covered in Type casting.

Aggregating expressions, and listing values

The argument can be any expression. Revenue is quantity times price per line:

SELECT SUM(quantity * unit_price) AS revenue_all_orders FROM order_items;
SELECT STRING_AGG(first_name, ', ' ORDER BY first_name) AS engineers
FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 1;
 revenue_all_orders
--------------------
           21924.00
(1 row)

                engineers
-----------------------------------------
 Arjun, Divya, Priya, Rahul, Vikram
(1 row)

STRING_AGG (PostgreSQL) glues values into one text; MySQL calls it GROUP_CONCAT(first_name ORDER BY first_name SEPARATOR ', '). Other useful PostgreSQL aggregates: BOOL_AND, BOOL_OR, ARRAY_AGG, JSONB_AGG. COUNT(DISTINCT x), SUM(DISTINCT x) work too, but SUM(DISTINCT salary) adds each different salary only once, which is rarely what you mean.

Rule: no aggregates in WHERE

WHERE salary > AVG(salary) fails with ERROR: aggregate functions are not allowed in WHERE. WHERE runs before any group exists. Use a subquery (salary > (SELECT AVG(salary) FROM employees)) or HAVING.

Mistake: COUNT(col) when you meant COUNT(*)

"How many customers do we have?" with COUNT(phone) answers 4, not 7. Count a column only when you want to skip its NULLs; otherwise use COUNT(*).

Edge cases

  • With no GROUP BY, an aggregate query always returns exactly one row, even over zero input rows. With GROUP BY and no input rows, you get zero rows.
  • SUM of an INT column returns BIGINT in PostgreSQL, and SUM of BIGINT returns NUMERIC, so overflow is rare. MIN and MAX work on text (alphabetical) and dates.
  • Nesting aggregates, MAX(AVG(x)), is an error. Aggregate in a subquery, then aggregate again.

Interview angle

"Difference between COUNT(*), COUNT(col) and COUNT(DISTINCT col)?" and "what do aggregates do with NULL?" are near-certain. A strong answer also mentions that SUM of nothing is NULL (use COALESCE) and that AVG(COALESCE(x,0)) differs from AVG(x).

GROUP BY and HAVING

GROUP BY splits rows into groups and runs the aggregates once per group; HAVING then filters the groups. WHERE filters rows before grouping, HAVING filters groups after.

How GROUP BY works

Think of it as sorting the rows into piles by the grouping columns, then collapsing each pile into one row. Every other column in the SELECT must either be in the GROUP BY or be inside an aggregate.

SELECT dept_id,
       COUNT(*)              AS headcount,
       ROUND(AVG(salary), 2) AS avg_salary
FROM employees
GROUP BY dept_id
ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id | headcount | avg_salary
---------+-----------+------------
       1 |         5 |  144000.00
       2 |         3 |  128333.33
       3 |         3 |   80000.00
  (null) |         1 |  300000.00
(4 rows)

NULL is treated as one group: all NULLs go together, although NULL is never equal to NULL in a comparison. To show the department name instead of the id you need a join (Joins); you then group by the id and the name, or only by the id (see the next section).

The GROUP BY rules

  • Every selected column that is not inside an aggregate must appear in GROUP BY. Otherwise PostgreSQL says column "e.first_name" must appear in the GROUP BY clause or be used in an aggregate function.
  • Exception: if you group by a table's primary key, PostgreSQL lets you select the other columns of that table, because they are determined by the key. GROUP BY d.dept_id allows d.name.
  • MySQL enforces the same rule through the ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY mode, on by default since MySQL 5.7.5 (ERROR 1055). Older setups with the mode off return an arbitrary value from the group, a silent bug. Keep the mode on.
  • You can group by an expression or several columns. Write the expression or use the alias (PostgreSQL and MySQL both allow an alias in GROUP BY, standard SQL does not).
  • The order of the output rows is not guaranteed. Add ORDER BY.

Grouping by several columns and by expressions

Each distinct combination becomes one group. Orders per customer and status:

SELECT customer_id, status, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY customer_id, status
ORDER BY customer_id, status;
 customer_id |  status   | orders
-------------+-----------+--------
           1 | DELIVERED |      2
           1 | SHIPPED   |      1
           2 | DELIVERED |      1
           2 | PLACED    |      1
           3 | CANCELLED |      1
           3 | DELIVERED |      1
           4 | DELIVERED |      1
           5 | PLACED    |      1
           5 | SHIPPED   |      1
(9 rows)

Grouping by an expression: how many people were hired per year, keeping only years with more than one hire (that is HAVING, next).

SELECT EXTRACT(YEAR FROM hire_date) AS hire_year, COUNT(*) AS hires
FROM employees
GROUP BY EXTRACT(YEAR FROM hire_date)
HAVING COUNT(*) > 1
ORDER BY hire_year;
 hire_year | hires
-----------+-------
      2023 |     2
      2024 |     2
(2 rows)

HAVING: filtering groups

HAVING is WHERE for groups. Its condition can use aggregates, which WHERE cannot. Departments with an average salary above 100,000:

SELECT dept_id, ROUND(AVG(salary), 2) AS avg_salary
FROM employees
GROUP BY dept_id
HAVING AVG(salary) > 100000
ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id | avg_salary
---------+------------
       1 |  144000.00
       2 |  128333.33
  (null) |  300000.00
(3 rows)

PostgreSQL does not allow the alias in HAVING (HAVING avg_salary > … fails); repeat the expression. MySQL allows the alias.

WHERE vs HAVING

WHEREHAVING
Filtersindividual rowsgroups
Runsbefore GROUP BYafter GROUP BY
Can use aggregatesNoYes
Can use plain columnsYesOnly grouped columns
SpeedReduces rows early (can use indexes)Works on the groups already built

Use both together. Question: which departments have at least two people earning 100,000 or more? The salary test is a row condition (WHERE); the "at least two" is a group condition (HAVING).

SELECT dept_id, COUNT(*) AS high_earners
FROM employees
WHERE salary >= 100000           -- row filter: runs first
GROUP BY dept_id
HAVING COUNT(*) >= 2             -- group filter: runs after grouping
ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id | high_earners
---------+--------------
       1 |            4
       2 |            2
(2 rows)

Step by step: WHERE drops Divya, Neha, Farhan and Isha (under 100,000); the rest are grouped into dept 1 (4 rows), dept 2 (2), dept 3 (Sunita, 1) and NULL (Meera, 1); HAVING keeps groups with two or more.
