Put a row condition in HAVING and it still works (HAVING dept_id = 1), but it is slower and confusing. Filter rows in WHERE.

Another everyday pattern: orders with a total above 4,000, from order_items:

SELECT order_id, SUM(quantity * unit_price) AS order_total
FROM order_items
GROUP BY order_id
HAVING SUM(quantity * unit_price) > 4000
ORDER BY order_id;
 order_id | order_total
----------+-------------
        2 |     4097.00
        7 |     4298.00
(2 rows)

Rule: SELECT may only contain grouped columns and aggregates

Think "one output row per group". Anything else has several possible values per group and the database refuses to guess.

Mistake: aggregates in WHERE

Wrong: aggregate in WHERE

SELECT dept_id FROM employees
WHERE COUNT(*) > 2 GROUP BY dept_id;
-- ERROR:  aggregate functions are not allowed in WHERE

Right: aggregate in HAVING

SELECT dept_id FROM employees
GROUP BY dept_id HAVING COUNT(*) > 2;

Edge cases

  • HAVING without GROUP BY treats the whole result as one group: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM employees HAVING COUNT(*) > 100 returns zero rows.
  • Departments with no employees (Marketing) do not appear in a GROUP BY on employees; they have no rows. To list them with a count of 0 you need a LEFT JOIN, see Join traps.
  • Join first, then group: if the join multiplies rows, the counts and sums multiply too.

Interview angle

"What is the difference between WHERE and HAVING?" Answer with the logical order: WHERE filters rows before grouping and cannot use aggregates; HAVING filters groups after aggregation. Then give one query using both. Often followed by "find departments with more than N employees" or "duplicates" (GROUP BY … HAVING COUNT(*) > 1, see Duplicates).

Conditional aggregation, FILTER and ROLLUP

Put a CASE (or PostgreSQL's FILTER) inside an aggregate to count or sum only some rows, and you can build several measures, or a pivot table, in a single pass. ROLLUP adds subtotal and grand-total rows.

Why it is useful

A report often needs several counts side by side: total orders, delivered orders, cancelled orders. Running one query per number is slow and clumsy. Conditional aggregation computes them all in one scan. The idea: the aggregate only sees the rows (or values) that your condition lets through.

SELECT COUNT(*)                                          AS all_orders,
       COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'DELIVERED')      AS delivered,
       SUM(CASE WHEN status = 'CANCELLED' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS cancelled,
       COUNT(CASE WHEN status IN ('PLACED', 'SHIPPED') THEN 1 END) AS open_orders
FROM orders;
 all_orders | delivered | cancelled | open_orders
------------+-----------+-----------+-------------
         10 |         5 |         1 |           4
(1 row)

Three equivalent spellings appear here:

  • COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE …) is the standard, readable form. PostgreSQL has it; MySQL does not.
  • SUM(CASE WHEN cond THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) works everywhere. This is the portable version.
  • COUNT(CASE WHEN cond THEN 1 END) relies on COUNT ignoring NULL: the CASE returns NULL when the condition is false (no ELSE), and NULL is not counted. Beginners write COUNT(CASE … ELSE 0 END) and wonder why every row is counted: 0 is not NULL.

How it works, step by step

  1. The database reads each row once (after WHERE has removed rows, and with GROUP BY it decides which group the row belongs to).
  2. For every aggregate in the SELECT list it checks that aggregate's own condition: the FILTER predicate, or the value of the CASE expression.
  3. If the row passes, its value goes into that aggregate's running state (a counter, a sum). If not, that one aggregate skips the row. The other aggregates still see it.
  4. When the group ends, each aggregate returns its result. One scan gives many answers.

What an aggregate returns when no row passes its condition depends on the aggregate, and this causes most surprises:

SpellingPostgreSQLMySQLIf no row matches
COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE c)yesno0
SUM(x) FILTER (WHERE c)yesnoNULL
SUM(CASE WHEN c THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)yesyes0
COUNT(CASE WHEN c THEN 1 END)yesyes0
SUM(CASE WHEN c THEN x END)yesyesNULL
AVG(CASE WHEN c THEN x END)yesyesNULL

A percentage is the classic use: divide a conditional count by the total count. Write 100.0, not 100, so the division is not an integer division.

SELECT ROUND(100.0 * COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'DELIVERED') / COUNT(*), 1)
           AS delivered_pct
FROM orders;
 delivered_pct
---------------
          50.0
(1 row)

Per group

Combined with GROUP BY it answers "for each department, how many high earners?" without hiding departments that have none:

SELECT dept_id,
       COUNT(*)                                          AS headcount,
       SUM(CASE WHEN salary >= 150000 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)   AS earn_150k_plus,
       ROUND(AVG(salary) FILTER (WHERE salary >= 100000), 0) AS avg_of_high
FROM employees
GROUP BY dept_id
ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id | headcount | earn_150k_plus | avg_of_high
---------+-----------+----------------+-------------
       1 |         5 |              3 |      157500
       2 |         3 |              2 |      155000
       3 |         3 |              0 |      120000
  (null) |         1 |              1 |      300000
(4 rows)

Department 1 high earners (at least 100,000): 220000, 150000, 150000, 110000, average 157,500. Department 3 has one (Sunita, 120,000), so the average is 120,000.

Pivoting rows into columns

A pivot turns values of a column into column headings. SQL has no simple pivot command in PostgreSQL or MySQL, so you write one conditional aggregate per output column. Revenue per product category by order status, a small report:

SELECT p.category,
       SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) FILTER (WHERE o.status = 'DELIVERED') AS delivered,
       SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) FILTER (WHERE o.status = 'SHIPPED')   AS shipped,
       SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) FILTER (WHERE o.status = 'PLACED')    AS placed
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders   o ON o.order_id   = oi.order_id
JOIN products p ON p.product_id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.category
ORDER BY p.category;
  category   | delivered | shipped | placed
-------------+-----------+---------+---------
 Electronics |   6793.00 | 5494.00 | 1098.00
 Home        |    450.00 |  (null) |  900.00
 Stationery  |   2910.00 |  (null) |  780.00
(3 rows)

(Joins are explained in the next articles; read this one again afterwards.) The cancelled order 4 is left out of all three columns on purpose. Wrap a column in COALESCE(…, 0) to show 0 instead of NULL. The same pivot idea shows up again in Pivot and median.

Subtotals with ROLLUP

GROUP BY ROLLUP (category) adds an extra row with the grand total. With two columns it also adds a subtotal per first column. The added rows show NULL in the rolled-up columns.

SELECT category,
       COUNT(*)   AS products,
       SUM(stock) AS units_in_stock
FROM products
GROUP BY ROLLUP (category)
ORDER BY category;
  category   | products | units_in_stock
-------------+----------+----------------
 Electronics |        3 |            465
 Home        |        2 |             60
 Stationery  |        2 |           1300
 (null)      |        7 |           1825
(4 rows)

MySQL spells it GROUP BY category WITH ROLLUP. The last row is the grand total. If a real group can also be NULL, you cannot tell it from the total row; the function GROUPING(category) returns 1 on the added rows (PostgreSQL and MySQL 8). Related: CUBE (all combinations) and GROUPING SETS (the exact set of groupings you list), both PostgreSQL.

Filtering groups by a conditional measure

A conditional aggregate can sit in HAVING too. "Customers who have at least one delivered order and at least one order still open" needs two conditions on the same group, which a plain WHERE cannot express:

SELECT customer_id,
       COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'DELIVERED')            AS delivered,
       COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status IN ('PLACED', 'SHIPPED')) AS open_orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY customer_id
HAVING COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'DELIVERED') >= 1
   AND COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status IN ('PLACED', 'SHIPPED')) >= 1
ORDER BY customer_id;
 customer_id | delivered | open_orders
-------------+-----------+-------------
           1 |         2 |           1
           2 |         1 |           1
(2 rows)

Customer 3 has a delivered order but only a cancelled one besides it, and customer 5 has open orders but nothing delivered, so both are left out.

ROLLUP with two columns

With ROLLUP (a, b) you get the detail rows (a, b), a subtotal for each a, and one grand total. The order of the columns matters: the grouping is rolled up from right to left. Units sold per product, with a subtotal per category:

SELECT p.category, p.name,
       COALESCE(SUM(oi.quantity), 0) AS units
FROM products p
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.product_id = p.product_id
GROUP BY ROLLUP (p.category, p.name)
ORDER BY p.category NULLS LAST, p.name NULLS LAST;
  category   |         name         | units
-------------+----------------------+-------
 Electronics | Mechanical Keyboard  |     3
 Electronics | USB-C Cable          |     8
 Electronics | Wireless Mouse       |     5
 Electronics | (null)               |    16
 Home        | Desk Lamp            |     0
 Home        | Water Bottle         |     3
 Home        | (null)               |     3
 Stationery  | Gel Pen Pack         |    17
 Stationery  | Notebook A5          |    18
 Stationery  | (null)               |    35
 (null)      | (null)               |    54
(11 rows)

The LEFT JOIN keeps Desk Lamp, which was never sold. A (null) in name marks a subtotal row, and NULL in both columns marks the grand total. To print readable labels, test with GROUPING, not with IS NULL:

Wrong: a real NULL looks like a total

SELECT COALESCE(category, 'TOTAL') AS category,
       COUNT(*) AS products
FROM products
GROUP BY ROLLUP (category);

Right: GROUPING() is 1 only on added rows

SELECT CASE WHEN GROUPING(category) = 1 THEN 'TOTAL'
            ELSE category END AS category,
       COUNT(*) AS products
FROM products
GROUP BY ROLLUP (category);

The wrong version works on the sample data, because no category is NULL. It breaks the day a product with a NULL category is added: that group would also be labelled TOTAL.

ROLLUP, CUBE and GROUPING SETS compared

ClauseGroupings it produces for (a, b)PostgreSQLMySQL
ROLLUP (a, b)(a, b), (a), ()yesWITH ROLLUP
CUBE (a, b)(a, b), (a), (b), ()yesno
GROUPING SETS ((a), (b))exactly the sets you listyesno

ROLLUP is shorthand for a particular list of GROUPING SETS, and the database still reads the table only once. The alternative, a UNION ALL of one GROUP BY per level, reads it once per level and is much longer. In MySQL, write GROUP BY p.category, p.name WITH ROLLUP.

Convention: prefer FILTER, fall back to CASE

In PostgreSQL code FILTER (WHERE …) reads best. In code that must also run on MySQL (or that a JPA native query shares between databases) use SUM(CASE WHEN …).

Rule: the condition belongs inside the aggregate, not in WHERE

Use WHERE for conditions that apply to every measure in the query. Use FILTER or CASE for conditions that apply to one measure. Mix them freely: first WHERE shrinks the rows, then each aggregate filters further.

Edge cases

  • Empty group, empty result. SUM(…) FILTER and SUM(CASE … END) give NULL when nothing matches. Wrap them in COALESCE(…, 0) for reports, or use ELSE 0 if zero is the right answer.
  • NULLs in the tested column. A condition like status = 'DELIVERED' is unknown (not true) for a NULL status, so the row is simply not counted. It is not counted as "not delivered" either; that is why the sums of all buckets may be lower than COUNT(*).
  • Ties and overlaps. Buckets such as salary >= 100000 and salary >= 150000 overlap, so a row counts in both. Make ranges exclusive when the columns must add up to the total.
  • MySQL shortcut. MySQL treats a comparison as 0 or 1, so SUM(status = 'CANCELLED') works there. PostgreSQL rejects it (a boolean cannot be summed); write the CASE or FILTER form instead.

Mistake: ELSE 0 inside AVG (and COUNT)

A zero is a value, so it takes part in the average. Average salary of people earning at least 100,000:

Wrong: the 4 low earners drag it down

SELECT AVG(CASE WHEN salary >= 100000 THEN salary ELSE 0 END)
FROM employees;          -- 113333.33  (1,360,000 / 12)

Right: skipped rows give NULL

SELECT AVG(CASE WHEN salary >= 100000 THEN salary END)
FROM employees;          -- 170000.00  (1,360,000 / 8)
-- PostgreSQL: AVG(salary) FILTER (WHERE salary >= 100000)

The same trap makes COUNT(CASE … ELSE 0 END) count every row. Use ELSE 0 only with SUM.

In real projects

Dashboards and admin screens ("orders today: placed, shipped, delivered, cancelled") usually run one conditional-aggregate query instead of four counts, which saves round trips and gives a consistent snapshot. In a Spring Data repository this is typically a native query returning a projection interface.

Mistake: using WHERE when you need several measures

If you put WHERE status = 'DELIVERED' on the query, the other columns (cancelled, open) become zero. A WHERE limits all measures; a FILTER limits one.

Interview angle

"Show orders per status as columns" or "count male and female employees in one row per department" are pivot questions: answer with SUM(CASE WHEN … THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) per output column plus GROUP BY. Mention FILTER as the PostgreSQL form.

Likely follow-ups: "what does SUM return when no row matches?" (NULL, so use COALESCE), "why does COUNT(CASE … ELSE 0 END) count everything?" (0 is not NULL), and "how do you add a total row?" (ROLLUP, with GROUPING() to label it).

Logical order of a query

You write SELECT first, but the database evaluates a query in a different order: FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, SELECT, ORDER BY, then LIMIT. That order explains almost every "why can't I use this here?" error.

The order

  1. FROM / JOIN: find the tables and combine them into one big set of rows.
  2. WHERE: throw away rows that do not match. No groups and no select aliases exist yet.
  3. GROUP BY: collapse the remaining rows into groups.
  4. HAVING: throw away groups. Aggregates are available now.
  5. SELECT: compute the output columns, expressions and aliases. Window functions (Module 3) are computed here too.
  6. DISTINCT: remove duplicate output rows.
  7. ORDER BY: sort the result. The select aliases exist now.
  8. LIMIT / OFFSET (FETCH FIRST): cut the sorted result.

This is the logical order, the meaning of the query. The optimizer is free to reorder the real work (it uses an index, filters early, joins in a smarter order) as long as the answer is the same.

What the order explains

SELECT   dept_id,                              -- 5. compute columns
         ROUND(AVG(salary), 0) AS avg_sal      --    alias avg_sal is born here
FROM     employees                             -- 1. read table
WHERE    hire_date >= '2018-01-01'             -- 2. keep rows hired from 2018
GROUP BY dept_id                               -- 3. one group per department
HAVING   COUNT(*) >= 2                         -- 4. keep groups with 2+ rows
ORDER BY avg_sal DESC                          -- 7. alias is allowed here
LIMIT    2;                                    -- 8. top two groups
 dept_id | avg_sal
---------+---------
       1 |  125000
       2 |  117500
(2 rows)

Check by hand. WHERE keeps nine people hired from 2018 (Meera, Vikram and Ananya drop out). GROUP BY makes three groups: department 1 (Priya, Rahul, Arjun, Divya, average 125,000), department 2 (Karan, Neha, average 117,500) and department 3 (Sunita, Farhan, Isha, average 80,000). HAVING keeps all three (each has two or more rows), ORDER BY sorts them by the alias and LIMIT 2 cuts off department 3.

QuestionAnswer from the order
Why can't I use a SELECT alias in WHERE? WHERE (step 2) runs before SELECT (step 5) creates the alias. Repeat the expression, or use a subquery/CTE.
Why can I use it in ORDER BY?ORDER BY (step 7) runs after SELECT.
Why can HAVING use COUNT(*) but WHERE cannot? Groups exist only after step 3.
Why does LIMIT need ORDER BY to be meaningful? It cuts the result at step 8, after sorting. Without a sort, "the first rows" is arbitrary.
Why can't I filter on a window function in WHERE? Windows are computed in step 5, after WHERE. Wrap the query in a subquery or CTE and filter outside.
Why can a LEFT JOIN's ON and WHERE give different results?ON is part of step 1 (builds the rows, keeps unmatched left rows); WHERE (step 2) then deletes rows, including those NULL-extended ones.

Where PostgreSQL and MySQL are more relaxed

Both databases accept a select alias in GROUP BY, and MySQL also in HAVING. This is a convenience, not standard SQL, and it does not extend to WHERE. Do not rely on it if the query must be portable.

Rule: a clause can only use what earlier steps produced

If an error says a column, alias or aggregate "does not exist" or "is not allowed", find the step where the thing is created and the step where you used it. Fix the order: repeat the expression, move it into a subquery, or move the condition to the right clause.

Tip: build queries in this order too

Write FROM and JOIN first, then WHERE, then GROUP BY, then the select list. Check the rows after each step. Many people start from the select list and end up confused.

Interview angle

"In which order does SQL execute a query?" Name all eight steps and explain the alias rule. It is also the clean way to answer "WHERE vs HAVING". The optional follow-up is that the optimizer may physically reorder work but must keep the logical result.

Joins: INNER, LEFT, RIGHT and FULL

A join combines rows from two tables where a condition matches, usually "foreign key equals primary key". INNER JOIN keeps only the matches; LEFT, RIGHT and FULL joins also keep the rows that found no partner and fill the gap with NULL.

Why joins exist

Good database design stores each fact once: an employee row holds dept_id = 1, and only the departments table knows that 1 means Engineering in Bengaluru. To show "Priya works in Engineering" you must put the two tables back together at query time. That is a join. Joins are the reason relational databases split data into tables without losing anything, and they are the single most tested SQL topic in interviews.

How an inner join works

Conceptually, the database takes every row of the left table and looks for rows of the right table where the ON condition is true. Each matching pair becomes one output row. Rows without a partner disappear. (Internally the database picks a smart method, such as a hash join or an index lookup. The result is the same as this simple picture.)

SELECT e.first_name, d.name AS department
FROM employees AS e
INNER JOIN departments AS d ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
ORDER BY d.dept_id, e.emp_id;
 first_name | department
------------+-------------
 Vikram     | Engineering
 Priya      | Engineering
 Rahul      | Engineering
 Arjun      | Engineering
 Divya      | Engineering
 Ananya     | Sales
 Karan      | Sales
 Neha       | Sales
 Sunita     | HR
 Farhan     | HR
 Isha       | HR
(11 rows)

Twelve employees went in but only eleven came out: Meera has dept_id = NULL, and NULL equals nothing, so she has no partner. Marketing is missing too, since nobody works there. JOIN alone means INNER JOIN. Use table aliases (e, d) and qualify every column; without them, a column present in both tables fails with ERROR: column reference "dept_id" is ambiguous.

The four types side by side

LEFT JOIN

A left outer join (OUTER is optional) keeps every row of the left table. When there is no match, the right table's columns are NULL. Use it when the left table is the "main" thing and the right table is optional information.

SELECT e.first_name, d.name AS department
FROM employees AS e
LEFT JOIN departments AS d ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
ORDER BY e.emp_id;
 first_name | department
------------+-------------
 Meera      | (null)
 Vikram     | Engineering
 Ananya     | Sales
 Sunita     | HR
 Priya      | Engineering
 Rahul      | Engineering
 Arjun      | Engineering
 Divya      | Engineering
 Karan      | Sales
 Neha       | Sales
 Farhan     | HR
 Isha       | HR
(12 rows)

Meera is back, with a NULL department. A left join always returns at least as many rows as the left table has.

RIGHT JOIN

A right join is a left join seen in a mirror: all rows of the right table are kept. Departments, including empty ones:

SELECT d.name AS department, e.first_name
FROM employees AS e
RIGHT JOIN departments AS d ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
ORDER BY d.dept_id, e.emp_id;
  department  | first_name
-------------+------------
 Engineering | Vikram
 Engineering | Priya
 Engineering | Rahul
 Engineering | Arjun
 Engineering | Divya
 Sales       | Ananya
 Sales       | Karan
 Sales       | Neha
 HR          | Sunita
 HR          | Farhan
 HR          | Isha
 Marketing   | (null)
(12 rows)

Now Marketing appears with no employee, and Meera is gone. Most teams never write RIGHT JOIN: swap the table order and use LEFT JOIN, so every join in a query reads in the same direction. The query above equals FROM departments d LEFT JOIN employees e ON e.dept_id = d.dept_id.

FULL JOIN

A full outer join keeps unmatched rows from both sides: Meera (no department) and Marketing (no employees).

SELECT e.first_name, d.name AS department
FROM employees AS e
FULL JOIN departments AS d ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
ORDER BY d.dept_id, e.emp_id;
 first_name | department
------------+-------------
 Vikram     | Engineering
 Priya      | Engineering
 Rahul      | Engineering
 Arjun      | Engineering
 Divya      | Engineering
 Ananya     | Sales
 Karan      | Sales
 Neha       | Sales
 Sunita     | HR
 Farhan     | HR
 Isha       | HR
 (null)     | Marketing
 Meera      | (null)
(13 rows)

13 = 11 matches + 1 employee without department + 1 department without employees. MySQL has no FULL JOIN. Simulate it with LEFT JOIN … UNION ALL … RIGHT JOIN … WHERE left.id IS NULL, or UNION of a left and a right join. Full joins are used for reconciliation: "what is in system A but not B, and the reverse".

Summary

JoinRows keptEmployees ⨝ departmentsTypical use
INNERmatches only11most reports
LEFTall left + matches12"all customers, with their orders if any"
RIGHTall right + matches12rewrite as LEFT
FULLeverything13comparing two data sets

Joining on more than equality

The ON condition can be any boolean expression, not only a foreign key. Several conditions are joined with AND, and a range join is legal (ON o.order_date BETWEEN p.start_date AND p.end_date). A join on a foreign key is by far the most common, and gets an index (Module 5).

Rule: pick the join type by asking "which rows must survive?"

If a row without a partner must still appear, the table that holds that row is on the preserved side of an outer join. If it must disappear, use an inner join. Wrong choice = missing rows (inner when you needed outer) or extra NULL rows.

Mistake: forgetting the ON condition

FROM employees e, departments d with no WHERE (the old comma style) or a typo in ON produces every combination: 12 × 4 = 48 rows. A query that returns "too many rows" almost always has a missing or wrong join condition. Always use explicit JOIN … ON.

Edge cases

  • NULL never joins to NULL. Two NULL keys do not match each other. Use ON a.x IS NOT DISTINCT FROM b.x if you want them to.
  • If the right side has several matches, the left row repeats once per match (one department row for each of its employees). That is correct, and a source of trouble when you aggregate afterwards (Join traps).
  • Joining INT to VARCHAR fails in PostgreSQL (operator does not exist: integer = character varying); cast one side.

Interview angle

Draw the Venn diagram, then give rows from real tables: "inner returns 11, left 12, full 13 because of Meera and Marketing". Be ready for "difference between inner and outer join", "what does a left join return when there is no match" (NULLs), and "why do you avoid right join" (readability, same as swapped left).

Self joins, cross joins and joining three or more tables

A self join joins a table to itself (employee to manager), a cross join pairs every row with every row (all combinations), and a query can chain as many joins as it needs. USING shortens equal-name joins; avoid NATURAL JOIN.

Self join: employee and manager

A table can reference itself: employees.manager_id holds the emp_id of another employee. To show both names in one row, use the table twice with two different aliases. Think of them as two copies: e is the employee, m is the manager.

SELECT e.emp_id,
       e.first_name AS employee,
       m.first_name AS manager
FROM employees AS e
LEFT JOIN employees AS m ON m.emp_id = e.manager_id
ORDER BY e.emp_id;
 emp_id | employee | manager
--------+----------+---------
      1 | Meera    | (null)
      2 | Vikram   | Meera
      3 | Ananya   | Meera
      4 | Sunita   | Meera
      5 | Priya    | Vikram
      6 | Rahul    | Priya
      7 | Arjun    | Priya
      8 | Divya    | Priya
      9 | Karan    | Ananya
     10 | Neha     | Ananya
     11 | Farhan   | Sunita
     12 | Isha     | Sunita
(12 rows)

It is a LEFT JOIN because the CEO has no manager; an inner join would drop Meera. Reverse the direction to count direct reports:

SELECT m.first_name AS manager, COUNT(*) AS direct_reports
FROM employees AS e
JOIN employees AS m ON m.emp_id = e.manager_id
GROUP BY m.emp_id, m.first_name
ORDER BY direct_reports DESC, manager;
 manager | direct_reports
---------+----------------
 Meera   |              3
 Priya   |              3
 Ananya  |              2
 Sunita  |              2
 Vikram  |              1
(5 rows)

A self join also finds pairs inside one group. The condition a.emp_id < b.emp_id keeps each pair once and removes pairs of a person with themselves:

SELECT a.first_name AS person_1, b.first_name AS person_2
FROM employees AS a
JOIN employees AS b ON a.dept_id = b.dept_id AND a.emp_id < b.emp_id
WHERE a.dept_id = 3
ORDER BY a.emp_id, b.emp_id;
 person_1 | person_2
----------+----------
 Sunita   | Farhan
 Sunita   | Isha
 Farhan   | Isha
(3 rows)

Self joins only follow one level. For the whole chain of command (manager of manager of …) you need a recursive query: Recursive CTEs.

Cross join: every combination

A cross join has no ON. It returns the Cartesian product: every row of A with every row of B, so A × B rows. That is a bug when it happens by accident and a tool when it is intended: generating a grid of combinations (every product with every size, every employee with every month for a leave plan, every day with every department for a report that shows zeros).

SELECT d.name AS department, q.quarter
FROM departments AS d
CROSS JOIN (VALUES ('Q1'), ('Q2')) AS q(quarter)
WHERE d.dept_id <= 2
ORDER BY d.dept_id, q.quarter;
 department | quarter
-------------+---------
 Engineering | Q1
 Engineering | Q2
 Sales       | Q1
 Sales       | Q2
(4 rows)

The size explodes: employees CROSS JOIN departments is 12 × 4 = 48 rows; two tables of a million rows give a trillion. A forgotten join condition is a cross join in disguise. The comma form FROM a, b is also a cross join, which is one more reason to write joins explicitly.

Joining three or more tables

Joins chain: each JOIN adds one table to the result built so far. Follow the foreign keys in the diagram of the sample database. Customer, order, line and product for the first two orders:

SELECT c.name AS customer, o.order_id, o.order_date,
       p.name AS product, oi.quantity, oi.unit_price
FROM customers   AS c
JOIN orders      AS o  ON o.customer_id = c.customer_id
JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id   = o.order_id
JOIN products    AS p  ON p.product_id  = oi.product_id
WHERE o.order_id <= 2
ORDER BY o.order_id, p.product_id;
  customer   | order_id | order_date |       product       | quantity | unit_price
-------------+----------+------------+---------------------+----------+------------
 Aarav Shah  |        1 | 2026-01-05 | Wireless Mouse      |        1 |     799.00
 Aarav Shah  |        1 | 2026-01-05 | Notebook A5         |        3 |     120.00
 Diya Kapoor |        2 | 2026-01-18 | Mechanical Keyboard |        1 |    3499.00
 Diya Kapoor |        2 | 2026-01-18 | USB-C Cable         |        2 |     299.00
(4 rows)

Tips: join in a logical chain, put each ON next to its join, and mix join types only on purpose. If you use LEFT JOIN in the middle, later joins on its columns should also be LEFT JOIN, otherwise an inner join silently removes the NULL-extended rows again. A many-to-many link goes through the junction table: employees to projects needs employee_projects in the middle.

SELECT p.name AS project, e.first_name, ep.role
FROM projects AS p
JOIN employee_projects AS ep ON ep.project_id = p.project_id
JOIN employees         AS e  ON e.emp_id      = ep.emp_id
WHERE p.project_id = 1
ORDER BY ep.hours_per_week DESC;
      project      | first_name |   role
-------------------+------------+-----------
 Payments Platform | Rahul      | Developer
 Payments Platform | Priya      | Lead
 Payments Platform | Arjun      | Developer
(3 rows)

ON, USING and NATURAL JOIN

USING (col) is shorthand when both tables have a column with the same name: JOIN departments d USING (dept_id) equals ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id, and the result shows dept_id once. It does not work when names differ (manager_id to emp_id).

NATURAL JOIN joins on all columns that have the same name, automatically. Avoid it: it changes meaning when someone adds a column. See what happens when departments and projects, which both have dept_id and name, are joined naturally:

SELECT COUNT(*) FROM departments NATURAL JOIN projects;      -- joins on dept_id AND name
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM departments d JOIN projects p USING (dept_id);
 count
-------
     0
(1 row)

 count
-------
     5
(1 row)

The natural join compares department names with project names and finds nothing; the explicit join finds all 5 projects.

Convention: alias well, qualify always

Short, meaningful aliases (e and m for employee and manager, not t1 and t2), and every column written as alias.column. In a self join the aliases are the only way to tell the two copies apart.

Mistake: inner join in a self join drops the top of the tree

The CEO has no manager, so JOIN employees m removes her. Use LEFT JOIN whenever "no parent" is a valid state, and the same goes for any optional foreign key.

Edge cases

  • Cross join with an empty table returns zero rows (A × 0 = 0).
  • A self join with ON a.dept_id = b.dept_id and no inequality also pairs each person with themselves and returns both (A,B) and (B,A).
  • In MySQL CROSS JOIN and JOIN without ON are the same thing; in PostgreSQL JOIN without ON is a syntax error.

Interview angle

"Show each employee with the manager's name" is the standard self-join question; the follow-up is "what about the CEO?" (left join). "When would you use a cross join?" (generating combinations, calendars with zeros) and "what is the result size of A cross join B?" (|A| × |B|).

Join traps: LEFT JOIN filters, anti-joins, NOT IN and row multiplication

Four mistakes cause most wrong join results: a WHERE that cancels a LEFT JOIN, counting with COUNT(*) after an outer join, NOT IN meeting a NULL, and a join that multiplies rows before you sum.

Trap 1: a WHERE condition turns LEFT JOIN into INNER JOIN

Goal: list every customer with their delivered orders; customers with none should still appear. Compare two versions:

Wrong: filter in WHERE

SELECT c.name, o.order_id
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.customer_id
WHERE o.status = 'DELIVERED'
ORDER BY c.customer_id, o.order_id;

Right: filter in ON

SELECT c.name, o.order_id
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.customer_id
                  AND o.status = 'DELIVERED'
ORDER BY c.customer_id, o.order_id;
    name     | order_id
-------------+----------
 Aarav Shah  |        1
 Aarav Shah  |        3
 Diya Kapoor |        2
 Kabir Singh |        6
 Ishita Joshi |       5
(5 rows)
     name     | order_id
--------------+----------
 Aarav Shah   |        1
 Aarav Shah   |        3
 Diya Kapoor  |        2
 Kabir Singh  |        6
 Ishita Joshi |        5
 Rohan Das    |   (null)
 Sara Khan    |   (null)
 Tanvi Desai  |   (null)
(8 rows)

Why? The join builds the rows first: Rohan, Sara and Tanvi (and orders that are not delivered) appear with o.status = NULL. Then WHERE o.status = 'DELIVERED' removes every row where the status is NULL or something else, including the NULL-extended rows. The left join is now an inner join in disguise. Rule: a condition on the optional (right) table belongs in ON; conditions on the preserved (left) table belong in WHERE. The exception is a deliberate WHERE o.order_id IS NULL, the anti-join below.

Trap 2: counting after an outer join

Headcount per department including empty ones:

SELECT d.name,
       COUNT(*)        AS wrong_count,
       COUNT(e.emp_id) AS headcount
FROM departments d
LEFT JOIN employees e ON e.dept_id = d.dept_id
GROUP BY d.dept_id, d.name
ORDER BY d.dept_id;
    name     | wrong_count | headcount
-------------+-------------+-----------
 Engineering |           5 |         5
 Sales       |           3 |         3
 HR          |           3 |         3
 Marketing   |           1 |         0
(4 rows)

Marketing has one row in the join result (the NULL-extended one), so COUNT(*) says 1. Count a column of the optional table; COUNT(e.emp_id) skips NULLs and gives 0. The same applies per customer: COUNT(o.order_id) gives Sara and Tanvi 0 orders.

Anti-joins: rows with no match

"Customers who never ordered" is an anti-join. There are three ways, and they should give the same answer when there are no NULLs:

-- 1. LEFT JOIN ... IS NULL
SELECT c.customer_id, c.name
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.customer_id
WHERE o.order_id IS NULL;

-- 2. NOT EXISTS (usually the safest)
SELECT c.customer_id, c.name
FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.customer_id);

-- 3. NOT IN (works only while the subquery has no NULLs)
SELECT customer_id, name
FROM customers
WHERE customer_id NOT IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders);
 customer_id |    name
-------------+-------------
           6 | Sara Khan
           7 | Tanvi Desai
(2 rows)

With the LEFT JOIN … IS NULL form, test a column that cannot be NULL in a real row, such as the right table's primary key (o.order_id). PostgreSQL turns both the first and second form into the same anti join plan, so choose for clarity. Many teams prefer NOT EXISTS.

Trap 3: NOT IN and NULL

Which employees are not managers? manager_id contains NULL (Meera has no manager), and that breaks NOT IN.

Wrong: returns nothing

SELECT first_name FROM employees
WHERE emp_id NOT IN (SELECT manager_id FROM employees);
-- (0 rows)

Right: NOT EXISTS (or filter NULLs)

SELECT e.first_name FROM employees e
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM employees x
                  WHERE x.manager_id = e.emp_id)
ORDER BY e.emp_id;
 first_name
------------
 Rahul
 Arjun
 Divya
 Karan
 Neha
 Farhan
 Isha
(7 rows)

The reason: 6 NOT IN (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, NULL) expands to 6 <> 1 AND … AND 6 <> NULL, and the last test is unknown, so the whole condition can never be true (see NULL and three-valued logic). The other fix is WHERE manager_id IS NOT NULL inside the subquery.

Trap 4: row multiplication (fan-out)

A join with a one-to-many relationship repeats the "one" side. If you then sum a column of the "one" side, it is counted many times. Total salary of the people who work on projects:

SELECT SUM(e.salary) AS wrong_total
FROM employees e
JOIN employee_projects ep ON ep.emp_id = e.emp_id;

SELECT SUM(salary) AS correct_total
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id IN (SELECT emp_id FROM employee_projects);
 wrong_total
-------------
  1330000.00
(1 row)

 correct_total
---------------
    1070000.00
(1 row)

Priya and Arjun are on two projects each, so their salaries were added twice (150,000 + 110,000 = 260,000 extra). Spot it by comparing row counts: 9 people, 11 joined rows. Fixes: aggregate before joining (in a subquery or CTE), use EXISTS/IN when you only need to test existence, or SUM(DISTINCT …) carefully (it also merges different people with equal salaries, so it is rarely right). The general rule is to know the cardinality of each join: one-to-one, one-to-many or many-to-many.

Rule: for a LEFT JOIN, filter the right table in ON and count a right-table column

Put conditions about the optional side in ON. Use COUNT(right.id), never COUNT(*). Use IS NULL on the right side only to find non-matches.

Tip: check row counts at every step

Run SELECT COUNT(*) after each added join. If the count jumps unexpectedly, a join is multiplying rows. Compare COUNT(*) with COUNT(DISTINCT e.emp_id) to see the duplication.

In real projects

A Spring Data JPA query SELECT d FROM Department d LEFT JOIN d.employees e WHERE e.salary > 1 has the same trap as Trap 1. And Hibernate's JOIN FETCH of a collection repeats the parent row once per child, which is the fan-out of Trap 4. Understanding the SQL makes both easy to debug.

Interview angle

"Find customers who never ordered" (three ways) and "why does NOT IN return no rows?" are standard. A strong answer names the NULL rule, shows NOT EXISTS, and mentions the left-join trap with WHERE vs ON. For fan-out, say "I check the join cardinality and aggregate before joining".

Subqueries: scalar, IN, derived tables and ANY/ALL

A subquery is a SELECT inside another statement, in parentheses. It can return one value (scalar), a list (for IN) or a whole table (a derived table in FROM). Use it when one query needs the answer of another.

Why and where

"Employees who earn more than the company average" needs two steps: compute the average, then compare. With a subquery you write both in one statement and the database runs the inner part first (or rewrites it into a join). A subquery can sit in three places:

PositionMust returnExample use
WHERE / HAVINGone value, or one column for INcompare with an average
FROMany table (needs an alias)aggregate, then join the result
SELECT listexactly one valuea per-row lookup or count

Scalar subquery

A scalar subquery returns one row with one column, and can be used wherever a single value fits.

SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
WHERE salary > (SELECT AVG(salary) FROM employees)
ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Meera      | 300000.00
 Vikram     | 220000.00
 Karan      | 160000.00
 Ananya     | 150000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00
(6 rows)

The inner query does not depend on the outer one, so it runs once. If it returns two or more rows where one value is expected, the statement fails:

SELECT first_name FROM employees
WHERE salary = (SELECT salary FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 1);
-- ERROR:  more than one row returned by a subquery used as an expression

MySQL says ERROR 1242 (21000): Subquery returns more than 1 row. Fix by using an aggregate (MAX(salary)), IN, or LIMIT 1 with an ORDER BY when you truly want one row. If the subquery returns no row, the scalar value is NULL, and the comparison matches nothing. If it returns two columns you get ERROR: subquery has too many columns.

Subquery with IN

IN (subquery) tests whether a value appears in the single column the subquery returns. Products that were sold at least once:

SELECT product_id, name
FROM products
WHERE product_id IN (SELECT product_id FROM order_items)
ORDER BY product_id;
 product_id |        name
------------+---------------------
          1 | Wireless Mouse
          2 | Mechanical Keyboard
          3 | USB-C Cable
          4 | Notebook A5
          5 | Gel Pen Pack
          6 | Water Bottle
(6 rows)

The Desk Lamp (id 7) was never sold. The inner query returns product ids with duplicates, which does not matter: IN only asks "is it there?" and never multiplies the outer rows (unlike a join). The negated form NOT IN has the NULL trap described in Join traps.

Derived tables: a subquery in FROM

A subquery in FROM acts as a temporary table for this one statement. It must have an alias (required by MySQL, and by PostgreSQL before version 16). It is the usual way to aggregate first and then join or filter on the result. Employees paid above their own department's average:

SELECT e.first_name, e.salary, d.avg_salary
FROM employees AS e
JOIN (SELECT dept_id, ROUND(AVG(salary), 2) AS avg_salary
      FROM employees
      GROUP BY dept_id) AS d ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
WHERE e.salary > d.avg_salary
ORDER BY e.emp_id;
 first_name |  salary   | avg_salary
------------+-----------+------------
 Vikram     | 220000.00 |  144000.00
 Ananya     | 150000.00 |  128333.33
 Sunita     | 120000.00 |   80000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00 |  144000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00 |  144000.00
 Karan      | 160000.00 |  128333.33
(6 rows)

Meera is missing: her dept_id is NULL, so she matches no group. This is also where the logical order bites: you cannot compare a salary with AVG(salary) in the same query level, because the aggregate does not exist at WHERE time, but you can compare with a column of a derived table that was already computed. Nested derived tables get hard to read; CTEs are the cleaner way to write them.

Subquery in the SELECT list

A scalar subquery in the select list computes one extra value per row, for example the share of the company payroll:

SELECT first_name,
       salary,
       ROUND(100 * salary / (SELECT SUM(salary) FROM employees), 1) AS pct_of_payroll
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 3
ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name |  salary   | pct_of_payroll
------------+-----------+----------------
 Sunita     | 120000.00 |            7.3
 Farhan     |  65000.00 |            4.0
 Isha       |  55000.00 |            3.3
(3 rows)

(Window functions do this more elegantly: percent of total.)

ANY and ALL

ANY (same as SOME) is true if the comparison holds for at least one value of the subquery; ALL if it holds for every value. = ANY (…) is the same as IN. Employees who earn more than everyone in Sales:

SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
WHERE salary > ALL (SELECT salary FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 2)
ORDER BY salary DESC;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Meera      | 300000.00
 Vikram     | 220000.00
(2 rows)

salary > ALL (…) equals salary > (SELECT MAX(…)); the second form is easier to read, so ALL and ANY are rare in practice. Beware: if the subquery returns an empty set, ALL is true for every row and ANY is false. A NULL inside the list makes ALL unknown.

Subquery, join or CTE?

The optimizer often rewrites a subquery into a join, so do not choose for speed alone. Choose for readability: a scalar comparison is clearest as a subquery, a lookup of columns is clearest as a join, and a multi-step calculation is clearest as a CTE.

Rule: know how many rows and columns a subquery returns

After =, >, <: one row, one column. After IN: one column, any number of rows. In FROM: anything, with an alias. In SELECT: one row, one column.

Mistake: using = where IN is needed

Wrong: breaks as soon as 2 rows come back

WHERE dept_id = (SELECT dept_id FROM departments WHERE location = 'Pune')

Right: IN accepts any number of rows

WHERE dept_id IN (SELECT dept_id FROM departments WHERE location = 'Pune')

The wrong version works today because only one department is in Pune, and fails the day a second one opens. Code that works only by luck is a bug waiting.

Edge cases

  • MySQL (before 8.0.14 in many cases) could not use LIMIT inside an IN subquery (This version of MySQL doesn't yet support 'LIMIT & IN/ALL/ANY/SOME subquery'); wrap it in a derived table.
  • MySQL does not let you UPDATE or DELETE a table while selecting from the same table in a subquery (ERROR 1093); wrap the subquery in a derived table or use a join.
  • The subquery's columns are limited to its own FROM unless it is correlated (next article).

Interview angle

Likely questions: "employees earning more than the average", "what error does a scalar subquery with two rows give", "does a derived table need an alias" and "subquery vs join, which is faster" (usually the same plan; measure with EXPLAIN).

Correlated subqueries and EXISTS

A correlated subquery uses a column of the outer query, so conceptually it runs once per outer row. EXISTS is the most useful correlated test: it asks "does at least one row match?" and stops at the first.

How a correlated subquery runs

In the earlier subqueries, the inner query stood alone. A correlated one refers to the outer row:

SELECT e.first_name, e.salary
FROM employees AS e
WHERE e.salary > (SELECT AVG(x.salary)
                  FROM employees AS x
                  WHERE x.dept_id = e.dept_id)      -- refers to the outer row e
ORDER BY e.emp_id;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Vikram     | 220000.00
 Ananya     | 150000.00
 Sunita     | 120000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00
 Karan      | 160000.00
(6 rows)
  1. Take the first outer row (Meera, dept_id = NULL).
  2. Run the inner query with e.dept_id filled in from that row. Here nothing matches (NULL equals nothing), so AVG is NULL, the comparison is unknown, and Meera is dropped.
  3. Take the next outer row (Vikram, dept 1), average department 1 again (144000), compare, keep.
  4. Repeat for every row.

This "once per row" picture is the meaning. The optimizer often turns it into a join or computes each department average once. But sometimes it cannot, and a correlated subquery on a big table is then slow: 1 million outer rows means 1 million inner runs. The same result via a derived table (previous article) computes the averages once.

Correlated subquery in the SELECT list

SELECT d.name,
       (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM employees e WHERE e.dept_id = d.dept_id) AS headcount
FROM departments AS d
ORDER BY d.dept_id;
    name     | headcount
-------------+-----------
 Engineering |         5
 Sales       |         3
 HR          |         3
 Marketing   |         0
(4 rows)

Marketing shows 0 because a scalar COUNT over no rows is 0. This reads simply, but for big tables prefer LEFT JOIN … GROUP BY.

EXISTS

EXISTS (subquery) is true when the subquery returns at least one row, and false when it returns none. What the subquery selects does not matter (convention: SELECT 1), the database only checks whether a row exists and stops at the first match.

SELECT c.customer_id, c.name
FROM customers AS c
WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1
              FROM orders AS o
              WHERE o.customer_id = c.customer_id)
ORDER BY c.customer_id;
 customer_id |     name
-------------+--------------
           1 | Aarav Shah
           2 | Diya Kapoor
           3 | Kabir Singh
           4 | Ishita Joshi
           5 | Rohan Das
(5 rows)

NOT EXISTS gives the opposite (customers 6 and 7), and is the safest anti-join (Join traps). Because it is a yes/no test, EXISTS never multiplies rows and is NULL-safe: it only looks at whether a row came back.

IN vs EXISTS vs JOIN

The same question, "customers with orders", written three ways:

-- IN
SELECT name FROM customers WHERE customer_id IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders);
-- EXISTS
SELECT name FROM customers c WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.customer_id);
-- JOIN (needs DISTINCT, otherwise one row per order)
SELECT DISTINCT c.name FROM customers c JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.customer_id;
INEXISTSJOIN
Rows returned5 customers5 customers10 rows (one per order) unless DISTINCT
Columns of the inner tablenot availablenot availableavailable
Negated formNOT IN: breaks on NULLNOT EXISTS: safeLEFT JOIN … IS NULL
Best fora short list, a simple filter"has any" / "has none" testswhen you need the other table's columns

On performance: modern PostgreSQL and MySQL 8 turn IN and EXISTS into a semi join and usually pick the same plan, so the old advice "EXISTS is always faster" is outdated. What does matter is an index on the inner table's join column (orders.customer_id). Verify with EXPLAIN (Module 5).

More EXISTS patterns

SELECT first_name FROM employees e
WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM employee_projects ep WHERE ep.emp_id = e.emp_id);

SELECT d.name FROM departments d
WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM projects p
              WHERE p.dept_id = d.dept_id AND p.end_date IS NULL);

The first returns 9 people (Sunita, Ananya, Priya, Rahul, Arjun, Divya, Karan, Neha, Farhan). The second returns Engineering, HR and Marketing; the Sales project has ended. Also useful: "WHERE NOT EXISTS (…) for the opposite", and the "relational division" question "customers who bought all products of a category", written as a double NOT EXISTS.

Rule: qualify every column inside a correlated subquery

Inside (SELECT … FROM employees x WHERE x.dept_id = e.dept_id) use different aliases for the outer and inner copies. If you leave a name unqualified, SQL resolves it to the innermost table first and the "correlation" quietly becomes x.dept_id = x.dept_id, which is always true.

Mistake: a correlated subquery in SELECT on a large table

A per-row subquery over a million-row table without an index on the inner join column is a slow query. Rewrite it as a join with GROUP BY, or a window function. If you see N+1 queries from the application, that is the same idea at another level: Hibernate's lazy loading runs one query per parent.

Edge cases

  • EXISTS is true even if the subquery's selected value is NULL; only the existence of a row counts. SELECT NULL, SELECT 1 and SELECT * behave alike.
  • A correlated subquery can reference only the enclosing query levels, not sibling tables in the outer FROM (use LATERAL in PostgreSQL for that).

Interview angle

"What is a correlated subquery?" (depends on the outer row, conceptually runs per row.) "IN vs EXISTS?" (same result for positive tests, EXISTS is NULL-safe when negated; planners treat both as semi joins.) "Customers without orders", "departments with no employees" and "employees who earn more than their department average" are the usual tasks.

UNION, UNION ALL, INTERSECT and EXCEPT

Set operators stack the results of two queries on top of each other. UNION removes duplicates, UNION ALL keeps them and is faster, INTERSECT keeps rows found in both, and EXCEPT keeps rows found only in the first.

Joins go sideways, set operations go down

A join adds columns from another table. A set operation adds rows from another query. Both queries must produce the same number of columns, with compatible types in the same order. The column names of the result come from the first query.

SELECT location AS city FROM departments
UNION
SELECT city FROM customers
ORDER BY city;
   city
-----------
 Ahmedabad
 Bengaluru
 Delhi
 Kolkata
 Mumbai
 Pune
(6 rows)

UNION vs UNION ALL

The departments give 4 cities and the customers give 7 (Mumbai twice), so there are 11 rows in total. UNION sorts or hashes the result to remove duplicates and returns 6 distinct cities. UNION ALL simply appends and returns all 11:

SELECT COUNT(*) FROM (
    SELECT location AS city FROM departments
    UNION ALL
    SELECT city FROM customers
) AS all_cities;
 count
-------
    11
(1 row)
UNIONUNION ALL
Duplicatesremoved (whole rows)kept
Costextra sort or hash stepnone; just appends
Use whenyou need a distinct listsources cannot overlap, or you want every row

Default to UNION ALL when you know the inputs are disjoint (this month's orders plus last month's) and use UNION only when you really need de-duplication. Note that UNION compares whole rows, so it also merges two different people who happen to have the same selected values.

A practical UNION ALL

A single contact list of HR employees and Mumbai customers, with a label for the source:

SELECT first_name || ' ' || last_name AS name, email, 'employee' AS kind
FROM employees WHERE dept_id = 3
UNION ALL
SELECT name, email, 'customer'
FROM customers WHERE city = 'Mumbai'
ORDER BY kind, name;
      name       |           email            |   kind
-----------------+----------------------------+----------
 Aarav Shah      | aarav.shah@example.com     | customer
 Sara Khan       | sara.khan@example.com      | customer
 Farhan Shaikh   | farhan.shaikh@example.com  | employee
 Isha Banerjee   | isha.banerjee@example.com  | employee
 Sunita Patel    | sunita.patel@example.com   | employee
(5 rows)

ORDER BY goes once, at the very end, and applies to the combined result; it can only use the column names (or positions) of the output. To sort or limit one part, wrap it in parentheses or a subquery.

INTERSECT and EXCEPT

SELECT location FROM departments
INTERSECT
SELECT city FROM customers
ORDER BY 1;

SELECT city FROM customers
EXCEPT
SELECT location FROM departments
ORDER BY 1;
 location
-----------
 Bengaluru
 Delhi
 Mumbai
 Pune
(4 rows)

   city
-----------
 Ahmedabad
 Kolkata
(2 rows)

INTERSECT is "in A and in B"; EXCEPT is "in A but not in B" (Oracle calls it MINUS). Order matters for EXCEPT: A except B differs from B except A. Both remove duplicates; PostgreSQL also has INTERSECT ALL and EXCEPT ALL. Unlike NOT IN, EXCEPT treats NULLs as equal, so it is safe with NULLs. The same results can be written with IN, NOT EXISTS or joins; set operators read more like the question. Precedence: INTERSECT binds tighter than UNION and EXCEPT; use parentheses.

MySQL support

UNION and UNION ALL work everywhere. MySQL added INTERSECT and EXCEPT only in version 8.0.31; before that you must write them with joins or NOT EXISTS.

Rule: same column count, compatible types, same order

SELECT dept_id FROM departments UNION SELECT name, city FROM customers;
ERROR:  each UNION query must have the same number of columns

SELECT dept_id FROM departments UNION SELECT name FROM customers;
ERROR:  UNION types integer and character varying cannot be matched

Cast when needed: SELECT dept_id::TEXT …. Columns are matched by position, not name, so a swapped order silently mixes the data.

Mistake: UNION where UNION ALL is meant

Using UNION by habit costs a sort of the whole result and can quietly drop legitimate duplicate rows (two orders of the same amount on the same day). Write UNION ALL unless you want de-duplication.

Edge cases

  • NULLs count as equal for removing duplicates in UNION, INTERSECT and EXCEPT (different from =).
  • You cannot use ORDER BY on a column that is not in the output of a set operation.
  • A literal such as NULL in the first query takes its type from the other query in PostgreSQL; add a cast if you get a type error.

Interview angle

"UNION vs UNION ALL" (duplicates, speed), "UNION vs JOIN" (rows vs columns), "how do you find rows in table A that are not in table B" (EXCEPT, NOT EXISTS, LEFT JOIN … IS NULL), and "what rules must the two queries follow" (column count, types, position).

CASE WHEN: conditional logic in SQL

CASE is SQL's if/else: it returns the value of the first branch whose condition is true. It works in SELECT, WHERE, ORDER BY, GROUP BY, inside aggregates and in UPDATE.

CASE is an expression, not a statement

Beginners from Java expect CASE to be like an if statement that runs code. It is not. It is an expression: it produces one value, like a + b does. That is why you can put it anywhere a value is allowed: a SELECT column, a WHERE condition, an argument of SUM, a sort key, the right side of SET. In Java terms it is closer to the ternary operator cond ? x : y (or a switch expression) than to an if block.

  1. The database takes the first WHEN and evaluates its condition for the current row.
  2. If the condition is true, it returns the matching THEN value and stops. Later branches are not looked at.
  3. If it is false or unknown (NULL), it moves to the next WHEN.
  4. If every branch fails, it returns the ELSE value, or NULL when there is no ELSE.
FormShapeCompares withUse it when
SearchedCASE WHEN cond THEN x … ENDany condition ranges, several columns, IS NULL
SimpleCASE expr WHEN v THEN x … END= only mapping codes to labels

Searched CASE and simple CASE

The searched form tests any condition in each branch; the simple form compares one expression with several values (like Java's switch).

SELECT first_name, salary,
       CASE
           WHEN salary >= 200000 THEN 'Senior'
           WHEN salary >= 100000 THEN 'Mid'
           ELSE 'Junior'
       END AS band
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id <= 5
ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name |  salary   |  band
------------+-----------+--------
 Meera      | 300000.00 | Senior
 Vikram     | 220000.00 | Senior
 Ananya     | 150000.00 | Mid
 Sunita     | 120000.00 | Mid
 Priya      | 150000.00 | Mid
(5 rows)
SELECT order_id,
       CASE status
           WHEN 'PLACED'    THEN 'We received your order'
           WHEN 'SHIPPED'   THEN 'On the way'
           WHEN 'DELIVERED' THEN 'Delivered'
           ELSE 'Cancelled'
       END AS message
FROM orders;

Rules of evaluation: branches are tested top to bottom and the first true one wins, so order the conditions from most to least specific (Meera is 300000, which matches both the first and second condition; she gets 'Senior'). If nothing matches and there is no ELSE, the result is NULL. All THEN/ELSE values must have compatible types; mixing a number and text fails with ERROR: CASE types integer and text cannot be matched. In a simple CASE, the comparison is =, so WHEN NULL never matches; use a searched CASE WHEN x IS NULL.

CASE in GROUP BY and aggregates

To count people per band, compute the band in a derived table (or repeat the expression in GROUP BY):

SELECT band, COUNT(*) AS people
FROM (SELECT CASE WHEN salary >= 200000 THEN 'Senior'
                  WHEN salary >= 100000 THEN 'Mid'
                  ELSE 'Junior' END AS band
      FROM employees) AS t
GROUP BY band
ORDER BY band;
  band  | people
--------+--------
 Junior |      4
 Mid    |      6
 Senior |      2
(3 rows)

Inside an aggregate, CASE decides which rows count: SUM(CASE WHEN status = 'CANCELLED' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END). This is conditional aggregation, covered in its own article.

Several bands as columns

Put one CASE inside each aggregate and you get a ready cross-tab: employees per department, split by salary band. Each row counts in exactly one band because the ranges do not overlap.

SELECT dept_id,
       SUM(CASE WHEN salary >= 200000 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)                       AS senior,
       SUM(CASE WHEN salary >= 100000 AND salary < 200000 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS mid,
       SUM(CASE WHEN salary < 100000 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)                       AS junior
FROM employees
GROUP BY dept_id
ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id | senior | mid | junior
---------+--------+-----+--------
       1 |      1 |   3 |      1
       2 |      0 |   2 |      1
       3 |      0 |   1 |      2
  (null) |      1 |   0 |      0
(4 rows)

The column totals (2, 6, 4) match the band counts above. More about this pattern, including FILTER, is in Conditional aggregation.

Handling NULL with CASE

A searched CASE can test IS NULL, which is how you turn "missing" into a label. Grouping by that label needs the expression repeated, or the output position (PostgreSQL and MySQL both accept GROUP BY 1; the logical order that allows aliases is in Logical order of a query):

SELECT CASE WHEN phone IS NULL THEN 'missing' ELSE 'present' END AS phone_status,
       COUNT(*) AS customers
FROM customers
GROUP BY 1
ORDER BY 1;
 phone_status | customers
--------------+-----------
 missing      |         3
 present      |         4
(2 rows)

CASE in ORDER BY

Alphabetical order is rarely the business order. CASE lets you define your own:

SELECT order_id, status
FROM orders
ORDER BY CASE status
             WHEN 'PLACED'    THEN 1
             WHEN 'SHIPPED'   THEN 2
             WHEN 'DELIVERED' THEN 3
             ELSE 4
         END,
         order_id;
 order_id |  status
----------+-----------
        9 | PLACED
       10 | PLACED
        7 | SHIPPED
        8 | SHIPPED
        1 | DELIVERED
        2 | DELIVERED
        3 | DELIVERED
        5 | DELIVERED
        6 | DELIVERED
        4 | CANCELLED
(10 rows)

CASE in UPDATE

One statement can apply different changes to different rows, and it is atomic:

BEGIN;
UPDATE employees
SET salary = salary * CASE WHEN salary < 100000 THEN 1.10
                           WHEN salary < 200000 THEN 1.05
                           ELSE 1.00 END;
ROLLBACK;   -- practice only

MySQL vs PostgreSQL: UPDATE with several columns

PostgreSQL evaluates every right-hand side with the old row values, so SET a = b, b = a really swaps the two columns. MySQL applies assignments left to right and later ones see the new values, so the same statement leaves both columns equal to the old b. When you need a swap on MySQL, put the logic in one CASE on a single column or use a temporary value.

Guarding errors, and related functions

SELECT 10 / 0;                                          -- ERROR:  division by zero
SELECT CASE WHEN 0 = 0 THEN NULL ELSE 10 / 0 END;       -- NULL
SELECT 10 / NULLIF(0, 0);                               -- NULL: NULLIF turns 0 into NULL
FunctionMeaningEquivalent CASE
COALESCE(a, b, c)first non-NULL argumentCASE WHEN a IS NOT NULL THEN a WHEN b IS NOT NULL THEN b ELSE c END
NULLIF(a, b)NULL if a equals b, else aCASE WHEN a = b THEN NULL ELSE a END
GREATEST, LEASTlargest / smallest of several valueschain of branches
MySQL IF(c, x, y), IFNULL(a, b)MySQL shortcutsnot portable; use CASE, COALESCE

CASE and the vendor shortcuts

NeedStandard (works in both)Shortcut and where it exists
if / elseCASE WHEN c THEN x ELSE y ENDMySQL IF(c, x, y); SQL Server IIF
replace NULLCOALESCE(a, b)MySQL IFNULL; Oracle NVL
map code to labelsimple CASEOracle DECODE
custom sort orderORDER BY CASE …MySQL FIELD(status, …)

Interviewers sometimes ask for IF or DECODE. Name them, then say you prefer CASE because it is standard SQL and portable. A Spring Boot app that may change databases should keep to the standard forms.

Rule: every branch must produce the same kind of value

The database picks one result type for the whole CASE. THEN 1 … ELSE 'none' fails in PostgreSQL with ERROR: CASE types integer and text cannot be matched. Cast one side (CAST(n AS TEXT)) or return the same type everywhere. Mixing 1 and 1.5 is fine; the result becomes numeric.

CASE and indexes

A CASE around a column in WHERE or ORDER BY hides the column from a normal index, so large tables may be scanned and sorted fully. Prefer plain conditions in WHERE: (:status IS NULL OR status = :status) instead of a CASE that returns true or false. For a business sort order on a big table, store the rank in a column (or a small lookup table with a sort_order) and sort by that.

Mistake: comparing with NULL inside CASE

Wrong: never true

SELECT name,
       CASE phone WHEN NULL THEN 'missing' ELSE 'present' END AS s
FROM customers;      -- Diya shows 'present'

Right: IS NULL in a searched CASE

SELECT name,
       CASE WHEN phone IS NULL THEN 'missing' ELSE 'present' END AS s
FROM customers;

The simple form turns into phone = NULL, which is unknown, so the branch is skipped. Other slips in the same family: forgetting END (a syntax error near the next keyword) and writing ELSE before the last WHEN.

Convention: always write ELSE, and format one branch per line

An explicit ELSE makes the "nothing matched" case a decision rather than an accident. Keep CASE for simple mappings; if you have twenty branches, the mapping belongs in a lookup table you can join to.

Mistake: overlapping branches in the wrong order

Wrong: everyone is 'Junior'

CASE WHEN salary >= 0      THEN 'Junior'
     WHEN salary >= 100000 THEN 'Mid' END

Right: most specific first

CASE WHEN salary >= 100000 THEN 'Mid'
     ELSE 'Junior' END

Edge cases

  • A false condition and an unknown (NULL) condition both skip the branch.
  • Branches are evaluated lazily, so a division inside a skipped branch normally does not run. Do not rely on it: the optimizer may fold constants early.
  • The result type is the common type of all branches; NULL alone does not set a type.

Interview angle

Typical tasks: "grade students by marks", "count pass and fail in one query", "pivot rows to columns", "swap values with one UPDATE" (SET gender = CASE gender WHEN 'M' THEN 'F' ELSE 'M' END). Know that CASE returns NULL when nothing matches.

A strong answer also says: the first true branch wins, so branch order matters; all branches share one type; a simple CASE cannot test NULL; and CASE inside SUM is how you pivot. If asked to "avoid division by zero", show NULLIF(b, 0) as the short form of the CASE.

String functions

SQL has a standard toolbox for text: join (||, CONCAT), measure (LENGTH), change case, trim, cut (SUBSTRING, LEFT, RIGHT), search (POSITION), replace and pad. Names differ a little between PostgreSQL and MySQL, so know both.

Why use them in SQL?

Cleaning and shaping text inside the query is faster than pulling every row into Java: build a display name, split an email into user and domain, mask a phone number, find the rows with a certain pattern, format an invoice number with leading zeros. Remember that a function around a column in WHERE (WHERE LOWER(email) = '…') stops a normal index on that column from being used; create an expression index (CREATE INDEX … ON employees (LOWER(email))) or store the data in the form you search for.

The common functions

TaskPostgreSQLMySQL 8
Join texta || b, CONCAT(a, b, …), CONCAT_WS(sep, …) CONCAT(a, b), CONCAT_WS(sep, …)
Length in charactersLENGTH(s), CHAR_LENGTH(s) CHAR_LENGTH(s) (LENGTH counts bytes)
CaseUPPER, LOWER, INITCAPUPPER, LOWER (no INITCAP)
Remove spacesTRIM(s), LTRIM, RTRIM, TRIM(BOTH 'x' FROM s)same
Part of a stringSUBSTRING(s FROM 2 FOR 3) or SUBSTRING(s, 2, 3); LEFT(s, n), RIGHT(s, n) SUBSTRING(s, 2, 3) / SUBSTR; LEFT, RIGHT
Find a substringPOSITION('@' IN s), STRPOS(s, '@')LOCATE('@', s), INSTR(s, '@')
ReplaceREPLACE(s, 'old', 'new')same
Split on a separatorSPLIT_PART(s, '@', 2)SUBSTRING_INDEX(s, '@', 1) (the part before the 1st separator)
Pad to a widthLPAD(s, 5, '0'), RPADsame
Repeat, reverseREPEAT, REVERSEsame
Regexs ~ 'pattern', REGEXP_REPLACE, REGEXP_MATCHs REGEXP 'pattern', REGEXP_REPLACE

SQL strings are 1-based: the first character is position 1, not 0 as in Java. A position of 0 from POSITION means "not found".

Examples on the sample data

SELECT LENGTH(email)                      AS len,
       UPPER(last_name)                   AS upper_last,
       LEFT(first_name, 1) || '. ' || last_name AS short_name,
       POSITION('@' IN email)             AS at_pos,
       SPLIT_PART(email, '@', 1)          AS user_part,
       SPLIT_PART(email, '@', 2)          AS domain
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id = 5;
 len | upper_last | short_name | at_pos |  user_part  |   domain
-----+------------+------------+--------+-------------+-------------
  23 | MENON      | P. Menon   |     12 | priya.menon | example.com
(1 row)
SELECT REPLACE(email, 'example.com', 'company.in')   AS new_email,
       LPAD(emp_id::TEXT, 5, '0')                    AS badge_no,
       '[' || TRIM('   hello  ') || ']'              AS trimmed,
       SUBSTRING(email FROM 1 FOR 5)                 AS first_five
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id = 5;

SELECT name, LEFT(phone, 2) || 'XXXXXX' || RIGHT(phone, 2) AS masked_phone
FROM customers
WHERE customer_id = 1;
        new_email         | badge_no | trimmed | first_five
--------------------------+----------+---------+------------
 priya.menon@company.in   | 00005    | [hello] | priya
(1 row)

    name    | masked_phone
------------+--------------
 Aarav Shah | 98XXXXXX01
(1 row)

A frequent real task is turning "first and last name" columns into a display name. Use CONCAT_WS(' ', first_name, last_name) when parts may be NULL: || returns NULL if any piece is NULL, and CONCAT_WS skips NULL pieces.

Searching text

LIKE and ILIKE (Module 1) cover simple patterns. For more, PostgreSQL has regular expressions with ~ (case-sensitive) and ~* (case-insensitive):

SELECT first_name, email
FROM employees
WHERE email ~ '^[a-k]'          -- starts with a letter from a to k
ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name |           email
------------+----------------------------
 Ananya     | ananya.rao@example.com
 Arjun      | arjun.reddy@example.com
 Divya      | divya.krishnan@example.com
 Karan      | karan.malhotra@example.com
 Farhan     | farhan.shaikh@example.com
 Isha       | isha.banerjee@example.com
(6 rows)

Regular expressions are powerful but cannot use a normal index. For serious text search (words, ranking, stemming) PostgreSQL has full-text search (to_tsvector), and large systems use Elasticsearch or similar.

Rule: NULL in, NULL out

Almost every string function returns NULL when an argument is NULL: UPPER(NULL), NULL || 'x', LENGTH(NULL). Wrap possibly-missing values in COALESCE(col, '') or use CONCAT_WS.

Mistake: LENGTH in MySQL on non-English text

LENGTH('नमस्ते') in MySQL returns the number of bytes (18 in UTF-8), not characters (6). Use CHAR_LENGTH. PostgreSQL's LENGTH counts characters.

Tip: store clean, display late

Trim and lowercase data when it enters (emails), and format for display in the application or in the query. Data that is stored dirty needs TRIM(LOWER(…)) in every query, and defeats indexes.

Edge cases

  • SUBSTRING(s FROM 0 FOR 3) returns only 2 characters in PostgreSQL, since position 0 is "before the string". Start at 1.
  • SPLIT_PART(s, '@', 5) past the last part returns an empty string, not NULL (PostgreSQL). A position of 0 raises ERROR: field position must not be zero.
  • CHAR(n) values are space padded, which makes length and comparison results surprising; another reason to avoid CHAR.

Interview angle

Typical: "get the domain from an email" (SPLIT_PART or SUBSTRING after POSITION), "pad ids with zeros" (LPAD), "first letter capital" (INITCAP or UPPER(LEFT(…)) || LOWER(SUBSTRING(…))), "count a character in a string" (LENGTH(s) - LENGTH(REPLACE(s, 'a', ''))).

Date and time functions

Get the current time with NOW() and CURRENT_DATE, pull parts out with EXTRACT, round down with DATE_TRUNC, do arithmetic with INTERVAL, and measure gaps with AGE or by subtracting dates. MySQL uses DATE_FORMAT, DATEDIFF and DATE_ADD instead.

Current date and time

You wantPostgreSQLMySQL 8
Today's dateCURRENT_DATECURDATE(), CURRENT_DATE
Now (with zone)NOW(), CURRENT_TIMESTAMP (both timestamptz)NOW(), CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
Now, no zoneLOCALTIMESTAMPsame
Real clock timeclock_timestamp()SYSDATE()

In PostgreSQL, NOW() returns the time the transaction started and stays the same for every statement inside it. clock_timestamp() returns the real, moving time. Use DEFAULT now() for created_at columns.

EXTRACT and DATE_TRUNC

EXTRACT(field FROM value) pulls out one part as a number. DATE_TRUNC('unit', value) cuts a timestamp down to the start of the year, month, week or day, which is the key to grouping by period. The examples use Priya's hire date, 2019-01-07, a Monday.

SELECT hire_date,
       EXTRACT(YEAR  FROM hire_date)           AS yr,
       EXTRACT(MONTH FROM hire_date)           AS mon,
       EXTRACT(DOW   FROM hire_date)           AS dow,          -- 0 = Sunday
       DATE_TRUNC('month', hire_date)::DATE    AS month_start,
       TO_CHAR(hire_date, 'Dy DD Mon YYYY')    AS pretty
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id = 5;
 hire_date  |  yr  | mon | dow | month_start |     pretty
------------+------+-----+-----+-------------+-----------------
 2019-01-07 | 2019 |   1 |   1 | 2019-01-01  | Mon 07 Jan 2019
(1 row)

Other EXTRACT fields: DAY, QUARTER, WEEK, DOY (day of year), HOUR, EPOCH (seconds since 1970). MySQL has YEAR(d), MONTH(d), DAYOFWEEK(d) (1 = Sunday) and also accepts EXTRACT. It has no DATE_TRUNC; use DATE_FORMAT(d, '%Y-%m-01') or LAST_DAY().

Grouping by month is a very common report:

SELECT TO_CHAR(DATE_TRUNC('month', order_date), 'YYYY-MM') AS month,
       COUNT(*)                                             AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY DATE_TRUNC('month', order_date)
ORDER BY DATE_TRUNC('month', order_date);
  month  | orders
---------+--------
 2026-01 |      2
 2026-02 |      3
 2026-03 |      3
 2026-04 |      2
(4 rows)

MySQL: DATE_FORMAT(order_date, '%Y-%m') in both the select list and GROUP BY.

Date arithmetic and intervals

An interval is a length of time (INTERVAL '3 days', INTERVAL '1 month'). The results follow clear rules:

ExpressionType of resultExample
date + integerdateDATE '2019-01-07' + 30 → 2019-02-06
date + intervaltimestampDATE '2019-01-07' + INTERVAL '1 month' → 2019-02-07 00:00:00
date - dateinteger (days)DATE '2026-04-10' - DATE '2019-01-07' → 2650
timestamp - timestampintervalTIMESTAMP '2026-04-10 18:00' - TIMESTAMP '2026-04-10 09:30' → 08:30:00
AGE(later, earlier)interval in years, months, days7 years 3 mons 3 days
 
SELECT DATE '2026-04-10' - hire_date                AS days,
       AGE(DATE '2026-04-10', hire_date)            AS tenure,
       EXTRACT(YEAR FROM AGE(DATE '2026-04-10', hire_date)) AS full_years,
       (hire_date + INTERVAL '1 year')::DATE        AS first_anniversary
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id = 5;
 days |         tenure          | full_years | first_anniversary
------+-------------------------+------------+-------------------
 2650 | 7 years 3 mons 3 days   |          7 | 2020-01-07
(1 row)

In real queries replace the fixed date with CURRENT_DATE. Month arithmetic is calendar based: DATE '2026-01-31' + INTERVAL '1 month' gives 2026-02-28 (the last valid day), not 2026-03-03.

MySQL equivalents

TaskPostgreSQLMySQL 8
Days betweend2 - d1DATEDIFF(d2, d1)
Add timed + INTERVAL '7 days'DATE_ADD(d, INTERVAL 7 DAY)
Subtract timed - INTERVAL '1 month'DATE_SUB(d, INTERVAL 1 MONTH)
Years betweenEXTRACT(YEAR FROM AGE(d2, d1))TIMESTAMPDIFF(YEAR, d1, d2)
Format as textTO_CHAR(d, 'DD Mon YYYY')DATE_FORMAT(d, '%d %b %Y')
Text to dateTO_DATE('10-04-2026', 'DD-MM-YYYY')STR_TO_DATE('10-04-2026', '%d-%m-%Y')
Last day of month(DATE_TRUNC('month', d) + INTERVAL '1 month - 1 day')::DATELAST_DAY(d)

The format letters differ too: PostgreSQL YYYY-MM-DD HH24:MI:SS, MySQL %Y-%m-%d %H:%i:%s.

Rule: keep dates as dates

Store dates in DATE or TIMESTAMPTZ columns, never in text. Compare dates with dates, and format only at the edge, for display. Use ISO 'YYYY-MM-DD' literals in SQL.

Mistake: DATE_TRUNC returns timestamptz, not date

DATE_TRUNC('month', hire_date) on a DATE returns a timestamp with time zone, so the printed value changes with the session's time zone. Cast back with ::DATE when you want a day, as in the examples above.

Edge cases

  • Invalid dates raise ERROR: date/time field value out of range: "2026-02-30". A bad text input raises invalid input syntax for type date.
  • February 29: + INTERVAL '1 year' from 2024-02-29 gives 2025-02-28.
  • "Week" numbering is a trap: EXTRACT(WEEK …) is the ISO week (Monday start; the first days of January may belong to week 52 or 53 of the previous year).

Interview angle

Expect "orders per month", "employees hired in the last year", "age from date of birth", "customers who joined in the last 30 days", and "find the day of week with most orders". Group with DATE_TRUNC/DATE_FORMAT, filter with ranges (next article).

Filtering date ranges and time zones

Filter dates with a half-open range, >= start AND < next_start, never with BETWEEN on timestamps. Store moments as TIMESTAMPTZ, convert to a local zone with AT TIME ZONE only when you display or bucket by local day.

The half-open range

Hard question: "all orders in March 2026". For a DATE column BETWEEN '2026-03-01' AND '2026-03-31' works. For a TIMESTAMP it silently loses data, because the end point '2026-03-31' means 2026-03-31 00:00:00:

SELECT TIMESTAMP '2026-03-31 15:00:00' BETWEEN '2026-03-01' AND '2026-03-31' AS between_says,
       TIMESTAMP '2026-03-31 15:00:00' >= '2026-03-01'
       AND TIMESTAMP '2026-03-31 15:00:00' <  '2026-04-01'                  AS half_open_says;
 between_says | half_open_says
--------------+----------------
 f            | t
(1 row)

The half-open form includes the start and excludes the next period's start. It works for dates, timestamps and any precision, and the end of one range is exactly the start of the next, so ranges never overlap or leave gaps.

-- a fixed month
SELECT order_id, order_date
FROM orders
WHERE order_date >= DATE '2026-03-01' AND order_date < DATE '2026-04-01'
ORDER BY order_id;

-- the last 30 days
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders WHERE order_date >= CURRENT_DATE - 30;

-- the current month, whatever today is
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders
WHERE order_date >= DATE_TRUNC('month', CURRENT_DATE)
  AND order_date <  DATE_TRUNC('month', CURRENT_DATE) + INTERVAL '1 month';
 order_id | order_date
----------+------------
        6 | 2026-03-08
        7 | 2026-03-21
        8 | 2026-03-30
(3 rows)

Keep the column bare so indexes work

A condition that wraps the column in a function cannot use a normal index on that column. The database must compute the function for every row. Rewrite it as a range on the bare column:

Wrong: function on the column (index not used)

WHERE EXTRACT(YEAR FROM order_date) = 2026
  AND EXTRACT(MONTH FROM order_date) = 3

Right: range on the bare column

WHERE order_date >= DATE '2026-03-01'
  AND order_date <  DATE '2026-04-01'

A condition written so that an index can be used is called sargable (search-argument able). Same idea as WHERE LOWER(email) = … in the string article.

Time zones

Chapter one rule: store the instant, not the local clock. In PostgreSQL that means TIMESTAMPTZ (data types). It is saved as UTC; each session shows it in its TimeZone setting. AT TIME ZONE converts between the two kinds of timestamp, and its direction trips people up:

  • timestamptz AT TIME ZONE 'zone' returns a plain timestamp: the wall-clock time in that zone.
  • timestamp AT TIME ZONE 'zone' returns a timestamptz: it says "this wall-clock reading was taken in that zone".
SET TIME ZONE 'UTC';
SELECT TIMESTAMPTZ '2026-04-10 09:00:00+00' AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata' AS ist_clock,
       TIMESTAMP   '2026-04-10 14:30:00'    AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata' AS instant;
      ist_clock      |        instant
---------------------+------------------------
 2026-04-10 14:30:00 | 2026-04-10 09:00:00+00
(1 row)

Typical use: counting orders per local day. A purchase at 23:30 IST on 9 April is 18:00 UTC on 9 April, but 00:10 IST on 10 April is 18:40 UTC on 9 April, so grouping on the UTC date puts it on the wrong day for an Indian shop. Group on the local date:

SELECT (created_at AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata')::DATE AS local_day, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM web_orders                        -- a hypothetical table with created_at TIMESTAMPTZ
GROUP BY (created_at AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata')::DATE
ORDER BY local_day;

India has no daylight saving time (always +05:30), but many countries do, and across a DST change a "day" has 23 or 25 hours. Use the zone name ('Asia/Kolkata', 'America/New_York'), not a fixed offset such as '+05:30', so the database knows the rules. List names with SELECT name FROM pg_timezone_names.

MySQL: TIMESTAMP converts to UTC on storage according to the session time_zone variable; DATETIME does not. CONVERT_TZ(ts, 'UTC', 'Asia/Kolkata') converts, but named zones work only after the time-zone tables have been loaded into MySQL. A frequent production fix is running all servers, JVMs and databases in UTC.

In Java use Instant or OffsetDateTime for TIMESTAMPTZ, and LocalDate for DATE. Never store java.util.Date without knowing the JVM's time zone.

Rule: ranges are closed at the start and open at the end

col >= start AND col < end_exclusive. For "the whole of 30 April" the end is 1 May. Compute it with + INTERVAL '1 day' instead of writing 23:59:59, which loses fractions of a second.

Mistake: comparing a timestamp column with a date literal
