WHERE created_at = '2026-04-10' matches only rows at exactly midnight. To match a day use created_at >= '2026-04-10' AND created_at < '2026-04-11', or created_at::DATE = '2026-04-10' for small tables only.

Edge cases

  • A date literal compared with a TIMESTAMPTZ is read in the session time zone, so the same query can return different rows for two sessions in different zones.
  • BETWEEN with a reversed range (BETWEEN 10 AND 5) returns nothing; use BETWEEN SYMMETRIC in PostgreSQL if order is unknown.
  • The OVERLAPS operator checks whether two periods overlap: (s1, e1) OVERLAPS (s2, e2).

Interview angle

"Why not BETWEEN for timestamps?" is a favourite. Give the half-open answer and add that it keeps indexes usable. "How do you handle time zones?" Store UTC with TIMESTAMPTZ, convert at the edges, run servers in UTC, use named zones.

Type casting

A cast converts a value from one type to another: CAST(x AS INTEGER), or the PostgreSQL shorthand x::INTEGER. Do it explicitly, and expect an error when the text cannot be converted.

Why you need it

Types decide behaviour. Integer division truncates, text sorts alphabetically ('10' before '9'), dates compare as dates and JDBC parameters arrive with a type. Casting is how you say what you mean: divide as decimals, compare a number stored as text, turn a timestamp into a day, or build a string from an id. SQL also converts some types automatically (implicit casts); the explicit form is safer.

The two syntaxes

SELECT CAST('42' AS INTEGER) + 1       AS a,    -- standard SQL, works in every database
       '42'::INTEGER + 1               AS b,    -- PostgreSQL shorthand
       7::NUMERIC / 2                  AS c,    -- decimal division
       CAST(150000.60 AS INTEGER)      AS d,    -- numeric to integer rounds
       TRUNC(150000.60)                AS e,    -- cuts off the decimals
       '2026-04-10'::DATE + 1          AS f,
       NOW()::DATE                     AS g,    -- timestamp to date
       emp_id::TEXT || '-' || last_name AS h
FROM employees WHERE emp_id = 5;
  a | b  |          c          |   d    |   e    |     f      |     g      |    h
----+----+---------------------+--------+--------+------------+------------+----------
 43 | 43 |  3.5000000000000000 | 150001 | 150000 | 2026-04-11 | 2026-04-10 | 5-Menon
(1 row)

(Column g shows the day you run it.) Note the rounding: casting a decimal to an integer rounds in PostgreSQL (150000.60 becomes 150001), unlike Java's (int) cast, which truncates.

Common conversions

From → toPostgreSQLMySQL 8
text → integer'42'::INTCAST('42' AS SIGNED)
text → decimal'12.5'::NUMERIC(10,2)CAST('12.5' AS DECIMAL(10,2))
number → textid::TEXTCAST(id AS CHAR)
text → date'2026-04-10'::DATECAST('2026-04-10' AS DATE)
timestamp → datets::DATEDATE(ts)
text → boolean'true'::BOOLEAN (also 't', 'yes', '1')no real boolean; use 1/0
formatted text → number / dateTO_NUMBER('1,234.50', '9,999.99'), TO_DATE(…)STR_TO_DATE(…), REPLACE then CAST

MySQL's CAST accepts only a few target types: SIGNED, UNSIGNED, DECIMAL, CHAR, DATE, DATETIME, JSON and a few more (not INT).

Errors and implicit casts

SELECT 'abc'::INTEGER;
-- ERROR:  invalid input syntax for type integer: "abc"

SELECT '2026-13-45'::DATE;
-- ERROR:  date/time field value out of range: "2026-13-45"

SELECT first_name + 1 FROM employees;
-- ERROR:  operator does not exist: character varying + integer

SELECT '10' + 5;        -- 15: a bare quoted literal is "unknown" and adopts the number type

PostgreSQL is strict: a text column is not converted silently. MySQL is lax: it converts silently, and SELECT 'abc' + 5 returns 5 with a warning, because 'abc' becomes 0. The same laxness makes WHERE phone = 9876500001 compare numbers (and scan the whole table, ignoring the index on phone). Be explicit.

A related pitfall is casting inside a join or WHERE: ON o.customer_id::TEXT = c.ref works, but it blocks index use. Better to fix the column types.

Safe conversion of dirty data

PostgreSQL has no built-in "try cast" before version 16 (PostgreSQL 16 added pg_input_is_valid for this check). The classic approach is a regular expression guard:

SELECT CASE WHEN raw ~ '^[0-9]+$' THEN raw::INT END AS safe_value
FROM (VALUES ('12'), ('abc'), ('7')) AS t(raw);
 safe_value
------------
         12
     (null)
          7
(3 rows)

Rule: cast one side to match the other, and cast the value, not the indexed column

Write WHERE phone = '9876500001' (text to text) rather than casting phone::BIGINT. Cast the literal or parameter so the indexed column stays untouched.

Mistake: integer division in percentages

Wrong: always 0 or 1

SELECT delivered / total * 100;      -- 5 / 10 = 0

Right: cast first, or multiply first

SELECT 100.0 * delivered / total;    -- 50.0
SELECT delivered::NUMERIC / total * 100;

Edge cases

  • Casting to VARCHAR(5) silently truncates in an explicit cast, but raises "value too long" on an INSERT.
  • NULL casts to any type and stays NULL.
  • ' 42 '::INT works (PostgreSQL trims spaces around numbers); '4 2'::INT does not.

Interview angle

"What does SELECT 5/2 return?" (2 in PostgreSQL, 2.5000 in MySQL), "how do you get decimals?" (cast one operand), "explicit vs implicit casting" and "how do you convert text to a date".

Interview questions — Intermediate SQL

What is the difference between WHERE and HAVING?

WHERE filters individual rows before grouping and cannot use aggregate functions. HAVING filters groups after GROUP BY and can use aggregates. Example: keep salaries of at least 100000 with WHERE, then keep departments with two or more such people with HAVING COUNT(*) >= 2. Put every condition that does not need an aggregate in WHERE; it is cheaper.

COUNT(*), COUNT(column) and COUNT(DISTINCT column): what is the difference?

COUNT(*) counts rows. COUNT(col) counts rows where col is not NULL. COUNT(DISTINCT col) counts different non-NULL values. In the sample data, COUNT(*) on employees is 12, COUNT(dept_id) is 11 and COUNT(DISTINCT dept_id) is 3.

In which order does SQL evaluate a query?

FROM and JOIN, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, SELECT (expressions, aliases, window functions), DISTINCT, ORDER BY, LIMIT/OFFSET. This is why a select alias cannot be used in WHERE but can be used in ORDER BY, and why HAVING can use aggregates and WHERE cannot.

Explain INNER, LEFT, RIGHT and FULL joins with the employees and departments tables.

Inner returns only employees whose department exists: 11 rows (Meera has no department, Marketing has no employees). Left keeps all employees: 12 rows, Meera with a NULL department. Right keeps all departments: 12 rows, Marketing with a NULL employee. Full keeps both: 13 rows. MySQL has no full join; emulate it with a union of a left and a right join.

Why does a WHERE condition on the right table turn a LEFT JOIN into an INNER JOIN?

The left join first adds unmatched left rows with NULLs in the right table's columns. A later WHERE o.status = 'DELIVERED' is false (unknown) for those NULLs, so the rows are removed again. Put conditions about the optional table in the ON clause, or use WHERE … OR o.id IS NULL when that is what you mean.

How do you find customers who have never placed an order? Give two or three ways.

(1) LEFT JOIN orders … WHERE o.order_id IS NULL. (2) WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.customer_id), the safest. (3) WHERE customer_id NOT IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders), which fails (returns no rows) if the subquery produces a NULL. The answer is Sara Khan and Tanvi Desai.

What happens with NOT IN when the subquery contains a NULL?

It returns no rows. x NOT IN (1, NULL) becomes x <> 1 AND x <> NULL; the second test is unknown, so the whole is never true. Use NOT EXISTS or add WHERE col IS NOT NULL to the subquery.

What is a self join? Show how to list each employee with the manager's name.

A table joined to itself using two aliases. SELECT e.first_name, m.first_name AS manager FROM employees e LEFT JOIN employees m ON m.emp_id = e.manager_id. It must be a left join, otherwise the CEO (no manager) disappears. A self join follows one level only; use a recursive CTE for the whole hierarchy.

What is a correlated subquery, and how is it different from a normal one?

A normal subquery is independent and runs once. A correlated subquery refers to a column of the outer query, so it conceptually runs once per outer row, for example "salary greater than the average salary of the same department". The optimizer may rewrite it into a join; where it cannot, it is slow on big tables, and an aggregate in a derived table or a window function is the faster rewrite.

IN vs EXISTS vs JOIN: which should you use?

For "does a matching row exist", IN and EXISTS give the same result and modern optimizers use the same semi-join plan. EXISTS is clearer for correlated tests and NULL-safe when negated. A JOIN is for when you need columns of the other table, but it multiplies rows when there are several matches (needs DISTINCT or aggregation).

UNION vs UNION ALL?

Both stack two result sets with the same number of compatible columns. UNION removes duplicate rows (an extra sort or hash step); UNION ALL keeps everything and is faster. Use UNION ALL unless you need distinct rows. Joins add columns; unions add rows.

Why must every non-aggregated column in the SELECT list appear in GROUP BY?

The output has one row per group, so each column needs one value per group. A column that is neither grouped nor aggregated has several values, and the database refuses to pick one (PostgreSQL error: "must appear in the GROUP BY clause or be used in an aggregate function"; MySQL with ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY on, error 1055). Exception: columns functionally dependent on a grouped primary key are allowed in PostgreSQL.

How do you select all orders placed in March 2026 from a timestamp column?

Use a half-open range: WHERE created_at >= '2026-03-01' AND created_at < '2026-04-01'. BETWEEN '2026-03-01' AND '2026-03-31' would stop at midnight at the start of 31 March and lose that day. The range form keeps ranges adjacent and lets an index on the bare column work, unlike EXTRACT(MONTH FROM created_at) = 3.

What does SELECT 7 / 2 return, and how do you get 3.5?

In PostgreSQL both operands are integers, so it is integer division and the answer is 3. Make one side decimal: 7 / 2.0, 7::NUMERIC / 2 or CAST(7 AS NUMERIC) / 2. MySQL returns 3.5000 for / and has DIV for integer division. It matters for percentages: delivered / total * 100 is 0 until you cast.

A join made my SUM too large. Why, and how do you fix it?

A one-to-many join repeats the "one" side once per matching row, so its values are summed several times (employee salaries joined to a table with several project rows per employee). Fix: aggregate before the join in a subquery or CTE, or use EXISTS/IN if you only need to test existence. Check by comparing COUNT(*) with the distinct count of the key.

Cheat sheet — Intermediate SQL

  • Aggregates COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX ignore NULL; COUNT(*) counts rows; SUM of nothing is NULL.
  • Count variants COUNT(*) all rows, COUNT(col) non-NULL, COUNT(DISTINCT col) different values.
  • GROUP BY every non-aggregated select column must be grouped; NULLs form one group; add ORDER BY.
  • HAVING filters groups and may use aggregates; WHERE filters rows first and may not.
  • Conditional aggregate SUM(CASE WHEN c THEN 1 ELSE 0 END), or COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE c) in PostgreSQL.
  • ROLLUP GROUP BY ROLLUP (a) adds subtotal and grand-total rows (MySQL WITH ROLLUP).
  • Logical order FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, SELECT, DISTINCT, ORDER BY, LIMIT.
  • Alias rule select alias is visible in ORDER BY, not in WHERE.
  • INNER JOIN matches only. LEFT all left rows. RIGHT all right rows. FULL both (no FULL in MySQL).
  • LEFT JOIN trap right-table filters go in ON; count COUNT(right.id), not COUNT(*).
  • Anti-join LEFT JOIN … WHERE r.id IS NULL or NOT EXISTS; avoid NOT IN with NULLs.
  • Self join same table, two aliases (e, m); use LEFT so the root stays.
  • CROSS JOIN every pair, |A| × |B| rows; a missing ON does this by accident.
  • USING / NATURAL USING (col) is fine; avoid NATURAL JOIN.
  • Fan-out one-to-many joins repeat the "one" side; aggregate before joining.
  • Subquery shapes scalar (one value), IN (one column), derived table (needs alias), EXISTS (yes/no).
  • Correlated uses an outer column; conceptually runs per outer row.
  • Set operators UNION distinct, UNION ALL keep all, INTERSECT, EXCEPT; same column count and types.
  • CASE first true branch wins; no match and no ELSE gives NULL; works in ORDER BY and aggregates.
  • NULL helpers COALESCE(a, b), NULLIF(a, b); 10 / NULLIF(x, 0) avoids division by zero.
  • Strings ||/CONCAT, LENGTH, UPPER, TRIM, SUBSTRING, POSITION, SPLIT_PART, LPAD; 1-based positions.
  • Dates NOW(), CURRENT_DATE, EXTRACT, DATE_TRUNC, AGE, d + INTERVAL '1 month'; MySQL DATE_FORMAT, DATEDIFF, DATE_ADD.
  • Date ranges col >= start AND col < next_start; never BETWEEN on timestamps; keep the column bare.
  • Time zones store TIMESTAMPTZ; AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata' for local days; run servers in UTC.
  • Casting CAST(x AS INT) or x::INT; 7/2 is 3 in PostgreSQL, so cast one side to NUMERIC.

Practice — Intermediate SQL

Use the sample database. Write each query first, then check the row count given.

  1. Aggregates. One query that returns the number of employees, the payroll, the highest and lowest salary, and the number of employees with a department. Done when it shows 12, 1645000, 300000, 55000 and 11.
  2. GROUP BY and HAVING. For each department id: headcount, average salary (rounded to 2 decimals) and highest salary; keep only departments with an average above 100000. Then show departments with at least two employees hired after 2020. Explain which parts belong in WHERE and which in HAVING.
  3. Every join type. Join employees and departments with inner, left, right and full joins and record the row counts (11, 12, 12, 13). Then list departments with their employee count, including Marketing with 0, and explain why COUNT(*) would be wrong.
  4. Customer report. For each customer: number of orders and total spent on delivered orders, showing 0 for customers with nothing. Use LEFT JOIN with the status filter in ON. Done when Sara and Tanvi show 0 and Aarav shows 2 delivered orders worth 1908.
  5. Self join. List employees with their manager's name, and a second query with the number of direct reports per manager (Meera 3, Priya 3, Ananya 2, Sunita 2, Vikram 1).
  6. Subqueries. Find employees paid above their department's average in three ways: a correlated subquery, a derived table join, and (after Module 3) a window function. All must return 6 rows. Add the employees who earn more than the company average, and compare.
  7. Anti-joins. Customers who never ordered, products never sold, departments with no employees and employees who are not managers (7 rows). Write each with NOT EXISTS, then show what NOT IN does for the last one.
  8. Sets and CASE. Build one list of all email addresses (employees and customers) with a column saying where each came from; then label each product 'Cheap' under 300, 'Normal' under 1000 and 'Premium' otherwise, and count products per label. Sort orders as PLACED, SHIPPED, DELIVERED, CANCELLED.
  9. Strings and dates. Show each employee as "P. Menon (priya.menon)" style text; count hires per year; show orders per month; list orders from the last 30 days using a half-open range. Done when the monthly counts are 2, 3, 3, 2.
  10. Debug challenge. This query returns 1330000 instead of 1070000 for the total salary of people on projects: SELECT SUM(e.salary) FROM employees e JOIN employee_projects ep ON ep.emp_id = e.emp_id. Explain why, and fix it two ways.
3

Module 3·Month 2 · revise in month 11Must for interviews

Advanced SQL

13 topics · about 2 h of reading

Basic and intermediate SQL answer "what is in the table" and "how do I combine tables". Advanced SQL answers the harder questions: "rank employees inside each department", "what was the change from last month", "walk the whole management chain", "who logged in five days in a row". The tools are common table expressions (WITH), window functions (OVER), views, and the ability to put logic into the database with functions, procedures and triggers. These are also the topics that separate a strong SQL interview answer from an average one.

The module ends with the classic interview queries (second highest salary, duplicates, top N per group, consecutive days, median), each solved in two or three ways on the sample database from Module 1. If your skills feel rusty, revisit GROUP BY and joins from Module 2 first. How the database executes these queries, and how indexes speed them up, is in Module 5.

  • Write readable multi-step queries with CTEs, and walk trees and hierarchies with recursive CTEs.
  • Use ROW_NUMBER, RANK, DENSE_RANK, LAG, LEAD, running totals and moving averages, and explain window frames.
  • Create views and materialized views, and write a simple function, procedure and trigger, and judge when they are a good idea.
  • Solve the standard interview problems in more than one way and explain the trade-offs.
  • Know how to keep practising with a college database and a LeetCode-style plan.

Common table expressions (WITH)

A CTE is a named, temporary result defined with WITH name AS (…) at the top of a statement. It lets you write a complex query as a sequence of small, readable steps instead of nested subqueries.

What and why

Nested subqueries read inside-out, and when the same subquery is needed twice you have to copy it. A CTE gives the step a name, defined once, read top to bottom. It exists only for the statement that follows; nothing is saved in the database. Both PostgreSQL and MySQL (8.0 and later) support it.

WITH step_one AS (
    SELECT ...
),
step_two AS (                 -- a later CTE can use an earlier one
    SELECT ... FROM step_one ...
)
SELECT ...
FROM step_two
JOIN step_one ON ...;         -- the main query can use all of them

Example: a multi-step report

Goal: for each customer, the number of orders and the total spent, ignoring cancelled orders. Step one computes the total of each order, step two summarises per customer, and the main query adds the name.

WITH order_totals AS (
    SELECT o.order_id, o.customer_id,
           SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) AS total
    FROM orders AS o
    JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.order_id
    WHERE o.status <> 'CANCELLED'
    GROUP BY o.order_id, o.customer_id
),
customer_totals AS (
    SELECT customer_id, COUNT(*) AS orders, SUM(total) AS spent
    FROM order_totals
    GROUP BY customer_id
)
SELECT c.name, ct.orders, ct.spent
FROM customer_totals AS ct
JOIN customers AS c ON c.customer_id = ct.customer_id
ORDER BY ct.spent DESC;
     name     | orders |  spent
--------------+--------+---------
 Aarav Shah   |      3 | 6206.00
 Diya Kapoor  |      2 | 5597.00
 Rohan Das    |      2 | 2474.00
 Kabir Singh  |      1 | 2100.00
 Ishita Joshi |      1 | 2048.00
(5 rows)

Sara and Tanvi are absent because they have no orders and the report starts from customer_totals. Kabir has one order because his other order (4) was cancelled.

Using a CTE twice

The big win over a subquery: a CTE can be referenced several times. "Customers who spent more than the average customer":

WITH spend AS (
    SELECT o.customer_id, SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) AS spent
    FROM orders o
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.order_id
    WHERE o.status <> 'CANCELLED'
    GROUP BY o.customer_id
)
SELECT c.name, s.spent
FROM spend AS s
JOIN customers AS c ON c.customer_id = s.customer_id
WHERE s.spent > (SELECT AVG(spent) FROM spend)      -- second use of the same CTE
ORDER BY s.spent DESC;
    name     |  spent
-------------+---------
 Aarav Shah  | 6206.00
 Diya Kapoor | 5597.00
(2 rows)

(6206 + 5597 + 2474 + 2100 + 2048 = 18425, divided by 5 customers = 3685.)

CTE vs subquery vs view vs temporary table

Subquery / derived tableCTEViewTemp table
Lives forone place in a statementone statementstored in the databaseyour session
Reusable in the statementno (copy it)yesyes (and in any query)yes, many statements
Data storednono (see below)noyes
Can have an indexnononoyes
Best forsmall inline stepreadable multi-step logic, recursionshared definition, securityvery large intermediate results used many times

Performance: in PostgreSQL 12 and later, a non-recursive CTE that is referenced once and has no side effects is inlined like a subquery, so the optimizer treats it the same. A CTE referenced more than once is computed once and reused (materialized). You can force the behaviour: WITH x AS MATERIALIZED (…) or AS NOT MATERIALIZED (…). Before PostgreSQL 12 every CTE was an "optimization fence", always computed first, which made some old advice ("CTEs are slow") true then. Today a CTE is mostly a readability tool, not a performance trick. MySQL 8 behaves similarly (it merges simple CTEs, or materializes them).

Data-modifying CTEs (PostgreSQL)

A CTE can contain INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE with RETURNING, and the main statement can use what came back. This moves rows between tables in one atomic statement:

WITH removed AS (
    DELETE FROM orders
    WHERE status = 'CANCELLED' AND order_date < DATE '2026-03-01'
    RETURNING *
)
INSERT INTO orders_archive
SELECT * FROM removed;

(Run it only on a copy: order 4 is cancelled and older than March, but it has order lines that this delete would also cascade. A real archive job would copy the lines first.) MySQL does not allow data-modifying CTEs.

Convention: name steps for what they hold

Use names like order_totals and active_customers, not t1 and x. One logical step per CTE, ordered from raw data to final shape, and let the last SELECT stay short. Teams often format the WITH block at the left edge so reviews read like a recipe.

Mistake: expecting a CTE to behave like a table or a temp table

A CTE is visible only inside its statement, has no index, and (unless materialized) is not computed once and cached for the next statement. If you run five queries on the same expensive intermediate result, create a temporary table or a materialized view instead (Views).

Rules and edge cases

  • CTE names must be unique in the WITH list. A later CTE can read an earlier one, but not the other way round: ERROR: relation "customer_totals" does not exist.
  • A CTE shadows a real table with the same name for that statement.
  • Column names can be listed after the name: WITH t(day, total) AS (…).
  • A CTE can be used in INSERT … WITH …, UPDATE and DELETE in PostgreSQL and MySQL 8 (WITH before the statement).

Interview angle

"What is a CTE and when would you use one?" Readability, reuse inside one statement, recursion. Know the difference from a view (stored vs temporary) and a temp table (data stored, indexable). Many "top N per group" and "Nth highest" answers use a CTE with a window function.

Recursive CTEs and generating series

A recursive CTE starts with an anchor query and repeatedly joins its own previous result until nothing new appears. It is how SQL walks trees and graphs (org charts, category trees) and generates sequences of numbers or dates.

The structure

A recursive CTE has two parts joined by UNION ALL:

  1. Anchor member: a normal query that returns the starting rows (the root of the tree).
  2. Recursive member: a query that refers to the CTE's own name. It runs on the rows produced by the previous round and returns the next level.
  3. Termination: the loop stops when a round returns zero rows. All rounds are then combined into the final result.
WITH RECURSIVE name (columns) AS (
    SELECT ...                       -- anchor: runs once
    UNION ALL
    SELECT ... FROM name ... WHERE ...   -- recursive: runs again and again
)
SELECT * FROM name;

MySQL 8 also needs the word RECURSIVE. (SQL Server and Oracle do not use the keyword.)

Counting from 1 to 5

WITH RECURSIVE counter (n) AS (
    SELECT 1                         -- anchor
    UNION ALL
    SELECT n + 1 FROM counter WHERE n < 5   -- recursive; stops when n reaches 5
)
SELECT n FROM counter;
 n
---
 1
 2
 3
 4
 5
(5 rows)

Round 0 returns 1. Round 1 reads the 1 and returns 2. This repeats until n < 5 fails, the round returns nothing, and the loop ends.

The org chart

The classic use. Start with the employee that has no manager, then repeatedly add the people whose manager_id equals an emp_id found in the previous round. Track the level and the path as you go:

WITH RECURSIVE org AS (
    SELECT emp_id, first_name, 1 AS level, first_name::TEXT AS path
    FROM employees
    WHERE manager_id IS NULL                      -- anchor: the CEO
    UNION ALL
    SELECT e.emp_id, e.first_name, o.level + 1, o.path || ' > ' || e.first_name
    FROM employees AS e
    JOIN org AS o ON e.manager_id = o.emp_id      -- recursive: direct reports of the previous level
)
SELECT level, path
FROM org
ORDER BY path;
 level |              path
-------+--------------------------------
     1 | Meera
     2 | Meera > Ananya
     3 | Meera > Ananya > Karan
     3 | Meera > Ananya > Neha
     2 | Meera > Sunita
     3 | Meera > Sunita > Farhan
     3 | Meera > Sunita > Isha
     2 | Meera > Vikram
     3 | Meera > Vikram > Priya
     4 | Meera > Vikram > Priya > Arjun
     4 | Meera > Vikram > Priya > Divya
     4 | Meera > Vikram > Priya > Rahul
(12 rows)

The path column gives you a handy sort that keeps each branch together. In MySQL, cast the path to a long enough type (CAST(first_name AS CHAR(200))), because the column width is fixed by the anchor row.

All subordinates of one manager

Change only the anchor to start from any node. Everyone under Vikram, directly or not:

WITH RECURSIVE team AS (
    SELECT emp_id, first_name FROM employees WHERE emp_id = 2      -- Vikram
    UNION ALL
    SELECT e.emp_id, e.first_name
    FROM employees e
    JOIN team t ON e.manager_id = t.emp_id
)
SELECT first_name FROM team WHERE emp_id <> 2 ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name
------------
 Priya
 Rahul
 Arjun
 Divya
(4 rows)

Going up works the same way with the join reversed. The chain of command above Divya (id 8) joins e.emp_id = c.manager_id. A category tree (categories(id, name, parent_id)) is the same pattern: breadcrumbs "Electronics > Computers > Laptops", or all sub-categories of a category for a product filter. Comment threads and bill-of-materials tables are further examples.

Generating series

Reports often need a row for every day, even days with no data. A recursive CTE can create the days, and in PostgreSQL the built-in generate_series is shorter:

SELECT d::DATE AS day, COUNT(o.order_id) AS orders
FROM generate_series(DATE '2026-04-01', DATE '2026-04-10', INTERVAL '1 day') AS d
LEFT JOIN orders AS o ON o.order_date = d::DATE
GROUP BY d
ORDER BY d;
    day     | orders
------------+--------
 2026-04-01 |      0
 2026-04-02 |      0
 2026-04-03 |      1
 2026-04-04 |      0
 2026-04-05 |      0
 2026-04-06 |      0
 2026-04-07 |      0
 2026-04-08 |      0
 2026-04-09 |      0
 2026-04-10 |      1
(10 rows)

Without the generated calendar, the days with no orders would simply be missing. MySQL has no generate_series; use a recursive CTE that adds one day (d + INTERVAL 1 DAY). Also handy: generate_series(1, 100000) to create a large test table for practising indexes and EXPLAIN:

CREATE TABLE big_numbers AS
SELECT n, md5(n::TEXT) AS hash FROM generate_series(1, 100000) AS n;

Protecting against endless loops

If the data contains a cycle (A manages B, B manages A), the recursion never finishes. Defences:

  • Carry a depth counter and stop: … WHERE o.level < 20.
  • Track visited ids in an array and refuse repeats: WHERE e.emp_id <> ALL (o.visited).
  • PostgreSQL 14 and later has a CYCLE clause that detects and marks cycles for you.
  • MySQL stops with an error after cte_max_recursion_depth rounds (default 1000).
  • Prevent bad data: a CHECK (manager_id <> emp_id) stops the simplest cycle.

Rule: anchor, UNION ALL, and a way out

The recursive member must reference the CTE exactly once and must eventually return no rows. Use UNION ALL (plain UNION removes duplicates each round and is slower, but also prevents some cycles). The column types come from the anchor, so cast the anchor to a type wide enough for later rounds (first_name::TEXT).

Mistake: no termination condition

Wrong: never stops

WITH RECURSIVE c(n) AS (
    SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT n + 1 FROM c
)
SELECT * FROM c;

Right: bounded

WITH RECURSIVE c(n) AS (
    SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT n + 1 FROM c WHERE n < 100
)
SELECT * FROM c;

The wrong query runs until memory or time runs out (add LIMIT while testing, which stops a PostgreSQL recursive CTE early).

Edge cases

  • A tree with several roots (or none) works: the anchor returns all roots; if no row has a NULL parent, you get nothing.
  • Rows are not guaranteed to come out level by level unless you order them. Always add ORDER BY.
  • For huge or very deep graphs, a dedicated graph database, or a materialized path column, may be a better design (Module 4).

Interview angle

"How do you query a hierarchy in SQL?" Describe the anchor and recursive member and the stop condition, then write the org-chart query. Follow-ups: "how do you detect cycles", "print the level/indentation" and "generate dates between two days". A self join only goes one level; a recursive CTE goes all the way down.

Window functions: OVER, PARTITION BY and ORDER BY

A window function calculates something across a set of related rows (the "window") but, unlike GROUP BY, keeps every row. OVER (PARTITION BY … ORDER BY …) defines the window: partition = which rows belong together, order = their sequence.

The problem GROUP BY cannot solve

You want each employee's salary next to the average salary of their department, to see who is above or below. GROUP BY dept_id returns one row per department and loses the employees. A subquery or a join back to a grouped result works, but is clumsy. A window function does it directly:

SELECT first_name,
       salary,
       ROUND(AVG(salary) OVER w, 2)          AS dept_avg,
       ROUND(salary - AVG(salary) OVER w, 2) AS diff
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 1
WINDOW w AS (PARTITION BY dept_id)
ORDER BY emp_id;
 first_name |  salary   | dept_avg  |   diff
------------+-----------+-----------+-----------
 Vikram     | 220000.00 | 144000.00 |  76000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00 | 144000.00 |   6000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00 | 144000.00 |   6000.00
 Arjun      | 110000.00 | 144000.00 | -34000.00
 Divya      |  90000.00 | 144000.00 | -54000.00
(5 rows)

All five employees are still there, and every row has the average of its own department. The WINDOW w AS (…) clause names a window so you do not repeat it.

Syntax

function_name(arguments) OVER (
    PARTITION BY column1, column2      -- optional: split rows into independent groups
    ORDER BY column3 [DESC]            -- optional: order within each partition
    frame_clause                       -- optional: which rows around the current row (next article)
)
  • OVER () with nothing inside: the window is the whole result set. SUM(salary) OVER () is the grand total on every row.
  • PARTITION BY: like GROUP BY but without collapsing rows. The function restarts for every partition. Omit it and there is one partition.
  • ORDER BY inside OVER: required by ranking and navigation functions (ROW_NUMBER, LAG); for aggregates it makes the result cumulative (running total) instead of the same value on every row. It is independent from the final ORDER BY of the query.

Three families of window functions

FamilyFunctionsAnswers
AggregatesSUM, AVG, COUNT, MIN, MAX with OVERtotals, running totals, averages, share of total
RankingROW_NUMBER, RANK, DENSE_RANK, NTILE, PERCENT_RANKposition, top N, percentile buckets (next)
Value / navigationLAG, LEAD, FIRST_VALUE, LAST_VALUE, NTH_VALUEprevious or next row, first or last in group (after that)

Window functions vs GROUP BY

GROUP BYWindow function
Rows returnedone per groupevery input row
Can show row details with the aggregateno (only grouped columns)yes
Rank, previous row, running totalnot possibleyes
Filter on the resultHAVINGsubquery or CTE (below)
Needs an aggregateyesno; ranking functions have none

You can combine them: grouping runs first, then windows run over the grouped rows (RANK() OVER (ORDER BY SUM(salary) DESC) is legal in a GROUP BY query).

Where windows run, and why you cannot filter on them

From the logical order: windows are computed in the SELECT step, after WHERE, GROUP BY and HAVING. Two consequences:

  • A window only sees rows that survived WHERE. Filtering first changes the result: the average over "dept 1 only" differs from the average over everyone.
  • You cannot use a window function in WHERE, since it does not exist yet: ERROR: window functions are not allowed in WHERE. To filter on a rank or running total, compute it in a subquery or CTE and filter outside.

Wrong: filter on the window in WHERE

SELECT first_name, salary
FROM employees
WHERE ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC) <= 3;
-- ERROR:  window functions are not allowed in WHERE

Right: compute in a CTE, filter outside

WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT first_name, salary,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id) AS rn
    FROM employees
)
SELECT first_name, salary FROM ranked WHERE rn <= 3;

Databases like Snowflake and BigQuery have a QUALIFY clause for this; PostgreSQL and MySQL do not, so the CTE pattern above is the one to memorise.

Rule: windows need PostgreSQL 8.4+ or MySQL 8.0+

MySQL 5.7 has no window functions at all, so interview answers for MySQL 5.7 use variables or correlated subqueries. Every maintained MySQL (8.x) supports everything in this module.

Mistake: forgetting that ORDER BY inside OVER changes an aggregate

SUM(salary) OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id) gives the department total on every row. SUM(salary) OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id ORDER BY hire_date) gives a running total. Adding an ORDER BY is a change of meaning, not a cosmetic choice.

Edge cases

  • NULL partition keys form one partition (Meera, with dept_id = NULL, is a partition of one).
  • Window functions cannot be nested inside each other or inside aggregates of the same level; wrap one in a subquery.
  • Query-level ORDER BY sorts the final output only; the window ORDER BY decides the calculation.

Interview angle

"What is a window function and how does it differ from GROUP BY?" Rows are kept; the function looks at a window defined by PARTITION BY/ORDER BY. "Why can't you use it in WHERE?" Logical order. Then expect ranking and top N per group.

ROW_NUMBER, RANK, DENSE_RANK and NTILE

All four number rows in an ordered window. They differ only in how they treat ties: ROW_NUMBER never repeats, RANK repeats and skips, DENSE_RANK repeats and does not skip, and NTILE(n) splits rows into n equal buckets.

The four on the same data

Ananya, Priya and Rahul all earn 150,000, so the sample data is ideal for seeing ties.

SELECT first_name, salary,
       ROW_NUMBER() OVER w AS row_num,
       RANK()       OVER w AS rnk,
       DENSE_RANK() OVER w AS dense_rnk,
       NTILE(4)     OVER w AS quartile
FROM employees
WINDOW w AS (ORDER BY salary DESC)
ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id;
 first_name |  salary   | row_num | rnk | dense_rnk | quartile
------------+-----------+---------+-----+-----------+----------
 Meera      | 300000.00 |       1 |   1 |         1 |        1
 Vikram     | 220000.00 |       2 |   2 |         2 |        1
 Karan      | 160000.00 |       3 |   3 |         3 |        1
 Ananya     | 150000.00 |       4 |   4 |         4 |        2
 Priya      | 150000.00 |       5 |   4 |         4 |        2
 Rahul      | 150000.00 |       6 |   4 |         4 |        2
 Sunita     | 120000.00 |       7 |   7 |         5 |        3
 Arjun      | 110000.00 |       8 |   8 |         6 |        3
 Divya      |  90000.00 |       9 |   9 |         7 |        3
 Neha       |  75000.00 |      10 |  10 |         8 |        4
 Farhan     |  65000.00 |      11 |  11 |         9 |        4
 Isha       |  55000.00 |      12 |  12 |        10 |        4
(12 rows)
FunctionTiesAfter a tieUse for
ROW_NUMBER()different numbers (arbitrary among ties unless you add a tie-breaker)continues 1, 2, 3, 4, 5…exactly N rows; de-duplication; pagination
RANK()same numberskips: 4, 4, 4, then 7competition ranking ("top 3 places")
DENSE_RANK()same numberno gap: 4, 4, 4, then 5Nth highest value; "top 3 salaries"
NTILE(n)n buckets of equal size (sizes differ by at most one)n/aquartiles, deciles, splitting work

Ranking functions take no argument (except NTILE) and require an ORDER BY in the window. With ties, ROW_NUMBER is not deterministic: the database may number Ananya, Priya and Rahul in any order, and a different one on the next run. Add a unique tie-breaker (ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id) to make it repeatable. Also available: PERCENT_RANK() (relative rank from 0 to 1) and CUME_DIST() (share of rows at or below).

Ranking inside groups

PARTITION BY restarts the numbering for every group. Rank within each department:

SELECT dept_id, first_name, salary,
       DENSE_RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id ORDER BY salary DESC) AS pos
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 1
ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id;
 dept_id | first_name |  salary   | pos
---------+------------+-----------+-----
       1 | Vikram     | 220000.00 |   1
       1 | Priya      | 150000.00 |   2
       1 | Rahul      | 150000.00 |   2
       1 | Arjun      | 110000.00 |   3
       1 | Divya      |  90000.00 |   4
(5 rows)

Top N per group

The most common use. Wrap the ranking in a CTE and filter on it. "Top 3 salaries in each department":

WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT dept_id, first_name, salary,
           DENSE_RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id ORDER BY salary DESC) AS pos
    FROM employees
    WHERE dept_id IS NOT NULL
)
SELECT dept_id, first_name, salary, pos
FROM ranked
WHERE pos <= 3
ORDER BY dept_id, pos, first_name;
 dept_id | first_name |  salary   | pos
---------+------------+-----------+-----
       1 | Vikram     | 220000.00 |   1
       1 | Priya      | 150000.00 |   2
       1 | Rahul      | 150000.00 |   2
       1 | Arjun      | 110000.00 |   3
       2 | Karan      | 160000.00 |   1
       2 | Ananya     | 150000.00 |   2
       2 | Neha       |  75000.00 |   3
       3 | Sunita     | 120000.00 |   1
       3 | Farhan     |  65000.00 |   2
       3 | Isha       |  55000.00 |   3
(10 rows)

Department 1 returns four rows because two people share second place, so the top 3 salaries are 220000, 150000 and 110000. Which function you pick answers a different question:

QuestionUseDept 1 returns
The 3 highest salary valuesDENSE_RANK() <= 3Vikram, Priya, Rahul, Arjun (4 rows)
Everyone in the first 3 places (gaps after ties)RANK() <= 3Vikram, Priya, Rahul (3 rows)
Exactly 3 employeesROW_NUMBER() <= 3Vikram, Priya, Rahul (3 rows; tie broken by emp_id)

In an interview, ask which one the interviewer means; that question alone shows maturity. Two other ways to write top N per group, both without a window function:

-- Correlated subquery: fewer than 3 distinct higher salaries in the same department
SELECT e.dept_id, e.first_name, e.salary
FROM employees e
WHERE e.dept_id IS NOT NULL
  AND (SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT x.salary)
       FROM employees x
       WHERE x.dept_id = e.dept_id AND x.salary > e.salary) < 3;

-- LATERAL join (PostgreSQL): the top 3 rows of each department
SELECT d.name, t.first_name, t.salary
FROM departments d
CROSS JOIN LATERAL (SELECT first_name, salary FROM employees e
                    WHERE e.dept_id = d.dept_id
                    ORDER BY salary DESC LIMIT 3) AS t;

The first has the same meaning as DENSE_RANK and works on MySQL 5.7; the second returns exactly 3 rows per department and is fast with an index on (dept_id, salary DESC).

Other uses

  • The latest row per group: ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY order_date DESC) = 1 gives each customer's most recent order. PostgreSQL's DISTINCT ON (customer_id) is a shortcut (DISTINCT).
  • De-duplication: number the copies of each duplicate and delete those above 1 (Duplicates).
  • Buckets: NTILE(4) labels each employee's pay quartile; NTILE(100) approximates percentiles. With 12 rows and 4 buckets each gets 3; with 10 rows and 4 buckets the sizes are 3, 3, 2, 2 (the extra rows go to the first buckets).
  • Pagination with a numbered list (rarely better than LIMIT).

Rule: pick the function from the tie behaviour you need

Same rank for equal values and no gaps: DENSE_RANK. Same rank but gaps: RANK. Always unique: ROW_NUMBER, with a deterministic tie-breaker in ORDER BY.

Mistake: ROW_NUMBER for "Nth highest salary"

If three people share the second salary, ROW_NUMBER() = 2 returns just one of them and may skip the real second value entirely (the second row can hold the same salary as the first). For "Nth highest value" use DENSE_RANK.

Edge cases

  • NULLs in the ordering column sort last in ASC and first in DESC in PostgreSQL (MySQL: smallest). Use NULLS LAST or filter them out.
  • NTILE(5) on 3 rows gives buckets 1, 2, 3 and leaves 4 and 5 empty.
  • Rank values are BIGINT; comparing with an integer literal is fine.

Interview angle

"Difference between RANK, DENSE_RANK and ROW_NUMBER" with the 150000-tie example is the single most common window-function question. Then "top 3 salaries in each department" and "latest order per customer". Mention the CTE needed to filter on the rank.

Frames, running totals, LAG/LEAD and FIRST/LAST_VALUE

A frame narrows the window around the current row (ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW). Frames power running totals and moving averages. LAG/LEAD read the previous or next row, and LAST_VALUE needs a full frame to behave as expected.

What a frame is

Inside a partition, the frame is the set of rows the function actually reads for the current row. Syntax: ROWS | RANGE | GROUPS BETWEEN start AND end, where the boundaries are UNBOUNDED PRECEDING, n PRECEDING, CURRENT ROW, n FOLLOWING and UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING.

Defaults matter. With no ORDER BY in the window, the frame is the whole partition. With an ORDER BY and no explicit frame, it is RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW: from the first row up to the current row and all its peers (rows with the same sort value). That default causes the next two surprises.

ROWS vs RANGE

ROWS counts physical rows. RANGE works on values and includes every peer of the current row. The difference only shows when the sort column has ties. Department 1, running total by salary:

SELECT first_name, salary,
       SUM(salary) OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC)                                  AS default_range,
       SUM(salary) OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id
                         ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)      AS rows_frame
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 1
ORDER BY salary DESC, emp_id;
 first_name |  salary   | default_range | rows_frame
------------+-----------+---------------+------------
 Vikram     | 220000.00 |     220000.00 |  220000.00
 Priya      | 150000.00 |     520000.00 |  370000.00
 Rahul      | 150000.00 |     520000.00 |  520000.00
 Arjun      | 110000.00 |     630000.00 |  630000.00
 Divya      |  90000.00 |     720000.00 |  720000.00
(5 rows)

With the default RANGE, Priya and Rahul are peers (same salary), so both include both of them: 520000 each. ROWS with a unique ordering gives the true row-by-row running total. Rule of thumb: for running totals, write ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW and order by a unique key. PostgreSQL also has GROUPS mode (counts groups of peers). MySQL supports ROWS and RANGE.

Running totals and moving averages

Employees in hire order: the cumulative payroll and a 3-row moving average.

SELECT first_name, hire_date, salary,
       SUM(salary) OVER (ORDER BY hire_date, emp_id
                         ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS running_total,
       ROUND(AVG(salary) OVER (ORDER BY hire_date, emp_id
                         ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW), 2)      AS moving_avg_3
FROM employees
ORDER BY hire_date, emp_id;
 first_name | hire_date  |  salary   | running_total | moving_avg_3
------------+------------+-----------+---------------+--------------
 Meera      | 2015-04-01 | 300000.00 |     300000.00 |    300000.00
 Vikram     | 2016-07-15 | 220000.00 |     520000.00 |    260000.00
 Ananya     | 2017-02-20 | 150000.00 |     670000.00 |    223333.33
 Sunita     | 2018-06-11 | 120000.00 |     790000.00 |    163333.33
 Priya      | 2019-01-07 | 150000.00 |     940000.00 |    140000.00
 Rahul      | 2020-03-02 | 150000.00 |    1090000.00 |    140000.00
 Karan      | 2021-05-24 | 160000.00 |    1250000.00 |    153333.33
 Arjun      | 2022-08-16 | 110000.00 |    1360000.00 |    140000.00
 Neha       | 2023-09-04 |  75000.00 |    1435000.00 |    115000.00
 Farhan     | 2023-11-20 |  65000.00 |    1500000.00 |     83333.33
 Divya      | 2024-01-08 |  90000.00 |    1590000.00 |     76666.67
 Isha       | 2024-07-01 |  55000.00 |    1645000.00 |     70000.00
(12 rows)

The first two rows average over fewer rows because the frame is cut off at the start of the partition. The last running total, 1,645,000, equals the payroll. Add PARTITION BY dept_id and the running total restarts for each department. The same pattern gives "running total per customer" and "year-to-date sales".

Percent of total

A window aggregate without ORDER BY reads the whole partition, so each row can be divided by its group's total:

SELECT first_name, salary,
       ROUND(100 * salary / SUM(salary) OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id), 1) AS pct_of_dept
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 3
ORDER BY salary DESC;
 first_name |  salary   | pct_of_dept
------------+-----------+-------------
 Sunita     | 120000.00 |        50.0
 Farhan     |  65000.00 |        27.1
 Isha       |  55000.00 |        22.9
(3 rows)

LAG and LEAD

LAG(expr, offset, default) returns the value from offset rows before the current row (default offset 1); LEAD looks after. When no such row exists you get NULL, or the default you provide. They ignore frames and need an ORDER BY.

SELECT first_name, hire_date,
       LAG(first_name)             OVER w AS hired_before,
       LEAD(first_name, 1, 'nobody') OVER w AS hired_after
FROM employees
WHERE emp_id <= 5
WINDOW w AS (ORDER BY hire_date)
ORDER BY hire_date;
 first_name | hire_date  | hired_before | hired_after
------------+------------+--------------+-------------
 Meera      | 2015-04-01 | (null)       | Vikram
 Vikram     | 2016-07-15 | Meera        | Ananya
 Ananya     | 2017-02-20 | Vikram       | Sunita
 Sunita     | 2018-06-11 | Ananya       | Priya
 Priya      | 2019-01-07 | Sunita       | nobody
(5 rows)

Typical uses: the change from the previous month (revenue - LAG(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month), see month-over-month growth), days since the previous login, and comparing a row with the next event. Note the WHERE ran first: the window only saw five employees.

FIRST_VALUE, LAST_VALUE and NTH_VALUE

FIRST_VALUE(expr) returns the value from the first row of the frame, LAST_VALUE from the last row, NTH_VALUE(expr, n) from the n-th. Here is the famous gotcha, in HR (the highest salary first):

SELECT first_name, salary,
       FIRST_VALUE(first_name) OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC) AS top_earner,
       LAST_VALUE(first_name)  OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC) AS last_wrong,
       LAST_VALUE(first_name)  OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC
            ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING) AS lowest_paid
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 3
ORDER BY salary DESC;
 first_name |  salary   | top_earner | last_wrong | lowest_paid
------------+-----------+------------+------------+-------------
 Sunita     | 120000.00 | Sunita     | Sunita     | Isha
 Farhan     |  65000.00 | Sunita     | Farhan     | Isha
 Isha       |  55000.00 | Sunita     | Isha       | Isha
(3 rows)

The default frame ends at the current row, so "the last row of the frame" is simply the current row (last_wrong). To see the real last row, extend the frame to UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING. FIRST_VALUE works with the default frame because the frame always starts at the first row. Alternatively, flip the ordering and use FIRST_VALUE.

Rule: spell out the frame when it matters

Running totals: ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW with a unique order key. LAST_VALUE and NTH_VALUE: … AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING. Relying on the default hides the peer behaviour from the next reader.

Mistake: a running total ordered by a non-unique column

Ordering a running total by order_date when several orders share a date gives the same total for all of them (the default RANGE frame, peers included) or a random split inside the day with ROWS. Add a tie-breaker such as the id.

Edge cases

  • ROWS frames with n PRECEDING near the start just use fewer rows; no error, no padding.
  • RANGE with an offset (RANGE BETWEEN 7 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) needs one numeric or date ordering column and means "values within 7 of the current value".
  • LAG/LEAD with PARTITION BY do not cross partitions; the first row of each partition gets NULL.
  • Window frames need an ORDER BY for ROWS to be meaningful.

Interview angle

Likely: "running total per customer", "month-over-month change", "7-day moving average", "difference between ROWS and RANGE", and "why does LAST_VALUE return the current row?" The last two are the questions that separate memorised answers from understanding.

Views, updatable views and materialized views

A view is a saved query with a name that you use like a table. It stores no data, so it is always current. A materialized view (PostgreSQL) does store the result and must be refreshed. Views hide complexity and sensitive columns.

What and why

Some queries are used again and again: the join of orders, items and products, or the department summary every dashboard needs. Instead of pasting that SQL into ten places, save it once as a view. Callers write SELECT * FROM department_summary and the database substitutes the saved query. Reasons to use views:

  • Reuse: one definition of a business rule ("what counts as revenue").
  • Simplicity: hide joins and calculations from report writers and from the application.
  • Security: expose some columns or rows and not others.
  • Stable interface: change the tables underneath and keep the view's shape.

Creating a view

CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW department_summary AS
SELECT d.dept_id,
       d.name,
       COUNT(e.emp_id)              AS headcount,
       COALESCE(SUM(e.salary), 0)   AS payroll
FROM departments AS d
LEFT JOIN employees AS e ON e.dept_id = d.dept_id
GROUP BY d.dept_id, d.name;

SELECT * FROM department_summary ORDER BY dept_id;
 dept_id |    name     | headcount |  payroll
---------+-------------+-----------+-----------
       1 | Engineering |         5 | 720000.00
       2 | Sales       |         3 | 385000.00
       3 | HR          |         3 | 240000.00
       4 | Marketing   |         0 |         0
(4 rows)

The view stores only the SELECT. When you query it, the database expands the definition into your query and optimizes the whole thing together, so a filter you add on the view can still use the base tables' indexes. Querying a view is exactly as fast as running its query. Remove it with DROP VIEW department_summary. CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW can change the query, but in PostgreSQL it can only add columns at the end; renaming, removing or retyping columns needs DROP and CREATE. Avoid SELECT * inside a view: the column list is fixed when the view is created, and later table changes do not flow in.

Views for security

Give a role access to the view, not the table. The payroll team sees salaries, everybody else gets the directory without them:

CREATE VIEW employee_directory AS
SELECT emp_id, first_name, last_name, email, dept_id     -- no salary, no manager
FROM employees;

REVOKE ALL ON employees FROM reporting_role;
GRANT SELECT ON employee_directory TO reporting_role;

A PostgreSQL view runs with the privileges of its owner by default, which is what makes this work. Since PostgreSQL 15 you can create it WITH (security_invoker = true) to check the caller's rights instead. Row-level restrictions are also possible with a WHERE in the view, or with row level security policies (Module 5).

Updatable views

A simple view (one table, no GROUP BY, DISTINCT, aggregates, window functions or set operations) is automatically updatable in PostgreSQL (since 9.3) and MySQL: INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on the view change the base table. A view with a join, an aggregate or DISTINCT is read-only unless you add INSTEAD OF triggers.

CREATE VIEW engineering_staff AS
SELECT emp_id, first_name, last_name, email, dept_id, salary
FROM employees
WHERE dept_id = 1
WITH CHECK OPTION;

UPDATE engineering_staff SET salary = 95000 WHERE emp_id = 8;     -- fine: Divya stays visible
UPDATE engineering_staff SET dept_id = 2 WHERE emp_id = 8;
-- ERROR:  new row violates check option for view "engineering_staff"

WITH CHECK OPTION forbids changes through the view that would make the row disappear from the view (moving Divya to Sales). Without it, the update would succeed and she would vanish from engineering_staff.

Materialized views

A materialized view runs the query once and stores the result like a table. Reads are fast (and the result can be indexed), but the data is only as fresh as the last refresh. Perfect for expensive aggregations: a dashboard that reads yesterday's totals.

CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW monthly_revenue AS
SELECT DATE_TRUNC('month', o.order_date)::DATE AS month,
       SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price)         AS revenue
FROM orders AS o
JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.order_id
WHERE o.status <> 'CANCELLED'
GROUP BY DATE_TRUNC('month', o.order_date);

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_monthly_revenue_month ON monthly_revenue (month);

SELECT * FROM monthly_revenue ORDER BY month;

REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY monthly_revenue;   -- rebuild without blocking readers
   month    | revenue
------------+---------
 2026-01-01 | 5256.00
 2026-02-01 | 2797.00
 2026-03-01 | 7594.00
 2026-04-01 | 2778.00
(4 rows)

REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW recomputes everything and locks readers while it works. CONCURRENTLY keeps the old data readable, but needs a unique index on the view. Schedule refreshes with pg_cron, an operating-system cron job, or a Spring @Scheduled task. MySQL has no materialized views: build a summary table and refresh it with a scheduled job or event.

ViewMaterialized viewSummary table you maintain
Stores datanoyesyes
Always currentyesno, until refreshedno, until your job runs
Speedsame as the underlying queryfast reads, can be indexedfast reads
Maintenancenonea refresh scheduleyour own code

Views and Spring

From Java a view looks like a table. You can map a read-only JPA entity to a view (@Entity @Immutable on a class with @Table(name = "department_summary")), or select from it with a native query or a Spring Data projection. Flyway or Liquibase migrations create the view like any other object, in the right order after its tables.

Rule: a view depends on its tables

You cannot drop or retype a column used by a view (ERROR: cannot drop column salary of table employees because other objects depend on it). Drop the view first, or drop with CASCADE and recreate it. Keep view definitions in version-controlled migrations.

Mistake: building views on views on views

Each layer hides the real cost, and the optimizer may not flatten them well. Nested views are hard to debug and easy to slow down. Prefer a few well-named views over a tower of them.

Edge cases

  • An ORDER BY inside a view definition is not a promise about the order of the final result; sort in the outer query.
  • A materialized view is not updated by changes to its base tables, not even in the same transaction.
  • Views cannot take parameters. For that use a function that returns a table (Functions, procedures and triggers).
  • MySQL view algorithms: MERGE (inlined) or TEMPTABLE (materialized temporarily, slower); MySQL picks by itself.

Interview angle

"What is a view? Does it store data?" (no, a stored query), "view vs materialized view" (freshness vs speed), "can you update through a view" (simple views yes, with WITH CHECK OPTION to protect the filter), "why use views" (reuse, security, abstraction).

Functions, stored procedures and triggers

A function is reusable code stored in the database that returns a value, a procedure is called with CALL and can control transactions, and a trigger runs code automatically when rows change. They are powerful, and many teams use them sparingly.

Why code lives in the database at all

SQL alone has no variables, loops or if-statements. Databases add a procedural language to the server: PL/pgSQL in PostgreSQL, a SQL/PSM dialect in MySQL. Code stored there runs next to the data, so it avoids network round trips, can be shared by every application that connects, and can enforce rules nobody can bypass. The price is discussed at the end of this article.

Functions

A function takes arguments and returns a value (or a table). Simple ones can be written in plain SQL:

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION dept_headcount(p_dept INT)
RETURNS INT
LANGUAGE sql STABLE
AS $$
    SELECT COUNT(*)::INT FROM employees WHERE dept_id = p_dept;
$$;

SELECT dept_headcount(1) AS engineering, dept_headcount(4) AS marketing;
 engineering | marketing
-------------+-----------
           5 |         0
(1 row)

The body sits between $$ … $$ (dollar quoting, so you need no escaping for single quotes). STABLE tells the planner that the function does not change data and gives the same answer inside one statement; the options are IMMUTABLE (same inputs always give the same output, can be used in an index), STABLE and VOLATILE (the default; may change anything). A function can also return a table, which acts like a parameterised view: RETURNS TABLE (name TEXT, salary NUMERIC) AS $$ SELECT … $$, called with SELECT * FROM top_earners(3).

With PL/pgSQL you get variables, conditions and loops, and errors:

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION salary_grade(p_salary NUMERIC)
RETURNS TEXT
LANGUAGE plpgsql IMMUTABLE
AS $$
BEGIN
    IF p_salary IS NULL THEN
        RETURN NULL;
    ELSIF p_salary >= 200000 THEN
        RETURN 'Senior';
    ELSIF p_salary >= 100000 THEN
        RETURN 'Mid';
    END IF;
    RETURN 'Junior';
END;
$$;

SELECT first_name, salary_grade(salary) AS grade FROM employees WHERE emp_id IN (1, 8);
 first_name | grade
------------+--------
 Meera      | Senior
 Divya      | Junior
(2 rows)

Stored procedures

A procedure (CREATE PROCEDURE, PostgreSQL 11 and later; MySQL has them too) is invoked with CALL, does not return a value in a SELECT, and, unlike a function, can COMMIT or ROLLBACK inside it. It fits batch work and multi-step operations.

CREATE OR REPLACE PROCEDURE raise_salary(p_emp INT, p_percent NUMERIC)
LANGUAGE plpgsql
AS $$
BEGIN
    IF p_percent <= 0 OR p_percent > 50 THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Percent must be between 0 and 50, got %', p_percent;
    END IF;

    UPDATE employees
    SET salary = ROUND(salary * (1 + p_percent / 100), 2)
    WHERE emp_id = p_emp;

    IF NOT FOUND THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Employee % does not exist', p_emp;
    END IF;
END;
$$;

CALL raise_salary(8, 10);      -- Divya: 90000 becomes 99000
CALL raise_salary(99, 10);     -- ERROR:  Employee 99 does not exist

Spring calls them with JdbcTemplate / SimpleJdbcCall, or in JPA with @Procedure and @NamedStoredProcedureQuery. In MySQL the syntax needs a changed statement delimiter, because the body contains semicolons:

DELIMITER $$
CREATE PROCEDURE raise_salary(IN p_emp INT, IN p_percent DECIMAL(5,2))
BEGIN
    UPDATE employees SET salary = ROUND(salary * (1 + p_percent / 100), 2) WHERE emp_id = p_emp;
END$$
DELIMITER ;
CALL raise_salary(8, 10);

Triggers

A trigger runs a function automatically BEFORE or AFTER an INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE, once FOR EACH ROW (or once per statement). Inside, NEW is the new row and OLD the old one. In PostgreSQL a trigger is two objects: a trigger function that RETURNS TRIGGER, and the CREATE TRIGGER that attaches it.

Example 1: keep an updated_at column current.

ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now();

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION set_updated_at()
RETURNS TRIGGER
LANGUAGE plpgsql
AS $$
BEGIN
    NEW.updated_at := now();      -- change the row before it is written
    RETURN NEW;
END;
$$;

CREATE TRIGGER trg_products_updated_at
BEFORE UPDATE ON products
FOR EACH ROW
EXECUTE FUNCTION set_updated_at();

Now every UPDATE products … refreshes updated_at even if the application forgot. (EXECUTE FUNCTION is PostgreSQL 11+ syntax; older versions say EXECUTE PROCEDURE.) MySQL does this without a trigger: updated_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP ON UPDATE CURRENT_TIMESTAMP.

Example 2: an audit log of salary changes.

CREATE TABLE salary_audit (
    audit_id    BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    emp_id      INT           NOT NULL,
    old_salary  NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL,
    new_salary  NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL,
    changed_at  TIMESTAMPTZ   NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    changed_by  TEXT          NOT NULL DEFAULT current_user
);

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION log_salary_change()
RETURNS TRIGGER
LANGUAGE plpgsql
AS $$
BEGIN
    INSERT INTO salary_audit (emp_id, old_salary, new_salary)
    VALUES (OLD.emp_id, OLD.salary, NEW.salary);
    RETURN NEW;
END;
$$;

CREATE TRIGGER trg_employees_salary_audit
AFTER UPDATE OF salary ON employees
FOR EACH ROW
WHEN (OLD.salary IS DISTINCT FROM NEW.salary)
EXECUTE FUNCTION log_salary_change();

UPDATE employees SET salary = 95000 WHERE emp_id = 8;
SELECT audit_id, emp_id, old_salary, new_salary, changed_by FROM salary_audit;
 audit_id | emp_id | old_salary | new_salary | changed_by
----------+--------+------------+------------+------------
        1 |      8 |   90000.00 |   95000.00 | app_user
(1 row)

The trigger fires only when the salary column changes and the value really differs (the WHEN clause), and writes to the audit table in the same transaction: if the update rolls back, so does the audit row. That is why audit logs are the best use of triggers: nobody can forget them, whatever application or script changes the data.

TimingUse forNotes
BEFORE row triggerchange or validate NEW (timestamps, defaults, custom checks)return NEW to continue, NULL to skip the row
AFTER row triggeraudit log, update a summary table, notifyreturn value ignored
INSTEAD OFmake a complex view updatableviews only
Statement-levelonce per statement, regardless of the number of rowsuse transition tables for bulk logic

Pros and cons

Pros

  • Runs next to the data: fewer round trips for bulk work.
  • One implementation shared by every application and script.
  • Cannot be bypassed; ideal for audit trails and integrity rules.
  • Permissions can allow a procedure without allowing direct table access.

Cons

  • Hidden behaviour: an UPDATE that "also does something else" surprises developers.
  • Harder to test, debug, version and review than Java; weaker tooling.
  • Ties you to one database's dialect; scaling logic means scaling the database, the hardest tier to scale.
  • Deployment is split: the code and its database objects must be released together.

In real projects: why many teams keep business logic in the application

A Spring Boot team usually keeps business rules in Java services, where they are unit-tested, versioned with the code and portable. They use the database for what it is best at: constraints, indexes, transactions, sometimes audit triggers and the updated_at trigger, and stored procedures for heavy batch jobs or legacy systems (banks and ERPs often have thousands). In Spring, @CreatedDate and @LastModifiedDate (Spring Data auditing) or Hibernate's @UpdateTimestamp replace the updated_at trigger when only the application writes to the table. Triggers are the right tool when other writers exist.

Rule: triggers must be fast, small and idempotent-safe

A trigger runs inside the triggering statement's transaction, for every affected row. A slow trigger slows every write; a trigger that updates the same table can fire itself again (recursion). Keep them short, and document each one where developers will find it.

Mistake: business workflow in triggers

"When an order is inserted, a trigger reduces stock, sends an email row, updates the customer's total…" becomes a chain no one can follow, and a bulk load of 100,000 orders runs 100,000 extra statements. Put workflows in a service and use triggers only for simple, local rules.

Edge cases

  • Row triggers do not fire for TRUNCATE; statement-level truncate triggers do.
  • A BEFORE trigger returning NULL silently cancels the operation for that row. RAISE EXCEPTION cancels the whole statement.
  • Bulk loads such as COPY still fire triggers (unless disabled with ALTER TABLE … DISABLE TRIGGER).
  • In MySQL, triggers are not fired by cascading foreign-key actions.

Interview angle

"Function vs procedure" (function returns a value and is used inside queries; procedure is called with CALL and can manage transactions), "what is a trigger and when would you use one" (audit, timestamps), and the architecture question "would you put business logic in stored procedures?" A balanced answer lists the pros and cons above and says you prefer the application layer for business rules.

Classic query: second and Nth highest salary

"Find the second highest salary" is the most asked SQL interview question. Know three answers: a subquery with MAX, LIMIT … OFFSET on distinct values, and DENSE_RANK, then turn the last into an Nth-highest function.

The question and its traps

Distinct salaries in the sample data, highest first: 300000, 220000, 160000, 150000, 120000, 110000, 90000, 75000, 65000, 55000. Three people share 150000, which is why the question says "highest salary", not "second row". Clarify in the interview: do ties count once (the usual meaning)? What should be returned if there is no second salary (NULL, not an error)?

How each approach works

Knowing the mechanics lets you explain the answer, not just recite it.

  • MAX approach. The inner query finds the top value (300000). The outer query throws that value away with salary < 300000 and takes the maximum of what is left (220000). It is two passes over the table, or two index lookups when salary is indexed.
  • OFFSET approach. SELECT DISTINCT salary makes a list of unique values, ORDER BY … DESC sorts it, OFFSET 1 skips the top value and LIMIT 1 keeps the next one. Think "sort the distinct values, skip N-1".
  • DENSE_RANK approach. The window function numbers the rows by salary, giving tied rows the same number and never leaving gaps (1, 2, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5 …). Then a plain WHERE rnk = N picks the group. The CTE is needed because a window function cannot be used directly in WHERE (it runs after it; see Logical order of a query).

The rank each tool gives to the sample salaries shows why the choice matters:

SalaryROW_NUMBERRANKDENSE_RANK
300000111
220000222
160000333
150000 (three people)4, 5, 64, 4, 44, 4, 4
120000775

The fifth highest salary is 120000. Only DENSE_RANK = 5 finds it. RANK = 5 returns no row at all (the ranks jump from 4 to 7), and ROW_NUMBER = 5 returns one of the three 150000 earners, chosen arbitrarily. Details of the three functions are in ranking functions.

Approach 1: subquery with MAX

SELECT MAX(salary) AS second_highest
FROM employees
WHERE salary < (SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees);
 second_highest
----------------
      220000.00
(1 row)

Works everywhere, returns NULL if no second value exists (MAX of nothing), and is easy to explain. It does not generalise to the Nth value without nesting more.

Approach 2: LIMIT with OFFSET on distinct values

SELECT (SELECT DISTINCT salary
        FROM employees
        ORDER BY salary DESC
        LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1) AS second_highest;

Same output. The outer SELECT ( … ) is the trick that turns "no row" into NULL; without it the query returns an empty result. Forgetting DISTINCT is the classic bug: the rows 150000, 150000, 150000 count as three positions, so the fifth "row" is 150000 while the fifth highest salary is 120000. Standard SQL spelling: OFFSET 1 ROWS FETCH FIRST 1 ROW ONLY.

Approach 3: DENSE_RANK

WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT first_name, salary,
           DENSE_RANK() OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC) AS rnk
    FROM employees
)
SELECT first_name, salary
FROM ranked
WHERE rnk = 2;
 first_name |  salary
------------+-----------
 Vikram     | 220000.00
(1 row)

This version returns the people and returns all of them when several share the Nth salary: change to rnk = 4 and you get Ananya, Priya and Rahul (150000). It is the answer to give when the interviewer asks "who earns the Nth highest?".

Nth highest salary per department

The usual follow-up. Add PARTITION BY dept_id so the numbering restarts in every department:

WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT dept_id, first_name, salary,
           DENSE_RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id ORDER BY salary DESC) AS rnk
    FROM employees
)
SELECT dept_id, first_name, salary
FROM ranked
WHERE rnk = 2
ORDER BY dept_id, first_name;
 dept_id | first_name |  salary
---------+------------+-----------
       1 | Priya      | 150000.00
       1 | Rahul      | 150000.00
       2 | Ananya     | 150000.00
       3 | Farhan     |  65000.00
(4 rows)

Engineering has salaries 220000, 150000, 150000, 110000 and 90000, so both 150000 earners are second. Meera is alone in her group (no department), so there is no second salary and she does not appear. A department with one distinct salary simply has no row in the result; if the question wants one row per department with NULL for "none", join the result back to departments with a LEFT JOIN.

Nth highest as a function

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION nth_highest_salary(n INT)
RETURNS NUMERIC
LANGUAGE sql STABLE
AS $$
    SELECT (SELECT DISTINCT salary
            FROM employees
            ORDER BY salary DESC
            LIMIT 1 OFFSET n - 1);
$$;

SELECT nth_highest_salary(3) AS third, nth_highest_salary(20) AS twentieth;
   third   | twentieth
-----------+-----------
 160000.00 |    (null)
(1 row)

MySQL does not accept an expression after LIMIT or OFFSET inside a function, so the usual form first computes the offset in a variable:

CREATE FUNCTION getNthHighestSalary(N INT) RETURNS DECIMAL(10,2)
DETERMINISTIC
BEGIN
    SET N = N - 1;
    RETURN (SELECT DISTINCT salary FROM employees ORDER BY salary DESC LIMIT 1 OFFSET N);
END;

A fourth, window-free way that works on any version is the counting definition: a salary is the Nth highest if exactly N-1 distinct salaries are above it.

SELECT DISTINCT e1.salary
FROM employees e1
WHERE (SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT e2.salary) FROM employees e2 WHERE e2.salary > e1.salary) = 2;
-- 160000.00  (the third highest)

It is a correlated subquery, so it is slow on big tables; mention it as the "no window functions" answer.

ApproachHandles tiesMissing Nth valueNotes
MAX + subqueryyesNULLonly practical for 2nd
DISTINCT … OFFSETyes (needs DISTINCT)NULL if wrappedshort; fast with an index on salary
DENSE_RANKyesempty resultreturns the people; most flexible
Counting subqueryyesempty resultworks on old MySQL; slow

Edge cases: NULLs, one value, empty table

  • NULL salaries. The sample column is NOT NULL, but in other tables it may not be. PostgreSQL sorts NULL first in DESC order, so ORDER BY salary DESC LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1 can return NULL as the "highest". Add WHERE salary IS NOT NULL (or NULLS LAST). MySQL sorts NULL last in DESC, so the same query behaves differently there. MAX ignores NULL, so approach 1 is safe.
  • Only one distinct salary. All employees earn the same: there is no second value. Approach 1 returns NULL, approach 2 (wrapped) returns NULL, approach 3 returns no rows.
  • Empty table. MAX of nothing is NULL, so approach 1 returns NULL as well.
  • N larger than the number of values. nth_highest_salary(20) returns NULL as shown. A negative N makes OFFSET fail with a "OFFSET must not be negative" error.

Rule: say what "Nth highest" means before you write SQL

Two readings exist. Nth highest value: ties count once (what this article solves). Nth row in the sorted list: ties take several places. The first is the standard interview meaning. Return the salary for a "value" question and the employees for a "who" question. State your choice in one sentence.

Convention: pick by what you need back

Need only the number, once, on any database: the MAX subquery or the wrapped OFFSET. Need the people, ties, or a per-group answer: DENSE_RANK. Need it in a reusable function or API: the wrapped OFFSET inside a function, as above. In an application, prefer one query with DENSE_RANK over loading all salaries into Java and sorting them.

Mistake: ORDER BY salary DESC LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1 without DISTINCT

Without DISTINCT it counts rows, not values: the 4th, 5th and 6th rows all hold 150000, so "fifth highest" comes out wrong, and it also gives the wrong second value whenever two people share the top salary. Rank values, not rows.

Mistake: RANK() for the Nth highest value

Wrong: no row for N = 5

SELECT salary FROM (
    SELECT salary, RANK() OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC) AS rnk
    FROM employees) t
WHERE rnk = 5;        -- 0 rows: ranks go 4, 4, 4, 7

Right: DENSE_RANK has no gaps

SELECT DISTINCT salary FROM (
    SELECT salary, DENSE_RANK() OVER (ORDER BY salary DESC) AS rnk
    FROM employees) t
WHERE rnk = 5;        -- 120000.00

Interview angle

Give approach 1 first (always works), then the DENSE_RANK version for Nth and ties. Say what you return when there is no such salary. Variants: "Nth highest per department" (add PARTITION BY dept_id), "second highest without LIMIT or window functions" (approach 1).

Expect these follow-ups: "what if two people share the top salary?", "what if there is no second salary?", "do it for every department", and "how would you make it fast on 50 million rows?" (an index on salary lets the database walk the index from the top and stop; the counting subquery cannot use it well).

Classic query: find and delete duplicates

Find duplicates with GROUP BY … HAVING COUNT(*) > 1, see the extra rows with a window ROW_NUMBER, delete all but one copy with a self-join or window delete, and then add a UNIQUE constraint so it cannot happen again.

Duplicates enter a table in a few predictable ways: a form submitted twice, a retry after a timeout (the first request had actually succeeded), a CSV imported twice, or a missing UNIQUE constraint in the first place. The fix always has two parts: clean the existing rows, then close the door so it cannot happen again. First decide what "duplicate" means: the same email, or the same name and date of birth? That decision is the list of columns you group by.

A table with duplicates

The sample employees.email is unique, so create a scratch table that lacks the constraint, like a badly imported sign-up list:

CREATE TEMP TABLE signups (
    id    INT PRIMARY KEY,
    email VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO signups VALUES
    (1, 'a@x.com'), (2, 'b@x.com'), (3, 'a@x.com'),
    (4, 'c@x.com'), (5, 'b@x.com'), (6, 'a@x.com');

Finding duplicates

SELECT email, COUNT(*) AS copies
FROM signups
GROUP BY email
HAVING COUNT(*) > 1
ORDER BY email;
  email  | copies
---------+--------
 a@x.com |      3
 b@x.com |      2
(2 rows)

To see the rows themselves, with their ids, number the copies of each email. Row number 1 is the original, higher numbers are the extras:

SELECT id, email,
       ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY email ORDER BY id) AS copy_no
FROM signups
ORDER BY email, id;
 id |  email  | copy_no
----+---------+---------
  1 | a@x.com |       1
  3 | a@x.com |       2
  6 | a@x.com |       3
  2 | b@x.com |       1
  5 | b@x.com |       2
  4 | c@x.com |       1
(6 rows)

Another way is a self join: FROM signups a JOIN signups b ON a.email = b.email AND a.id < b.id returns each duplicate pair once. For several columns together (name and date of birth), group by all of them.

How GROUP BY … HAVING finds them. The database puts rows with the same email into one group, counts each group, and HAVING keeps only the groups with a count above 1. Each returned row stands for several table rows. That is why this query tells you which values repeat but not which ids; use the window query or a join when you need the ids.

Duplicates over several columns, and near-duplicates

Group by every column that together defines a duplicate. Another scratch table, where the same person is entered twice in different cities:

CREATE TEMP TABLE contacts (id INT PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(30), city VARCHAR(30));
INSERT INTO contacts VALUES
    (1,'Asha','Pune'), (2,'Asha','Pune'), (3,'Asha','Delhi'),
    (4,'Bilal','Delhi'), (5,'Bilal','Delhi');

SELECT name, city, COUNT(*) AS copies
FROM contacts
GROUP BY name, city
HAVING COUNT(*) > 1
ORDER BY name;
 name  | city  | copies
-------+-------+--------
 Asha  | Pune  |      2
 Bilal | Delhi |      2
(2 rows)

Asha in Delhi is a different group, so she is not reported. Real data is messier: 'A@x.com', 'a@x.com' and 'a@x.com ' (trailing space) are the same address to a human but three values to GROUP BY. Normalise inside the query: GROUP BY LOWER(TRIM(email)). Clean the data on the way in, too.

Deleting duplicates, keeping one row

Keep the row with the lowest id. Three ways; each deletes ids 3, 5 and 6 and leaves 1, 2 and 4. Try them inside BEGIN … ROLLBACK.

DELETE FROM signups AS s
USING signups AS keep
WHERE s.email = keep.email
  AND s.id > keep.id;
DELETE FROM signups
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT MIN(id) FROM signups GROUP BY email);
DELETE FROM signups
WHERE id IN (
    SELECT id
    FROM (SELECT id,
                 ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY email ORDER BY id) AS rn
          FROM signups) AS t
    WHERE rn > 1
);
 id |  email
----+---------
  1 | a@x.com
  2 | b@x.com
  4 | c@x.com
(3 rows)

Approach 2 is safe here because MIN(id) is never NULL; with a nullable column NOT IN is the trap from Join traps. In MySQL you cannot select from the table you delete from in a subquery (ERROR 1093), so use a join delete: DELETE s FROM signups s JOIN signups k ON s.email = k.email AND s.id > k.id;. If the table has no id column, PostgreSQL offers the hidden physical row id ctid to tell identical rows apart (DELETE FROM t WHERE ctid NOT IN (SELECT MIN(ctid) FROM t GROUP BY …)).

Another option for a huge table is to build a clean copy and swap: CREATE TABLE signups_clean AS SELECT DISTINCT ON (email) * FROM signups ORDER BY email, id;.

Before deleting, look at what would go. DELETE … RETURNING in PostgreSQL shows the rows it removed, which is handy for an audit log or a quick check inside a transaction:

BEGIN;
DELETE FROM signups s
USING signups keep
WHERE s.email = keep.email AND s.id > keep.id
RETURNING s.id, s.email;      -- 3 a@x.com, 5 b@x.com, 6 a@x.com
ROLLBACK;                     -- change to COMMIT when the list is right

Keeping the newest instead. Change the tie-breaker. With the window version, number the copies from the other end (ORDER BY id DESC) and still delete rn > 1; with MIN(id) use MAX(id). "Keep the most complete row" needs a rule such as "the row with the fewest NULLs", usually a window ORDER BY on a computed score.

Which method to choose

MethodStrengthWeakness
Self-join deleteShort; easy to reason aboutDifferent syntax in MySQL and PostgreSQL; slow without an index on the key column
NOT IN (SELECT MIN(id) …)ReadableNULL trap; MySQL error 1093
ROW_NUMBER then delete rn > 1Very flexible keep rule; the SELECT doubles as a previewNeeds MySQL 8 or PostgreSQL
