  • Does PostgreSQL index a foreign key automatically? No. MySQL InnoDB does.

Many-to-many relationships and join tables

A many-to-many relationship needs a third table, the join (junction) table, with one foreign key to each side; its primary key is either the pair of foreign keys or a surrogate id plus a unique constraint on the pair.

Why two tables are not enough

A student takes many courses, and a course has many students. A foreign key in students can point to only one course; a foreign key in courses can point to only one student. Neither side can hold "many". The fix is a third table where each row says "this student takes this course".

The join table

CREATE TABLE students (
    id   BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE courses (
    id    BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    title TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE enrollments (
    student_id  BIGINT NOT NULL,
    course_id   BIGINT NOT NULL,
    enrolled_on DATE   NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE,
    grade       TEXT,
    PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_enrollments_student FOREIGN KEY (student_id)
        REFERENCES students (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    CONSTRAINT fk_enrollments_course FOREIGN KEY (course_id)
        REFERENCES courses (id) ON DELETE CASCADE
);

-- The primary key index serves "courses of a student" (leftmost column).
-- "Students of a course" needs its own index on the second column.
CREATE INDEX idx_enrollments_course_id ON enrollments (course_id);

INSERT INTO students (name) VALUES ('Asha'), ('Bilal');
INSERT INTO courses (title) VALUES ('Databases'), ('Java'), ('Spring Boot');
INSERT INTO enrollments (student_id, course_id, grade) VALUES
    (1, 1, 'A'), (1, 2, 'B'), (2, 1, 'B'), (2, 3, 'A');

The primary key (student_id, course_id) does two jobs: it makes each row unique and it forbids enrolling the same student in the same course twice. Both columns are part of the key, so both are NOT NULL automatically.

-- Courses of Asha
SELECT c.title, e.grade
FROM enrollments e
JOIN courses c  ON c.id = e.course_id
JOIN students s ON s.id = e.student_id
WHERE s.name = 'Asha';

-- Students per course, including courses nobody took
SELECT c.title, COUNT(e.student_id) AS students
FROM courses c
LEFT JOIN enrollments e ON e.course_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.title
ORDER BY c.title;
   title   | grade
-----------+-------
 Databases | A
 Java      | B
(2 rows)

    title    | students
-------------+----------
 Databases   |        2
 Java        |        1
 Spring Boot |        1
(3 rows)

Two styles of primary key for a join table

Composite key (a_id, b_id)Surrogate id plus UNIQUE (a_id, b_id)
Duplicate pairsImpossibleImpossible while the unique constraint exists
Columns and indexesFewer; the key is the indexOne more column and one more index
Can the same pair repeat?NoYes, if you drop the unique constraint (a repeat purchase)
Other tables pointing at a link rowThey need two columnsThey need one column
JPANeeds @EmbeddedId or @IdClassPlain @Id

Use the composite key for a pure link that can exist only once (product to category, user to role). Use a surrogate key when the same pair can legitimately appear again, or when the link row is itself referenced by other tables.

Extra columns: the association entity

The join table may carry facts about the relationship itself: enrolled_on and grade above, or quantity and unit_price on order_items. When a join table has its own columns and its own life, it is a real entity. Give it a business name (enrollments, order_items, loans) rather than student_courses. This matters in JPA: @ManyToMany works only for a pure link with two columns. As soon as the link has extra columns, map the join table as an entity with two @ManyToOne associations (see Spring Boot · Module 3).

Many-to-many to itself

A user follows many users and is followed by many. Both columns point to the same table:

CREATE TABLE follows (
    follower_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    followed_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    followed_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (follower_id, followed_id),
    CHECK (follower_id <> followed_id)   -- nobody follows themselves
);
CREATE INDEX idx_follows_followed_id ON follows (followed_id);

Here the order of the pair matters (A follows B is not B follows A). For a symmetric relationship such as friendship, store each pair once with CHECK (user_a_id < user_b_id), so (3, 7) is allowed and (7, 3) is not.

Rule: a join table must forbid duplicate pairs

Always make the pair unique, by a composite primary key or a UNIQUE constraint, and make both foreign keys NOT NULL. Otherwise "enrolled twice" becomes a data bug that shows up as double counts in reports.

Convention: name it after the business fact, then index the second column

Prefer a noun the business uses (enrollments, order_items, memberships). For a plain link use both table names, such as book_authors or user_roles. A composite primary key indexes the pair in order, so add an index that starts with the second column (idx_enrollments_course_id) when you also look up from that side.

Mistake: a list of ids in one column

Wrong: comma-separated ids

CREATE TABLE students (
    id         BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    name       TEXT,
    course_ids TEXT   -- '1,2,5'
);
-- no foreign keys, no joins, LIKE '%2%' also matches 12 and 22

Right: one row per link

CREATE TABLE enrollments (
    student_id BIGINT REFERENCES students (id),
    course_id  BIGINT REFERENCES courses (id),
    PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id)
);

An array or JSONB column of ids has the same problem: the database cannot check that the ids exist, and cannot cascade deletes.

Edge cases

  • COUNT with LEFT JOIN: COUNT(*) counts the one row produced for a course with no students; COUNT(e.student_id) counts only real rows and returns 0.
  • Three-way links: a fact that relates three things (doctor, patient, clinic) is one join table with three foreign keys and a key over all three.
  • ON DELETE CASCADE on both sides is normal for a pure link: deleting a student removes only their link rows, never the courses.

In real projects

You will see this pattern everywhere: user_roles and role_permissions (security), product_categories and product_tags (a Flipkart-style catalogue), order_items (the link between orders and products, with quantity and the price at that time). Spring Data JPA models the first two with @ManyToMany and the last with an entity.

Interview angle

  • How do you model many-to-many in a relational database? A join table with a foreign key to each side; its primary key is the pair, or a surrogate id with a unique pair.
  • Where do attributes of the relationship go? Into the join table (quantity on order_items).
  • Why not store a list of ids in a column? It breaks first normal form, loses foreign key checks and cascades, and makes joins and searches slow or impossible.
  • Composite key or surrogate key for the join table? Composite for a pure unique link; surrogate when pairs can repeat or the row is referenced elsewhere.

Keys: super, candidate, primary, alternate, unique, foreign, composite

A key is a column (or set of columns) that identifies rows: of all the keys that could be the identifier (candidate keys), you pick one as the primary key; the rest are alternate keys, and a foreign key points at another table's key.

Why there are so many names

Interviewers love this topic because the words overlap. They all answer one question: "which columns identify a row?" Start from one idea: a super key is any set of columns whose values are unique across the table. Everything else is a refinement of it. Take this table of employees:

CREATE TABLE employees (
    id            BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    email         TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    pan           CHAR(10) UNIQUE,            -- government tax id, optional
    name          TEXT NOT NULL,
    department_id BIGINT REFERENCES departments (id)
);
 id |      email       |    pan     | name  | department_id
----+------------------+------------+-------+---------------
  1 | asha@corp.in     | ABCDE1234F | Asha  |             10
  2 | bilal@corp.in    | PQRST5678K | Bilal |             10
  3 | chitra@corp.in   |            | Chitra|             20
KeyMeaningIn employees
Super keyAny set of columns that is unique per row. It may carry useless extra columns.{id}, {id, name}, {email, name}, {pan} …
Candidate keyA minimal super key: remove any column and it is no longer unique.{id}, {email}, {pan}
Primary keyThe one candidate key you choose as the official identifier. Unique and NOT NULL; one per table.id
Alternate keyA candidate key that was not chosen as the primary key.email, pan
Unique keyHow you implement an alternate key: a UNIQUE constraint. A table can have many; columns may allow NULL. UNIQUE (email), UNIQUE (pan)
Foreign keyColumns that must match a primary or unique key in another (or the same) table.department_id → departments.id
Composite keyA key made of two or more columns. It can be primary, unique or foreign.PRIMARY KEY (order_id, menu_item_id) on a join table

Super key and candidate key are theory words; you never write them in SQL. You write PRIMARY KEY, UNIQUE and FOREIGN KEY. The theory tells you how to choose: list every candidate key first, then pick the primary key from them.

Primary key rules

  • It is unique and never NULL. The database builds a unique index for it.
  • A table has at most one primary key (which may be composite).
  • It should be stable: if a value can change (an email), every foreign key pointing at it must change too. ON UPDATE CASCADE hides the cost but not the risk.
  • It should be small, because every foreign key and every index entry repeats it.

Unique key vs primary key

Primary keyUnique key
How many per tableOneMany
NULL allowedNoYes. PostgreSQL allows many NULLs (each is "unknown", so not equal). SQL Server allows only one.
Can be referenced by a foreign keyYesYes
RoleThe row's identityA business rule: "no two users share an email"

Composite keys

A composite key is right when no single column identifies a row but a combination does, for example (order_id, menu_item_id) on order_items: one line per item per order. The column order matters for the index that backs it: this key serves lookups by order_id alone, but not by menu_item_id alone (see the leftmost-prefix rule in Module 5). Composite keys get awkward when other tables must reference the row: each of them repeats every column. That is a reason to prefer a single-column surrogate key for rows that other tables point at.

Rule: every table gets a primary key

A table without one allows duplicate rows that cannot be told apart, cannot be updated safely and cannot be referenced by a foreign key. JPA also requires an @Id. If you cannot find a natural identifier, add a surrogate one (next article).

Edge cases

  • MySQL's InnoDB stores the table inside the primary key index (a clustered index), so a large primary key such as a random UUID makes every secondary index bigger and inserts scatter across pages.
  • A foreign key may point to a UNIQUE column, not only to a primary key, but pointing at the primary key is clearer.
  • A composite foreign key must match the whole composite key, column for column. With MATCH SIMPLE (the default) a row with any NULL in it skips the check.
  • A unique constraint on a nullable column does not stop duplicate "missing" values. To allow only one row per non-null value, that is already what you get; to treat NULL as a value, use a partial or expression index.

Interview angle

  • Candidate key vs primary key? Candidate keys are all the minimal unique identifiers; the primary key is the one you chose. The others are alternate keys, enforced with UNIQUE.
  • Super key vs candidate key? A super key can have extra columns; a candidate key cannot lose any column and stay unique.
  • Can a table have two primary keys? No. It can have one composite primary key and many unique keys.
  • Can a primary key be a foreign key? Yes, in a shared-primary-key one-to-one, and in join tables whose composite key consists of two foreign keys.

Surrogate vs natural keys, and BIGINT vs UUID

A natural key is a real-world value (email, PAN, ISBN) used as the identifier; a surrogate key is a meaningless id generated by the database, and for most application tables a surrogate BIGINT identity primary key plus UNIQUE constraints on the natural keys is the safest choice.

The two kinds

A natural key comes from the business: an email, a mobile number, an IFSC code, an ISBN, a vehicle registration number. A surrogate key is invented by you: id = 42 tells you nothing about the row. The debate is old, but the practical answer is settled in most teams: use a surrogate primary key, and still declare the natural keys UNIQUE so the business rule is enforced.

Natural keySurrogate key
MeaningHas business meaningNone; just an identity
StabilityCan change: people change email and mobile; "permanent" ids get reissued or correctedNever changes
SizeOften long text; repeated in every foreign key and index8 bytes (BIGINT) or 16 (UUID)
PrivacySpreads personal data (an email or PAN) into every child tableSafe to expose and log
Uniqueness guaranteeOnly as good as the business rule; surprises are common Guaranteed by the generator
ReadabilitySelf-explaining in joins and logsNeeds a join to know what it is
Good fitTiny stable reference data: country code IN, currency INRAlmost every application entity

The classic failure of natural keys: a company uses email as the primary key, then a user asks to change it. Every orders, addresses and payments row that references it must change in the same transaction, and every other system that stored the old value breaks. With a surrogate key, you update one cell.

CREATE TABLE users (
    id         BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,   -- surrogate
    email      TEXT NOT NULL,
    mobile     TEXT,
    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT uq_users_email  UNIQUE (email),                    -- natural key
    CONSTRAINT uq_users_mobile UNIQUE (mobile)
);

Small code tables are the exception where a natural key is fine and even nicer, because the key is readable and stable:

CREATE TABLE currencies (
    code CHAR(3) PRIMARY KEY,          -- 'INR', 'USD'
    name TEXT NOT NULL
);
-- payments.currency CHAR(3) REFERENCES currencies (code)

Auto-generated numeric ids

In PostgreSQL write BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY (standard SQL, PostgreSQL 10+). The older BIGSERIAL still works but is not standard. In MySQL write BIGINT AUTO_INCREMENT. GENERATED ALWAYS refuses a manual value unless you say OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE; GENERATED BY DEFAULT allows one. Use BIGINT, not INT: a 32-bit integer ends at about 2.1 billion, and a busy table plus gaps from rolled-back inserts can reach that sooner than you think. Changing the type on a 500-million-row table later is a long migration.

Identity values have gaps. A rolled-back transaction still consumed its numbers; sequences never reuse them. Never use the id as a "count of rows" or expect it to be gapless. If the business needs gapless numbers (invoice numbers for GST), generate them in a controlled, separate step.

BIGINT identity vs UUID

A UUID is a 128-bit identifier shown as 36 characters, such as 018f3c2e-7a41-7b3d-9c10-2f5d8a6b1e90. It can be created anywhere, without asking the database.

  • v4 is 122 random bits. Collisions are practically impossible. But values arrive in random order.
  • v7 starts with a 48-bit millisecond timestamp, then random bits. Newer values sort after older ones, so it keeps most of the uniqueness benefit and is friendly to indexes.
-- v4: built in since PostgreSQL 13
CREATE TABLE sessions (
    id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    user_id BIGINT NOT NULL
);

-- v7: PostgreSQL 18 adds uuidv7(); on older versions generate it in the application
-- id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT uuidv7()
BIGINT identityUUID v4UUID v7
Size8 bytes16 bytes16 bytes
Insert patternAlways at the end of the indexRandom pages; more page splits, cache misses, bigger WALMostly at the end, like a counter
Guessable (/orders/42)Yes: easy to enumerateNo Partly: the creation time is visible
Generated byThe database onlyAnyone: app, mobile clientAnyone
Merging data from many systemsIds clashSafeSafe
Readable in logsVeryHardHard

A reasonable default: BIGINT identity for the primary key, and when you must expose an id in a URL, add a separate random public_id UUID UNIQUE column, so the internal key stays small and fast. Choose UUIDs as primary keys when ids are created outside the database (offline apps, many services, data merged from several sources). Prefer v7 over v4 for a primary key. In MySQL InnoDB, a random v4 primary key is the worst case, because the table itself is ordered by it.

Convention: surrogate primary key, natural keys as UNIQUE

Name it id, type BIGINT, generated by the database. Every real-world identifier (email, SKU, GSTIN) becomes a UNIQUE constraint with a name such as uq_users_email. Expose a public_id if the id must be unguessable.

Mistake: using a "stable" real-world value as the primary key

Phone numbers get recycled, emails change, PAN or Aadhaar numbers must not be copied into every table (and may be restricted by law), names are not unique. If you choose a natural key, ask: can it change, can it be missing or wrong at insert time, is it private, is it short?

In real projects

Spring Data JPA entities usually have @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY) Long id. If you use UUIDs, assign them in the application (or with a v7 generator) so the entity has its id before it is saved; see Spring Boot · Module 3.

Interview angle

  • Surrogate or natural key? Surrogate primary key for stability and size; natural keys stay UNIQUE. Natural keys are fine for tiny stable lookup tables.
  • Why not UUID everywhere? 16 bytes in every index and foreign key, hard to read, and v4 inserts fragment indexes. v7 reduces the last problem.
  • Why BIGINT over INT? 2.1 billion is reachable, and gaps use up numbers.

Functional dependencies and anomalies

A functional dependency "A → B" means that if you know A you know exactly one B; a table that mixes several such facts about different things repeats data and suffers insert, update and delete anomalies, which normalization removes.

Functional dependencies in simple words

Think of a functional dependency (FD) as "A decides B". Write it A → B. If two rows have the same A, they must have the same B. Examples from a food app:

  • restaurant_id → restaurant_name, city: knowing the restaurant tells you its name and city.
  • order_id → customer_id, order_date: one order has one customer and one date.
  • (order_id, menu_item_id) → quantity: the quantity needs both parts of the key.
  • menu_item_id → name is true; name → menu_item_id is not (two restaurants can both sell "Veg Biryani").

FDs come from the business rules, not from the data you happen to see. A sample may look like "city decides pincode" by luck. Always ask the business: "can one X ever have two Ys?" The left side of an FD that decides every other column is a key. Normalization is the process of making sure every FD in a table is justified by a key.

Kinds of dependencies

Four kinds of FD are worth naming, because the normal forms in Normalization are defined in terms of them. Take a table whose key is the pair (order_id, item):

KindExampleMeaningProblem it causes
Trivial(order_id, item) → order_idThe right side is part of the left sideNone; always true
Full(order_id, item) → qtyNeeds the whole keyNone; this is what a good non-key column looks like
Partialorder_id → customerOnly part of the key decides it Repeats on every item line; breaks 2NF
Transitiveorder_id → customer → cust_cityA non-key column decides another non-key columnRepeats the city for every order; breaks 3NF

From a few FDs you can derive others with three rules called Armstrong's axioms: reflexivity (a set determines its own subsets), augmentation (if A → B then A, C → B, C) and transitivity (if A → B and B → C then A → C). Transitivity is the one that creates "hidden" chains like order_id → customer → cust_city.

Finding the key: attribute closure

The closure of a set of columns is everything they decide, directly or through chains. A set whose closure is all columns is a key (a minimal one is a candidate key; see Keys). Take the FDs of the order sheet below (customer names are assumed unique to keep the sketch short):

  • order_id → customer, restaurant
  • customer → cust_city and restaurant → rest_city
  • (restaurant, item) → price
  • (order_id, item) → qty
  1. Start with {order_id, item}.
  2. The first FD adds customer and restaurant.
  3. The second adds cust_city and rest_city.
  4. Now restaurant and item are both known, so price joins in.
  5. The last FD adds qty. All eight columns are reached: (order_id, item) is a key.

Start with {order_id} alone and you stop after customer, restaurant and the two cities: no item, qty or price. So order_id is only part of the key, and the columns it decides are the partial dependencies.

Testing a suspected FD against data

Data can disprove an FD, never prove it. To test "customer decides cust_city", look for a customer that has two cities:

SELECT customer
FROM order_sheet
GROUP BY customer
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT cust_city) > 1;

On the sheet below the query returns no rows, so no counter-example exists yet. That is not proof; it only means the data does not contradict the rule. After the half-finished update shown later it returns Asha: the FD is now violated by the data, which is exactly an update anomaly.

An unnormalized table and its anomalies

A beginner stores everything about an order in one wide table:

 order_id | customer | cust_city | restaurant | rest_city | item         | qty | price
----------+----------+-----------+------------+-----------+--------------+-----+------
      101 | Asha     | Pune      | Spice Hub  | Pune      | Paneer Tikka |   2 |  220
      101 | Asha     | Pune      | Spice Hub  | Pune      | Butter Naan  |   4 |   40
      102 | Bilal    | Delhi     | Dosa Point | Delhi     | Masala Dosa  |   1 |  120
      103 | Asha     | Pune      | Dosa Point | Delhi     | Masala Dosa  |   2 |  120

Customer facts repeat on every line, restaurant facts repeat on every line, and the item price repeats on every order. Three problems follow.

Update anomaly

Asha moves to Mumbai. You must change every one of her rows (101, 101, 103). Miss one and the table says she lives in two cities. The same happens when Dosa Point changes its name.

UPDATE order_sheet SET cust_city = 'Mumbai'
WHERE order_id = 101;            -- forgot order 103!
SELECT DISTINCT customer, cust_city FROM order_sheet WHERE customer = 'Asha';
 customer | cust_city
----------+-----------
 Asha     | Mumbai
 Asha     | Pune
(2 rows)
Insert anomaly

A new restaurant signs up but has no orders yet. There is no row to put it in, unless you invent a fake order with NULL in the order columns. Facts about one thing cannot exist without facts about another.

-- order_sheet has PRIMARY KEY (order_id, item)
INSERT INTO order_sheet (restaurant, rest_city) VALUES ('Tandoor Town', 'Pune');
-- ERROR:  null value in column "order_id" of relation "order_sheet" violates not-null constraint
Delete anomaly

Asha cancels order 101 and you delete both of its rows. Spice Hub appears in no other order, so the only rows that said "Spice Hub exists in Pune and sells Paneer Tikka at 220 and Butter Naan at 40" are gone too. Deleting one order destroyed a restaurant and its menu. (Deleting order 102 would be harmless here, because Dosa Point also appears in order 103: whether you lose the fact depends on luck, which is the problem.)

DELETE FROM order_sheet WHERE order_id = 101;
SELECT * FROM order_sheet WHERE restaurant = 'Spice Hub';   -- 0 rows: Spice Hub is gone
AnomalyWhat goes wrongRoot cause
UpdateOne fact stored many times; some copies change, some do notRedundancy
InsertCannot record a fact until an unrelated fact existsTwo subjects in one table
DeleteRemoving one fact removes anotherTwo subjects in one table

All three disappear when each table describes one thing and every non-key column depends on the key of that thing. The next article does this step by step.

What the fixed design looks like

Each FD gets a home. The determinant becomes the key of a table that holds exactly what it decides:

CREATE TABLE customers   (customer_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
                          name VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL, city VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE restaurants (restaurant_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
                          name VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL, city VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE menu_items  (menu_item_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
                          restaurant_id INT NOT NULL REFERENCES restaurants,
                          name VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL, price NUMERIC(8,2) NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE orders      (order_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
                          customer_id INT NOT NULL REFERENCES customers,
                          restaurant_id INT NOT NULL REFERENCES restaurants);
CREATE TABLE order_lines (order_id INT REFERENCES orders,
                          menu_item_id INT REFERENCES menu_items,
                          qty INT NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
                          PRIMARY KEY (order_id, menu_item_id));

Now each anomaly is gone:

UPDATE customers SET city = 'Mumbai' WHERE customer_id = 1;         -- Asha moves: one row
INSERT INTO restaurants VALUES (4, 'Tandoor Town', 'Pune');          -- no order needed
DELETE FROM orders WHERE order_id = 101;  -- (after deleting its lines) restaurant stays

Fully worked, with all the steps from 1NF to 3NF, in Normalization and the food-delivery design. A price copied into the order line at purchase time (as order_items.unit_price in the sample database) is not a violation: it records a different fact, "the price on that day", which must not change when the menu price does.

Rule: an FD is a statement about every possible row, not only today's rows

"city decides pincode" may be true in your ten sample rows and false in real life (one city has many pincodes). Decide FDs from the business rules and confirm them with the people who own the data. Use SQL only to hunt for counter-examples.

Mistake: using a name as the determinant

"customer decides cust_city" fails the day two customers are both called Asha. Names, emails typed by hand and phone numbers are poor determinants; a surrogate id is a safe one.

Wrong: changes every Asha

UPDATE order_sheet SET cust_city = 'Mumbai'
WHERE customer = 'Asha';

Right: the id points to one person

UPDATE customers SET city = 'Mumbai'
WHERE customer_id = 1;

Edge cases: when redundancy is acceptable

  • Read-only copies. Reporting tables and data warehouses repeat values on purpose to avoid joins. There, nobody updates a single row; the table is rebuilt from the source of truth. See Denormalization.
  • Snapshots. A price or address stored on an order as it was at that moment is history, not redundancy.
  • NULL and FDs. If restaurant → rest_city and some restaurants have no city yet, a NULL city is a "not known" value, not a second city. Keep key columns NOT NULL so FDs stay reliable.

Tip: a quick smell test

If you see the same value typed again and again down a column (a city, a product name, a category), ask "which fact does this repeat, and which key decides it?" Then move it to its own table. If a column needs "N/A" or NULL for most rows because the row is about something else, you have mixed subjects.

Interview angle

  • What is a functional dependency? A → B: each value of A is linked to exactly one value of B. A key is an A that decides every column.
  • Name the three anomalies with an example. Update: change in many places. Insert: cannot add a restaurant without an order. Delete: deleting the last order erases the restaurant.
  • Why do anomalies happen? Redundancy from storing several subjects in one table.
  • Partial vs transitive dependency? Partial: a non-key column depends on only part of a composite key (breaks 2NF). Transitive: a non-key column depends on another non-key column (breaks 3NF).
  • Is every FD a key? No. A key is a left side whose closure is every column. customer → cust_city is an FD but customer is not a key of the order sheet.
  • How do you show an FD does not hold? Find two rows with the same left side and different right sides (a GROUP BY … HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT …) > 1 query).

Normalization: 1NF, 2NF, 3NF, BCNF (and 4NF)

Normalization splits a table until every non-key column depends on "the key, the whole key, and nothing but the key": 1NF removes repeating groups, 2NF removes partial dependencies, 3NF removes transitive dependencies, and BCNF closes the last gaps.

The idea

Each normal form is a stricter test. A table in 3NF is also in 2NF and 1NF. The memory line "the key (1NF), the whole key (2NF), and nothing but the key (3NF), so help me Codd" sums it up. We will use one running example, the order sheet from the previous article, and fix it step by step.

0NF: not even first normal form

Here the sheet keeps all items of an order in one row and stores several phone numbers in one cell:

 order_id | customer | phones            | restaurant | items
----------+----------+-------------------+------------+--------------------------------
      101 | Asha     | 98100, 98200      | Spice Hub  | Paneer Tikka x2, Butter Naan x4
      102 | Bilal    | 98300             | Dosa Point | Masala Dosa x1

1NF: atomic values, no repeating groups

Rule: every cell holds one value, there are no repeating groups or arrays of values, and rows are identified by a key. Give each item its own row and keep one phone per row. The key becomes (order_id, item).

 order_id | item         | qty | price | customer | cust_city | restaurant | rest_city
----------+--------------+-----+-------+----------+-----------+------------+----------
      101 | Paneer Tikka |   2 |   220 | Asha     | Pune      | Spice Hub  | Pune
      101 | Butter Naan  |   4 |    40 | Asha     | Pune      | Spice Hub  | Pune
      102 | Masala Dosa  |   1 |   120 | Bilal    | Delhi     | Dosa Point | Delhi

Phones move to their own table customer_phones (customer, phone). The wide table is now in 1NF, but still has the anomalies from before. Look at the dependencies:

  • (order_id, item) → qty: needs the whole key. Good.
  • order_id → customer, cust_city, restaurant, rest_city: needs only part of the key.
  • item → price: needs only the other part of the key.

2NF: no partial dependency on a composite key

Rule: in 1NF, and every non-key column depends on the whole key, not on part of it. This only matters when the key is composite. Split by the part of the key that decides the column:

orders(order_id PK, customer, cust_city, restaurant, rest_city)
   101 | Asha  | Pune  | Spice Hub  | Pune
   102 | Bilal | Delhi | Dosa Point | Delhi

menu_items(item PK, price)
   Paneer Tikka | 220
   Butter Naan  |  40
   Masala Dosa  | 120

order_lines(order_id PK/FK, item PK/FK, qty)
   101 | Paneer Tikka | 2
   101 | Butter Naan  | 4
   102 | Masala Dosa  | 1

Item price is now stored once. (Later we will bring the price at order time back on purpose; see Denormalization.)

One problem is left in orders: cust_city is not decided by order_id directly, but by customer, who is decided by order_id. If Asha places ten orders, her city repeats ten times.

3NF: no transitive dependency

Rule: in 2NF, and no non-key column depends on another non-key column. A transitive dependency is a chain order_id → customer → cust_city: the city depends on the key only through the customer. Move each chain to its own table:

customers(customer_id PK, name, city)
restaurants(restaurant_id PK, name, city)
menu_items(menu_item_id PK, restaurant_id FK, name, price)
orders(order_id PK, customer_id FK, restaurant_id FK, ordered_at)
order_lines(order_id PK/FK, menu_item_id PK/FK, qty)
customer_phones(customer_id FK, phone, PK(customer_id, phone))

Every table is about one thing. Changing Asha's city is one update, a restaurant can exist with no orders, and deleting an order never deletes a customer. Most business databases stop at 3NF, and it is the level interviews expect for a design question. Here it is as SQL:

CREATE TABLE customers   (customer_id   BIGINT PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT NOT NULL, city TEXT NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE restaurants (restaurant_id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT NOT NULL, city TEXT NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE menu_items (
    menu_item_id  BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    restaurant_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES restaurants (restaurant_id),
    name          TEXT NOT NULL,
    price         NUMERIC(8,2) NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
    order_id      BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id   BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES customers (customer_id),
    restaurant_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES restaurants (restaurant_id),
    ordered_at    TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE order_lines (
    order_id     BIGINT REFERENCES orders (order_id),
    menu_item_id BIGINT REFERENCES menu_items (menu_item_id),
    qty          INT NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, menu_item_id)
);

BCNF: every determinant is a key

Boyce-Codd normal form is a slightly stricter 3NF. Rule: for every functional dependency X → Y in the table, X must be a super key. 3NF allows one loophole: a non-key column may determine part of a candidate key. BCNF closes it. The loophole appears rarely and only with overlapping candidate keys. Example: riders work in zones, each dispatcher handles exactly one zone, and a rider in a zone is looked after by one dispatcher.

 rider_id | zone       | dispatcher
----------+------------+-----------
        7 | Kothrud    | Meena
        7 | Hinjewadi  | Sunil
        8 | Kothrud    | Meena
        9 | Hinjewadi  | Sunil

The dependencies are (rider_id, zone) → dispatcher and dispatcher → zone. The candidate keys are (rider_id, zone) and (rider_id, dispatcher). Now dispatcher → zone has a determinant (dispatcher) that is not a key, so BCNF fails, although 3NF passes (zone is part of a candidate key). The symptom is redundancy: "Meena handles Kothrud" is repeated, and renaming her means several updates. Decompose:

CREATE TABLE dispatchers (
    dispatcher TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    zone       TEXT NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE rider_dispatchers (
    rider_id   BIGINT NOT NULL,
    dispatcher TEXT   NOT NULL REFERENCES dispatchers (dispatcher),
    PRIMARY KEY (rider_id, dispatcher)
);

The price: the original rule "a rider has one dispatcher per zone" is no longer a single constraint, so you must enforce it another way (a trigger, or application logic). This is the known trade-off: BCNF can lose dependency preservation, while 3NF decomposition never does. In practice, aim for BCNF and accept 3NF when BCNF would make a rule hard to enforce.

4NF and beyond, in one paragraph

Fourth normal form removes multi-valued dependencies. If a restaurant has several cuisines and several delivery areas, and the two lists are independent, storing them in one table (restaurant_id, cuisine, area) forces every combination: 3 cuisines and 4 areas make 12 rows, and adding a cuisine means adding 4 rows. Split into restaurant_cuisines and restaurant_areas, two independent many-to-many tables. 5NF handles rarer join dependencies and is seldom needed. If you model each many-to-many relationship as its own join table, you get 4NF for free.

Summary

FormFixesTest question
1NFRepeating groups, multi-valued cellsIs every cell a single value and every row uniquely identified?
2NFPartial dependency on part of a composite keyDoes each non-key column need the whole key?
3NFTransitive dependency via a non-key columnDoes any non-key column decide another non-key column?
BCNFA non-key determinant, with overlapping keysIs every determinant a super key?
4NFIndependent multi-valued facts in one tableAre two unrelated lists crossed together?

Rule: normalize from the dependencies, not from guesses

List the functional dependencies first, find the keys, then check each form. A table with a single-column key is automatically in 2NF (no partial dependency is possible). 3NF is the practical target for transactional (OLTP) databases.

Edge cases

  • Is a TEXT[] or JSONB column a 1NF violation? Strictly, yes when you query inside it as a list of facts. Use it for opaque data you only store and return, not for things you join or count.
  • Splitting an address into street, city, pincode is "atomic enough" if you filter by city. Atomic depends on how you use the value, not on the type.
  • pincode → city is a real transitive dependency; many teams keep both columns on the address because the data is entered together and rarely changes. That is a conscious denormalization.

Mistake: normalizing past usefulness

Splitting gender, title and country into lookup tables for a 10-row demo gives joins and no benefit. Normalize facts that change or repeat in volume (customer, product, price). A CHECK constraint is enough for a tiny, fixed list.

Interview angle

  • Explain 1NF, 2NF, 3NF with an example. Use the order sheet: items to rows (1NF), item price out of the order line (2NF), customer city out of the order (3NF).
  • 3NF vs BCNF? BCNF needs every determinant to be a super key; 3NF lets a non-key column decide part of a key. BCNF may lose a dependency.
  • Is a higher normal form always better? No. It reduces anomalies but adds joins; see Denormalization.
  • What is a partial dependency? A transitive dependency? A column depending on part of a composite key; a column depending on the key through another non-key column.

Denormalization: when and how

Denormalization means deliberately storing redundant data to avoid joins or to freeze a historical value; do it only for a measured need, and keep the copies consistent in a planned way.

Normalize first, then break the rule on purpose

A normalized schema is the correct starting point: it protects the data. Denormalization is a conscious trade: faster or simpler reads in exchange for harder writes and a risk of inconsistency. "My join looks complicated" is not a reason. A proper index and a good query fix most slow joins. Denormalize when you have a measured problem or a real business rule.

When it is the right thing

CaseExampleWhy
History snapshotorder_items.unit_price copied from the menu item when the order is placedThis is not redundancy; it is a different fact: "the price at that time". The menu price will change.
Address snapshotDelivery address text copied onto the orderThe customer may edit or delete the saved address later; the order must remember where it went.
Derived counterrestaurants.rating_avg, rating_count Computing AVG over millions of reviews on every page view is wasteful.
Read-heavy reportsA nightly daily_sales summary tableDashboards read pre-aggregated rows instead of scanning all orders.
Hot-path joinRestaurant name copied to a search index or listing tableAvoid a join on the busiest page; accept a small delay in updates.
 
CREATE TABLE order_items (
    order_id     BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES orders (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    menu_item_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES menu_items (id),
    item_name    TEXT   NOT NULL,                   -- name at order time
    unit_price_paise BIGINT NOT NULL CHECK (unit_price_paise >= 0),  -- price at order time
    quantity     INT    NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, menu_item_id)
);

INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, menu_item_id, item_name, unit_price_paise, quantity)
SELECT 101, id, name, price_paise, 2 FROM menu_items WHERE id = 5;

Keeping redundant data consistent

A copy is a promise to keep two places equal. Choose one mechanism and make it the only way to write:

  1. Same transaction. Update the source and the copy in one transaction in the service layer. Simple, but every writer must remember.
  2. Trigger. The database updates the counter on every insert into the child table. It cannot be bypassed, but it hides logic and slows writes.
  3. Materialized view. CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW daily_sales AS … stores the result of a query; REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW rebuilds it (on a schedule). Good for reports.
  4. Periodic job. A nightly job recomputes counters from the source of truth. It also repairs any drift.
  5. Events. Publish "restaurant renamed" and let other services update their copies; consistency is eventual (see Module 6).
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO reviews (restaurant_id, user_id, stars) VALUES (3, 1, 5);
UPDATE restaurants
SET rating_count = rating_count + 1,
    rating_sum   = rating_sum + 5          -- average = rating_sum / rating_count
WHERE id = 3;
COMMIT;

Store the sum and the count, not the average: the average cannot be updated correctly from itself, but a sum and a count can.

Pros

  • Fewer joins; faster reads
  • Historical correctness (snapshots)
  • Simple queries for reports

Cons

  • Redundant storage
  • Every write must update the copies
  • Copies can drift and need repair jobs
  • More code to remember and test

Rule: one source of truth for every fact

For each denormalized value, decide which column is the truth and how the copy is refreshed. Write it in a comment on the column or in the team's design notes. A copy with no refresh plan is a bug waiting to happen.

Mistake: denormalizing before measuring

Copying customer_name onto every order "to avoid a join" gives you update anomalies from the previous article, on purpose. First check the plan with EXPLAIN ANALYZE (see Module 5) and add an index. Only denormalize if the cost is still too high.

In real projects

Transactional databases (OLTP) stay close to 3NF with a few snapshots and counters. Analytics databases (OLAP, data warehouses) often use wide, denormalized "star schemas" on purpose. Document stores such as MongoDB embed related data by design (see MongoDB).

Interview angle

  • When would you denormalize? Measured read-performance problems, derived counters and reports, and history snapshots such as price on an order line.
  • What are the risks? Inconsistent copies, slower writes, extra code.
  • Why store unit_price on order_items if it is on menu_items? Because the menu price changes and the order must keep the price actually charged.

Audit columns and soft delete

Audit columns (created_at, updated_at, created_by) record who changed a row and when; soft delete marks a row as deleted with deleted_at instead of removing it, which brings extra work for uniqueness, queries and privacy.

Audit columns

Almost every business table carries the same small set of columns. They answer support questions ("when did this order change?"), help debugging, and feed incremental data loads.

CREATE TABLE restaurants (
    id         BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name       TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    created_by BIGINT REFERENCES users (id),
    updated_by BIGINT REFERENCES users (id)
);

CREATE FUNCTION set_updated_at() RETURNS trigger AS $$
BEGIN
    NEW.updated_at := now();
    RETURN NEW;
END;
$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

CREATE TRIGGER trg_restaurants_updated_at
BEFORE UPDATE ON restaurants
FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION set_updated_at();

MySQL can do the same in the column itself: updated_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP ON UPDATE CURRENT_TIMESTAMP. PostgreSQL has no such clause, so use a trigger or set the value in the application. In Spring Data JPA, the annotations @CreatedDate, @LastModifiedDate, @CreatedBy and @LastModifiedBy fill these columns for you when auditing is enabled (Spring Boot · Module 3).

  • created_at never changes after insert. Give it DEFAULT now() and NOT NULL.
  • updated_at changes on each update. Keep it NOT NULL too, starting equal to created_at, so you never have to handle NULL.
  • created_by / updated_by point to the user (or a service account). Many teams skip them on small tables.

Audit columns say when and who, but not what changed. For a full history (compliance, payments), add a separate *_history table filled by a trigger or by the application, or use change data capture. A column updated_at is not a history.

Soft delete

A hard delete runs DELETE and the row is gone. A soft delete keeps the row and marks it:

ALTER TABLE menu_items ADD COLUMN deleted_at TIMESTAMPTZ;   -- NULL means "alive"

-- "Delete"
UPDATE menu_items SET deleted_at = now() WHERE id = 5 AND deleted_at IS NULL;

-- Every normal read must filter
SELECT id, name FROM menu_items WHERE restaurant_id = 3 AND deleted_at IS NULL;

-- Restore
UPDATE menu_items SET deleted_at = NULL WHERE id = 5;

Use deleted_at rather than a boolean is_deleted: it also tells you when. A timestamp is just as easy to filter, and it can be used to purge old rows later.

Soft delete is useful when history must survive (an order that references a deleted menu item), when users may undo, or when you must keep records for legal reasons. But it has real costs:

PitfallWhat happensFix
Forgotten filterOne query without deleted_at IS NULL shows deleted data A view, or Hibernate's @SQLRestriction / @SoftDelete (Hibernate 6.4+)
Unique constraintsA deleted user keeps the email, so a new sign-up with it fails Partial unique index, below
Foreign keysChild rows still point at a "deleted" parentDecide what a deleted parent means for its children
GrowthTables grow forever; indexes carry dead rowsArchive or purge old rows
Privacy law"Delete my data" requests need real removal or anonymisingAnonymise personal columns, or hard-delete after a period
 
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_users_email_live
    ON users (email)
    WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

-- A view so that application code cannot forget the filter
CREATE VIEW live_menu_items AS
SELECT * FROM menu_items WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

MySQL has no partial indexes; a common workaround is a unique index on (email, deleted_at), which works only if deleted_at is never NULL for live rows. Some teams store a sentinel date instead. That is one reason to avoid soft delete on tables where hard delete is acceptable.

Convention: soft delete only where you need it

Use it for business records you may need to show or restore (users, products, orders). Do not use it for link rows, sessions or temporary data; delete those for real. Keep the filter in one place (a view or a framework setting).

Mistake: "delete" that cascades into soft-deleted data

A soft delete runs UPDATE, so ON DELETE CASCADE never fires. If a restaurant is soft-deleted, its menu items are still "alive" unless you update them too or filter through the parent. Decide the rule and test it.

Interview angle

  • Soft vs hard delete? Soft keeps the row with a marker (history, undo), but needs filters, partial unique indexes and a purge plan. Hard removes it.
  • Why deleted_at not is_deleted? It records when, and works as the purge-date input.

Status columns, money, timestamps and JSONB

Model a status as a text column with a CHECK (or a lookup table when it needs data), money as NUMERIC or integer paise, time as UTC TIMESTAMPTZ, and use JSONB only for flexible data you do not join on.

Status: text + CHECK, lookup table, or ENUM

An order moves through PLACED, ACCEPTED, PREPARING, OUT_FOR_DELIVERY, DELIVERED or CANCELLED. Three ways to store it:

Text + CHECKLookup table + FKDatabase ENUM
Add a valueAlter the constraintINSERT a rowALTER TYPE … ADD VALUE
Remove or rename a valueAlter the constraint, update rowsUpdate rows and FKHard (PostgreSQL cannot drop a value)
Extra data per status (label, sort order, is_final)NoYesNo
Readable in queriesYesYes with a join, or text keyYes
Works the same in MySQLYes (8.0.16+ enforces CHECK)YesMySQL has its own ENUM syntax
 
-- 1. Text with CHECK: simple, my default for small fixed lists
status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PLACED'
    CONSTRAINT ck_orders_status CHECK (status IN
        ('PLACED','ACCEPTED','PREPARING','OUT_FOR_DELIVERY','DELIVERED','CANCELLED'))

-- 2. Lookup table: when statuses carry data or are edited by admins
CREATE TABLE order_statuses (
    code     TEXT PRIMARY KEY,           -- natural key is fine here
    label    TEXT NOT NULL,
    is_final BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT false
);
status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PLACED' REFERENCES order_statuses (code)

-- 3. PostgreSQL ENUM type
CREATE TYPE order_status AS ENUM ('PLACED','ACCEPTED','DELIVERED','CANCELLED');
status order_status NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PLACED'

Rule of thumb: text with CHECK for short fixed lists, a lookup table when the list changes at runtime or has attributes, and avoid ENUM when values may be renamed or removed. Do not store status as a bare number (status = 3): nobody remembers what 3 means. In JPA, use @Enumerated(EnumType.STRING), never ORDINAL, because reordering the Java enum would silently change the meaning of stored data. The legal transitions (PLACED → ACCEPTED, but never DELIVERED → PLACED) are business rules; enforce them in the service, or with a trigger if many writers exist.

Money

Never store money in FLOAT or DOUBLE. Binary floating point cannot represent 0.1 exactly, and errors add up.

SELECT 0.1::double precision + 0.2::double precision AS float_sum,
       0.1::numeric + 0.2::numeric AS numeric_sum;
      float_sum      | numeric_sum
---------------------+-------------
 0.30000000000000004 |         0.3

Two good options:

  • NUMERIC(12,2) (also DECIMAL): exact decimals; 12 digits in total, 2 after the point. Readable, and correct. In Java it maps to BigDecimal.
  • Integer minor units: store paise as BIGINT (₹250.50 is 25050). Fast, exact, no rounding surprises, and it maps to a Java long. Payment gateways like Razorpay also speak in paise. The column name should say so: amount_paise.

Add a CHECK (amount_paise >= 0) where negatives make no sense, and a currency column (CHAR(3), ISO code) when more than one currency is possible. Decide the rounding rule for tax and discounts once, and apply it in one place.

Timestamps: store UTC with TIMESTAMPTZ

In PostgreSQL TIMESTAMPTZ (timestamp with time zone) stores one exact instant, internally in UTC, and shows it in the session's time zone. TIMESTAMP (without time zone) stores a calendar-and-clock reading with no zone, which is ambiguous the moment two servers or two users are in different zones, or when clocks change.

SET TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata';
SELECT TIMESTAMPTZ '2026-11-10 09:00:00+00' AS tz_value;
SET TIME ZONE 'UTC';
SELECT TIMESTAMPTZ '2026-11-10 09:00:00+00' AS tz_value;
        tz_value
------------------------
 2026-11-10 14:30:00+05:30

        tz_value
------------------------
 2026-11-10 09:00:00+00

Same instant, shown in two zones. Use TIMESTAMPTZ for "when did it happen" (created_at, paid_at). Use DATE for a calendar date with no time (date of birth) and TIME sparingly (opening hours). In MySQL, TIMESTAMP is converted to UTC and back, while DATETIME stores the literal value; set the connection and server time zone explicitly. In Java use Instant or OffsetDateTime, and convert to the user's zone only when displaying.

JSONB: flexible data inside a relational table

PostgreSQL's JSONB stores JSON in a binary form that can be indexed and queried. It is useful for data whose shape varies or that you only read as a whole.

CREATE TABLE menu_items (
    id          BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name        TEXT NOT NULL,
    price_paise BIGINT NOT NULL,
    attributes  JSONB NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'
);
INSERT INTO menu_items (name, price_paise, attributes) VALUES
    ('Paneer Tikka', 22000, '{"spice": "medium", "allergens": ["milk"], "vegetarian": true}');

SELECT name FROM menu_items WHERE attributes @> '{"vegetarian": true}';   -- containment
SELECT name, attributes ->> 'spice' AS spice FROM menu_items;           -- text value

CREATE INDEX idx_menu_items_attributes ON menu_items USING gin (attributes);

Good uses

  • Optional, varying attributes (product specs per category)
  • Raw third-party payloads (a payment gateway response) kept for debugging
  • User preferences and settings

Bad uses

  • Data you join, filter, sort or aggregate on all the time: make real columns
  • Anything needing foreign keys or constraints
  • "I did not want to design the schema"

A good compromise: real columns for the stable core (name, price) and one JSONB column for the long tail. If a JSON key becomes important to queries, promote it to a column. In MySQL the type is JSON, with different functions.

Rule: never use floating point for money, never TIMESTAMP without zone for events

Money is NUMERIC or integer minor units. Events are TIMESTAMPTZ in UTC. Show local time only in the UI.

Mistake: storing status as a free-text string with no constraint

Without a CHECK or lookup, 'Delivered', 'DELIVERED' and 'delivred' all get in, and reports silently miss rows. Constrain it.

Interview angle

  • How do you store money? NUMERIC(p,s) or integer paise; never float; keep currency in a column.
  • TIMESTAMP vs TIMESTAMPTZ? TZ stores an exact instant (UTC internally); plain TIMESTAMP has no zone and is ambiguous.
  • When use JSONB? Flexible, rarely joined data; index with GIN; not for core relational facts.

Hierarchies, polymorphic associations and multi-tenancy

Store a tree with a self-referencing parent_id (adjacency list) and read it with a recursive query; avoid polymorphic (type, id) foreign keys by using one real foreign key per target; and for multi-tenancy put a tenant_id on every table.

Hierarchical data: the adjacency list

Categories, org charts, comment threads and folders are trees. The simplest model is a row that points at its parent:

CREATE TABLE menu_categories (
    id        BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name      TEXT NOT NULL,
    parent_id BIGINT REFERENCES menu_categories (id),   -- NULL for the root
    CHECK (parent_id <> id)
);
CREATE INDEX idx_menu_categories_parent_id ON menu_categories (parent_id);

INSERT INTO menu_categories (name, parent_id) VALUES
    ('Food', NULL), ('Starters', 1), ('Mains', 1), ('Veg Starters', 2);

-- All descendants of "Food" with their depth
WITH RECURSIVE tree AS (
    SELECT id, name, parent_id, 0 AS depth FROM menu_categories WHERE id = 1
    UNION ALL
    SELECT c.id, c.name, c.parent_id, t.depth + 1
    FROM menu_categories c
    JOIN tree t ON c.parent_id = t.id
)
SELECT repeat('  ', depth) || name AS category, depth FROM tree ORDER BY depth, id;
   category    | depth
---------------+-------
 Food          |     0
   Starters    |     1
   Mains       |     1
     Veg Starters |     2
(4 rows)

Recursive CTEs are covered in Module 3. The adjacency list is simple to insert and move (change one parent_id) and enforces the parent with a foreign key. Reading a whole subtree needs a recursive query, which is fine for modest trees. Alternatives exist for read-heavy trees:

ModelIdeaTrade-off
Adjacency listparent_idSimple writes; subtree needs recursion
Materialized pathStore '/1/2/4/' (or PostgreSQL ltree) Fast subtree with LIKE; moving a node rewrites many paths
Closure tableA table of every (ancestor, descendant, depth) pairFast reads of any relation; more rows and more write work
Nested setsLeft/right numbers per nodeFast subtree reads; very costly inserts

Start with an adjacency list. Move to another model only if recursion measurably hurts. Guard against cycles (A is parent of B and B of A): the recursive query would loop forever, so add a depth limit (WHERE t.depth < 20) or validate in the service.

Polymorphic associations: avoid them

A polymorphic association is one reference that can point to several tables, for example a comment that belongs to either a restaurant or a menu item:

Wrong: type + id, no foreign key possible

CREATE TABLE reviews (
    id          BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    target_type TEXT NOT NULL,   -- 'restaurant' or 'menu_item'
    target_id   BIGINT NOT NULL, -- which table? the database cannot check
    stars       INT NOT NULL
);

Right: one nullable FK per target + CHECK

CREATE TABLE reviews (
    id            BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    restaurant_id BIGINT REFERENCES restaurants (id),
    menu_item_id  BIGINT REFERENCES menu_items (id),
    stars         INT NOT NULL,
    CHECK (num_nonnulls(restaurant_id, menu_item_id) = 1)  -- exactly one
);

The first design cannot have a real foreign key, so orphan rows appear when targets are deleted, and no cascade works. The second keeps integrity at the cost of one column per target. Other fixes: separate tables (restaurant_reviews, menu_item_reviews), or a common parent table that both targets share a key with. Frameworks such as Rails use type + id (and JPA's @Any exists), but integrity then lives in application code only.

Multi-tenancy in brief

A multi-tenant system serves many customers (tenants), such as many restaurant chains using one SaaS billing product. Three layouts, from cheapest to most isolated:

ModelHowGood forCost
Shared tablestenant_id column on every tableMany small tenants A missing filter leaks data; noisy neighbours
Schema per tenantOne PostgreSQL schema eachDozens to hundreds of tenants Migrations run per schema
Database per tenantSeparate databaseFew large or regulated tenants Most operational work: backups, migrations, connections
 
CREATE TABLE invoices (
    tenant_id  BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants (id),
    id         BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    number     TEXT NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, id),
    CONSTRAINT uq_invoices_number UNIQUE (tenant_id, number)   -- unique per tenant
);
CREATE INDEX idx_invoices_tenant ON invoices (tenant_id, id);

ALTER TABLE invoices ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;               -- PostgreSQL
CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON invoices
    USING (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::bigint);

Put tenant_id first in indexes and unique constraints, and in every query. PostgreSQL row level security makes the database enforce the filter even if a query forgets it.

Convention: prefer real foreign keys over clever generic ones

A foreign key is the cheapest, strongest check you have. Give up generality before giving up a foreign key.

Edge cases

  • A recursive CTE on a cyclic graph never ends on UNION ALL; use UNION or a depth limit.
  • With shared tables, a global UNIQUE (email) is usually wrong: two tenants may both have admin@shop.com.

Interview angle

  • How do you store a category tree? parent_id self-reference, read with a recursive CTE; mention closure table or ltree for heavy reads.
  • Why avoid polymorphic associations? No foreign key, so no integrity or cascade.
  • Multi-tenancy options? Shared table + tenant_id, schema per tenant, database per tenant; pick by tenant count and isolation needs.

Naming conventions

Pick one simple, predictable style (lowercase snake_case, id primary keys, customer_id foreign keys, prefixed constraint and index names), write it down, and apply it everywhere.

Why it matters

Names are the user interface of a schema. A predictable name lets a new developer guess a column, and it makes error messages self-explaining ("violates foreign key constraint fk_orders_customer"). The exact style matters less than being consistent. Module 1 already covered SQL style in SQL style; this is the design side.

The conventions

ItemConventionExample
Case and wordslowercase snake_case; no spaces; ASCII order_items, not OrderItems
Table namePlural nouns or singular: choose one for the whole schema orders (plural, common in Spring projects)
Primary keyidorders.id
Foreign key columnSingular parent name + _idorders.customer_id
Role-named foreign keysThe role + _id when one table is referenced twice pickup_address_id, drop_address_id
Join tableBusiness noun, or both namesorder_items, user_roles
Booleansis_ / has_ prefixis_active, has_gst
Timestamps and dates_at for instants, _on / _date for dates created_at, enrolled_on
Units in namesPut the unit in the nameamount_paise, weight_grams
ConstraintsPrefix + table + column(s)pk_orders, fk_orders_customer, uq_users_email, ck_orders_status
Indexesidx_<table>_<columns>idx_orders_customer_id
 
CREATE TABLE payments (
    id           BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    order_id     BIGINT NOT NULL,
    amount_paise BIGINT NOT NULL,
    is_refunded  BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    paid_at      TIMESTAMPTZ,
    CONSTRAINT pk_payments PRIMARY KEY (id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_payments_order FOREIGN KEY (order_id) REFERENCES orders (id),
    CONSTRAINT ck_payments_amount CHECK (amount_paise > 0)
);
CREATE INDEX idx_payments_order_id ON payments (order_id);

INSERT INTO payments (order_id, amount_paise) VALUES (1, -5);
-- ERROR:  new row for relation "payments" violates check constraint "ck_payments_amount"

Names to avoid

  • Reserved or risky words: user, order, group, table, desc, end, references. In PostgreSQL user is reserved and needs quotes, which is why you see users and orders (plural) in most schemas. Check the database's keyword list when in doubt.
  • Quoted mixed-case names ("OrderItems"): PostgreSQL folds unquoted names to lowercase, so you would have to quote them forever.
  • Vague names: data, value, type, flag, temp. Say what it is: delivery_instructions.
  • Type or table prefixes: tbl_orders, order_order_date. The type is visible in the schema.
  • Abbreviations nobody knows: cust_nm. Disk is cheap; clarity is not.

Mapping to Java

Spring Boot's default naming strategy maps createdAt in an entity to the column created_at and OrderItem to order_item, so snake_case columns work without extra configuration. If your table is order_items, add @Table(name = "order_items").

Rule: PostgreSQL limits identifiers to 63 bytes

Longer names are cut silently, which can make two long index names collide. Keep index and constraint names short but meaningful. MySQL's limit is 64 characters.

Convention: write the naming rules down

One short page in the repository ("tables plural, ids id, FKs x_id") ends arguments and keeps migrations consistent when ten people add tables.

Interview angle

Interviewers look for consistency and reasons: plural vs singular is a choice; avoiding reserved words, naming FKs after the parent, and naming constraints so errors are readable are the points that show experience.

Choosing data types

Pick the smallest type that is correct for the data and its future: BIGINT ids, NUMERIC or paise for money, TEXT for names and phone numbers, TIMESTAMPTZ for instants, BOOLEAN for flags, and a URL (not the file) for files.

A guide table

The type is a constraint: it stops wrong data and decides storage and speed. Module 1 lists every type; see Data types. This table says what to pick for common design needs.

DataUseWhy / avoid
Primary and foreign idsBIGINT (identity) or UUID Avoid INT for growing tables
MoneyNUMERIC(12,2) or BIGINT paiseNever FLOAT
Quantity, countsINT (or SMALLINT for tiny ranges) Add a CHECK for ranges
Names, titles, addressesTEXT (PostgreSQL), VARCHAR(n) in MySQL In PostgreSQL VARCHAR(n) is no faster than TEXT; use a limit only when a rule demands it
EmailTEXT + UNIQUE (store lowercase)Use a CHECK or app validation for the format
Phone numberTEXT in E.164 form (+919812345678) Not a number: leading zeros, +, no arithmetic
Pincode, PAN, IFSC, account no.TEXT / CHAR(n) Identifiers, not quantities; may start with 0
Instant in timeTIMESTAMPTZPlain TIMESTAMP is ambiguous
Date only (birth date)DATENo time part to invent
DurationINTERVAL or integer seconds/minutesName the unit in a column such as prep_minutes
FlagBOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT falseAvoid 'Y'/'N' and 0/1 (MySQL TINYINT(1) is its boolean)
Fixed set of valuesTEXT + CHECK, or lookup table See status columns
Latitude / longitudeNUMERIC(9,6) or DOUBLE PRECISION, or PostGIS geographyFloating point is acceptable for coordinates
Long text (reviews, notes)TEXTMySQL: TEXT / MEDIUMTEXT
Flexible attributesJSONBNot for core relational data
Files and imagesTEXT holding a URL or object-store key (S3) Not BYTEA/BLOB blobs
PasswordsTEXT holding a salted hash (bcrypt/Argon2)Never plain text
IP addressINET (PostgreSQL)Validates and compares correctly

Why not store files in the database?

A 2 MB photo inside a row inflates the table, slows backups and replication, and fills the buffer cache with bytes you rarely query. The database is good at structured data; object storage (S3, Azure Blob) is good at files and serves them cheaply through a CDN. Store the object key or URL, plus metadata (content type, size, checksum), and keep the file elsewhere. A tiny icon (a few KB) in a column is acceptable.

Size and range thinking

  • SMALLINT holds up to 32,767; INT up to about 2.1 billion; BIGINT up to about 9.2 quintillion.
  • Every byte is repeated in each row, each index entry and each foreign key. Shave bytes on huge tables (use SMALLINT for a status code on a billion-row log), not on small ones.
  • Enforce what the type cannot: CHECK (quantity > 0), CHECK (stars BETWEEN 1 AND 5), NOT NULL.

NULL or a default?

Make a column NOT NULL unless "unknown or not applicable" is a real state. A nullable column forces three-valued logic into every query (x = NULL is never true; see NULL logic). Use NULL for "we do not know" (a delivery time not yet set), and a real default for "zero by default" (rating_count INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0).

Mistake: a phone number or pincode as a number

Wrong

phone   BIGINT,   -- 0 and + lost; 91 prefix ambiguous
pincode INT       -- 011001 becomes 11001

Right

phone   TEXT CHECK (phone ~ '^\+[0-9]{8,15}$'),
pincode CHAR(6) CHECK (pincode ~ '^[0-9]{6}$')

PostgreSQL vs MySQL

MySQL needs a length for VARCHAR(n) and has TINYINT, DATETIME and ENUM; PostgreSQL has real BOOLEAN, TIMESTAMPTZ, UUID, JSONB, arrays and range types. Regex CHECK as shown uses PostgreSQL's ~; MySQL uses REGEXP.

Interview angle

  • Which type for a phone number? Text, in E.164 form; it is an identifier.
  • Where do you keep images? In object storage; the table keeps the URL or key.
  • VARCHAR(255) or TEXT? In PostgreSQL they perform the same; use a length only for a real rule.

Worked design: a food-delivery app

Follow the design process end to end for a Swiggy-style app: requirements, entities, ER diagram, full PostgreSQL DDL in 3NF with constraints and indexes, then the queries the app must run.

1. Requirements

Write the requirements as plain sentences first. Every noun is a candidate entity, every verb a candidate relationship (see the design process).

  • A user signs up with an email and a mobile number and can save several addresses.
  • A restaurant has a name, a city and an open/closed flag, and sells many menu items, each with a price and an availability flag.
  • A user places an order with exactly one restaurant. It has many order items, each with a quantity. The price and name at the time of ordering must be kept, and the delivery address must be remembered even if the saved address changes later.
  • An order moves through PLACED, ACCEPTED, PREPARING, OUT_FOR_DELIVERY, DELIVERED or CANCELLED.
  • An order is paid with one or more payments: a failed UPI attempt can be retried by card. Each payment has a method, an amount and a status.

2. Entities, relationships and the ER diagram

Reading it: a user has zero or many addresses and orders; an order has exactly one user and one restaurant, at least one order item (||--|{), and zero or many payments (zero while unpaid, several if the first attempt fails). order_items is the join table between orders and menu items with extra columns, so it is a real entity.

3. Design decisions

  • Keys: surrogate BIGINT identity everywhere; email and mobile are UNIQUE. order_items uses the composite key (order_id, menu_item_id): one line per item, and the quantity says how many.
  • Money: integer paise (_paise suffix) with CHECKs.
  • 3NF check: user data lives only in users, restaurant data only in restaurants, and so on; no non-key column depends on another non-key column. Restaurant name on an order is reached by a join, not copied.
  • Deliberate denormalization: order_items.item_name and unit_price_paise (price at order time), the delivery address text on orders, and orders.total_paise (sum of lines, written in the same transaction). Each has a reason; see Denormalization.
  • Delete rules: lines die with their order (CASCADE); users, restaurants and menu items that have orders cannot be deleted (RESTRICT); a deleted saved address does not affect old orders.
  • Status: text with CHECK for orders and payments.

4. The DDL

CREATE TABLE users (
    id         BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    email      TEXT NOT NULL,
    mobile     TEXT NOT NULL,
    name       TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT pk_users PRIMARY KEY (id),
    CONSTRAINT uq_users_email  UNIQUE (email),
    CONSTRAINT uq_users_mobile UNIQUE (mobile),
    CONSTRAINT ck_users_mobile CHECK (mobile ~ '^\+[0-9]{8,15}$')
);

CREATE TABLE addresses (
    id      BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    user_id BIGINT NOT NULL,
    label   TEXT,                            -- 'Home', 'Office'
    line1   TEXT NOT NULL,
    city    TEXT NOT NULL,
    pincode CHAR(6) NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT pk_addresses PRIMARY KEY (id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_addresses_user FOREIGN KEY (user_id)
        REFERENCES users (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    CONSTRAINT ck_addresses_pincode CHECK (pincode ~ '^[0-9]{6}$')
);
CREATE INDEX idx_addresses_user_id ON addresses (user_id);

CREATE TABLE restaurants (
    id         BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    name       TEXT NOT NULL,
    city       TEXT NOT NULL,
    is_open    BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT pk_restaurants PRIMARY KEY (id)
);
CREATE INDEX idx_restaurants_city ON restaurants (city);

CREATE TABLE menu_items (
    id            BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    restaurant_id BIGINT NOT NULL,
    name          TEXT NOT NULL,
    price_paise   BIGINT NOT NULL,
    is_available  BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    CONSTRAINT pk_menu_items PRIMARY KEY (id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_menu_items_restaurant FOREIGN KEY (restaurant_id)
        REFERENCES restaurants (id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
    CONSTRAINT uq_menu_items_restaurant_name UNIQUE (restaurant_id, name),
    CONSTRAINT ck_menu_items_price CHECK (price_paise >= 0)
);

The unique pair (restaurant_id, name) also serves as the index for "items of a restaurant", so no separate index on restaurant_id is needed.

CREATE TABLE orders (
    id               BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    user_id          BIGINT NOT NULL,
    restaurant_id    BIGINT NOT NULL,
    status           TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PLACED',
    delivery_address TEXT NOT NULL,          -- snapshot of the address text
    total_paise      BIGINT NOT NULL,        -- sum of lines, set when the order is placed
    placed_at        TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    updated_at       TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT pk_orders PRIMARY KEY (id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_orders_user FOREIGN KEY (user_id)
        REFERENCES users (id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
    CONSTRAINT fk_orders_restaurant FOREIGN KEY (restaurant_id)
        REFERENCES restaurants (id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
    CONSTRAINT ck_orders_status CHECK (status IN ('PLACED','ACCEPTED','PREPARING',
        'OUT_FOR_DELIVERY','DELIVERED','CANCELLED')),
    CONSTRAINT ck_orders_total CHECK (total_paise >= 0)
);
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_placed ON orders (user_id, placed_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_restaurant_placed ON orders (restaurant_id, placed_at);
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_active ON orders (status)
    WHERE status NOT IN ('DELIVERED','CANCELLED');           -- partial index

CREATE TABLE order_items (
    order_id         BIGINT NOT NULL,
    menu_item_id     BIGINT NOT NULL,
    item_name        TEXT   NOT NULL,        -- snapshot
    unit_price_paise BIGINT NOT NULL,        -- snapshot
    quantity         INT    NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT pk_order_items PRIMARY KEY (order_id, menu_item_id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_order_items_order FOREIGN KEY (order_id)
        REFERENCES orders (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    CONSTRAINT fk_order_items_menu_item FOREIGN KEY (menu_item_id)
        REFERENCES menu_items (id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
    CONSTRAINT ck_order_items_qty CHECK (quantity > 0),
    CONSTRAINT ck_order_items_price CHECK (unit_price_paise >= 0)
);
CREATE INDEX idx_order_items_menu_item_id ON order_items (menu_item_id);

CREATE TABLE payments (
    id           BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    order_id     BIGINT NOT NULL,
    method       TEXT   NOT NULL,
    amount_paise BIGINT NOT NULL,
    status       TEXT   NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PENDING',
    gateway_ref  TEXT,                       -- id from the payment gateway
    created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT pk_payments PRIMARY KEY (id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_payments_order FOREIGN KEY (order_id)
        REFERENCES orders (id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
    CONSTRAINT uq_payments_gateway_ref UNIQUE (gateway_ref),
    CONSTRAINT ck_payments_method CHECK (method IN ('UPI','CARD','NETBANKING','COD')),
    CONSTRAINT ck_payments_status CHECK (status IN ('PENDING','SUCCESS','FAILED','REFUNDED')),
    CONSTRAINT ck_payments_amount CHECK (amount_paise > 0)
);
CREATE INDEX idx_payments_order_id ON payments (order_id);

Why payments is separate from orders: an order can have several attempts, and payment facts (gateway id, status, refund) change independently of the order. The gateway reference is UNIQUE, which makes a repeated callback (webhook) from the gateway harmless: the second insert fails instead of charging twice.

5. Sample data

INSERT INTO users (email, mobile, name) VALUES
    ('asha@example.com',  '+919810000001', 'Asha'),
    ('bilal@example.com', '+919810000002', 'Bilal');
INSERT INTO addresses (user_id, label, line1, city, pincode) VALUES
    (1, 'Home', '12 MG Road', 'Pune', '411001');
INSERT INTO restaurants (name, city) VALUES ('Spice Hub', 'Pune'), ('Dosa Point', 'Pune');
INSERT INTO menu_items (restaurant_id, name, price_paise) VALUES
    (1, 'Paneer Tikka', 22000), (1, 'Butter Naan', 4000), (2, 'Masala Dosa', 12000);

6. The key queries

The last design step is to run the queries the app needs and check each is simple and can use an index. If a query is awkward, the model may be wrong.

Show a restaurant's menu
SELECT id, name, price_paise / 100.0 AS price_rupees
FROM menu_items
WHERE restaurant_id = 1 AND is_available
ORDER BY name;
 id |     name     | price_rupees
----+--------------+--------------
  2 | Butter Naan  |        40.00
  1 | Paneer Tikka |       220.00
(2 rows)
Place an order, in one transaction
BEGIN;

WITH new_order AS (
    INSERT INTO orders (user_id, restaurant_id, delivery_address, total_paise)
    VALUES (1, 1, '12 MG Road, Pune 411001', 2 * 22000 + 4 * 4000)
    RETURNING id
)
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, menu_item_id, item_name, unit_price_paise, quantity)
SELECT new_order.id, m.id, m.name, m.price_paise, v.qty
FROM new_order
JOIN (VALUES (1, 2), (2, 4)) AS v(menu_item_id, qty) ON TRUE
JOIN menu_items m ON m.id = v.menu_item_id AND m.restaurant_id = 1 AND m.is_available;

INSERT INTO payments (order_id, method, amount_paise, status, gateway_ref)
VALUES (currval(pg_get_serial_sequence('orders', 'id')), 'UPI', 60000, 'SUCCESS', 'pay_001');

COMMIT;

The service computes the total from the same prices it reads, in one transaction, so the order, its lines and the payment appear together or not at all (transactions are explained in Module 5). In Spring this is a @Transactional method. A real service would read the prices first and compute the total from the rows it inserted; the literal total here only keeps the example short.

One order with its lines
SELECT o.id, o.status, r.name AS restaurant, oi.item_name, oi.quantity,
       oi.unit_price_paise * oi.quantity AS line_paise, o.total_paise
FROM orders o
JOIN restaurants r ON r.id = o.restaurant_id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.id = 1;
 id |  status  | restaurant |  item_name   | quantity | line_paise | total_paise
----+----------+------------+--------------+----------+------------+-------------
  1 | PLACED   | Spice Hub  | Paneer Tikka |        2 |      44000 |       60000
  1 | PLACED   | Spice Hub  | Butter Naan  |        4 |      16000 |       60000
(2 rows)
A user's order history, newest first
SELECT o.id, r.name AS restaurant, o.status, o.total_paise, o.placed_at
FROM orders o
JOIN restaurants r ON r.id = o.restaurant_id
WHERE o.user_id = 1
ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC
LIMIT 20;
Reports: revenue per restaurant per day, best-selling items
SELECT r.name, o.placed_at::date AS day, COUNT(*) AS orders, SUM(o.total_paise) / 100.0 AS revenue
FROM orders o
JOIN restaurants r ON r.id = o.restaurant_id
WHERE o.status = 'DELIVERED' AND o.placed_at >= now() - INTERVAL '30 days'
GROUP BY r.name, o.placed_at::date
ORDER BY day DESC, revenue DESC;

SELECT oi.item_name, SUM(oi.quantity) AS sold
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
WHERE o.restaurant_id = 1 AND o.status = 'DELIVERED'
GROUP BY oi.item_name
ORDER BY sold DESC
LIMIT 5;
Orders still unpaid, and the live kitchen queue
-- Orders with no successful payment
SELECT o.id, o.total_paise
FROM orders o
WHERE o.status <> 'CANCELLED'
  AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM payments p
                  WHERE p.order_id = o.id AND p.status = 'SUCCESS');

-- Live orders (served by the partial index idx_orders_active)
SELECT id, status FROM orders
WHERE status NOT IN ('DELIVERED','CANCELLED') ORDER BY id;

7. Review checklist

  1. Every table has a primary key and every relationship a named foreign key.
  2. Every foreign key column is indexed (the composite keys cover their first column).
  3. Every column is NOT NULL unless "unknown" is real.
  4. Every rule that data must follow has a CHECK, UNIQUE or foreign key.
  5. Each key query was written and its plan looked at with EXPLAIN (see Module 5).
  6. Denormalized columns have a documented source of truth.

What this design leaves out on purpose

  • Riders and delivery tracking, coupons, taxes and delivery fees, reviews and ratings, menu categories and restaurant opening hours. Each is a new table or two (for example coupons and order_coupons).
  • A status history table (order_status_history) for audit and for the "where is my order" timeline.
  • Soft delete for menu items: here is_available = false does the job, and old orders keep their snapshot.

Mistake: no snapshot of price or address

Joining an old order to the current menu price rewrites history: a ₹220 order becomes ₹250 the day the restaurant changes the menu. Copy what must not change into the order.

Interview angle

"Design the database for a food-delivery app" is a classic. A strong answer: list entities from the requirements, draw the ER diagram, name each relationship's cardinality, implement the many-to-many through order_items with price snapshot, put money in paise, add status CHECKs and foreign key indexes, normalize to 3NF, justify each denormalization, and finish by writing the key queries.

Interview questions — Database design

What are the steps of a database design process?

Collect requirements; find entities (nouns) and attributes; define relationships and their cardinality; draw the ER diagram; choose primary and foreign keys; normalize (usually to 3NF); add constraints and indexes; finally write the key queries and check they are simple and fast.
