If a needed query is awkward, go back and adjust the model.

How do you implement one-to-one, one-to-many and many-to-many in SQL?

One-to-many: a foreign key on the "many" table. One-to-one: a foreign key with a UNIQUE constraint, or a shared primary key. Many-to-many: a join table with a foreign key to each side, and a composite primary key (or a surrogate key plus a unique pair). Attributes of the relationship, such as quantity, live in the join table.

Candidate key vs primary key vs alternate key vs super key?

A super key is any column set that is unique. A candidate key is a minimal super key. The primary key is the candidate key you choose; the other candidate keys are alternate keys, implemented with UNIQUE. Example: in employees, id, email and pan are candidate keys, id is the primary key, and {id, name} is a super key but not a candidate key.

Surrogate or natural primary key? Why?

Prefer a surrogate key (a generated BIGINT): it never changes, is small, and does not leak personal data into child tables. Keep natural keys (email, SKU) as UNIQUE constraints. Natural keys are fine for tiny stable code tables such as currency INR. The risk with natural keys is that "stable" values change, forcing updates in every referencing table.

BIGINT identity or UUID for a primary key?

BIGINT is 8 bytes, readable, and inserts append at the end of the index. UUIDs are 16 bytes but can be created anywhere and are hard to guess. Random v4 UUIDs scatter inserts and bloat indexes; time-ordered v7 UUIDs fix most of that. A common compromise is a BIGINT primary key plus a public_id UUID for URLs.

What are update, insert and delete anomalies?

They are problems of a table that mixes subjects. Update: the same fact is stored in many rows and some copies change. Insert: you cannot record a restaurant until it has an order. Delete: deleting the last order deletes the only record of the restaurant. Normalization removes them by giving each fact one home.

Explain 1NF, 2NF and 3NF with an example.

1NF: atomic cells, no repeating groups (one row per ordered item). 2NF: no column depends on only part of a composite key (item price moves out of order_lines into menu_items). 3NF: no non-key column depends on another non-key column (customer city moves from orders to customers). Summary phrase: the key, the whole key, and nothing but the key.

What is the difference between 3NF and BCNF?

BCNF requires that for every dependency X → Y, X is a super key. 3NF also accepts it when Y is part of a candidate key. The difference shows only with overlapping composite candidate keys, for example dispatcher → zone in (rider_id, zone, dispatcher). BCNF can lose the ability to enforce a dependency with a plain constraint; 3NF decomposition never does.

When would you denormalize, and how do you keep the data correct?

For measured read problems (reports, counters like average rating) and for history snapshots such as the unit price on an order line. Keep one source of truth and refresh the copy in the same transaction, with a trigger, a materialized view, or a periodic recompute job. Store the sum and count, not the average.

How do you store money and why not FLOAT?

Use NUMERIC(p,s) or integer paise in a BIGINT. Floating point cannot represent values like 0.1 exactly, so sums drift (0.1 + 0.2 gives 0.30000000000000004). Name the unit in the column (amount_paise) and add a currency column if needed.

TIMESTAMP vs TIMESTAMPTZ?

In PostgreSQL, TIMESTAMPTZ stores an exact instant (converted to UTC) and shows it in the session time zone. TIMESTAMP keeps a wall-clock reading with no zone, which is ambiguous across servers and daylight changes. Use TIMESTAMPTZ for events and convert to local time in the UI.

What are the problems with soft delete?

Every query must filter deleted_at IS NULL (use a view or framework support); unique constraints clash with deleted rows (use a partial unique index); cascades do not fire; tables grow; and privacy deletion requests need real removal or anonymising.

Why are polymorphic associations discouraged?

A (target_type, target_id) pair cannot be a foreign key, so the database cannot prevent orphans or cascade deletes. Use one nullable foreign key per target with a CHECK that exactly one is set, or separate tables.

Why does a phone number not belong in an INT column?

It is an identifier, not a quantity: you never add phone numbers, and leading zeros, + and country codes get lost. Store it as text in E.164 form, with a CHECK on the pattern.

Design the tables for a food-delivery app.

Entities: users, addresses, restaurants, menu_items, orders, order_items, payments. Users to orders and restaurants to menu items are one-to-many; orders to menu items is many-to-many through order_items, which carries quantity and the price at order time. Orders to payments is one-to-many (retries). Use surrogate BIGINT keys, paise for money, CHECKs for status, and index every foreign key.

Cheat sheet — Database design

  • Process requirements → entities → attributes → relationships → keys → normalize → constraints and indexes → review queries
  • One-to-many foreign key on the many side; NOT NULL if the parent is mandatory
  • One-to-one foreign key + UNIQUE, or shared primary key
  • Many-to-many join table; composite PK, or surrogate id + UNIQUE (a_id, b_id)
  • Super key any unique column set; candidate key minimal one; primary key the chosen one; alternate key the others
  • Surrogate key generated id (default choice); natural key real-world value, keep as UNIQUE
  • IDs BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY; UUID v7 over v4; public_id for URLs
  • FD A → B means each A has exactly one B; keys decide all columns
  • Anomalies update (many copies), insert (no row for a new fact), delete (lose another fact)
  • 1NF atomic cells, no repeating groups
  • 2NF no dependency on part of a composite key
  • 3NF no transitive dependency; the practical target for OLTP
  • BCNF every determinant is a super key; may lose dependency preservation
  • 4NF split independent multi-valued facts into separate tables
  • Denormalize only after measuring, or for snapshots and counters; keep one source of truth
  • Audit columns created_at, updated_at (NOT NULL, DEFAULT now()), created_by
  • Soft delete deleted_at + partial unique index + view; purge old rows
  • Status text + CHECK, or a lookup table; avoid numeric codes and risky ENUMs
  • Money NUMERIC or paise BIGINT; never FLOAT
  • Time TIMESTAMPTZ in UTC; DATE for dates only
  • JSONB for variable, rarely joined data; GIN index; promote hot keys to columns
  • Trees parent_id + recursive CTE; polymorphic (type,id) is a trap
  • Names snake_case, id, customer_id, fk_orders_customer, uq_users_email, idx_orders_customer_id, is_active
  • Types phone and pincode as text; files as URL or key in the database

Practice — Database design

  1. Draw it. From the sentence "a library lends books to members; a book can have many authors; a loan has a due date" list entities, attributes and cardinalities, and draw the Mermaid erDiagram. Done when every relationship has the right crow's foot symbols and the many-to-many has a join table.
  2. Implement the relationships. Write CREATE TABLE for the library with named constraints: one-to-many (member to loans), many-to-many (books to authors) and a one-to-one (member to library card with UNIQUE). Insert rows and prove that duplicates and orphans are rejected.
  3. Find the keys. For employees(id, email, pan, name) list all super keys of size one and two, the candidate keys, the primary key and the alternate keys. Done when you can explain each choice.
  4. Normalize. Take the wide table order_sheet from the anomalies article, create it with data, demonstrate one update, one insert and one delete anomaly, then normalize step by step to 3NF and show the tables after each step.
  5. BCNF. Create rider_zones, show the redundancy, decompose it into two tables and note which rule can no longer be a single constraint.
  6. Patterns. Add audit columns with an updated_at trigger to a table, then add soft delete with a partial unique index on email. Show that a deleted user's email can be reused.
  7. Roadmap exercise: food delivery. Without looking at the worked design, design tables for users, restaurants, menu items, orders, order items and payments, normalized to 3NF, with an ER diagram. Compare with the article and list the differences. Done when every foreign key is indexed and the price snapshot is explained.
  8. Key queries. Load seed data and write the queries: menu, place order (transaction), order history, revenue per restaurant per day, best sellers, unpaid orders. Done when all six run and you can state which index each uses.
  9. Choose types. Review a table you wrote earlier and fix every wrong type (phone as number, money as float, timestamp without zone). Explain each fix in one sentence.
  10. Review a schema. Take a classmate's or an online sample schema and write a review: missing keys, missing indexes, wrong types, naming problems, normalization problems.
5

Module 5·Months 3–5Must for interviews

How databases work inside

12 topics · about 1.5 h of reading

Modules 1 to 4 taught you to write SQL and design tables. This module opens the box and shows what the database does with your statements: how rows sit on disk, why an index turns a minute-long query into a millisecond one, how to read a query plan, how transactions keep money safe when two users act at once, and how your Spring Boot app talks to the database through a connection pool. These are the most asked database topics in Java interviews, and the ones that decide whether your app is fast in production.

Everything here uses PostgreSQL, with MySQL InnoDB differences noted. You will run a scripted 1-million-row experiment, replay two-session timelines of every isolation anomaly, and meet the N+1 problem that JPA makes so easy to create (Spring Boot · Module 3). The tables come from the schema designed in Module 4.

  • Explain how a B-tree index works and decide when an index helps or hurts.
  • Design composite indexes with the leftmost-prefix rule, and find out why an index is ignored.
  • Read EXPLAIN and EXPLAIN ANALYZE output and fix a slow query.
  • Use transactions correctly, and explain ACID with a bank transfer.
  • Name each isolation level and the anomalies it prevents, and handle serialization failures.
  • Use row locks, SKIP LOCKED, and optimistic versus pessimistic locking.
  • Size a HikariCP pool and spot pool exhaustion.
  • Recognize, detect and fix the N+1 query problem.

How data is stored, and why a full scan is slow

A database stores rows in fixed-size pages (blocks) on disk; without an index it must read every page of a table (a sequential scan), so cost grows with table size.

Pages, not rows

Disks and memory do not read single rows. The database divides each table file into pages (also called blocks): 8 KB in PostgreSQL, 16 KB in MySQL InnoDB. A page holds many rows. To read one row the database loads the whole page into its memory cache (the buffer pool, called shared_buffers in PostgreSQL). Reading from memory takes microseconds; reading from disk takes far longer, even on SSD. Most database tuning is about touching fewer pages.

Heap tables and clustered tables

  • PostgreSQL uses a heap: rows sit in the table file in roughly the order they were written, with no sorting. Every index is separate and points to a row by its physical address, the tuple id (page number and slot).
  • MySQL InnoDB uses a clustered index: the table is the primary key B-tree, and rows are stored in primary key order. Secondary indexes store the primary key value, so a lookup through a secondary index does a second search in the primary key tree.

This is why a large random primary key (UUID v4) hurts InnoDB more: every insert lands in a random place in the table itself. In PostgreSQL the effect is smaller, because only the index suffers.

The full table scan

To answer WHERE customer_id = 42 with no index, the database has no choice: read every page of the table, test each row, and keep the matches. This is a sequential scan (Seq Scan in PostgreSQL, "ALL" in MySQL). If the table has 1 million rows in 10,000 pages, all 10,000 pages are read to return maybe 10 rows. Doubling the table doubles the work: the cost is O(n).

A sequential scan is not always bad. For a tiny table, or a query that needs a large part of the table (say 30% of rows), reading everything in order is the cheapest plan, because sequential reads are fast and an index adds extra lookups. The goal is to avoid scans that read a lot to return a little.

Selectivity and cardinality

Cardinality is the number of distinct values in a column. Selectivity is the fraction of rows a condition keeps. WHERE email = 'x' keeps one row in a million: very selective, perfect for an index. WHERE is_active = true keeps 95% of rows: not selective, and an index does not help. A column like status with 5 values has low cardinality; email has high cardinality. Rule of thumb: an index pays off when a query reads a small fraction of the table (a few percent or less). The planner decides using the statistics it collects, see EXPLAIN.

The buffer cache hides the first lesson

A table that fits in memory scans quickly, which is why a slow query can look fine on your laptop with 10,000 rows and collapse on production with 50 million. Always test with realistic row counts; the experiment in the EXPLAIN article shows how.

Interview angle

  • What is a page/block? The fixed-size unit (8 KB in PostgreSQL, 16 KB in InnoDB) the database reads and caches; many rows per page.
  • Heap vs clustered table? PostgreSQL keeps unsorted rows and separate indexes; InnoDB stores rows inside the primary key B-tree.
  • Why is a full scan slow? It reads every page, O(n) in table size.

Indexes and B-trees

An index is a separate sorted structure (usually a B-tree) that lets the database find rows in O(log n) steps instead of scanning the table; it speeds up reads but costs space and slows every insert, update and delete.

The idea: a book's index

To find "Normalization" in a thick book you do not read every page; you look it up in the index at the back, which is sorted and points to page numbers. A database index works the same way: a sorted copy of one or more columns, with a pointer to the row. Because it is sorted, the database can binary-search it.

CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_id ON orders (user_id);

SELECT id, total_paise FROM orders WHERE user_id = 42;   -- can now use the index

DROP INDEX idx_orders_user_id;

How a B-tree looks

The default index type everywhere is the B-tree (in practice a B+ tree). It is a balanced tree of pages: a root page, some internal pages, and leaf pages at the bottom. Each internal entry holds a key and a pointer to a child page that covers the range below it. The leaf pages hold every key in sorted order, plus the pointer to the table row, and are linked to their neighbours.

Looking up key 57: read the root (57 is between 40 and 80, go to the middle child), read that page (57 is between 50 and 65, go to its middle child), read the leaf, find 57 and its row pointer, then read the table page. That is three index pages and one table page, not 10,000.

Why lookups are O(log n)

A page holds hundreds of keys, so the tree is very wide and very shallow. With about 300 keys per page, 2 levels address 90,000 rows, 3 levels 27 million, and 4 levels 8 billion. A lookup in a table of tens of millions of rows touches about 3 or 4 pages. The top levels stay in memory, so usually only the leaf and the table page come from disk. Inserts keep the tree balanced by splitting a full page into two.

What a B-tree is good at

  • Equality: WHERE id = 5.
  • Ranges: WHERE placed_at >= '2026-11-01' AND placed_at < '2026-12-01'. Find the start in the tree, then walk the linked leaves in order. A hash index cannot do this.
  • Sorting: ORDER BY placed_at LIMIT 10 reads the first 10 leaf entries, no sort needed.
  • Prefix match: LIKE 'Pan%' is a range in disguise (with the right collation or operator class, see When an index is not used).
  • MIN/MAX: the first or last leaf entry.

Index types in PostgreSQL

TypeUse it for
B-tree (default)Equality, ranges, sorting on almost any column
HashEquality only; rarely better than a B-tree
GINValues that contain many items: JSONB, arrays, full-text search
GiSTGeometric and range types, nearest-neighbour, some full-text
BRINHuge tables where values follow the physical order (time-series logs); tiny index, coarse

MySQL InnoDB uses B-trees by default and also offers full-text and spatial indexes. In an interview, say "B-tree unless I have a special data type".

What an index costs

An index is not free. Each one is another structure on disk and in memory, and the database must keep it in step with the table.

OperationEffect of each extra index
SELECT with a matching conditionMuch faster
INSERTOne more entry to add, with possible page splits
UPDATE of an indexed columnThe entry must move; in PostgreSQL this can also block cheap in-place "HOT" updates
DELETEOne more entry to mark dead and clean later
Disk and memoryOften 10–30% of the table size per index, competing for cache
PlannerMore plans to consider (a small cost)

So how many indexes is too many? There is no number. Add an index for a query you have proven slow and that runs often; drop indexes that are never used. A read-heavy reporting table can carry many; a write-heavy table (event log, payment attempts) should have few. Find unused ones with PostgreSQL's statistics view:

SELECT relname AS table_name, indexrelname AS index_name, idx_scan
FROM pg_stat_user_indexes
WHERE idx_scan = 0
ORDER BY relname;

Do not drop primary key or unique indexes that show zero scans: they enforce rules, not just speed.

Indexes on foreign keys

PostgreSQL does not index a foreign key column automatically. Without an index on orders.user_id, the query "orders of user 42" scans the table, and so does every DELETE on users, which must check that no order still points at that user. Index every foreign key you join on or delete through. MySQL InnoDB creates the index for you.

Rule: primary key and UNIQUE create an index; nothing else does

Declaring PRIMARY KEY or UNIQUE builds a unique B-tree index automatically. A plain foreign key, a CHECK or a column you often filter on gets none; you add it with CREATE INDEX.

Convention: add indexes in migrations, named and reviewed

Name them idx_<table>_<columns>. On a busy production PostgreSQL table, use CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY: it builds without blocking writes (it takes longer and cannot run inside a transaction block).

Mistake: "index every column"

It slows writes, wastes memory and the planner often ignores indexes on low-selectivity columns anyway. Start from your slow queries, not from the column list.

Interview angle

  • What is an index and how does it work? A sorted B-tree of column values with row pointers; lookups take O(log n) page reads; leaves are linked for range scans.
  • When does an index slow things down? Writes (every index is updated), disk/cache use, and low-selectivity columns where it is not used.
  • B-tree vs hash? Hash does equality only; a B-tree also handles ranges, sorting and prefixes.
  • Does a primary key have an index? Yes, a unique one, created automatically.

Unique, partial, expression and covering indexes

Beyond a plain index you can enforce uniqueness, index only some rows (partial), index a computed value (expression), or store extra columns in the index so the table is never touched (covering with INCLUDE, enabling index-only scans).

A plain B-tree index (see Indexes and B-trees) covers every row and every value of one or more columns exactly as stored. The four variants below change one of those three words: which rows (partial), what value (expression), what else is stored (covering) or what the index promises (unique). They all use the same B-tree underneath, so the reading rules from composite indexes still apply. Examples use the food-app tables users and orders from this module.

Unique index

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX is what a UNIQUE constraint uses underneath. It speeds lookups and rejects duplicates.

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_users_email_lower ON users (lower(email));

INSERT INTO users (email, mobile, name) VALUES ('Asha@Example.com', '+919800000001', 'Asha');
INSERT INTO users (email, mobile, name) VALUES ('asha@example.com', '+919800000002', 'A2');
-- ERROR:  duplicate key value violates unique constraint "uq_users_email_lower"
-- DETAIL:  Key (lower(email))=(asha@example.com) already exists.

How uniqueness is enforced. On every insert or update the database searches the B-tree for the new key. If it already exists, the statement fails; if another transaction is inserting the same key at that moment, this one waits until the other commits or rolls back, then decides. That is why a unique index is the only reliable duplicate guard: a "check, then insert" in application code lets two parallel requests both pass the check. In Spring this failure arrives as DataIntegrityViolationException.

A UNIQUE constraint and a unique index do the same job. The constraint is the declaration ("this must be unique"); the index is the machinery. Use a plain unique index only when you need something a constraint cannot express: a WHERE condition (partial) or an expression. A unique index may span columns, as in UNIQUE (restaurant_id, name): the pair must be unique, not each column.

NULLs in a unique index

PostgreSQL and MySQL both allow many rows with NULL in a unique column, because NULL is never equal to NULL. PostgreSQL 15 added UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT for the case where at most one NULL is allowed. On older versions, make the column NOT NULL or build the unique index on an expression such as COALESCE(col, '').

Partial index

A partial index covers only rows that match a WHERE condition. It is smaller, cheaper to maintain, and exactly right when queries always target a small subset.

-- Only active orders: the kitchen screen reads these all day
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_active ON orders (restaurant_id)
    WHERE status NOT IN ('DELIVERED', 'CANCELLED');

-- Unique only among live rows (soft delete)
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_users_email_live ON users (email) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

The planner uses a partial index only when the query's WHERE clause implies the index condition. MySQL does not support partial indexes.

Typical uses of a partial index:

SituationIndexWhy it helps
Soft delete with a unique ruleUNIQUE (email) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL A deleted user's email can be registered again
Queue of "pending" rows(created_at) WHERE status = 'PENDING' Millions of finished rows are not in the index at all
Rare flag(user_id) WHERE is_featuredTiny index for 1% of rows
Mostly-NULL column(coupon_code) WHERE coupon_code IS NOT NULL NULL rows add nothing and are skipped

The planner may use a partial index only when it can prove that the query's condition implies the index condition. Repeat the same condition in the query to be safe:

SELECT order_id
FROM orders
WHERE status NOT IN ('DELIVERED', 'CANCELLED')   -- same condition as the index
  AND restaurant_id = 7;

A condition that depends on a bind parameter (status = $1) may not let the planner prove the match for a cached generic plan, so check with EXPLAIN from the real application, not only from psql (EXPLAIN). When a row's update changes whether it matches, the database adds or removes its entry in the index; this is cheap.

Expression (functional) index

If a query wraps a column in a function, a normal index on the column cannot help. Index the expression:

-- Slow: the function hides the column from a plain index on email
SELECT * FROM users WHERE lower(email) = 'asha@example.com';

CREATE INDEX idx_users_email_lower ON users (lower(email));
-- Now the same query can use the index. The expression must match exactly.

-- Dates: orders placed on a given day
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_placed_date ON orders ((placed_at::date));

MySQL 8.0.13 and later supports functional key parts in the same way, written with extra parentheses.

The database computes the expression when the row is written and stores the result in the index. At query time it computes the expression on your constant and searches the index. If your query writes the expression differently, there is no match and the index is ignored. Functions used in an expression index must be IMMUTABLE (same input, same output, always). lower(email) qualifies; now() or a function that depends on the session time zone does not.

MySQL differences. From 8.0.13 MySQL accepts functional key parts, with an extra pair of parentheses: CREATE INDEX idx_email_lower ON users ((LOWER(email)));. MySQL's default collations are case-insensitive, so a plain index on email already serves email = 'Asha@Example.com' there, and the lower() trick is less common. Before 8.0.13 the workaround was a generated column plus an index on it, which also works in PostgreSQL (12+) as GENERATED ALWAYS AS (…) STORED.

Covering index and index-only scan

Normally the index finds the row pointer, then the database fetches the table page for the other columns (a heap fetch). If the index itself contains every column the query needs, the table visit is skipped. This is a covering index, and PostgreSQL shows it as Index Only Scan. INCLUDE (PostgreSQL 11+) adds payload columns that are stored in the index but not used for searching or ordering.

CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_cover
    ON orders (user_id, placed_at DESC) INCLUDE (status, total_paise);

-- All columns are in the index: Index Only Scan
SELECT placed_at, status, total_paise
FROM orders
WHERE user_id = 42
ORDER BY placed_at DESC
LIMIT 10;

PostgreSQL keeps old row versions (MVCC, see Isolation levels), so an index-only scan must check the table's visibility map to know that a row is visible to everyone. That map is kept current by VACUUM; on a heavily updated table the scan may still visit the heap often. MySQL InnoDB gets the same effect when a secondary index includes all needed columns, because every secondary index already carries the primary key.

The plan shows whether it worked. The line to look for is Index Only Scan and, below it, Heap Fetches: 0 (or a small number):

Limit
  ->  Index Only Scan using idx_orders_user_cover on orders
        Index Cond: (user_id = 42)
        Heap Fetches: 0

Notice the split inside idx_orders_user_cover: user_id and placed_at are the key, used for searching and sorting; status and total_paise are payload that rides along in the leaf pages. If the query filters or sorts on status, put it in the key, not in INCLUDE. An INCLUDE column also takes no part in uniqueness: CREATE UNIQUE INDEX … (user_id) INCLUDE (status) still guarantees only that user_id is unique.

MySQL. There is no INCLUDE. You get a covering index by adding the extra columns to the end of the key itself, which makes the key larger. EXPLAIN then shows Using index in the Extra column. The primary key columns are always part of every InnoDB secondary index, so selecting the id is free.

Choosing between them

NeedUse
Rule: no duplicatesUnique index or UNIQUE constraint
Queries touch a small, fixed subsetPartial index
Query filters on lower(col), date(col), col + 1 Expression index (or rewrite the query)
Hot query reads 2-3 columns of many rowsCovering index with INCLUDE
JSONB / array / full-text searchGIN index

What each variant costs

VariantSizeWrite costMain risk
Uniquelike a plain indexone lookup per writeA violation fails the whole statement; plan for it in code
Partialsmaller than plainonly rows that matchIgnored if the query does not imply the condition
Expressionlike a plain indexfunction runs on every writeQuery must use the identical expression
Coveringlarger (extra columns)higher; payload updates touch the index Heap fetches stay high on tables rarely vacuumed

Every index adds work to each INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE and takes disk and memory. Add an index for a measured slow query (EXPLAIN), not "just in case". A covering index that exists for one query is easy to forget; name it for its purpose.

Rule: the query must match the index definition

Partial: the query's WHERE must imply the index WHERE. Expression: the query must use the same expression. Covering: every column the query reads or sorts by must be in the key or in INCLUDE, and SELECT * almost never qualifies. If the shape does not match, the index is silently ignored; see When an index is not used.

Convention: name, create concurrently, verify

  • Name them by role: uq_users_email_live, idx_orders_active, idx_orders_user_cover.
  • On a live PostgreSQL table use CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY: it does not block writes, but cannot run inside a transaction block, and a failed run leaves an INVALID index that you must drop. MySQL builds most secondary indexes online with InnoDB; check the LOCK clause when in doubt.
  • After creating one, run EXPLAIN to confirm it is used, and drop it if it is not.

Mistake: a plain unique index on a soft-deleted table

Wrong: blocks re-registration

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_users_email ON users (email);
-- a user with deleted_at set still owns the address forever

Right: unique among live rows

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_users_email_live
    ON users (email) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

A similar slip: index email, then query WHERE lower(email) = …. The plain index cannot be used; create the expression index or store the email already lower-cased.

Edge cases

  • An expression index works only if the query uses the same expression: lower(email) is not LOWER(TRIM(email)).
  • Wide INCLUDE lists make the index big and slow to write. Include only what the hot query needs.
  • Index-only scans do not help on tables that change constantly and are rarely vacuumed.
  • A unique index treats NULLs as distinct in PostgreSQL, so many rows can have a NULL value.

Interview angle

What is a covering index? An index that holds every column a query needs, so the database answers from the index alone (index-only scan) without reading the table. What is a partial index? An index over only the rows matching a condition: smaller and cheaper, for queries that always filter on that condition.

Follow-ups: "why was my index-only scan still reading the table?" (the visibility map is stale; VACUUM it), "how do you enforce unique emails ignoring case?" (unique index on lower(email)), "why not index every column?" (write cost and memory) and "how do you add an index to a busy table?" (CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY).

Composite indexes and the leftmost-prefix rule

A composite index on (a, b, c) is sorted by a, then b within a, then c within b, so it can serve queries that use a leftmost prefix of its columns (a; a, b; a, b, c) but not queries that skip the first column.

Why column order matters

Think of a phone book sorted by last name, then first name. You can find "Sharma" quickly. You can find "Sharma, Asha" quickly. But you cannot find everyone called "Asha" quickly, because Ashas are spread across the whole book. A composite B-tree index is that phone book. The first column is the primary sort; each following column sorts inside it.

CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_status_placed
    ON orders (user_id, status, placed_at);

Which queries can use (a, b, c)?

With a = user_id, b = status, c = placed_at:

Query conditionUses the index?Why
a = ?YesLeftmost prefix
a = ? AND b = ?YesPrefix of two
a = ? AND b = ? AND c = ?Yes, fullyAll three
a = ? AND c = ?Partly: seeks on a, then filters c insideb is skipped, so c cannot narrow the seek
b = ?Poorly (full index scan at best)Does not start with a
b = ? AND c = ?PoorlyDoes not start with a
c = ?PoorlyDoes not start with a
a = ? AND b > ? AND c = ?Seeks on a, b range; c only filtersAfter a range column, later columns cannot narrow the seek

This is the leftmost-prefix rule. (PostgreSQL 18 added a "skip scan" that can sometimes use the index without the first column, but do not design around it; write the index so the first column is in the query.) MySQL follows the same prefix rule.

How to choose the column order

  1. Equality columns first. Columns tested with = narrow the search to one spot in the tree.
  2. Range column last. A range (>, <, BETWEEN) or sort column goes after the equality columns, because the index can walk a range only along its last used column.
  3. Among equalities, put the most selective, or the most commonly used alone, first. A column that appears alone in other queries is a good first column, so one index serves several queries.
  4. Check the sort. If the query has ORDER BY, make the index order match so no separate sort step is needed.
-- Query: a user's delivered orders in the last 30 days, newest first
SELECT id, total_paise, placed_at
FROM orders
WHERE user_id = 42 AND status = 'DELIVERED' AND placed_at >= now() - INTERVAL '30 days'
ORDER BY placed_at DESC;

-- Best index: equalities (user_id, status), then the range/sort column
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_status_placed ON orders (user_id, status, placed_at);
-- A B-tree can be scanned backwards, so ASC also serves ORDER BY ... DESC.

With one index on (user_id) and another on (status), PostgreSQL can combine them (a bitmap AND) but a single composite index is faster and cheaper. Also notice: an index on (a, b) makes a separate index on (a) redundant. Drop the single-column one.

Sort order and ORDER BY

The index can return rows already sorted, which turns ORDER BY ... LIMIT 10 into reading 10 index entries. For mixed directions (ORDER BY user_id ASC, placed_at DESC) declare the same directions in the index: (user_id ASC, placed_at DESC). For a single direction, a backwards scan covers the opposite order.

Index with a join

For a join, the column used to match rows on the inner side needs an index. In the food-delivery schema the join order_items.order_id = orders.id uses the composite primary key (order_id, menu_item_id) of order_items, whose leftmost column is order_id, so no extra index is required. The reverse lookup ("all orders containing this item") filters on the second column and needs idx_order_items_menu_item_id.

Rule: the first column decides whether the index can be used

Put in the first position a column that appears in the WHERE clause of the queries you want to speed up. An index starting with a column your query never filters on is nearly useless to it.

Mistake: guessing the order from the table definition

Wrong: range column first

CREATE INDEX idx_o ON orders (placed_at, user_id);
-- WHERE user_id = 42 AND placed_at >= ...: seeks on placed_at,
-- then must test user_id on every entry in the range

Right: equality first

CREATE INDEX idx_o ON orders (user_id, placed_at);
-- seeks straight to user 42, then walks the date range

Tip: one index for several queries

List your top five queries on a table and look for columns they share as the first filter. One composite index with that column first often replaces three single-column ones.

Interview angle

  • Explain the leftmost-prefix rule. An index on (a, b, c) is sorted by a then b then c, so it helps queries filtering on a, on a and b, or on a, b and c, but not on b or c alone.
  • How do you order columns? Equality columns first, then the range or sort column last.
  • Is an index on (a, b) enough for queries on a? Yes; the extra index on (a) is redundant.

When an index is not used (and how to fix it)

An index is ignored when the query hides the column behind a function or cast, uses a leading wildcard, has low selectivity, queries a tiny table, uses OR across columns, or breaks the column order; check the plan with EXPLAIN and rewrite the query or the index.

The usual suspects

"I created an index but the query is still slow" almost always matches one of these cases. Each shows the wrong query, why, and the fix.

CauseExampleFix
Function on the columnWHERE lower(email) = 'x'Expression index, or store lowercase
Arithmetic on the columnWHERE total_paise + 100 > 5000 Move math to the constant: total_paise > 4900
Leading wildcardWHERE name LIKE '%tikka'Trigram index (pg_trgm) or full-text search
Implicit type castWHERE mobile = 9810000001 (text column, number) Use the right type: '+919810000001'
Low selectivityWHERE is_open = true (95% of rows)No index; or a partial index for the rare value
Tiny tableTable of 200 rowsNothing: a scan is cheaper
OR across columnsWHERE user_id = 1 OR restaurant_id = 2 An index on each (bitmap OR), or UNION two queries
Wrong column orderIndex (a, b), query WHERE b = ? Index (b, a), or add one on (b)
NOT IN, <>, !=WHERE status <> 'DELIVERED' Usually matches most rows; use a partial index on the positive condition
Stale statisticsPlan thinks the table is empty after a big loadRun ANALYZE

Example 1: function on the column

CREATE INDEX idx_users_email ON users (email);

EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM users WHERE lower(email) = 'asha@example.com';
-- Seq Scan on users  (Filter: (lower(email) = 'asha@example.com'::text))

-- Fix: index the expression the query uses
CREATE INDEX idx_users_email_lower ON users (lower(email));
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM users WHERE lower(email) = 'asha@example.com';
-- Index Scan using idx_users_email_lower on users

Example 2: leading wildcard

A B-tree is sorted from the first character. LIKE 'Pan%' is a range from "Pan" to "Pao", so it can use the index (in PostgreSQL the index needs the text_pattern_ops operator class unless the database uses the C collation). LIKE '%tikka' could match anywhere, so every entry must be tested.

CREATE INDEX idx_menu_items_name_prefix ON menu_items (name text_pattern_ops);
SELECT * FROM menu_items WHERE name LIKE 'Pan%';        -- can use the index

CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm;
CREATE INDEX idx_menu_items_name_trgm ON menu_items USING gin (name gin_trgm_ops);
SELECT * FROM menu_items WHERE name LIKE '%tikka%';     -- can use the trigram index

Example 3: implicit cast

-- mobile is TEXT with an index
SELECT * FROM users WHERE mobile = 9810000001;
-- ERROR:  operator does not exist: text = integer   (PostgreSQL refuses)
-- MySQL would silently convert every row's text to a number and ignore the index.

PostgreSQL is strict, but ORMs and JDBC can still send a parameter with the wrong type (a bigint parameter against a numeric column, or a varchar against a text/char mismatch in older drivers), which turns the index off. Keep Java types and column types aligned.

Example 4: low selectivity

-- 95% of restaurants are open
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM restaurants WHERE is_open = true;
-- Seq Scan on restaurants  (Filter: is_open)
-- Reading 95% of the table through an index is slower than just scanning it.

The planner is not broken; it compared costs and chose the cheaper plan. Don't force an index in this case.

How to diagnose

  1. Run EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) your_query and read the scan node.
  2. If it says Seq Scan on a big table, check which of the causes above applies.
  3. Check estimated vs actual rows. A big difference means stale statistics: run ANALYZE table_name.
  4. Fix the query or the index, then run EXPLAIN again and compare.

Edge cases

  • Sometimes the index is ignored because the planner is right. Test with SET enable_seqscan = off; in a session to see what the index plan would cost, but never leave that setting in production.
  • After a bulk load or a large delete, the planner may use old statistics. ANALYZE fixes it; autovacuum does this in the background but not instantly.
  • A query that returns a large fraction of rows can legitimately do a Seq Scan even with a good index.

Mistake: adding an index without looking at the plan

Adding an index and assuming it works is the most common tuning error. Always run EXPLAIN before and after.

Interview angle

  • Name cases when an index is not used. Function or cast on the column, leading wildcard, low selectivity, tiny table, OR over different columns, wrong column order, stale statistics.
  • How do you fix LIKE '%x%'? Trigram (GIN) index or full-text search.
  • You created an index; the query is still slow. What now? Read the EXPLAIN ANALYZE output, compare estimated and actual rows, and check each cause above.

EXPLAIN and EXPLAIN ANALYZE: reading query plans

EXPLAIN shows the plan the database chose and its cost estimate; EXPLAIN ANALYZE runs the query and adds real times and row counts, so you can see where time goes and whether an index is used.

What the planner does

SQL says what you want, not how to get it. The query planner (optimizer) considers several ways (scan with or without an index, join order, join method), estimates the cost of each using table statistics, and executes the cheapest. EXPLAIN prints the chosen plan as a tree of nodes. You read it from the innermost, most indented node outward; data flows up.

EXPLAIN SELECT ...;                      -- plan and estimates only; the query is NOT run
EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT ...;              -- runs the query; adds actual times and rows
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) SELECT ...;   -- also shows pages read from cache and disk

Mistake: EXPLAIN ANALYZE on INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE

EXPLAIN ANALYZE really executes the statement. To look at a write safely, wrap it: BEGIN; EXPLAIN ANALYZE DELETE ...; ROLLBACK;

The scripted experiment: 1 million rows

The best way to learn indexes is to feel the difference. Run this in psql on a test database. It uses generate_series to create a million rows without any outside file.

DROP TABLE IF EXISTS orders_big;
CREATE TABLE orders_big (
    id           BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id  INT         NOT NULL,
    status       TEXT        NOT NULL,
    amount_paise BIGINT      NOT NULL,
    placed_at    TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL
);

INSERT INTO orders_big (customer_id, status, amount_paise, placed_at)
SELECT (random() * 100000)::int + 1,                                -- 100,000 customers
       (ARRAY['PLACED','DELIVERED','CANCELLED'])[1 + floor(random() * 3)::int],
       (random() * 500000)::bigint + 1000,
       now() - random() * INTERVAL '365 days'
FROM generate_series(1, 1000000);

ANALYZE orders_big;      -- collect statistics so the planner knows the table size

SELECT count(*) FROM orders_big;
  count
---------
 1000000
(1 row)
\timing on
SELECT * FROM orders_big WHERE customer_id = 4242;

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT * FROM orders_big WHERE customer_id = 4242;
Gather  (cost=1000.00..14282.43 rows=10 width=34) (actual time=2.9..41.7 rows=11 loops=1)
  Workers Planned: 2
  Workers Launched: 2
  Buffers: shared hit=8334
  ->  Parallel Seq Scan on orders_big  (cost=0.00..13281.43 rows=4 width=34)
                                       (actual time=6.1..36.4 rows=4 loops=3)
        Filter: (customer_id = 4242)
        Rows Removed by Filter: 333330
        Buffers: shared hit=8334
Planning Time: 0.12 ms
Execution Time: 41.9 ms

The database read the whole table (8,334 pages) to return 11 rows. It ran the scan with parallel workers and still needed tens of milliseconds. On a slower disk, or with a cold cache, this is seconds.

CREATE INDEX idx_orders_big_customer_id ON orders_big (customer_id);
ANALYZE orders_big;

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT * FROM orders_big WHERE customer_id = 4242;
Bitmap Heap Scan on orders_big  (cost=4.46..42.07 rows=10 width=34)
                                (actual time=0.04..0.07 rows=11 loops=1)
  Recheck Cond: (customer_id = 4242)
  Heap Blocks: exact=11
  Buffers: shared hit=14
  ->  Bitmap Index Scan on idx_orders_big_customer_id
        (cost=0.00..4.46 rows=10 width=0) (actual time=0.03..0.03 rows=11 loops=1)
        Index Cond: (customer_id = 4242)
        Buffers: shared hit=3
Planning Time: 0.15 ms
Execution Time: 0.10 ms

From 8,334 pages to 14 pages, and from about 40 ms to 0.1 ms: hundreds of times less work. Plain Index Scan vs Bitmap Scan is a planner choice, explained below. The rest of the experiment shows the limits of an index:

-- Low selectivity: one third of all rows
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM orders_big WHERE status = 'DELIVERED';
--  Seq Scan on orders_big  (cost=0.00..21370.00 rows=333500 width=34)
--    Filter: (status = 'DELIVERED'::text)

-- Function on the column hides the index
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM orders_big WHERE customer_id + 0 = 4242;
--  Seq Scan on orders_big ...

-- Composite index for: one customer's delivered orders, newest first
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_big_cust_status_placed
    ON orders_big (customer_id, status, placed_at);
EXPLAIN ANALYZE
SELECT id, amount_paise FROM orders_big
WHERE customer_id = 4242 AND status = 'DELIVERED'
ORDER BY placed_at DESC;
--  Index Scan Backward using idx_orders_big_cust_status_placed ... (actual time=0.03..0.04)
--  (no Sort node: the index already returns rows in order)

Finally, compare the write cost: time INSERT INTO orders_big (...) SELECT ... FROM generate_series(1, 100000) before and after adding two or three extra indexes. It will be visibly slower with more indexes.

Reading the plan: the numbers

cost=0.42..48.90
Two numbers: the estimated cost to return the first row, then to return all rows. The unit is arbitrary (relative page-read costs). Only compare costs within the same database.
rows=10
The planner's estimate of rows this node returns.
width=34
Estimated average row size in bytes.
actual time=0.03..0.05
Real milliseconds to the first row and to the last row (only with ANALYZE). It is per loop.
rows=11 (in the actual part)
The rows really returned. Compare with the estimate: a big gap (10 vs 100,000) means the statistics are stale or the planner misjudges the data.
loops=N
How many times the node ran. For a node on the inner side of a nested loop the total time is actual time × loops.
Buffers: shared hit=14 read=3
hit pages came from memory, read pages from disk (or the OS cache). Lower is better.
Rows Removed by Filter
Rows read and then thrown away. A large number means the database read a lot to keep a little: look for a missing index.

The common nodes

NodeMeaningGood or bad?
Seq ScanReads the whole tableFine for small tables or big fractions; bad if it discards most rows
Index ScanWalks the index, fetches each row from the table Good for few rows
Index Only ScanAnswers from the index alone (covering index) Best
Bitmap Index Scan + Bitmap Heap ScanCollects matching row locations into a bitmap, then reads table pages in physical orderGood for medium numbers of rows or combining indexes
Nested LoopFor each row of the outer side, look up the inner side Good when the outer side is small and the inner side is indexed
Hash JoinBuilds a hash table of one side, probes it with the other Good for large unsorted inputs; needs memory
Merge JoinMerges two sorted inputsGood when both are already sorted (indexes)
SortSorts rowsWatch for Sort Method: external merge Disk (spilled to disk)
Aggregate / HashAggregateGROUP BY, COUNT, etc. Normal
LimitStops after N rowsMakes an ordered index scan very cheap
 
EXPLAIN ANALYZE
SELECT c.name, o.amount_paise
FROM customers c
JOIN orders_big o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE c.id = 4242;
Nested Loop  (cost=4.88..46.60 rows=10 width=22) (actual time=0.06..0.10 rows=11 loops=1)
  ->  Index Scan using customers_pkey on customers c
        (cost=0.28..8.29 rows=1 width=18) (actual time=0.01..0.01 rows=1 loops=1)
        Index Cond: (id = 4242)
  ->  Bitmap Heap Scan on orders_big o
        (cost=4.60..38.12 rows=10 width=12) (actual time=0.04..0.07 rows=11 loops=1)
        Recheck Cond: (customer_id = 4242)
        ->  Bitmap Index Scan on idx_orders_big_customer_id ...
Execution Time: 0.14 ms

Statistics and ANALYZE

The planner's choices are only as good as its statistics: row counts, distinct values and the most common values per column. ANALYZE table_name refreshes them; the autovacuum process runs it automatically after enough changes. Run it by hand after a bulk load. If estimated and actual rows differ by 10× or more, run ANALYZE first. If they still differ, look for correlated columns or skewed data.

MySQL EXPLAIN in brief

MySQL prints a table of rows instead of a tree. EXPLAIN ANALYZE exists too (MySQL 8.0.18+) and prints a tree similar to PostgreSQL's. The columns of the classic table:

ColumnWhat to look at
typeAccess method, best to worst: const, eq_ref, ref, range, index, ALL (full scan, bad)
possible_keys / keyIndexes that could be used / the one chosen (NULL means none)
rowsEstimated rows examined
ExtraUsing index (covering), Using filesort and Using temporary (extra work to avoid)

A tuning routine

  1. Find the slow query (application logs, pg_stat_statements, slow query log).
  2. Run EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) on production-like data.
  3. Find the node with the biggest actual time × loops, and big Rows Removed by Filter.
  4. Compare estimated and actual rows; run ANALYZE if they are far apart.
  5. Change one thing: add or reorder an index, rewrite the condition, select fewer columns.
  6. Run EXPLAIN ANALYZE again and compare. Keep the change only if it helps.

Rule: judge by actual time and buffers, not by cost alone

Cost is an estimate in made-up units. The truth is actual time and Buffers from EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS), run twice (the first run may be cold).

Edge cases

  • With loops=1000, a node showing actual time=0.05 costs 50 ms in total.
  • On a small test table the planner picks a Seq Scan even with a perfect index. Test with realistic volumes.
  • EXPLAIN on a parameterized query inside a prepared statement can differ from the literal version: PostgreSQL may use a generic plan after several executions.

Interview angle

  • EXPLAIN vs EXPLAIN ANALYZE? The first shows the estimated plan; the second runs the query and shows real times and row counts.
  • What do you look for? Seq Scan on big tables, big gaps between estimated and actual rows, rows removed by filter, sorts spilling to disk, nested loops with high loop counts.
  • Nested loop vs hash join vs merge join? Per-row indexed lookups (small outer); hash table of one side (large unsorted); merge of two sorted sides.
  • A query got slow after a bulk import. Why? Stale statistics; run ANALYZE.

Transactions and ACID

A transaction groups several statements into one all-or-nothing unit; ACID (Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability) names the four guarantees the database gives, shown best by a bank transfer.

Why transactions exist

Moving ₹500 from Asha's account to Bilal's takes two updates: subtract from one, add to the other. If the server crashes between them, ₹500 vanishes. If two transfers touch the same account at once, one update can overwrite the other. A transaction wraps the statements so they succeed together or not at all, and are protected from other users while they run.

CREATE TABLE accounts (
    id            BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    owner         TEXT   NOT NULL,
    balance_paise BIGINT NOT NULL CHECK (balance_paise >= 0)
);
INSERT INTO accounts VALUES (1, 'Asha', 100000), (2, 'Bilal', 50000);

BEGIN;                                                           -- start the transaction
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise = balance_paise - 50000 WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise = balance_paise + 50000 WHERE id = 2;
COMMIT;                                                          -- make both changes permanent

SELECT * FROM accounts ORDER BY id;
 id | owner | balance_paise
----+-------+---------------
  1 | Asha  |         50000
  2 | Bilal |        100000

BEGIN, COMMIT, ROLLBACK, SAVEPOINT

CommandEffect
BEGIN (or START TRANSACTION)Opens a transaction
COMMITMakes all changes permanent and visible to others
ROLLBACKUndoes all changes since BEGIN
SAVEPOINT nameMarks a point inside the transaction
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT nameUndoes only the work after that point
RELEASE SAVEPOINT nameForgets the savepoint, keeps the work
 
BEGIN;
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise = balance_paise - 80000 WHERE id = 1;   -- Asha has 50000 only
-- ERROR:  new row for relation "accounts" violates check constraint "accounts_balance_paise_check"
ROLLBACK;     -- PostgreSQL: after an error the transaction is "aborted"; only ROLLBACK works

SELECT balance_paise FROM accounts WHERE id = 1;     -- still 50000
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO orders (user_id, restaurant_id, delivery_address, total_paise)
VALUES (1, 1, 'MG Road', 60000);
SAVEPOINT before_coupon;
UPDATE coupons SET used_count = used_count + 1 WHERE code = 'BAD';   -- fails or is not wanted
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT before_coupon;      -- order insert is kept
COMMIT;

Autocommit

Outside BEGIN, every single statement is its own transaction that commits at once: autocommit. That is the default in psql and in the PostgreSQL JDBC driver's server behaviour. JDBC starts with connection.setAutoCommit(true); to run several statements together you call setAutoCommit(false), then commit() or rollback(). Spring does this for you: a method annotated with @Transactional gets one connection, turns off autocommit, commits on normal return and rolls back on a runtime exception (see Spring Boot · Module 3). In MySQL the same: autocommit is on by default; DDL statements such as CREATE TABLE cause an implicit commit, but PostgreSQL can roll DDL back.

ACID, letter by letter

LetterPromiseBank-transfer exampleHow the database does it
AtomicityAll statements apply, or noneDebit and credit both happen, or neither Undo information / old row versions; rollback discards changes
ConsistencyData moves from one valid state to another; rules always hold Balance never negative; foreign keys valid; total money unchanged Constraints (CHECK, FOREIGN KEY, UNIQUE) abort violating transactions; the application defines the business rules
IsolationConcurrent transactions do not see each other's half-done work A second transfer cannot read Asha's balance between the debit and the credit MVCC (versions) and locks; see isolation levels
DurabilityAfter COMMIT, data survives a crash After "transfer successful" the money stays moved even if the server loses power Write-ahead log (WAL, redo log), flushed to disk at commit

Durability in more detail: the database first appends a description of the change to a write-ahead log and forces it to disk (fsync) before telling you "committed". Data pages in memory are written to the table files later, in the background. After a crash, the database replays the log to restore every committed change and discards the uncommitted ones. Appending to a log is fast sequential I/O, which is why commits are cheap. (PostgreSQL's setting synchronous_commit = off trades a tiny window of lost commits for speed; do not use it for payments.) InnoDB calls its log the redo log and also keeps an undo log to roll back and to serve old versions.

Consistency has two meanings. In ACID it means "no rule is broken at commit". This is different from "consistency" in the CAP theorem (all nodes agree) in Module 6.

What a transaction does not do

  • It does not make a long operation safe if it contains a call to a payment gateway or an email: those side effects cannot be rolled back. Keep transactions short and do external calls outside them, or use patterns such as an outbox table.
  • It does not stop all concurrency bugs by itself: the isolation level decides what you can see (next article), and read-then-write logic needs locks (see Locking).

Rule: one transaction per business operation, kept short

Group the statements that must succeed together (transfer, place an order and its lines). Commit as soon as possible. A transaction left open holds locks and blocks cleanup (VACUUM); an "idle in transaction" connection is a classic production problem.

Mistake: catching the exception and carrying on in the same transaction

In PostgreSQL, after any error the whole transaction is aborted: every later statement fails with "current transaction is aborted, commands ignored until end of transaction block". Roll back, or use a savepoint around the risky statement. In Spring, swallowing a RuntimeException inside a @Transactional method means the transaction still commits whatever was done before.

Edge cases

  • MySQL's MyISAM engine has no transactions; use InnoDB (the default since MySQL 5.5).
  • COMMIT can itself fail (deferred constraints, serialization failure at the Serializable level). Handle the error and retry.
  • Sequences and identity values are not rolled back: a rolled-back insert leaves a gap in ids.

Interview angle

  • Explain ACID with an example. Bank transfer: atomic (both updates or none), consistent (balance never negative, total unchanged), isolated (no one sees the money in flight), durable (survives a crash thanks to the WAL).
  • How is durability implemented? Write-ahead log flushed at commit; replayed after a crash.
  • Autocommit? Each statement is its own transaction unless you start one explicitly.
  • ACID consistency vs CAP consistency? Rules hold after commit, versus all nodes see the same data.

Isolation levels and concurrency problems

The isolation level decides how much one transaction sees of others running at the same time: higher levels prevent more anomalies (dirty, non-repeatable and phantom reads, lost update, write skew) at the price of more blocking or more retries; PostgreSQL defaults to Read Committed and MySQL InnoDB to Repeatable Read.

The problem: transactions run at the same time

Real databases serve hundreds of transactions at once. If each one saw every change of every other one instantly, results would be unpredictable. If each one waited for the others to finish (strict serial order), the database would be safe but slow. Isolation levels are the dial between the two. The anomalies below are what can go wrong at lower settings.

MVCC in simple words

MVCC (multi-version concurrency control) is how PostgreSQL and InnoDB achieve isolation without making readers wait for writers. An UPDATE does not overwrite a row; it writes a new version and keeps the old one. Each transaction (or each statement) works with a snapshot: the set of versions that were committed when the snapshot was taken. Readers read old versions while a writer prepares new ones, so "readers never block writers and writers never block readers". Writers still block each other on the same row. Old versions are cleaned later (VACUUM in PostgreSQL, purge in InnoDB).

The anomalies

Dirty read
Reading data another transaction has changed but not yet committed. If it rolls back, you read data that never existed.
Non-repeatable read
Reading the same row twice in one transaction and getting different values, because another transaction committed a change in between.
Phantom read
Running the same query twice and getting different rows (new or missing), because another transaction committed an insert or delete that matches your condition.
Lost update
Two transactions read a value, both compute a new one, and the second write overwrites the first, so one update is lost.
Write skew
Two transactions read overlapping data, each makes a decision, and each writes different rows; together they break a rule that each alone respected.

Levels versus anomalies

The SQL standard defines four levels. The table shows what can happen at each, in PostgreSQL's actual behaviour (the standard is weaker in places, and MySQL differs slightly).

LevelDirty readNon-repeatable readPhantom readLost update / write skew
Read UncommittedPossible in the standard; PostgreSQL treats it as Read Committed (never)PossiblePossiblePossible
Read Committed (PostgreSQL default)NoPossiblePossible Both possible
Repeatable Read (MySQL InnoDB default)NoNo Standard: possible. PostgreSQL: no (snapshot). InnoDB: no for plain reads Lost update: PostgreSQL aborts with an error; write skew still possible
SerializableNoNoNoPrevented. Transactions behave as if run one by one; some are aborted and must retry (PostgreSQL)

Setting the level

-- PostgreSQL: for one transaction
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
-- or, as the first statement inside it:
SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;

SHOW transaction_isolation;               -- PostgreSQL: current level
SELECT @@transaction_isolation;           -- MySQL

-- MySQL, for the next transaction only
SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED;
@Transactional(isolation = Isolation.REPEATABLE_READ)
public void closeDayReport() { /* ... */ }

Demo 1: dirty read (and why you never see it in PostgreSQL)

Starting state: Asha's balance is 50000.

Session A                                 Session B
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
BEGIN;
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise = 0
  WHERE id = 1;                           -- not committed
                                          BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL READ UNCOMMITTED;
                                          SELECT balance_paise FROM accounts
                                            WHERE id = 1;     --> 0  (dirty!)
ROLLBACK;                                 -- the 0 never existed
                                          -- PostgreSQL would show 50000 here

Demo 2: non-repeatable read at Read Committed

In Read Committed each statement gets a fresh snapshot, so it sees everything committed before it started.

Session A                                 Session B
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
BEGIN;  -- READ COMMITTED
SELECT balance_paise FROM accounts
  WHERE id = 1;                --> 50000
                                          BEGIN;
                                          UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise = 30000
                                            WHERE id = 1;
                                          COMMIT;
SELECT balance_paise FROM accounts
  WHERE id = 1;                --> 30000   (same query, different answer)
COMMIT;

At Repeatable Read the second select in session A would still return 50000, because the snapshot is taken once, at the first statement of the transaction.

Demo 3: phantom read

Session A                                 Session B
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
BEGIN;  -- READ COMMITTED
SELECT count(*) FROM orders
  WHERE restaurant_id = 1
    AND status = 'PLACED';     --> 3
                                          INSERT INTO orders (...)  -- restaurant 1, PLACED
                                          COMMIT;
SELECT count(*) FROM orders
  WHERE restaurant_id = 1
    AND status = 'PLACED';     --> 4   (a phantom row)
COMMIT;

Non-repeatable reads are about changes to a row you already saw; phantoms are about new or removed rows that match your condition.

Demo 4: lost update

Two customers buy the last two units of an item. The application reads the stock, subtracts in Java and writes the result back:

Session A                                 Session B
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
BEGIN;                                    BEGIN;
SELECT stock FROM items WHERE id = 7;     SELECT stock FROM items WHERE id = 7;
  --> 10                                     --> 10
-- app computes 10 - 2 = 8                -- app computes 10 - 3 = 7
UPDATE items SET stock = 8
  WHERE id = 7;
COMMIT;
                                          UPDATE items SET stock = 7
                                            WHERE id = 7;     -- overwrites A!
                                          COMMIT;
-- final stock = 7, but 5 units were sold: should be 5

Fixes, from simplest: (1) let the database do the arithmetic, which takes a row lock for the update: UPDATE items SET stock = stock - 2 WHERE id = 7 AND stock >= 2; and check the row count; (2) lock the row when you read it: SELECT ... FOR UPDATE (see Locking); (3) add a version column and detect the conflict (optimistic locking); (4) use Repeatable Read, where PostgreSQL makes the second writer fail with "could not serialize access due to concurrent update" and you retry.

Demo 5: write skew

Rule: at least one rider must stay on shift. Two riders go off shift at the same moment:

Session A (rider 1 leaves)                Session B (rider 2 leaves)
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;    BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
SELECT count(*) FROM riders
  WHERE on_shift;              --> 2       SELECT count(*) FROM riders
                                            WHERE on_shift;       --> 2
-- 2 >= 2, safe to leave                   -- 2 >= 2, safe to leave
UPDATE riders SET on_shift = false
  WHERE id = 1;
                                          UPDATE riders SET on_shift = false
                                            WHERE id = 2;   -- different row: no conflict
COMMIT;                                   COMMIT;
-- nobody is on shift: the rule is broken

Each transaction was correct alone. They wrote different rows, so row-level conflict detection never fired. Only Serializable (in PostgreSQL, serializable snapshot isolation) notices the dependency and aborts one transaction with an error. You can also lock the rows you read (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE on all on-shift riders) or enforce the rule with a constraint or a single row that all transactions must update.

Serialization failures and retry

At Repeatable Read and Serializable, PostgreSQL may abort a transaction rather than allow an anomaly. The error has SQLSTATE 40001 (serialization_failure); a detected deadlock gives 40P01. These are not bugs: the transaction did nothing wrong and should simply be run again.

for (int attempt = 1; attempt <= 3; attempt++) {
    try {
        transferService.transfer(fromId, toId, amountPaise);   // @Transactional inside
        break;
    } catch (CannotSerializeTransactionException | DeadlockLoserDataAccessException e) {
        if (attempt == 3) throw e;
        Thread.sleep(20L * attempt);                           // small back-off
    }
}

Spring translates these SQL states into CannotSerializeTransactionException and DeadlockLoserDataAccessException. Retry the whole transaction (outside the @Transactional method), never a single statement. Spring Retry's @Retryable does this declaratively.

MySQL InnoDB differences

  • Default is Repeatable Read: plain SELECTs read a snapshot fixed at the first read.
  • Locking reads and writes (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, UPDATE) read the latest committed data, not the snapshot, and use next-key locks (row plus gap locks) to block phantoms. A transaction can therefore see rows its own snapshot select did not show.
  • InnoDB Repeatable Read does not abort lost updates; the second writer waits for the lock and then writes. Atomic updates (SET stock = stock - 2) or FOR UPDATE remain the right tools.
  • Serializable turns plain selects into shared locking reads, so transactions block rather than abort.

Which level should you use?

LevelUse when
Read CommittedThe default for most OLTP code: each statement sees fresh data and transactions rarely abort. Combine with atomic updates or row locks for read-then-write logic.
Repeatable ReadReports and multi-statement reads that must see a consistent picture (monthly statement totals); be ready for serialization errors on writes.
SerializableRules spanning several rows that you cannot lock or constrain easily; accept retries.
Read UncommittedAlmost never.

Rule: a higher level is not a substitute for correct write logic

Read-modify-write in application code is a lost-update bug at Read Committed. Use an atomic UPDATE ... SET x = x - n, a lock, or a version check, and know what your level really guarantees.

Edge cases

  • The standard allows phantoms at Repeatable Read; PostgreSQL and InnoDB (plain reads) do not show them. Interview answers should name the standard first and mention the difference.
  • In PostgreSQL the snapshot of a Repeatable Read transaction starts at its first query, not at BEGIN.
  • Sequences and now() are not affected by isolation: now() is the transaction start time, so it stays fixed inside the transaction.

Interview angle

  • Name the isolation levels and the anomalies each prevents. Use the table: RU allows all, RC stops dirty reads, RR stops non-repeatable reads, Serializable stops phantoms and write skew.
  • Default levels? PostgreSQL Read Committed; MySQL InnoDB Repeatable Read.
  • Dirty vs non-repeatable vs phantom? Uncommitted data; a changed row; changed set of rows.
  • What is MVCC? Keeping several versions of a row, so readers use a snapshot and do not block writers.
  • You get "could not serialize access". What do you do? Retry the whole transaction.

Locking: row locks, FOR UPDATE, SKIP LOCKED and deadlocks

Databases lock rows (and sometimes tables) so concurrent writers do not collide; SELECT ... FOR UPDATE locks the rows you read, NOWAIT and SKIP LOCKED control what happens when they are busy, and two transactions waiting for each other cause a deadlock that the database breaks by aborting one.

Row locks and table locks

MVCC lets readers proceed, but two transactions that change the same row must take turns. The first UPDATE or DELETE takes an exclusive row lock; a second one on the same row waits until the first commits or rolls back. Plain SELECT takes no row lock.

LockTaken byBlocks
Exclusive row lockUPDATE, DELETE, SELECT ... FOR UPDATE Other writers and lockers of that row; not plain reads
Shared row lockSELECT ... FOR SHAREWriters of that row; other shared lockers are fine
Table lock (various modes)LOCK TABLE, most ALTER TABLE, CREATE INDEX (not concurrently), TRUNCATEDepends on the mode; the strongest blocks all reads and writes

Row locks are held until the end of the transaction. That is why long transactions are dangerous: the longer you hold locks, the longer others wait. Table-level locks from schema changes are the most disruptive (an ALTER TABLE waiting behind a long query makes every new query queue behind it). Set a lock_timeout for migrations.

SELECT ... FOR UPDATE: lock what you read

This is the pessimistic fix for read-then-write logic. Read the row and lock it in one statement; nobody else can change it until you commit.

BEGIN;
SELECT stock FROM items WHERE id = 7 FOR UPDATE;     -- locks the row; others wait here
-- application checks stock >= 2 and computes the new value
UPDATE items SET stock = stock - 2 WHERE id = 7;
COMMIT;                                              -- lock released
Session A                                 Session B
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
BEGIN;                                    BEGIN;
SELECT stock FROM items WHERE id = 7
  FOR UPDATE;            --> 10
                                          SELECT stock FROM items WHERE id = 7
                                            FOR UPDATE;      -- waits...
UPDATE items SET stock = 8 WHERE id = 7;
COMMIT;                                   --> 8   (B continues with the new value)
                                          UPDATE items SET stock = 5 WHERE id = 7;
                                          COMMIT;

If the check and update can be one statement, prefer it: UPDATE items SET stock = stock - 2 WHERE id = 7 AND stock >= 2 is atomic, needs no explicit lock, and returns 0 rows when stock is short.

FOR SHARE, NOWAIT, SKIP LOCKED

ClauseBehaviour
FOR UPDATEExclusive lock on the selected rows; waits if busy
FOR SHAREShared lock: the row cannot change while you hold it (for example the parent row while you insert children)
... NOWAITFail immediately with an error if any row is locked, instead of waiting
... SKIP LOCKEDSilently skip rows that are locked and return the rest

SKIP LOCKED is the building block of a database job queue: many workers each claim a different job without waiting for each other.

BEGIN;

SELECT id, payload
FROM jobs
WHERE status = 'PENDING'
ORDER BY id
LIMIT 1
FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED;          -- a row another worker holds is skipped, not waited for

-- ... process the job, then:
UPDATE jobs SET status = 'DONE' WHERE id = 101;
COMMIT;

If a worker crashes, its transaction rolls back and the job returns to PENDING by itself. FOR UPDATE NOWAIT suits "if this seat is being booked right now, tell the user to try another". Both clauses exist in PostgreSQL (SKIP LOCKED since 9.5) and MySQL 8.0+.

Deadlocks

A deadlock happens when two transactions each hold a lock the other needs. Neither can continue, ever.

Session A (1 -> 2)                         Session B (2 -> 1)
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
BEGIN;                                    BEGIN;
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise =
  balance_paise - 100 WHERE id = 1;       -- A holds row 1
                                          UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise =
                                            balance_paise - 50 WHERE id = 2;  -- B holds row 2
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise =
  balance_paise + 100 WHERE id = 2;       -- A waits for B
                                          UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise =
                                            balance_paise + 50 WHERE id = 1;  -- B waits for A
-- ERROR:  deadlock detected               (one session is chosen as the victim and rolled back)
-- DETAIL:  Process 1201 waits for ShareLock on transaction 5532; blocked by process 1244.
--          Process 1244 waits for ShareLock on transaction 5531; blocked by process 1201.
-- HINT:  See server log for query details.

The database finds the cycle (PostgreSQL checks after deadlock_timeout, 1 second by default; InnoDB detects it at once), aborts one transaction with an error (SQLSTATE 40P01 in PostgreSQL, error 1213 in MySQL), and the other proceeds. Your code must retry the aborted transaction.

How to prevent deadlocks:

  • Lock in a consistent order. Always update the lower account id first, whatever the transfer direction. This removes the cycle.
  • Keep transactions short and touch as few rows as possible.
  • Lock all needed rows up front: SELECT ... WHERE id IN (1, 2) ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE.
  • Index foreign keys: a missing index can make a delete lock many rows.
  • Retry on deadlock errors.
BEGIN;
SELECT id FROM accounts WHERE id IN (1, 2) ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE;   -- always ascending
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise = balance_paise - 100 WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE accounts SET balance_paise = balance_paise + 100 WHERE id = 2;
COMMIT;

Lock waits and timeouts

A transaction that waits for a lock waits forever by default in PostgreSQL. Set limits so a stuck session does not freeze the application:

SET lock_timeout = '3s';                       -- PostgreSQL: error if a lock wait exceeds 3 s
SET statement_timeout = '30s';
SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s';

-- Who is blocking whom? (PostgreSQL)
SELECT pid, pg_blocking_pids(pid) AS blocked_by, state, query
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(pid)) > 0;

MySQL has innodb_lock_wait_timeout (50 seconds by default), after which the waiting statement fails with error 1205 "Lock wait timeout exceeded".

Rule: take locks in a fixed order and release them fast

Commit or roll back quickly. Never keep a transaction open while waiting for a user, an HTTP call or a message queue.

Mistake: FOR UPDATE on a wide range

SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'PLACED' FOR UPDATE locks every placed order for as long as the transaction lives. Lock the few rows you need, with LIMIT or by id.

Edge cases

  • In PostgreSQL, inserting a row with a foreign key takes a shared lock on the referenced parent row, so many child inserts and a parent update can conflict.
  • In MySQL InnoDB, a locking read on a range also locks the gaps between index records, which can block inserts you did not expect. Use an exact unique key lookup when possible.
  • A locking read without a useful index may lock (or scan and lock) more rows than you expect; check the plan with EXPLAIN.

Interview angle

  • What does SELECT ... FOR UPDATE do? Locks the selected rows until commit so no one else can update them; the pessimistic fix for read-then-write.
  • What is SKIP LOCKED for? Work queues: workers take unlocked jobs without waiting.
  • What is a deadlock and how do you avoid it? A cycle of waits; the database aborts one victim. Consistent lock order, short transactions, retry.
  • How do you find what is blocking? pg_stat_activity and pg_blocking_pids (PostgreSQL), SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS (MySQL).

Optimistic vs pessimistic locking

Pessimistic locking locks the row before you change it and makes others wait; optimistic locking lets everyone proceed, stores a version number in the row, and rejects an update whose version has changed, so you retry or tell the user.

Two philosophies

  • Pessimistic: "Conflicts are likely, so prevent them." Lock the row first (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE). Others wait. Safe, simple to reason about, but holds locks and can deadlock.
  • Optimistic: "Conflicts are rare, so detect them." Read without locking, and at update time check that nobody changed the row since you read it. No locks are held while the user thinks.

Optimistic locking with a version column

Add an integer version column. Every update includes the version it read in the WHERE clause and increments it. If another transaction changed the row meanwhile, the version no longer matches, zero rows are updated, and you know there was a conflict.

ALTER TABLE items ADD COLUMN version INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0;

-- Read (no lock)
SELECT id, stock, version FROM items WHERE id = 7;      --> stock 10, version 3

-- Update only if still version 3
UPDATE items
SET stock = 8, version = version + 1
WHERE id = 7 AND version = 3;
-- UPDATE 1  -> success.   UPDATE 0 -> someone else changed it: reload and retry or report
User A                                    User B
------------------------------------      ------------------------------------
reads stock 10, version 3                 reads stock 10, version 3
UPDATE ... SET stock = 8, version = 4
  WHERE id = 7 AND version = 3;  --> 1 row
                                          UPDATE ... SET stock = 7, version = 4
                                            WHERE id = 7 AND version = 3;  --> 0 rows
                                          -- conflict detected: reload (stock 8) and retry

This catches the lost update without any lock held across the user's think time (a form open for two minutes), which is why it is the standard for web apps and REST APIs. A timestamp column works too, but an integer version has no clock-precision problems.

JPA: @Version

JPA does this for you. Add @Version to an entity; Hibernate adds the version check to every UPDATE and increments it.

@Entity
public class Item {
    @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;
    private int stock;

    @Version
    private int version;          // managed by JPA: never set it yourself
}
// SQL issued: update item set stock=?, version=? where id=? and version=?
// If 0 rows are updated: OptimisticLockException
// (Spring translates it to ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException)

In a REST API this maps well to HTTP: return the version (or an ETag) with the resource, and reject an update with 412 Precondition Failed or 409 Conflict when it is stale. See Spring Boot · Module 3 for entities and the repository layer.

Pessimistic locking in JPA

public interface ItemRepository extends JpaRepository<Item, Long> {

    @Lock(LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE)                 // SELECT ... FOR UPDATE
    @Query("select i from Item i where i.id = :id")
    Optional<Item> findByIdForUpdate(@Param("id") Long id);
}

@Transactional
public void reserve(Long itemId, int qty) {
    Item item = repo.findByIdForUpdate(itemId).orElseThrow();   // row locked until commit
