    if (item.getStock() < qty) throw new IllegalStateException("Out of stock");
    item.setStock(item.getStock() - qty);
}

Comparison

OptimisticPessimistic
MechanismVersion check at updateRow lock at read (FOR UPDATE)
Locks heldNoneUntil commit
On conflictUpdate fails; retry or reportSecond transaction waits
Best whenConflicts are rare; long think time; read-heavyConflicts are common; short transaction; failure is costly
ThroughputHigh with few conflicts; wasted work and retries when hotPredictable, but waiting lowers throughput; deadlock risk
Schema changeNeeds a version columnNone
Works across HTTP requestsYesNo: locks live in one transaction only

Which one when?

ScenarioChoiceWhy
Edit profile, edit a product in an admin formOptimisticRare conflicts; the form is open a long time
Flash sale: thousands buy the same itemAtomic UPDATE ... stock = stock - n WHERE stock >= n, or pessimisticHot row: optimistic would fail and retry constantly
Seat booking (last seat on a train)Pessimistic with NOWAIT, or a unique constraint on (show, seat)Two people must never get the same seat
Wallet balance debitAtomic update with a CHECK, or pessimisticMoney must not be lost; contention on one account is real
Job queueFOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKEDWorkers take distinct rows

Often the best "lock" is a constraint: a UNIQUE (show_id, seat_no) makes the database reject the second booking with no application locking at all. Catch the unique violation and say "seat taken".

Convention: add @Version to entities that users edit

It costs one column and protects against silent lost updates. Handle ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException by returning HTTP 409 or retrying a small, safe operation.

Edge cases

  • Bulk JPQL/SQL updates bypass @Version unless you increment the version yourself.
  • Retrying an optimistic failure must reload the entity first; re-sending the same stale object fails again.
  • A pessimistic lock without a timeout can hang a request; set a lock timeout hint (jakarta.persistence.lock.timeout) or use NOWAIT.

Interview angle

  • Optimistic vs pessimistic? Detect conflicts with a version vs prevent them with a lock; choose by how often conflicts happen and how long the user holds data.
  • How does @Version work? Hibernate adds AND version = ? to the update, increments it, and throws OptimisticLockException if no row matched.
  • How do you prevent overselling the last item? Atomic conditional UPDATE, FOR UPDATE, or a version check with retry.

Connection pooling and HikariCP

Opening a database connection is slow and each one costs memory on the server, so applications keep a small pool of open connections and lend them out; Spring Boot uses HikariCP, and a small pool is usually faster than a large one.

Why connections are expensive

Opening a connection to PostgreSQL involves a TCP handshake, usually TLS, authentication, and the server starting a process (PostgreSQL) or thread (MySQL) with its own memory. That can take several milliseconds to tens of milliseconds, more than a typical indexed query. If every web request opened and closed a connection, the database would spend more time connecting than working. And the server can only hold so many: PostgreSQL's max_connections is 100 by default, and each connection uses memory.

How a pool works

  1. At start-up the pool opens a few connections.
  2. A request calls dataSource.getConnection(). If an idle connection exists, it is handed out at once.
  3. If none is idle and the pool is below its maximum, a new connection is opened.
  4. If the pool is at its maximum, the caller waits, up to connectionTimeout, then fails with an exception.
  5. When the code calls connection.close(), the pooled wrapper returns the connection to the pool for reuse; the real connection stays open.

The connection is only busy while a transaction or statement uses it. In Spring a @Transactional method holds one connection for its whole duration, so long transactions mean long borrow times.

HikariCP in Spring Boot

HikariCP is the default pool in Spring Boot (since 2.0): fast, small and well tested. If spring-boot-starter-data-jpa or -jdbc is on the classpath, you already have it. Configure it in application.yml under spring.datasource.hikari:

spring:
  datasource:
    url: jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/fooddb
    username: app
    password: ${DB_PASSWORD}
    hikari:
      pool-name: food-pool
      maximum-pool-size: 10        # max connections (default 10)
      minimum-idle: 10             # idle connections kept ready (default = maximum)
      connection-timeout: 30000    # ms to wait for a free connection (default 30 s)
      idle-timeout: 600000         # ms before an extra idle connection is closed (default 10 min)
      max-lifetime: 1800000        # ms before any connection is retired (default 30 min)
      leak-detection-threshold: 20000   # log a warning if borrowed longer than 20 s (default off)
PropertyMeaningSensible value
maximumPoolSizeMost connections the pool will hold, in use plus idle Start at 10; see sizing below
minimumIdleIdle connections to keep ready. HikariCP advises leaving it unset so the pool has a fixed sizeSame as maximum for steady traffic
connectionTimeoutHow long a caller waits for a connection before an exception 2–5 s in an API, so requests fail fast instead of piling up
idleTimeoutIdle time after which extra connections (above minimumIdle) are closed; only matters if minimumIdle is lower than maximumDefault 10 min
maxLifetimeA connection is retired after this age (replaced when idle) A few minutes less than any database or firewall timeout; default 30 min
leakDetectionThresholdLogs a stack trace if a connection stays out too long Enable in dev and staging to catch unclosed connections

maxLifetime exists because a firewall, a load balancer or the database may silently drop connections that live too long. HikariCP retires its own connections just before that, so your code never receives a dead one. Set it shorter than the database's or network's own limit.

Pool sizing: smaller than you think

It is tempting to think "more connections, more speed." The opposite is often true. A database has a limited number of CPU cores and disk operations; beyond that, extra concurrent queries just compete, causing context switching and lock contention. HikariCP's own guidance gives a starting formula for a pool: connections = (core_count × 2) + effective_spindle_count, and notes that a pool around 10 can serve thousands of front-end users. A good rule: start with 10, measure, and raise it only if requests wait for connections while the database is not yet busy.

  • Across instances: total connections = pool size × number of app instances. Ten instances with a pool of 20 need 200 connections: more than the default PostgreSQL max_connections of 100. Keep the sum well under the database limit, leaving room for admin tools, migrations and monitoring.
  • Many instances or serverless: put an external pooler such as PgBouncer in front of PostgreSQL.
  • Keep transactions short. A pool of 10 serves far more users if each borrows a connection for 5 ms than for 500 ms.
  • Do not hold a connection while calling a slow API. A @Transactional method that makes an HTTP call keeps its connection idle but locked.

Pool exhaustion: the symptoms

All connections are in use and new requests wait. After connectionTimeout they fail:

java.sql.SQLTransientConnectionException: food-pool - Connection is not available,
request timed out after 30000ms (total=10, active=10, idle=0, waiting=17)

What you see: requests suddenly slow and then fail with timeouts, CPU low on the app server, many threads parked in HikariPool.getConnection in a thread dump, and waiting greater than 0. Typical causes:

  • A slow query or lock wait that keeps connections busy (the pool is a victim, not the cause).
  • Long transactions, or external calls inside a transaction.
  • A connection leak: code that borrows a connection and never closes it (plain JDBC without try-with-resources).
  • A pool too small for the traffic, or too many instances for the database limit.
  • Open-session-in-view (spring.jpa.open-in-view, on by default in Spring Boot) holding the connection until the HTTP response is written. Many teams turn it off.
try (Connection con = dataSource.getConnection();
     PreparedStatement ps = con.prepareStatement("SELECT name FROM users WHERE id = ?")) {
    ps.setLong(1, 42L);
    try (ResultSet rs = ps.executeQuery()) {
        if (rs.next()) System.out.println(rs.getString("name"));
    }
}   // close() is called automatically: the connection goes back to the pool

Monitor the pool with Actuator and Micrometer metrics (hikaricp.connections.active, hikaricp.connections.pending, hikaricp.connections.timeout) and alert when pending is above zero for a while. See Spring Boot · Module 6.

Rule: total pool size across all instances must stay below the database's max_connections

Do the multiplication before deploying: instances × maximum-pool-size, plus scheduled jobs, plus admin connections.

Mistake: fixing timeouts by raising the pool size

If connections are exhausted because queries are slow or locked, a bigger pool adds more load to an already struggling database. Find the slow query first (EXPLAIN ANALYZE, slow query log).

Edge cases

  • A pool can hold a connection that the database already closed (restart, failover). HikariCP validates connections on borrow and retires old ones, so you normally only see a few failed requests, then recovery.
  • With Java 21 virtual threads you can have thousands of threads, but the pool is still the limit: a thousand virtual threads still share the same 10 connections.
  • Session state (SET ..., temporary tables, advisory locks) lives on the connection and leaks to the next borrower. Reset it or avoid it.

Interview angle

  • Why use a connection pool? Connections are expensive to open; a pool reuses them and caps load on the database.
  • What is the default pool in Spring Boot and its default size? HikariCP, 10 connections.
  • How big should the pool be? Smaller than you think; start around 10, measure, and keep instances × pool size under max_connections.
  • "Connection is not available, request timed out" - what do you check? Slow queries, long transactions, leaks, pool size versus load, and the thread dump.

The N+1 query problem

N+1 means running one query for N parent rows and then one more query for each parent to load its children (N+1 queries in total); detect it in the SQL log and fix it with a JOIN, an IN-list batch, or JPA fetch joins and entity graphs.

What it is, in SQL terms

You want each restaurant with its menu items. The slow way:

SELECT id, name FROM restaurants WHERE city = 'Pune';           -- 1 query, returns 50 rows

SELECT * FROM menu_items WHERE restaurant_id = 1;               -- then 1 query per restaurant
SELECT * FROM menu_items WHERE restaurant_id = 2;
SELECT * FROM menu_items WHERE restaurant_id = 3;
-- ... 50 times: 51 queries in total

Each query is fast, but 51 round trips to the database add up (network latency plus parsing plus scheduling, per query). With 1,000 restaurants it becomes 1,001 queries for one page. The cost grows with the data, which is why the bug stays hidden in development with 5 rows and appears in production.

How ORMs cause it

With JPA, relationships are loaded lazily by default: restaurant.getMenuItems() looks like a simple getter but runs a query the first time it is called. A loop hides this:

@Entity
public class Restaurant {
    @Id private Long id;
    private String name;
    @OneToMany(mappedBy = "restaurant")           // LAZY by default
    private List<MenuItem> menuItems = new ArrayList<>();
    // getters
}

// Service
List<Restaurant> list = restaurantRepository.findByCity("Pune");     // query 1
for (Restaurant r : list) {
    System.out.println(r.getName() + ": " + r.getMenuItems().size());  // query 2..N+1
}
select r.id, r.name, r.city from restaurants r where r.city=?
select m.id, m.name, m.restaurant_id from menu_items m where m.restaurant_id=?
select m.id, m.name, m.restaurant_id from menu_items m where m.restaurant_id=?
select m.id, m.name, m.restaurant_id from menu_items m where m.restaurant_id=?
... (one more for each restaurant)

It also happens with @ManyToOne set to EAGER (the JPA default for that mapping): loading 100 orders then loads each order's user separately, and when a serializer (Jackson) walks an entity graph and triggers lazy loads. The fix is never "make everything EAGER": that loads too much everywhere.

How to detect it

  • Read the SQL log for the request. Many near-identical queries differing only in an id mean N+1. Enable spring.jpa.show-sql=true or, better, logging.level.org.hibernate.SQL=DEBUG (and bind parameters with org.hibernate.orm.jdbc.bind=TRACE in Hibernate 6).
  • Count queries per request. Hibernate statistics (spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.generate_statistics=true) report the statement count; libraries like datasource-proxy can assert it in tests.
  • Database side: in PostgreSQL pg_stat_statements shows one statement called thousands of times; a slow endpoint with fast individual queries is the typical sign.
  • Tests: write an integration test that asserts "this endpoint runs at most 3 queries".

The fixes

1. A JOIN (one query)

Let the database join the two tables and return everything at once:

SELECT r.id, r.name, m.id AS item_id, m.name AS item_name, m.price_paise
FROM restaurants r
LEFT JOIN menu_items m ON m.restaurant_id = r.id
WHERE r.city = 'Pune'
ORDER BY r.id, m.id;

The result repeats each restaurant's columns on every item row; the app groups them. For a parent with many children and wide columns, this duplication costs bandwidth, which leads to the next option.

2. IN-list batching (two queries)
SELECT id, name FROM restaurants WHERE city = 'Pune';                    -- 50 rows
SELECT * FROM menu_items WHERE restaurant_id IN (1, 2, 3, /* ... */ 50);  -- all children at once

No duplicated parent columns, and the number of queries is constant (2) instead of N+1. Hibernate does this when you set a batch size.

3. JPA: fetch join, entity graph, batch size
public interface RestaurantRepository extends JpaRepository<Restaurant, Long> {

    // (a) JPQL fetch join: one query with a JOIN
    @Query("select distinct r from Restaurant r left join fetch r.menuItems where r.city = :city")
    List<Restaurant> findWithMenuByCity(@Param("city") String city);

    // (b) Entity graph: same idea without writing JPQL
    @EntityGraph(attributePaths = "menuItems")
    List<Restaurant> findByCity(String city);
}
spring:
  jpa:
    properties:
      hibernate:
        default_batch_fetch_size: 50     # lazy collections load 50 parents per IN query

With default_batch_fetch_size the loop above issues ... where restaurant_id in (?, ?, ...) once per 50 restaurants instead of once per restaurant. It is a cheap global safety net that makes N+1 into N/50 + 1. Per association, @BatchSize(size = 50) does the same.

4. Select only what you need (DTO projection)
public record RestaurantItemCount(Long id, String name, long items) {}

@Query("""
    select new com.example.food.RestaurantItemCount(r.id, r.name, count(m))
    from Restaurant r left join r.menuItems m
    where r.city = :city
    group by r.id, r.name
    """)
List<RestaurantItemCount> countItemsByCity(@Param("city") String city);

If a screen needs only counts or a few columns, a projection with GROUP BY is one cheap query and avoids loading entities at all. JPA details are in Spring Boot · Module 3.

Choosing a fix

SituationFix
Need parents with their children on one screenFetch join or @EntityGraph
Several collections on one entityBatch size or separate queries (fetching two lists in one query gives MultipleBagFetchException or a huge row product)
Pagination of parents with childrenPage the parent ids first, then load children with an IN query (a fetch join with Pageable pages in memory and Hibernate warns)
Only counts or a few columnsDTO projection with GROUP BY
Many places, no time to auditdefault_batch_fetch_size as a safety net

Rule: know how many queries a request runs

A page that depends on a list should run a small, fixed number of queries, not a number that grows with the data. Check the SQL log for every new list endpoint.

Mistake: switching everything to EAGER

Wrong: eager everywhere

@OneToMany(mappedBy = "restaurant", fetch = FetchType.EAGER)
private List<MenuItem> menuItems;
// every query for a Restaurant now loads items, even when not needed;
// N+1 can still happen through derived queries

Right: lazy by default, fetch on demand

@OneToMany(mappedBy = "restaurant")           // lazy
private List<MenuItem> menuItems;
// use @EntityGraph or JOIN FETCH in the specific query that needs them

Edge cases

  • LazyInitializationException is the cousin: touching a lazy collection after the transaction or session has closed. Fix by fetching in the query, not by opening the session longer.
  • A fetch join with distinct may still return duplicate parents with older Hibernate versions unless distinct is set; Hibernate 6 de-duplicates entity results automatically.
  • N+1 is not only an ORM problem: a hand-written JDBC loop that queries inside a for loop has the same issue.

Interview angle

  • What is the N+1 problem? One query for N parents plus one per parent for children: N+1 round trips.
  • How do you find it? SQL logging, Hibernate statistics, query-count assertions in tests, pg_stat_statements.
  • How do you fix it? JOIN FETCH or @EntityGraph, batch fetching with an IN list, or a DTO projection; not blanket EAGER.
  • Why does it not show in development? Small data; the number of queries grows with the data size.

Interview questions — How databases work inside

What is an index and how does a B-tree index speed up a query?

An index is a separate sorted structure (usually a B+ tree) of column values with pointers to rows. The tree is wide and shallow, so finding a key reads only 3 or 4 pages: O(log n) instead of reading the whole table. Leaf pages are linked in order, so range scans and sorted output are cheap too.

When does an index hurt?

Every insert, update of an indexed column and delete must also update each index, so writes get slower and storage and cache use grow. On low-selectivity columns or small tables, the planner ignores the index anyway. Keep indexes for queries you have measured, and drop unused ones.

Explain the leftmost-prefix rule.

An index on (a, b, c) is sorted by a, then b, then c. It can serve queries filtering on a; a and b; or a, b and c. It cannot efficiently serve b or c alone, because the entries for one b value are scattered through the index. Put equality columns first and the range or sort column last.

Name reasons why an index may not be used.

A function or arithmetic on the column (lower(email)), a leading wildcard (LIKE '%x'), an implicit cast, low selectivity, a tiny table, OR across columns, wrong column order, and stale statistics. Check with EXPLAIN; fix with an expression index, a trigram index, correct types, a reordered index, or ANALYZE.

What is a covering index?

An index that contains every column the query needs, so the database answers from the index alone without reading the table (an Index Only Scan). In PostgreSQL you add extra columns with INCLUDE.

What is the difference between EXPLAIN and EXPLAIN ANALYZE?

EXPLAIN shows the planned steps and estimated costs without running the query. EXPLAIN ANALYZE runs it and adds actual time, actual rows and loops per node; with BUFFERS it adds page counts. Compare estimated and actual rows to spot stale statistics, and look for Seq Scans that discard many rows.

Explain ACID with an example.

Bank transfer of ₹500: Atomicity - the debit and credit both happen or neither. Consistency - the rules (balance not negative, total unchanged) hold after the transaction. Isolation - other transactions do not see the half-finished state. Durability - once committed, the change survives a crash because the write-ahead log was flushed to disk.

What are dirty, non-repeatable and phantom reads?

Dirty read: reading another transaction's uncommitted change. Non-repeatable read: the same row gives a different value on the second read because another transaction committed a change. Phantom read: the same query returns a different set of rows because another transaction committed an insert or delete.

List the isolation levels and the defaults of PostgreSQL and MySQL.

Read Uncommitted, Read Committed, Repeatable Read, Serializable. PostgreSQL defaults to Read Committed (and treats Read Uncommitted as Read Committed); MySQL InnoDB defaults to Repeatable Read. Higher levels prevent more anomalies but cause more blocking or more serialization failures that must be retried.

What is MVCC?

Multi-version concurrency control: updates create new row versions and keep the old ones, so each transaction reads from a snapshot. Readers do not block writers and writers do not block readers; old versions are removed later (VACUUM in PostgreSQL).

What is a deadlock and how do you prevent it?

Two transactions each hold a lock the other needs. The database detects the cycle and aborts one with an error. Prevent it by taking locks in a consistent order (lowest id first), keeping transactions short, locking all needed rows up front, and retrying the aborted transaction.

Optimistic vs pessimistic locking: which and when?

Pessimistic locks the row first (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE) and makes others wait; use it when conflicts are likely and transactions are short (seat booking, wallet). Optimistic reads freely and checks a version column at update time (@Version in JPA), failing on conflict; use it when conflicts are rare or the user holds the data for a while (editing forms).

Two users buy the last item at the same time. How do you stop overselling?

Make the check and update atomic: UPDATE items SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = ? AND stock >= 1 and check that one row was updated. Alternatives: SELECT ... FOR UPDATE first, or a version check with retry. Don't read the stock, subtract in Java and write it back at Read Committed: that is a lost update.

Why use a connection pool, and how do you size it?

Opening connections is slow and the server can hold only a limited number. A pool (HikariCP, default 10) reuses open connections. Keep it small (around 10 to start), because more concurrent queries than the database can run just compete; make sure instances × pool size stays below max_connections. Pool exhaustion shows up as "Connection is not available, request timed out".

What is the N+1 problem and how do you fix it?

One query loads N parent rows and then N more queries load each parent's children, usually from lazy loading inside a loop. Detect it in the SQL log. Fix it with a JOIN FETCH or @EntityGraph, batch loading (default_batch_fetch_size), or a DTO projection, not by making everything EAGER.

Cheat sheet — How databases work inside

  • Page 8 KB (PostgreSQL) / 16 KB (InnoDB) unit of I/O; PostgreSQL heap vs InnoDB clustered table
  • Seq Scan reads all pages, O(n); fine for tiny tables or large fractions
  • B-tree balanced, wide, shallow; lookup O(log n); equality, range, ORDER BY, prefix LIKE
  • Other index types hash (equality), GIN (JSONB, arrays, full-text), GiST, BRIN (huge ordered tables)
  • Index cost slower writes, more disk and cache; drop unused ones (pg_stat_user_indexes)
  • FK indexes PostgreSQL does not create them; InnoDB does
  • Variants unique, partial (WHERE), expression (lower(col)), covering (INCLUDE)
  • Composite (a,b,c) serves a; a,b; a,b,c. Equality first, range last, match ORDER BY
  • Index ignored function or cast on column, LIKE '%x', low selectivity, tiny table, OR, wrong order, stale stats
  • EXPLAIN estimate only; EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) runs it, shows actual time, rows, loops, pages
  • Plan nodes Seq Scan, Index (Only) Scan, Bitmap Scan, Nested Loop, Hash Join, Merge Join, Sort
  • Estimated vs actual rows far apart means run ANALYZE
  • Transaction BEGIN / COMMIT / ROLLBACK / SAVEPOINT; autocommit = one statement per transaction
  • ACID atomic (all or none), consistent (rules hold), isolated (no half-done reads), durable (WAL flushed at commit)
  • Anomalies dirty read, non-repeatable read, phantom read, lost update, write skew
  • Levels RU, RC (PostgreSQL default), RR (MySQL default), Serializable; RR/Serializable may need retry on SQLSTATE 40001
  • MVCC row versions + snapshots; readers do not block writers
  • Row locks FOR UPDATE, FOR SHARE, NOWAIT, SKIP LOCKED (job queues)
  • Deadlock cycle of waits; lock in consistent order, keep short, retry (40P01)
  • Atomic update SET stock = stock - n WHERE stock >= n beats read-modify-write
  • Optimistic version column / @Version; pessimistic FOR UPDATE / PESSIMISTIC_WRITE
  • HikariCP maximum-pool-size 10, connection-timeout 30 s, max-lifetime 30 min; small pools win
  • Pool math instances × pool size < max_connections (100 default in PostgreSQL)
  • N+1 1 query for parents + N for children; fix with fetch join, @EntityGraph, batch size, projection

Practice — How databases work inside

  1. Roadmap exercise: the index experiment. Build the 1-million-row orders_big table with generate_series from the EXPLAIN article. Time WHERE customer_id = 4242, save the EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) output, add the index, run ANALYZE, and compare execution time and buffers. Done when you can explain every line of both plans.
  2. Break the index. On the same table try a function on the column, a leading wildcard, and a low-selectivity filter (status). For each, show the plan and apply the fix (expression index, trigram index, partial index) and show the new plan.
  3. Composite index lab. Create an index on (customer_id, status, placed_at). Write six queries that filter on different combinations (a; a,b; b; a,c; ...) and record which ones use the index and how. Reorder the columns and repeat.
  4. Covering index. Make a query return customer_id, placed_at, amount_paise for one customer as an Index Only Scan with INCLUDE. Run VACUUM and check that heap fetches go to zero.
  5. Roadmap exercise: bank transfer. Create accounts, then simulate a transfer with BEGIN, two updates and COMMIT. Repeat with a ROLLBACK and with a failing CHECK. Add a SAVEPOINT to retry one step. Done when balances are always correct.
  6. Roadmap exercise: isolation in action. Open two psql sessions. Reproduce a non-repeatable read at Read Committed, then show it disappears at Repeatable Read. Reproduce the lost update on items.stock, then fix it three ways (atomic update, FOR UPDATE, version column). Try the write-skew rider example at Repeatable Read and at Serializable.
  7. Deadlock. Cause the opposite-order transfer deadlock between two sessions, read the error, then fix it with ordered locking.
  8. Job queue. Create a jobs table with 20 rows. In two sessions run the FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED claim query and show each session gets a different job.
  9. Optimistic locking in Spring. Add @Version to an entity, update the same row from two threads or two requests, and show ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException. Return HTTP 409.
  10. N+1 hunt. In a Spring Boot app with Restaurant and MenuItem, load 50 restaurants and print item counts. Count the queries in the log, then fix it with @EntityGraph and again with default_batch_fetch_size. Write a test that asserts the query count. Also set maximum-pool-size to 2 and run a load of 20 parallel requests to watch the pool time out.
6

Module 6·Month 9Concepts

Beyond relational

7 topics · about 54 min of reading

Relational databases are the right default, but they are not the only tool. Some jobs, such as caching a product page for 60 seconds, counting page views at 50,000 per second, storing flexible documents, or searching text, fit other kinds of stores better. This module is a tour of the "beyond relational" world: what the main NoSQL families are, how Redis and MongoDB work, what the CAP theorem really says, how databases copy and split data across servers, and how you change a schema safely over time with Flyway or Liquibase.

You do not need to master every tool. The goal is to choose well, speak about the trade-offs in an interview, and use Redis and MongoDB at a working level. Redis caching connects to Spring's cache abstraction in Spring Boot · Module 6; replication and sharding return as system design topics in System Design · Module 5; and migrations are part of every Spring Boot project with a database (Spring Boot · Module 3). It builds on the relational basics of Modules 1–3, the design rules of Module 4 and the transaction ideas of Module 5.

  • Compare SQL and NoSQL families and pick a store for a given workload.
  • Use Redis strings, hashes, lists, sets and sorted sets, expiry, and the main caching patterns.
  • Model and query documents in MongoDB, including embedding versus referencing and a basic aggregation.
  • Explain CAP, PACELC and eventual consistency without mixing them up with ACID.
  • Describe replication, replication lag, partitioning and sharding, and choose a shard key.
  • Manage schema changes with Flyway migrations, and release them safely.

SQL vs NoSQL, and when to use each

NoSQL is a family of non-relational stores (key-value, document, wide-column, graph, search) that trade some relational features, such as joins and strict schemas, for scale, flexibility or speed; start with a relational database and add a NoSQL store when a specific need justifies it.

What "NoSQL" means

NoSQL ("not only SQL") is a loose label for databases that do not use the relational model of tables, rows and joins. They appeared because some workloads (web-scale traffic, huge write volumes, flexible data, caching) were awkward or expensive to run on one relational server. Different NoSQL databases have little in common except that they are not relational, so always ask "which family?"

The main families

FamilyModelExamplesTypical use
Key-valueKey to opaque valueRedis, DynamoDB, Memcached Cache, sessions, counters, rate limits
DocumentJSON-like documents in collectionsMongoDB, Couchbase Catalogues, user profiles, content, flexible records
Wide-columnRows with many sparse columns, partitioned by key Cassandra, HBaseHuge write volumes, time series, event logs
GraphNodes and relationshipsNeo4jSocial graphs, recommendations, fraud rings
SearchInverted index over documentsElasticsearch, OpenSearch Full-text search, log analytics

ACID vs BASE

Relational databases promise ACID (Module 5). Many distributed NoSQL systems relax it to BASE: Basically Available (the system keeps answering even when parts fail), Soft state (data may change without input while copies converge) and Eventually consistent (if writes stop, all copies become equal after some time). BASE is a description of the trade-off, not a standard. Note that several NoSQL stores now offer transactions (MongoDB multi-document transactions, DynamoDB transactions), and PostgreSQL scales further than most people expect, so the line is blurrier than the slogans.

SQL vs NoSQL: a decision table

QuestionRelational (PostgreSQL, MySQL)NoSQL
Data shapeStructured, related, known upfrontVaried, nested, changing, or simple key lookups
Relationships and joinsFirst class: foreign keys, joinsWeak or absent; you embed or denormalize
TransactionsStrong multi-row ACIDVaries: often single-record only
Ad-hoc queriesFlexible: any column, any join, reportsFast only along the access patterns you designed for
SchemaEnforced by the databaseFlexible; the application enforces it
ScalingScale up easily; scale out needs effort (replicas, sharding)Many are built to scale out
ConsistencyStrong by defaultOften tunable or eventual
Maturity and skillsDecades of tools and experienceVaries by product

Practical rules:

  • Money, orders, inventory, anything with relationships and correctness rules: relational.
  • Cache, sessions, rate limiting, leaderboards: Redis next to your main database.
  • Documents read and written as a whole, with changing fields: a document store (or PostgreSQL JSONB, which is often enough).
  • Massive append-only writes with a known access pattern: wide-column.
  • Search with ranking and typo tolerance: a search engine, fed from the main database.
  • Deep relationship queries (friends of friends): a graph database.

Polyglot persistence

Polyglot persistence means using several databases in one system, each for what it does best. A food-delivery platform might keep orders and payments in PostgreSQL (the source of truth), cache menus and sessions in Redis, search restaurants in Elasticsearch, and store the rider location stream in a wide-column store. The price is real: more systems to run, monitor, back up and keep consistent. A copy in another store is a derived copy; the relational database stays the source of truth, and the copies are rebuilt from it (via events or change data capture). Add each new store only when a measured need appears.

Convention: default to PostgreSQL, justify every addition

A single PostgreSQL gives you transactions, joins, JSONB, full-text search and decent queue support. Many products never need anything else. Add Redis when caching or counters demand it, and others only with a clear reason.

Mistake: choosing NoSQL "because it scales" or "to avoid schema design"

A schema-less store moves the schema into your code and your data, where it is harder to check. Teams that start with a document database and later need joins and transactions face a painful migration. Choose by access patterns and consistency needs, not fashion.

Interview angle

  • SQL vs NoSQL? Relational: structured, relationships, ACID, flexible queries. NoSQL: specific models (key-value, document, wide-column, graph, search) for scale, flexibility or speed on known access patterns, often with weaker consistency.
  • ACID vs BASE? Strict transactional guarantees versus availability and eventual convergence.
  • Which database for X? Name the family and justify with the access pattern and the consistency need; mention that the relational database stays the source of truth.
  • What is polyglot persistence? Several stores, each for its strength, with one source of truth.

Redis: an in-memory data structure store

Redis keeps data in memory and offers rich types (strings, lists, hashes, sets, sorted sets) with sub-millisecond commands, key expiry and optional persistence; it is mostly used as a cache, session store, rate limiter or leaderboard next to the main database.

What Redis is

Redis (Remote Dictionary Server) is a key-value store where values are data structures. All data lives in RAM, so reads and writes take well under a millisecond, and a single server can handle hundreds of thousands of operations per second. Keys are strings; values can be several types, each with its own commands.

  • Mostly single-threaded command execution. One thread runs commands one at a time, so every command is atomic and there are no locks or race conditions inside a command. Recent versions use extra threads for network I/O, but commands still execute serially. The consequence: one slow command (such as KEYS * on millions of keys) blocks everyone.
  • Memory is the limit. Data must fit in RAM (or you set a limit and an eviction policy).
  • Persistence is optional (below).

Run Redis in Docker and connect

$ docker run -d --name redis -p 6379:6379 redis:7
$ docker exec -it redis redis-cli
127.0.0.1:6379> PING
PONG

Strings, expiry and counters

127.0.0.1:6379> SET greeting "hello"
OK
127.0.0.1:6379> GET greeting
"hello"
127.0.0.1:6379> SET session:abc123 "user:42" EX 60       # expire after 60 seconds
OK
127.0.0.1:6379> TTL session:abc123
(integer) 58
127.0.0.1:6379> EXPIRE greeting 300                      # set or change expiry
(integer) 1
127.0.0.1:6379> SET lock:order:7 "worker-1" EX 30 NX     # NX: only set if the key does not exist
OK
127.0.0.1:6379> SET lock:order:7 "worker-2" EX 30 NX
(nil)
127.0.0.1:6379> INCR page:views                          # atomic counter
(integer) 1
127.0.0.1:6379> INCR page:views
(integer) 2
127.0.0.1:6379> DEL greeting
(integer) 1

A key with no expiry lives forever. TTL returns remaining seconds, -1 if there is no expiry and -2 if the key does not exist. SET key value EX 60 NX is one atomic command: create only if absent, with an expiry. It is the basis of simple locks and of "do this only once" checks.

Data types

TypeWhat it isKey commandsUse
StringText, number or bytes (up to 512 MB)SET GET INCR EXPIRE Cached JSON, counters, flags
ListOrdered list (linked)LPUSH RPUSH LPOP RPOP LRANGE Simple queues, recent items
HashField-value map under one keyHSET HGET HGETALL HINCRBY Objects: user profile, cart
SetUnordered unique membersSADD SMEMBERS SISMEMBER SINTER Tags, unique visitors, "who liked this"
Sorted setUnique members ordered by a scoreZADD ZRANGE ZREVRANGE ZINCRBY ZRANK Leaderboards, priority queues, time-ordered feeds
 
# List: a queue of order ids
127.0.0.1:6379> RPUSH queue:orders 101 102 103
(integer) 3
127.0.0.1:6379> LPOP queue:orders
"101"
127.0.0.1:6379> LRANGE queue:orders 0 -1
1) "102"
2) "103"

# Hash: a cart
127.0.0.1:6379> HSET cart:42 item:5 2 item:9 1
(integer) 2
127.0.0.1:6379> HINCRBY cart:42 item:5 1
(integer) 3
127.0.0.1:6379> HGETALL cart:42
1) "item:5"
2) "3"
3) "item:9"
4) "1"

# Set: users who liked a restaurant
127.0.0.1:6379> SADD likes:restaurant:1 u1 u2 u3 u2
(integer) 3
127.0.0.1:6379> SISMEMBER likes:restaurant:1 u2
(integer) 1
127.0.0.1:6379> SCARD likes:restaurant:1
(integer) 3

# Sorted set: leaderboard of riders by deliveries
127.0.0.1:6379> ZADD board 120 asha 95 bilal 140 chitra
(integer) 3
127.0.0.1:6379> ZINCRBY board 10 bilal
"105"
127.0.0.1:6379> ZREVRANGE board 0 2 WITHSCORES
1) "chitra"
2) "140"
3) "asha"
4) "120"
5) "bilal"
6) "105"

Also good to know: Streams (XADD, XREAD, XREADGROUP) are an append-only log with consumer groups, a lightweight message queue. HyperLogLog (PFADD, PFCOUNT) counts unique items approximately in about 12 KB however many there are. Bitmaps (SETBIT, GETBIT, BITCOUNT) store one bit per id, such as "did user N log in today".

Persistence: RDB and AOF

RDB snapshotAOF (append-only file)
HowPeriodic point-in-time dump of all data to a fileLogs every write command; replayed at start
Data loss on crashChanges since the last snapshot (minutes)About 1 second with appendfsync everysec; none with always (slower)
Restart speedFastSlower (replays commands)
File sizeCompactLarger (rewritten periodically)

Use both for important data, or neither for a pure cache (a cache can always be rebuilt from the database). Redis is not a replacement for your primary database for data you cannot lose.

Memory limit and eviction

Set maxmemory and an maxmemory-policy in redis.conf (or with CONFIG SET). When memory is full, Redis evicts keys by the policy:

PolicyEvicts
noeviction (default)Nothing; writes fail with an out-of-memory error
allkeys-lruLeast recently used key among all keys (a good cache default)
allkeys-lfuLeast frequently used among all keys
volatile-lru, volatile-lfuSame, but only keys that have a TTL
volatile-ttlKeys with the shortest time to live
allkeys-random, volatile-randomRandom keys

Use cases

  • Cache: store query results or rendered pages with a TTL (next article).
  • Sessions: a login session as a hash or string with a TTL, shared by all app servers.
  • Rate limiting: INCR rate:user:42:minute, set EXPIRE 60 on first use, block when the count passes the limit.
  • Leaderboards: sorted sets give rank and top-N in logarithmic time.
  • Queues and streams: lists or Streams for simple work queues (for serious messaging use Kafka or RabbitMQ).
  • Distributed locks: SET lock:x token NX PX 30000 and release only if the token still matches (a small Lua script). Use with caution: a lock with a timeout can expire while the holder is still working, and a failover can lose the lock. Use libraries such as Redisson rather than hand-rolling, and never rely on it alone for money.
127.0.0.1:6379> INCR rate:user:42
(integer) 1
127.0.0.1:6379> EXPIRE rate:user:42 60 NX     # set expiry only if none yet (Redis 7+)
(integer) 1
# the app rejects the request when INCR returns a value greater than 5

From Java

@Service
public class MenuCache {
    private final StringRedisTemplate redis;

    public MenuCache(StringRedisTemplate redis) { this.redis = redis; }

    public void put(long restaurantId, String json) {
        redis.opsForValue().set("menu:" + restaurantId, json, Duration.ofMinutes(5));
    }
    public String get(long restaurantId) {
        return redis.opsForValue().get("menu:" + restaurantId);   // null if missing or expired
    }
}

Setup and the @Cacheable abstraction are in Spring Boot · Module 6. Official docs: redis.io/docs.

Rule: every cache key gets a TTL, and keys have a naming scheme

Use type:id:field style names (menu:12, session:abc) and always set an expiry for cached data, so stale or forgotten keys clean themselves up.

Mistake: KEYS * in production, and unbounded keys

KEYS * scans every key and blocks the single thread. Use SCAN (cursor-based, incremental). Also avoid huge values and giant lists/sets (millions of members in one key): one slow operation delays every client.

Edge cases

  • Setting a key again with SET removes its previous TTL unless you pass KEEPTTL.
  • Expiry is lazy plus periodic sampling, so an expired key can occupy memory for a short time.
  • Redis transactions (MULTI/EXEC) run commands together without interleaving, but have no rollback of commands that fail at run time. They are not SQL transactions.

Interview angle

  • Why is Redis fast? In-memory data, simple data structures, mostly single-threaded execution with no lock overhead, efficient protocol.
  • RDB vs AOF? Snapshots (fast restart, may lose minutes) versus a write log (less loss, larger and slower to replay).
  • When is Redis the wrong choice? As the only copy of important data, or for complex queries and joins.
  • Which type for a leaderboard? A sorted set (ZADD, ZREVRANGE).

Caching patterns: cache-aside, read-through, write-through, write-behind

A cache keeps recent results in fast storage in front of the database; cache-aside (the app checks the cache, then the database, then fills the cache) is the most common pattern, and the hard parts are invalidation and stampedes.

Why cache

The database is the slow, shared, expensive part of the request path. A menu that changes once a day but is read 10,000 times an hour should not hit PostgreSQL 10,000 times. A cache answers repeats from memory: faster responses and less load on the database. The price is the possibility of stale data: the cache and the database can disagree for a while.

Cache-aside (lazy loading)

The application owns the logic. It is the default pattern with Redis and Spring's @Cacheable.

public Menu getMenu(long restaurantId) {
    String key = "menu:" + restaurantId;
    String cached = redis.opsForValue().get(key);
    if (cached != null) {
        return json.fromJson(cached, Menu.class);                 // hit
    }
    Menu menu = menuRepository.loadMenu(restaurantId);            // miss: read the database
    redis.opsForValue().set(key, json.toJson(menu), Duration.ofMinutes(5));
    return menu;
}

public void updatePrice(long itemId, long restaurantId, long newPaise) {
    menuRepository.updatePrice(itemId, newPaise);                 // write the database first
    redis.delete("menu:" + restaurantId);                         // then invalidate the cache
}

Pros: only requested data is cached; a cache outage only slows things down. Cons: the first request after a miss is slow, and there is a window where cache and database differ.

Read-through

The application talks only to the cache. On a miss, the cache layer itself loads the data from the database and stores it. The logic moves out of your code into a cache library or provider (for example a loading cache with a loader function). From the caller's view it is the same as cache-aside, with less boilerplate.

Write-through

Every write goes to the cache, and the cache writes to the database synchronously before returning.

Cache and database stay consistent and reads are always fresh. Writes are slower (two writes), and you cache data that may never be read.

Write-behind (write-back)

Writes go to the cache and return immediately; the cache saves them to the database later, in batches.

Very fast writes and fewer database writes (good for counters like "views"), but if the cache fails before flushing, data is lost. Not for payments or orders.

PatternRead pathWrite pathRisk
Cache-asideApp checks cache, then DBApp writes DB, invalidates cache Stale window; cold misses
Read-throughCache loads from DB on a missUsually combined with another write pattern Cold misses
Write-throughAlways from cacheCache and DB together, synchronouslySlower writes
Write-behindFrom cacheCache first, DB laterData loss on cache failure

Cache invalidation

"There are only two hard things in computer science: cache invalidation and naming things." Your options:

  • TTL: let entries expire after N seconds. Simple; accept staleness up to N.
  • Explicit delete on write: delete (or update) the key when the data changes, as in the code above. Delete rather than update the cached value; it avoids two writers racing to set different values.
  • Versioned keys: include a version in the key (menu:12:v7) so old entries simply stop being used.
  • Combine TTL and delete: delete for freshness, TTL as a safety net against missed deletes.

A subtle race: reader A misses and loads old data; writer updates the database and deletes the key; reader A then writes the old data into the cache. A short TTL limits the damage.

Cache stampede (thundering herd)

When a popular key expires, hundreds of requests miss at once and all hit the database to rebuild it, which can overload it. Defences:

  • Single-flight / lock: only the first request rebuilds; others wait briefly or serve the old value.
  • TTL jitter: add a random few seconds to TTLs so many keys do not expire together.
  • Refresh ahead: refresh popular keys in the background before they expire.
  • Cache negative results too (a short TTL for "not found") so missing ids don't hammer the database.

Rule: the database is the source of truth; the cache must be rebuildable

You should be able to flush the entire cache and lose nothing but speed. If you cannot, the data does not belong only in a cache.

Mistake: caching everything, and caching without measuring

Cache data that is read often, changes rarely and is costly to compute. Caching per-user or rapidly changing data gives a low hit ratio and more staleness bugs than speed. Track the hit ratio; if it is low, the cache is not helping.

In real projects

Most Spring Boot services use cache-aside through @Cacheable and @CacheEvict with a Redis cache manager, for reference data (menus, configuration, product details), sessions and rate limits. Hot paths such as login, checkout and payments stay on the database.

Interview angle

  • Explain cache-aside. Check the cache; on a miss read the database and fill the cache; on a write update the database and delete the key.
  • Write-through vs write-behind? Synchronous write to both (consistent, slower) versus cache first and database later (fast, risk of loss).
  • How do you invalidate? TTL plus delete on write; versioned keys.
  • What is a cache stampede and how do you stop it? Many misses at once on an expired hot key; use a lock/single-flight, TTL jitter or background refresh.

MongoDB basics: documents, collections and queries

MongoDB stores JSON-like documents (BSON) in collections without a fixed schema; you query them with filter documents, update them with operators like $set and $inc, and analyse them with the aggregation pipeline.

The document model

A relational row is flat: columns of simple values. A MongoDB document is a nested structure of fields, arrays and sub-documents, stored as BSON (binary JSON, which adds types such as dates, 64-bit integers, decimals and ObjectId). A collection groups documents, roughly like a table, and a database groups collections. Documents in one collection need not have the same fields.

RelationalMongoDB
DatabaseDatabase
TableCollection
RowDocument
ColumnField
Primary key_id (every document has one, unique)
JoinEmbedding, $lookup

_id is the primary key. If you do not supply one, the server generates an ObjectId: a 12-byte value that includes a timestamp, so ids are roughly time-ordered and unique across servers. A single document can be up to 16 MB.

Run MongoDB and open mongosh

$ docker run -d --name mongo -p 27017:27017 mongo:7
$ docker exec -it mongo mongosh
test> use fooddb
switched to db fooddb

Create

db.restaurants.insertOne({
  name: "Spice Hub",
  city: "Pune",
  cuisines: ["North Indian", "Chinese"],
  rating: 4.3,
  isOpen: true,
  address: { line1: "12 MG Road", pincode: "411001" },
  menu: [
    { name: "Paneer Tikka", pricePaise: 22000 },
    { name: "Butter Naan",  pricePaise: 4000 }
  ]
})

db.restaurants.insertMany([
  { name: "Dosa Point", city: "Pune",  cuisines: ["South Indian"], rating: 4.6, isOpen: true },
  { name: "Biryani House", city: "Delhi", cuisines: ["Mughlai"], rating: 3.9, isOpen: false }
])
{ acknowledged: true,
  insertedId: ObjectId('6731a0c2f1d2a4b5c6d7e8f9') }

Read: find, filters, projection, sort, limit

db.restaurants.find({ city: "Pune" })                        // all Pune restaurants

db.restaurants.find(
  { city: "Pune", rating: { $gt: 4.0 } },                    // filter
  { name: 1, rating: 1, _id: 0 }                             // projection: which fields to return
).sort({ rating: -1 }).limit(5)                              // sort descending, top 5

db.restaurants.findOne({ name: "Dosa Point" })
db.restaurants.countDocuments({ isOpen: true })
[ { name: 'Dosa Point', rating: 4.6 }, { name: 'Spice Hub', rating: 4.3 } ]

The first argument is the filter document; { city: "Pune" } means equality, like WHERE city = 'Pune'. Several fields in one filter are combined with AND. The second argument is the projection (1 include, 0 exclude).

OperatorMeaningSQL equivalent
$eq, $neEqual, not equal=, <>
$gt, $gte, $lt, $lteComparisons >, >=, <, <=
$in, $ninValue in / not in a listIN, NOT IN
$and, $or, $notLogicAND, OR, NOT
$existsField is presentIS NOT NULL (roughly)
$regexPattern matchLIKE / regex
 
db.restaurants.find({ city: { $in: ["Pune", "Mumbai"] }, isOpen: true })

db.restaurants.find({
  $or: [ { rating: { $gte: 4.5 } }, { cuisines: "Chinese" } ]    // array field: matches if any element equals
})

db.restaurants.find({ "address.pincode": "411001" })             // dot notation for nested fields
db.restaurants.find({ "menu.pricePaise": { $lt: 10000 } })       // inside an array of sub-documents

Update and delete

db.restaurants.updateOne(
  { name: "Spice Hub" },                                      // which document
  {
    $set:  { isOpen: false },                                 // set or add a field
    $inc:  { reviewCount: 1 },                                // add to a number (creates it at 0 first)
    $push: { cuisines: "Biryani" }                            // append to an array
  }
)
db.restaurants.updateMany({ city: "Pune" }, { $set: { zone: "West" } })
db.restaurants.updateOne({ name: "New Place" }, { $set: { city: "Pune" } }, { upsert: true })

db.restaurants.deleteOne({ name: "Biryani House" })
db.restaurants.deleteMany({ isOpen: false })

Always use update operators. Passing a plain document (updateOne(filter, {isOpen: false})) is rejected by updateOne (only replaceOne replaces whole documents). Single-document operations are atomic, including updates of nested fields and arrays inside that document.

Indexes

db.restaurants.createIndex({ city: 1, rating: -1 })            // compound index, same prefix rule as SQL
db.restaurants.createIndex({ name: 1 }, { unique: true })
db.restaurants.find({ city: "Pune" }).sort({ rating: -1 }).explain("executionStats")
// winningPlan: IXSCAN (index scan) good;  COLLSCAN (collection scan) = full scan, bad on big collections

MongoDB uses B-tree indexes with the same ideas as Module 5: compound indexes follow the leftmost-prefix rule; indexes speed reads and cost writes. Without an index, a query scans the whole collection.

Aggregation pipeline basics

The aggregation pipeline passes documents through stages, each transforming the stream: the MongoDB equivalent of WHERE, GROUP BY, ORDER BY and joins.

db.restaurants.aggregate([
  { $match:   { isOpen: true } },                                     // WHERE
  { $group:   { _id: "$city",                                         // GROUP BY city
                avgRating: { $avg: "$rating" },
                count:     { $sum: 1 } } },
  { $sort:    { avgRating: -1 } },                                    // ORDER BY
  { $project: { _id: 0, city: "$_id", avgRating: 1, count: 1 } }      // choose and rename fields
])
[ { avgRating: 4.45, count: 2, city: 'Pune' } ]
db.orders.aggregate([
  { $match: { status: "DELIVERED" } },
  { $lookup: { from: "restaurants",            // other collection
               localField: "restaurantId",     // field in orders
               foreignField: "_id",            // field in restaurants
               as: "restaurant" } },           // result array
  { $unwind: "$restaurant" }
])

Put $match and $sort early so they can use indexes and shrink the stream. $lookup works but is not a replacement for relational joins; if you use it all the time, the data may belong in a relational database.

Embedding vs referencing

The central design decision. Embed related data inside the document, or reference it by storing the other document's _id (like a foreign key, without enforcement).

Embed whenReference when
Data is read together (menu with restaurant)Data is used on its own, from many places
One-to-few; the child list is small and boundedOne-to-many with unbounded growth (orders of a user)
The child belongs to one parent and dies with itMany-to-many, or shared between parents
You want atomic updates of parent and child togetherThe child changes often, or the document would approach 16 MB

Rule of thumb: model the data the way the application reads it. A restaurant page needs the menu: embed it. A user's order history grows forever: store orders in their own collection with userId. Duplicating a little data (restaurant name inside an order, like the snapshot columns in Module 4) is normal in document design.

When MongoDB fits, and when not

Good fit

  • Content, catalogues and profiles with varying fields
  • Data read and written as whole documents
  • Rapidly evolving schemas in early products
  • Horizontal scaling through sharding for large volumes

Poor fit

  • Many relationships and frequent joins
  • Multi-record transactions as the main workload (money, inventory)
  • Ad-hoc reporting across entities
  • Teams that need schema enforcement everywhere (possible with JSON Schema validation, but optional)

Spring Boot supports it with Spring Data MongoDB (MongoRepository, @Document). MongoDB does support multi-document ACID transactions on replica sets, but if you need them everywhere, a relational database is simpler. Official docs: mongodb.com/docs.

Mistake: unbounded arrays inside a document

Pushing every order into user.orders makes the document grow without limit, slows each update and eventually hits 16 MB. Embed only bounded data.

Edge cases

  • No foreign keys: nothing stops a reference to a deleted document. Your code must clean up.
  • Types are per value: rating: "4.3" (string) and rating: 4.3 (number) are different, and a filter on one will not match the other.
  • A compound index must match the query's leading fields, as in SQL.

Interview angle

  • Document vs row? Nested, schema-flexible BSON versus flat, fixed-column rows.
  • Embed or reference? Embed for bounded data read together; reference for unbounded or shared data.
  • How do you do a join? $lookup in an aggregation, or better, model so you do not need one.
  • When not MongoDB? Heavy relationships, multi-record transactions and strict consistency as the main need.

CAP theorem, PACELC and eventual consistency

When the network splits a distributed database into parts that cannot talk to each other (a partition), it must choose between answering with possibly stale data (availability) or refusing to answer until it can be sure (consistency); you cannot have both during a partition.

The three properties

Consistency (C)
Every read sees the latest write, or an error. All clients see the same data at the same time, as if there were a single copy. (This is not the ACID "consistency" about constraints.)
Availability (A)
Every request to a node that is working gets a non-error response, even if it might not be the newest data.
Partition tolerance (P)
The system keeps working when the network drops or delays messages between nodes.

Why you must choose

Imagine a balance stored on two servers, in Mumbai and Delhi, each with a copy. The link between them breaks (a partition). A client changes the balance in Mumbai. Now a client reads from Delhi.

  • Choose consistency (CP): Delhi refuses to answer (an error or a timeout) because it cannot be sure its value is current. The system is consistent but not available at Delhi.
  • Choose availability (AP): Delhi answers with its old value 100. The system is available but returns stale data, and the two copies must be reconciled when the network heals.

You cannot answer correctly and always answer. That is the CAP theorem: in the presence of a partition, you pick C or A. Partitions are not optional in a real network (cables fail, packets are lost, nodes pause), so "P" is a fact of life, and the actual decision is CP or AP. When there is no partition, a system can be both consistent and available. A single-node database has no partition to worry about.

CP and AP systems

CP: consistency firstAP: availability first
During a partitionMinority side stops serving (or reads and writes fail)Every side keeps serving; copies diverge
Typical examplesZooKeeper, etcd, HBase, MongoDB with majority settings, relational databases with synchronous replicationCassandra, DynamoDB (default reads), CouchDB, DNS
Good forMoney, inventory counts, leader election, configurationShopping carts, social feeds, likes, session data
CostErrors or higher latency when nodes are downStale reads, conflicts to resolve

Most modern products are tunable: Cassandra and DynamoDB let you ask for stronger consistency per query (at a cost in latency), and MongoDB lets you choose write and read concerns. Treat the CP/AP labels as defaults and tendencies, not as fixed properties.

Eventual consistency

Eventual consistency means: if no new writes arrive, all copies eventually become the same. In between, reads may return older values. A like count that shows 1,204 on one phone and 1,206 on another for a few seconds is fine. A bank balance that shows two different values is not. AP systems use eventual consistency, and need a rule for conflicts: last write wins (by timestamp), version vectors, or application-level merging. Read-your-writes problems (you post a comment and do not see it) are the usual user-visible symptom.

PACELC in one paragraph

CAP only talks about the rare case of a partition. PACELC extends it: if there is a Partition, choose A or C; Else (normal operation), choose between Latency and Consistency. Keeping replicas strictly in sync costs round trips, so even when everything works, a system that waits for all copies is slower than one that answers from the nearest copy. So a database is described by two choices, for example PA/EL (available during partitions and low latency otherwise, Cassandra's default style) or PC/EC (consistent always, paying latency, as in many strongly consistent stores). PACELC is a better way to talk about real trade-offs, but CAP is what interviewers ask first.

ACID consistency vs CAP consistency

ACID "C"CAP "C"
MeaningA transaction leaves the data valid: constraints and rules holdAll nodes return the same, latest value (linearizability)
ScopeRules inside one databaseAgreement between replicas of the data
QuestionIs the data valid?Is the data up to date everywhere?

Rule: CAP applies to distributed systems during a partition

A single PostgreSQL server is not "CA"; it is simply not distributed. Don't say "pick any two of three": P is not optional, so the choice is C or A when a partition happens.

Mistake: calling a database "CP" or "AP" without context

Behaviour depends on configuration (replication factor, read and write consistency, quorum). Say "by default, and with these settings" in an interview.

Interview angle

  • Explain CAP simply. In a network partition a distributed system must choose between consistency (refuse or error) and availability (answer, possibly stale).
  • Give a CP and an AP example, and when you would use each. CP for money and coordination (etcd, relational with sync replication); AP for carts, feeds and likes (Cassandra, DynamoDB).
  • What is eventual consistency? Copies converge when writes stop; reads can be stale meanwhile.
  • PACELC? Else, when no partition, trade latency against consistency.

Replication, partitioning and sharding

Replication copies the same data to several servers for availability and read capacity; partitioning splits a big table into pieces, and sharding spreads those pieces across servers to scale writes and storage, with the shard key as the critical design choice.

Replication: primary and replicas

In primary-replica replication (older name: master-slave) one server, the primary, accepts all writes. It sends its changes (the write-ahead log in PostgreSQL, the binary log in MySQL) to one or more replicas, which apply them and serve reads.

Why replicate:

  • High availability: if the primary dies, promote a replica (failover).
  • Read scaling: reporting and read-heavy traffic go to replicas.
  • Backups and analytics on a replica without disturbing production.
  • Geography: a replica near the users.

Replication does not scale writes (one primary) and is not a backup (a bad DELETE replicates too).

Synchronous vs asynchronous

AsynchronousSynchronous
Commit returns whenThe primary has written itAt least one replica also has it
Write latencyLowHigher (network round trip)
Data loss if the primary diesPossible: the last changes may not have reached a replica None for acknowledged commits
If a replica is downPrimary continuesWrites may block (unless configured otherwise)
Common useMost deploymentsCritical data; often one synchronous replica plus asynchronous others

Replication lag and read-your-writes

With asynchronous replication a replica is always a little behind: replication lag, usually milliseconds but seconds or more under load. A user updates their profile (write to primary), the page reloads and reads from a replica that has not received the change yet, and shows the old data. This breaks read-your-writes consistency. Fixes:

  • Read from the primary for a short time after a write by that user (for example 5 seconds), or for the critical pages (checkout, order confirmation).
  • Route reads that must be fresh to the primary and tolerant reads (browse, search) to replicas.
  • Wait until the replica reaches the write's log position (supported by some systems).

In Spring Boot you typically configure two data sources and a routing rule, or let a proxy/managed service (such as an RDS or Cloud SQL reader endpoint) split the traffic. @Transactional(readOnly = true) is the natural hook for choosing a replica.

Failover

If the primary fails, a replica is promoted to be the new primary and clients are pointed to it. Tools such as Patroni (PostgreSQL) or managed cloud databases automate this. Risks: the promoted replica may be missing the last few async commits; and split brain, when the old primary comes back and still thinks it is the leader, so two servers accept writes. Good setups use a fencing/consensus mechanism to ensure only one primary.

Partitioning: splitting one big table

Partitioning divides a large table into smaller pieces on the same server, while queries still treat it as one table. Vertical partitioning splits columns (rarely used big columns into a side table, as in one-to-one splits). Horizontal partitioning splits rows by a rule. PostgreSQL has built-in declarative partitioning with three methods:

CREATE TABLE orders (
    id          BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    user_id     BIGINT NOT NULL,
    status      TEXT NOT NULL,
    placed_at   TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (id, placed_at)               -- the key must include the partition column
) PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at);

CREATE TABLE orders_2026_10 PARTITION OF orders
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-10-01') TO ('2026-11-01');
CREATE TABLE orders_2026_11 PARTITION OF orders
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-11-01') TO ('2026-12-01');

-- Only the November partition is scanned (partition pruning)
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM orders WHERE placed_at >= '2026-11-05' AND placed_at < '2026-11-10';

-- Removing a month of old data is instant, no huge DELETE
DROP TABLE orders_2026_10;
CREATE TABLE restaurants_by_region (id BIGINT, name TEXT, region TEXT NOT NULL)
    PARTITION BY LIST (region);
CREATE TABLE restaurants_west  PARTITION OF restaurants_by_region FOR VALUES IN ('Pune', 'Mumbai');
CREATE TABLE restaurants_north PARTITION OF restaurants_by_region FOR VALUES IN ('Delhi', 'Noida');

CREATE TABLE sessions (id BIGINT, user_id BIGINT NOT NULL) PARTITION BY HASH (user_id);
CREATE TABLE sessions_p0 PARTITION OF sessions FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 4, REMAINDER 0);
CREATE TABLE sessions_p1 PARTITION OF sessions FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 4, REMAINDER 1);
-- ... p2 and p3
MethodRuleUse
RANGEValue rangesDates: fast pruning and cheap removal of old data
LISTExplicit valuesRegion, tenant, country
HASHHash of a column modulo NEven spread when there is no natural range

Partitioning helps maintenance (drop old data, vacuum smaller pieces) and queries that filter on the partition key. It does not help queries that don't, and it adds management work. Don't partition a table until it is large (tens or hundreds of millions of rows).

Sharding: splitting across servers

Sharding is horizontal partitioning where the pieces (shards) live on different servers. When one machine can no longer handle the write load or the data volume, sharding spreads both. Each row belongs to exactly one shard, chosen by the shard key.

StrategyHowProsCons
RangeKey ranges map to shardsRange queries stay on few shards Hot spots: new ids or recent dates all hit the last shard
Hashhash(key) mod N picks the shardEven spread Range queries touch all shards; changing N moves most data
Directory / lookupA table maps key to shardFlexible placement The directory is a bottleneck and extra hop

Consistent hashing reduces data movement when you add or remove a shard: only a small part of the keys move instead of almost all. It is covered in System Design · Module 5.

Choosing a shard key
  • High cardinality with many distinct values, so data spreads out.
  • Even access: avoid a key where one value gets most of the traffic (a celebrity user, country = 'IN', or placed_at with range sharding). That shard becomes a hot spot.
  • Matches the main query: if most queries include user_id, shard by it so each query hits one shard.
  • Stable: changing a row's key means moving it between servers.
What gets harder
  • Cross-shard queries: a report over all users must ask every shard and merge the results (scatter-gather).
  • Joins and transactions across shards: expensive or unavailable; you need distributed transactions or sagas, or you design so related rows share a shard key (all of a user's orders on the user's shard).
  • Resharding: adding shards and moving data while the system runs.
  • Unique ids: sequences are per server, so use UUIDs or snowflake-style ids.
  • Operations: backups, schema changes and monitoring multiply.

Some databases shard for you (MongoDB sharded clusters, Cassandra, DynamoDB, Citus for PostgreSQL, Vitess for MySQL); with plain PostgreSQL or MySQL you build routing in the application.

Scale in order

  1. Fix queries and add indexes (Module 5).
  2. Add caching (caching patterns).
  3. Scale the server up (more CPU, RAM, faster disks).
  4. Add read replicas.
  5. Partition huge tables.
  6. Shard, as the last resort.

Rule: a replica is not a backup

Replication copies mistakes instantly. Keep real backups with point-in-time recovery and test the restore.

Mistake: sharding too early

Many systems that think they need sharding actually need an index, a cache or a replica. Sharding makes every later feature (joins, reports, transactions) harder. One well-tuned PostgreSQL server handles a very large workload.

Edge cases

  • A unique constraint in a PostgreSQL partitioned table must include the partition key, so a global unique email across partitions needs a different mechanism.
  • Replicas can apply changes in order but still lag behind each other, so two replicas may disagree briefly.
  • Long-running queries on a replica can be cancelled by replication conflicts (PostgreSQL "canceling statement due to conflict with recovery").

Interview angle

  • Replication vs sharding? Replication copies the same data (availability, read scale); sharding splits different data across servers (write and storage scale).
  • Sync vs async replication? Sync waits for a replica (no loss, slower); async does not (fast, may lose the last commits, lag).
  • How do you choose a shard key? High cardinality, even load, matches the main query, rarely changes.
  • Range vs hash sharding? Range keeps range queries local but creates hot spots; hash spreads evenly but scatters ranges.
  • Partitioning vs sharding? Same server versus many servers.

Schema migrations with Flyway and Liquibase

A migration tool keeps the database schema in versioned, reviewed files that run in order and are recorded in a history table; Flyway uses plain SQL files like V1__create_users.sql, Liquibase uses changelogs, and an applied migration must never be edited.

Why migrations

The code lives in Git; the database should too. Without migrations, developers run ad-hoc ALTER TABLE statements by hand, staging and production drift apart, nobody knows which version a database is at, and a new teammate cannot build a working database. A schema migration is a small, ordered, versioned script that moves the schema from one version to the next. The tool:

  • keeps a history table of which migrations ran,
  • runs the missing ones, in order, when the application starts or in the pipeline,
  • checks that already-applied scripts were not changed, and
  • makes every environment (laptop, CI, staging, production) reach the same schema.

Do not use Hibernate's spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=update for this. It guesses changes, never drops, has no history and no review. In production use validate (or none) and let the migration tool own the schema.

Flyway

Flyway is the simplest tool: migrations are plain SQL files named by a convention. In Spring Boot they live in src/main/resources/db/migration.

src/main/resources/db/migration/
  V1__create_users.sql
  V2__create_restaurants_and_menu_items.sql
  V3__add_orders.sql
  V4__add_users_mobile_index.sql
  R__refresh_reporting_views.sql
V<version>__<description>.sql
A versioned migration: capital V, a version number, two underscores, a description with underscores. Runs exactly once, in version order.
R__<description>.sql
A repeatable migration: no version. It runs again whenever its content (checksum) changes, after all versioned ones. Use it for views, functions and procedures that you recreate.
flyway_schema_history
The table Flyway creates to record every applied migration.
CREATE TABLE users (
    id         BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    email      TEXT NOT NULL,
    name       TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT uq_users_email UNIQUE (email)
);
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN mobile TEXT;
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_users_mobile ON users (mobile);
CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW v_daily_sales AS
SELECT placed_at::date AS day, count(*) AS orders, sum(total_paise) AS revenue_paise
FROM orders
GROUP BY placed_at::date;

The history table after a run:

 installed_rank | version |          description          | success
----------------+---------+-------------------------------+---------
              1 | 1       | create users                  | t
              2 | 2       | create restaurants and menu   | t
              3 | 3       | add orders                    | t
              4 | 4       | add users mobile index        | t
              5 |         | refresh reporting views       | t

The table also stores a checksum of each script. On the next start Flyway recomputes the checksums of already-applied files. If one differs (someone edited a past migration), it stops with an error such as "Migration checksum mismatch for migration version 2", because that database no longer matches what the file says. That is the reason for the iron rule below.

Flyway in Spring Boot

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.flywaydb</groupId>
    <artifactId>flyway-core</artifactId>
</dependency>
<!-- Flyway 10 and later: add the module for your database -->
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.flywaydb</groupId>
    <artifactId>flyway-database-postgresql</artifactId>
</dependency>
spring:
  flyway:
    enabled: true                         # default when flyway-core is on the classpath
    locations: classpath:db/migration     # default
    baseline-on-migrate: false            # set true only to adopt an existing database
  jpa:
    hibernate:
      ddl-auto: validate                  # Hibernate checks entities against the schema, never changes it

With flyway-core on the classpath, Spring Boot runs pending migrations at start-up, before JPA uses the schema. The version of the Flyway artifacts is managed by Spring Boot's dependency management. See Spring Boot · Module 3 for the data-access setup.

The Flyway command-line tool has the same main commands: migrate (apply pending), info (status of each migration), validate (check checksums), baseline (mark an existing database as a given version, so only later migrations run), repair (fix the history table), and clean (drops everything; never in production, disable it).

Liquibase

Liquibase describes changes in a changelog made of changesets. The file can be XML, YAML, JSON or SQL, and Liquibase can generate SQL for the target database, which helps when you support several databases. Each changeset is identified by id + author + file path, and recorded in the DATABASECHANGELOG table (with a checksum; a lock table DATABASECHANGELOGLOCK prevents two runs at once).

databaseChangeLog:
  - changeSet:
      id: 1-create-users
      author: asha
      changes:
        - createTable:
            tableName: users
            columns:
              - column:
                  name: id
                  type: BIGINT
                  autoIncrement: true
                  constraints: { primaryKey: true, nullable: false }
              - column:
                  name: email
                  type: VARCHAR(255)
                  constraints: { nullable: false, unique: true }
  - changeSet:
      id: 2-add-users-mobile
      author: asha
      changes:
        - addColumn:
            tableName: users
            columns:
              - column: { name: mobile, type: VARCHAR(20) }
      rollback:
        - dropColumn: { tableName: users, columnName: mobile }

Spring Boot looks for classpath:/db/changelog/db.changelog-master.yaml by default when liquibase-core is on the classpath (change it with spring.liquibase.change-log).

Flyway vs Liquibase

FlywayLiquibase
FormatPlain SQL files (also Java migrations)XML, YAML, JSON or SQL changesets
Learning curveVery low: you already know SQLHigher: its own change vocabulary
Database independenceWrite SQL per databaseAbstract changes; one changelog for many databases
RollbackUndo is a paid/edition feature; normally you write a new forward migration Rollback blocks per changeset
History tableflyway_schema_historyDATABASECHANGELOG
Typical choiceOne database, SQL-first teamsMulti-database products, teams that want abstraction

Either is fine. Most Spring Boot teams on PostgreSQL pick Flyway for its simplicity. Official docs: Flyway documentation and Liquibase documentation.

The iron rule: never edit an applied migration

Once a migration has run anywhere beyond your own laptop (shared dev, CI, staging, production), it is history. Editing it breaks the checksum and leaves different databases with different schemas. To change something, add a new migration: V5__fix_users_mobile_length.sql. If you made a mistake before pushing, you may delete and redo it locally.

Safe migration practices

On a running system, old and new versions of your application run together for a moment during a deploy, and a long lock on a hot table can take the site down. Follow these habits:

  • Make changes backward compatible. The old code must keep working against the new schema.
  • Add, don't change in place. Adding a nullable column or a new table is safe; renaming or dropping a column the old code uses is not.
  • Watch locks. Many ALTER TABLE forms take a strong lock. Use short lock_timeout; create indexes with CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY in PostgreSQL (it cannot run in a transaction, so configure that migration to run outside one).
  • Backfill in batches (thousands of rows per transaction), not one giant UPDATE that locks the table and bloats the log.
  • Test migrations against a copy of production-size data and a real PostgreSQL (Testcontainers), not only H2.
  • Keep each migration small and reviewed like code. Take a backup before risky ones.

Expand, migrate, contract

To change something incompatible, such as renaming users.full_name to name, split it across several releases:

  1. Expand. Add the new column (nullable). Old code is unaffected. 
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN name TEXT;
  2. Migrate. Deploy code that writes both columns and still reads the old one. Copy existing data in batches, then deploy code that reads the new column. 
    UPDATE users SET name = full_name
WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM users WHERE name IS NULL LIMIT 10000);
  3. Contract. When no running code uses the old column, drop it in a later release. 
    ALTER TABLE users ALTER COLUMN name SET NOT NULL;
ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;

Each step can be deployed and rolled back independently, with zero downtime. The same pattern covers changing a type, splitting a table or adding a NOT NULL column.

Rule: migrations are append-only and run in the same order everywhere

Never edit or delete an applied migration; never reuse a version number. If two branches create V7, renumber one before merging (Flyway can also be set to allow out-of-order versions, but avoid relying on it).

Mistake: ddl-auto=update in production, and edited migrations

"It worked on my machine" because Hibernate quietly changed a table. Move all schema changes to migrations and use ddl-auto=validate. When a checksum error appears after someone edited an old file, revert the edit and add a new migration; use repair only when you understand why.

Edge cases

  • Adopting Flyway on an existing database: set a baseline (baseline-on-migrate: true or flyway baseline) so existing objects are treated as the starting version.
